← oral practice

NCLC 7 floor

15 prompts · A 7 · B 8Writing →
A-01A-02A-03A-06A-04A-12A-09B-21B-03B-05B-22B-04B-07B-26B-09
The question

What’s the LEAST amount of things I can say in the response for this prompt, such that after I have said that, the examiner would go: “surely i can’t give this kid less than an NCLC 7/B2+?????”. Like past that point, the rest of my response would have to be DISASTEROUS for me to score any below an NCLC 7/ B2+ . What’s that point exactly? And what’s the least amount of words that point can be?

One prompt from each of the 15 consolidated families (the most frequent prompt of each). For each: the point (where in the response it falls), the box (every word you say up to that point, in English; the examiner’s turns in brackets), its word count, then why that box and why nothing less. Built from scratch: three subagents derived the first principles, one subagent per prompt built its box, and reviewer subagents attacked every box.

What came out

The answer. Section A: 100 to 105 English words, said over four or five of your turns, which is about one minute of your French. The point falls about 1:15 to 1:25 into the five-minute call. Section B: 100 to 128 English words over three turns. The point falls about 1:10 to 1:30 into the ten minutes. The table below gives each prompt's count.

Why the bar is higher than "B2". On the TEF's own score tables, NCLC 6 is the lower half of B2 and NCLC 7 is the upper half. So the box has to show B2 beyond doubt on every criterion, plus a glimpse of C1, not B2 on the balance of probabilities.

What sets the length. In Section A the evidence itself fits in about 75 to 85 words. What pushes the box to about 100 is time: a rater needs about a minute of your speech before taking your control of grammar and pace as given. In Section B the evidence sets the length: a presentation in your own words plus two objection replies.

Where the proof sits. In your replies, not your opener, because a rater discounts anything that could have been learned by heart. Section A: ask the price, so the examiner gives the "it depends" their booklet tells them to give, then reword their answer and draw a consequence from it, then ask a hypothetical that only fits this ad. Section B: present the ad in your own words, name the objection yourself, then answer two pushes, each opening on the friend's own words.

What the box assumes about you. After the point you need to sound like a solid NCLC 6 on a quiet day: plain, correct, still asking or still answering. The box supplies the "beyond doubt" and the C1 glimpse that a 6 lacks. It can't lift a remainder that sits at NCLC 5.

What is least certain. The publisher doesn't say how the grid's ticks become a score, or whether the second rater (who only hears the recording) rates your best evidence or your typical level. And the examiner may not take the bait you set: every box's working has fallback lines for the other likely replies.

First principles

Merged from three independent derivations (what the test measures, how the examiner decides, the shortest speech that carries the evidence). "You" is Min. Facts marked with a source come from the publisher's own documents; the rest is reasoning, and the least certain parts are listed at the end.

A. The facts everything stands on

F1. Two raters, and the one that counts only hears audio. The examiner in the room plays the role and fills in a grid. Every test is recorded, and the recording goes to the publisher's (CCI Paris Île-de-France) pool of raters, who give the definitive evaluation. [source: CCI, Livret de l'examinateur TEF, expression orale, 2024, pp. 4, 7; TEF Canada results page] The only published rule for combining the two raters is for the sister test TEF IRN: when they agree or sit one level apart, the result is the mean of their marks on each criterion; further apart, a CCI lead arbitrates. [source: France Compétences, TEF IRN reference document] We assume TEF Canada works the same way.

F2. The grid: four criteria per section, two boxes per level. Section A: ability to obtain information, syntax, lexis, delivery (fluency, pronunciation, intonation). Section B: ability to present and debate, then the same three. Each criterion runs from below A1 to C2, and every level from A2 up has a lower and an upper box. [source: CCI, TEF oral evaluation grid, 2025; Livret 2024, Annexe 5a] Connectors and linking (coherence) are scored under syntax; register ("adapting to the person you're talking to") under syntax, lexis and the task. You get about a minute to read each subject before it starts; that minute is not recorded. [source: Livret 2024, Annexes 1 and 6a]

F3. NCLC 7 is the upper half of B2 on the TEF. IRCC: NCLC 6 = 271-309, NCLC 7 = 310-348 (0-450 scale). The CCI's own table puts both NCLC 6 and NCLC 7 inside B2 (271-348). On the 0-699 scale printed on certificates since December 2023, NCLC 7 = 456-493 inside B2 = 400-499. [source: canada.ca language-testing page; CCI NCLC correspondence tables] So the 6 / 7 line runs through the middle of B2. "B2" gets you a 6. "B2 beyond doubt, with a glimpse of C1" gets you a 7.

F4. What B1, B2 and C1 look like on the grid (paraphrased):

Criterion B1 B2 C1
A: obtaining information satisfactory questions; asks for some unclear answers to be made precise appropriate questions; reacts with confidence to the answers, even unexpected ones; the exchange holds together complete, precise questions; well-judged interventions; the conversation is sustained
B: presenting and debating simple presentation with an effort to reword; arguments clear but thin; justifies clear, detailed presentation; arguments illustrated or detailed; defends ideas clearly and firmly; the exchange holds together developed, structured presentation; varied, well-developed arguments; well-judged interventions
Syntax some common complex sentences; frequent errors, meaning clear simple and common complex sentences used correctly; errors cause no misunderstanding a wide variety of structures; rare errors
Lexis adapts with paraphrase or borrowings from other languages fairly wide repertoire; approximations do not hinder wide, well-controlled repertoire; gaps covered without visible effort
Delivery frequent hesitation; pronunciation sometimes hinders fairly even pace; clear, natural intonation natural and fluid

Below B1 the grid names the habits that sink a response: memorised, stereotyped phrases (A1/A2 syntax); in A, a conversation that depends entirely on the examiner (A1) or "simple, very general" questions (A2); in B, reading the document (A1), reading plus paraphrase and repetitive persuasion (A2). [source: CCI grid 2025]

F5. The examiner's playbook is published. [source: Livret 2024, pp. 8-10]

  • Section A: answer partially first. The booklet's own example: to "how much is it?" the examiner says "it depends, we have several packages", so the candidate has to follow up. The examiner prepares unexpected elements, restarts a candidate who runs dry with statements, never asks the candidate questions, and ends the call at 5 minutes.

  • Section B: the examiner says beforehand "I won't agree, you'll have to convince me". Objections come from a confidential list for the subject plus common stereotypes. The examiner pushes thin arguments for development and asks for new ones ("the same argument often comes back"), steers back to the presentation if it is skipped, and does not shut the discussion with objections that cannot be answered.

F6. Raters discount rehearsed speech. In oral tests, raters spot rehearsed stretches through recurring prep-book wording and through jumps in speaking speed, and rehearsed material does not count as evidence of level. The research is on other tests (ACTFL, OPIc, English tests), not the TEF. [source: Gates et al. 2020, Language Testing in Asia; Language Assessment Quarterly 17(3), 2020]

B. The principles

P1. Design for an audio listener. Nods and smiles count for nothing. Every piece of evidence must be audible on the recording, and must make sense without seeing the room. A French subjunctive that sounds like the indicative (que j'apporte) proves nothing.

P2. Each criterion has a "can" half and a "keeps doing it" half, and they are proved in opposite ways. "Uses complex sentences correctly", "reacts to answers", "arguments illustrated", "defends firmly" are capacities: one clear instance proves them, because a weaker speaker rarely produces one. "Errors cause no misunderstanding", "fairly even pace", "the exchange holds together" are control: no single sentence proves them; they are presumed from clean speech and can only be disproved by a pattern. So the box proves every capacity and starts the control. After it, nothing new needs proving; the rest can only fail to disprove. That asymmetry is why "a point" exists.

P3. Most words carry no evidence. For the 6 / 7 decision, a sentence counts only if a 7 says it much more often than a 6. Three kinds of words carry nothing:

  1. prep-book phrases every candidate learns ("I'm calling about your ad", "I'd like some information");
  2. words printed on the ad (you are reading them);
  3. generic questions that ignore the last answer ("and what about the price?"). A bare "how much? where? when?" is the grid's own A2 description ("simple, very general questions"). Every question needs an anchor: something in the ad or in your own situation. Keep these only where the task needs them (the opening, the register, naming the ad), and keep them short.

P4. The proof has to sit in your replies. A rehearsed opener can't show level (F6). A sentence that builds on what the examiner just said can't have been memorised, and it proves four things at once: you listened, you can build French on the fly, you have the words, and you can do it at speed. So the hard structures go into reactive turns. The opener's only job is register and purpose.

P5. Call the probe yourself. The examiner's moves are predictable (F5), so ask for the test you can prepare for.

  • Section A: ask early the question the booklet scripts a partial answer for: price and what it includes. The "it depends" arrives in turn 2 instead of minute 3, and your follow-up can be prepared and still be a genuine reaction.

  • Section B: after the presentation and an argument, name the weak spot you have a counter for (the tent, the cost, the effort) and hand over. The first objection is then one you are ready for.

P6. One good sentence is a glimpse; a level needs replication. One fine reply can be luck or rehearsal and buys the lower B2 box. The rule: at least three moments of clear evidence in three different turns of yours, at least two of them replies to something the examiner said. In A that means two reactive turns, not one. In B it means two objection replies, not one; the second is what "defends firmly" means.

P7. The reply ladder. The same move sits at different levels depending on how it is made.

  • A2: "Sorry, can you repeat?" (more than once).
  • B1: "Can you tell me more about that?" (asks for detail; not enough for a 7).

  • B2: uses the answer to react and steer: "Ah, so it depends on the package. In that case, which one would suit a complete beginner?"

  • C1 glimpse: rewords the answer and draws a consequence: "If I understand correctly, the flights only come after the ground training, so if the weather is bad that day, would I lose both?" In B: B1 = "but it's nice!"; B2 = concede, counter with a detail or example, keep going when pushed again; C1 glimpse = a second argument of a different kind, or a presentation with clear structure. Every reactive turn in the box must be at the B2 rung or above, and the box needs one C1 glimpse on the task criterion.

P8. Pack across criteria, not within one. Every sentence is sampled by all four criteria at once: a single sentence can be a task move, a grammar sample, a word sample and a delivery sample. That stacking is free. Stacking grammar inside one sentence is not: each extra hard structure raises the chance the sentence comes out wrong, and an audible error in a hard structure is counter-evidence. Rules: at most two marked structures per sentence, at most one of them hard (subjunctive, if + past + conditional, dont, past infinitive); about 18 English words per sentence at most; never two dependent mood choices in one sentence. Use different structure types across the box, one of each: variety is itself the C1 glimpse for syntax.

P8b. Links count as grammar. Coherence is scored under syntax, so the connectors carry evidence too. Use at least three different kinds of link across a section (contrast, concession, consequence, addition: "on the other hand", "even if", "so", "besides"), beyond and / but / because. In B one of them must be a concession.

P9. In French, the upgrade is often shorter. "Are there any left?" becomes il en reste ?; "how do we get there?" becomes comment on y va ?; "the gear we'll need" becomes le matériel dont on aura besoin; "by paddling every day" becomes a gérondif. Make each needed sentence denser at the same length before adding a sentence.

P10. Precise words that are not on the ad. At least one precise word per turn, never one printed on the ad, no clichés everyone says ("unforgettable", "get out of your comfort zone"). Two kinds: transaction words that fit any Section A call (deposit, cancellation, postpone, refund, slot, the day before, in advance, on site, group rate) and domain words for this ad only (for paragliding: instructor, tandem, wind, take-off, landing, harness, weight limit).

P11. The "only here" test. A question or argument that makes sense for any ad ("what are your hours?", "it'll be fun") is what a prep list produces. One that only makes sense for this ad shows you understood the document and have its words. The box contains at least one only-here question (A) or only-here argument (B).

P12. Register is free evidence. A: vous, polite conditionals, one formal topic marker ("regarding"). B: tu, on, light informal markers ("listen", "honestly"). These swap words rather than add them, and the switch between sections shows you adapt register, a CLB 7 indicator.

P13. Scripted, yet robust to whatever the examiner says. The box assumes the examiner's most likely reply. Two techniques keep your next line valid whatever they actually say:

  1. Questions at right angles to the answer. Follow up on a dimension the answer can't settle (notice, cost, who, conditions, what if). "Is that included in the price?" works after any answer about content.

  2. Reword only a gist you can predict. "So if I understand correctly, it all depends on the weather" is safe after a weather question, because every real answer has that gist. Never script a reword of a price or a schedule.

P14. Turn length. Turns of a few words read as A2/B1 however correct they are ("exchanges are brief"); a 90-word speech turns a phone call into a monologue. Reactive turns of roughly 15 to 40 words: two or three clauses, then hand the floor back.

P15. Calibrate the box to your real level. A box far above how you speak unscripted is a problem twice over: a sentence clearly beyond the speaker's level suggests a script, and a high first impression makes later errors hit harder by contrast. Aim at clean upper B2 with one or two C1 glimpses, not a C1 showcase, and say it at your natural pace with pauses at clause boundaries. The right box is one whose remainder sounds like the same speaker on a quieter day.

P15b. Aim at the top of the band, using the criteria preparation can reach. Raters differ in severity about as much as the NCLC 7 band is wide, and a one-level split between the two raters counts as normal. A box aimed at the bottom of NCLC 7 is a coin flip. Within a section the criteria are averaged, so put the margin where preparation works: the task criterion (precise questions, a well-judged reword, a structured presentation, varied arguments), the range of structures, and precise words. That buys room for accuracy and delivery, which depend on your real control, to land at plain B2. Worked example with equal weights: task at C1-low, syntax and lexis at upper B2, delivery at lower B2 still averages to upper B2.

P16. Each section must reach the bar alone. The two grids are filled separately and combined by a formula that is not public. The only plan that works whatever the weights are is that every one of the eight ratings clears the bar. A strong A may lift a weaker B; don't count on it.

P17. Put the point early, never first. Early, because the first minute anchors both raters and every second after the point is buffer. Never first, because "reacts to answers" and "defends" need the examiner to have spoken. Expect the point at the end of your fourth or fifth turn: about 1:15 to 2:15 into Section A, about 2:00 to 3:30 into Section B.

P17b. The skeletons the sections impose.

  • Section A has no presentation: an opening that states purpose and your situation, then questions in chains. It needs at least one partial-answer chain (price: "it depends" then "depends on what?") and one reword-and-adapt chain ("so if I understand correctly, X; in that case, Y?"), because those can't happen without the examiner's turns, plus a contingency or hypothetical ("if the weather is bad on the day...").

  • Section B has a fixed order: present first (the examiner steers back if you skip it), then convince, and the friend objects. The presentation is tied to the friend (why I'm telling you), gives the ad's facts in your own words, says why it fits the two of you, and is signposted. Then illustrated arguments of two different kinds, a question that hands the turn over, and a full objection reply: restate the worry, grant part of it, counter with a concrete fact or solution, reaffirm. A second, shorter reply when the friend pushes again is what "defends firmly" sounds like.

P18. Don't spend the reserve, and don't invent facts. The rest of the section still needs fuel: six to eight plain questions for A, two or three further arguments for B. So the box takes the moves with the most evidence per word, not every good idea. And never state facts the ad doesn't print ("everything is included"); the examiner knows the ad. Hedge instead ("I'd be surprised if it cost more than a hotel"), which also gives you a subjunctive.

C. "The point", defined

The point is the end of the first turn of yours after which all five of these hold on the recording:

  1. Frame done. Register set (vous in A, tu in B), the ad identified, and in B the ad presented in your own words, not read.

  2. Every capacity proved.

    • Task: at least two reactive turns at the B2 rung, one of them a reaction to a partial answer, an unexpected condition or an objection, plus one C1 glimpse. In B, also a detailed presentation and at least one illustrated argument.

    • Syntax: at least four (A) or five (B) different marked structure types, used correctly, at least one inside a reactive turn; at least three kinds of link (P8b).

    • Lexis: about five (A) or six (B) precise words not on the ad, no clichés, no English words; in B, one natural informal expression.

    • Delivery: nothing in the box forces a pause (no word you can't say reliably). 3. Replication. Three moments of clear evidence in three turns, two of them replies (P6). 4. Control presumed. About 60 to 90 seconds of your speech with no pattern of trouble. 5. Credibility intact. Nothing sounds recited against what the examiner just said.

If an element can be removed and all five still hold, it was a dead word and goes.

D. "Disaster", defined

After the point, only a pattern pulls the score below 7: evidence that one criterion is B1 or lower, or something that makes the rater reread the box as recited.

Disaster What it looks like on the recording
D1 You can't be understood the examiner has to ask what you meant two or more times, or answers a different question
D2 The same basic error, again and again the same verb-form or gender error in most turns over a minute or more, or errors that change the meaning more than once
D3 Fluency collapse repeated long pauses inside sentences, strings of false starts, switching to English
D4 English words in French sentences more than once
D5 Section A runs dry the examiner has to restart you twice in a row, or you end the call well before 5 minutes
D6 Section B gives up you give in ("you're right, forget it"), repeat one argument three or more times, or stop answering what the friend says
D7 The script shows a learned line that ignores what the examiner just said, more than once
D8 Wrong register tu with the business or vous with the friend, kept up

Rough arithmetic behind "pattern" (equal weights, two raters averaged): one criterion dropping half a level for both raters costs about 5 points on the 0-450 scale; the NCLC 7 band is 39 points wide; from the middle of the band it takes about four such drops to fall to NCLC 6, and about seven from the top. One bad minute is one drop at most.

Not a disaster: the rest in plainer but correct sentences; reusing the box's structures; a few gender or agreement slips; self-corrections (a quick repair is evidence for upper B2, not against it); one "sorry, could you say that again?"; one question they had already answered; one weak reply to a hard objection; repeating one argument once; running dry 20 seconds before the end and closing politely; not convincing the friend (the grid rewards defending, not winning).

What this assumes about you. The box supplies the "beyond doubt" and the C1 glimpse that a 6 lacks. It cannot lift a remainder that sits at NCLC 5. After the point you need to sound like a solid NCLC 6 on a quiet day: plain, correct, still asking, still answering.

E. English that produces the right French

The box is English, spoken later in French, so the English wording decides which French structure appears.

English Natural French Structure Risk
I'd like to know whether... J'aimerais savoir si... conditional + indirect question low
Could you tell me how long...? Pourriez-vous me dire combien de temps... ? polite conditional + indirect question low
It depends on what, exactly? Ça dépend de quoi, exactement ? reaction to a partial answer low
So if I understand correctly... Donc, si je comprends bien... rewording check low
In that case, ... Dans ce cas, ... link to the last answer low
That's exactly why... Justement, c'est pour ça que... turning an objection around low
What I like is that... Ce qui me plaît, c'est que... cleft low
Are there any left? / How do we get there? Il en reste ? / Comment on y va ? en / y low
Is the equipment provided? Est-ce que l'équipement est fourni ? passive low
by paddling a bit every day en pagayant un peu chaque jour gérondif low
It's true that..., but... / even if... C'est vrai que..., mais... / même si... concession low
We've been saying for months that... Ça fait des mois qu'on dit que... ça fait... que + present low-med
the trip I told you about le voyage dont je t'ai parlé dont med
If I came with a friend, would you...? Si je venais avec un ami, est-ce que vous... ? if + past + conditional med
How about we...? Et si on... ? suggestion with imperfect med
When I get there... Quand j'arriverai... future after quand med
so that you can... pour que vous puissiez... audible subjunctive med
I understand that it scares you Je comprends que ça te fasse peur audible subjunctive med
I'd be surprised if it cost more than a hotel Ça m'étonnerait que ce soit plus cher qu'un hôtel subjunctive + hedge med
unless the weather is bad à moins qu'il fasse mauvais audible subjunctive med
after doing the ground training après avoir fait la formation au sol past infinitive med-high
in case it rains au cas où il pleuvrait conditional after au cas où high

Wording to avoid: "so that I can" (same subject gives pour pouvoir, no subjunctive); subjunctives of regular -er verbs in je / tu / il forms (inaudible; use être, avoir, faire, aller, pouvoir, venir, prendre or vous forms); "I'd like to know what..." (invites qu'est-ce que in an indirect question; prefer whether / how much / how / when); "although" (pulls bien que, risky and memorised-sounding; prefer "even if"); English idioms with no plain French equivalent.

F. What we are least sure of

  1. That NCLC 7 equals the upper B2 box. Inferred from the CCI tables and the two boxes per level; the tick-to-score conversion is not public. The direction of the advice (B2 beyond doubt plus a C1 glimpse) holds either way.

  2. How the room rating and the recording rating combine, and whether the central rater rates the best evidence heard or the typical level. If it is the typical level, the box locks less and the remainder matters more.

  3. That both sections weigh the same. Plausible from the grid layout, not documented.

  4. That TEF raters discount rehearsed speech. The evidence comes from other tests.
  5. Video administration. The publisher's documents describe face to face only.
  6. The numbers (three moments, two replies, two structures per sentence, the disaster thresholds) are working estimates derived from the descriptors, not published cut-offs. The structure behind them is the robust part.
The standards every box was held to

These came out of the red-team reviews. They apply to all 15 boxes so the boxes are held to one standard.

  1. Audible subjunctives only. A line counted as carrying a subjunctive must be English whose natural French can only come out audible (fasse, soit, puisse, aille, ait, vienne, prenne, or a vous form). English that first comes out as a regular -er verb in je / tu / il / ils (te méfies, coûte, apporte) proves nothing on the recording: reword it or stop counting it.

  2. No indirect "what". "I know what you'll say", "tell him what you think", "I'd like to know what..." invite qu'est-ce que in an indirect question, a common learner error. Reword ("You'll tell me...", "give him your verdict on...").

  3. Section B hand-over. Naming the weak spot is the hand-over. Add a question after it only if the stage direction that follows answers that question.

  4. Grants and reaffirms. At least one concession across the two objection replies (the link condition needs it). A grant or reaffirm stays only when it carries a needed structure or link, or the logic of the next sentence.

  5. Time floor, one conversion for all. Condition 4 of the point (about 60 to 90 seconds of your speech) is a floor. Convert the same way everywhere: French words = English words x 1.05; you speak 120 French words a minute; add 10% for pauses. So seconds of your speech = English words x 0.58, and 60 seconds = about 104 English words. A box must reach at least about 100 English words (58 seconds). Below that, cutting does not move the point earlier; the box just ends before the point. So in a box at the floor, a dead word is swapped for evidence (a precise word, a link, an uptake), not deleted; above the floor, dead words go. State the timing on this conversion after any change.

  6. What counts as a link. Only connectors that join clauses or turns. Three different relations per section, from: contrast (on the other hand, whereas), concession (even if, it's true that... but), consequence (so, in that case, that's why), addition (besides, what's more), cause (since, given that). Plain and / but / because don't count. An "if" clause is syntax, not a link (don't count it twice). A topic marker ("regarding"), a negated noun phrase or a preposition before a noun is not a link. In Section B one of the three must be a concession.

  7. One timing basis. The clock starts at the examiner's first line of the section ("Hello?" in A, "Hi! How are you?" in B). Time spent explaining the roles before that does not count.

  8. Lexis the English doesn't produce. Count a precise word only if the English produces it on its own. If the box relies on a specific French rendering ("not cheap" as pas donné, "company" as boîte), put a translator note in Working, in a short list headed "Translator notes". A note may only choose between natural renderings of the English; it never overrules the English (see 15).

  9. Facts not on the ad. Anything the ad doesn't print is hedged ("probably", "I'd be surprised if"), asked as a question, or cut. An unhedged invented fact is a target for the examiner.

  10. Uptake on the "it depends" reply (Section A). The reply to the partial answer must audibly take something from it: a connector ("In that case"), the examiner's own noun ("which package"), or a reword. Say the noun rather than "which one" (lequel has to agree with a noun you may not have caught).

  11. One-sentence opener. In Section A, merge "I'm calling about X" into the purpose sentence when it can be ("I'm calling to find out how much your paragliding introduction costs").

  12. Precise means precise. Everyday words (advise, free, right away, meanwhile) and words printed on the ad are not precise words. Lists in Why and Working count only real ones.

  13. Lines that depend on what the examiner chose. Where a line echoes content the examiner picks (a workshop name, a transport mode), the box writes the most likely version, and Working says how to say it as a frame ("repeat their noun", "so I'd depend on...") so it never ignores what was said.

  14. Format. A Section A box opens with [examiner: "Hello?"]; a Section B box opens with [examiner: "Hi! How are you?"].

  15. The English makes its own French. The English is the box, so every line must produce the intended French on its own. If a line needs a note to overrule what it says ("paddle" meant to come out as ramer), change the English ("row"). If the English pulls in a structure the French in Working leaves out, fix one or the other.

  16. One C1 glimpse on the Section B task is enough. A structured presentation or a second argument of a different kind is enough (P7). Don't spend words on a second glimpse.

  17. Every objection reply opens on the friend's words (their noun, a reword, "even if you..."), so it is audibly a reply and not a recited line.

  18. Greeting in B: one word ("Great!"), then straight into the ad.

  19. Count only the precise words you choose. Not words the examiner supplies, not nouns made from verbs the ad prints, not everyday words.
Lens 1 · What is measured and how the score is formed

Lens 1: the instrument — what TEF Canada Expression orale measures and how the score is formed

Question for this lens: what exactly gets rated, by whom, on what scale, and how the ratings become an NCLC level? From that, what evidence rules out NCLC 6, what evidence can be settled early in a response, and what can't?

Every factual claim is labelled [source: …] (names are in the source list below) or [reasoned].

0. The answer in eight lines

  1. Each section has its own grid with four criteria: one task criterion and three language criteria (syntax, lexis, fluency/pronunciation). That makes 8 ratings for each rater. Each rating is a CEFR column, and every column from A1 to C2 has two tick boxes [source: CCI grid 2025; CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexe 5a].

  2. Two people rate: the live examiner and a second rater at CCI Paris, who works from the audio recording [source: CCI TEF Canada results page; CCI examiner handbook 2024].

  3. On the TEF's own tables, NCLC 7 is the upper half of B2 and NCLC 6 is the lower half of B2. B1 corresponds to NCLC 4–5 [source: CCI/AF Ottawa 2018 table; CCI sample attestation; CCI NCLC table 2024]. So the 6/7 line runs through the middle of B2. It is not the B1/B2 line.

  4. So NCLC 7 means: B2 is met clearly on the grid as a whole, with some C1 features. It does not mean "just B2" [reasoned].

  5. Some descriptor clauses are existential ("asks for clarification", "illustrated arguments", "common complex sentences"). One clean instance proves them early, and the proof can't be taken back. Others are proportional ("errors don't cause misunderstanding", "fairly even tempo", "exchanges follow on"). Those are judged on the whole section and are never locked [reasoned from the grid wording].

  6. The point = the moment every existential clause needed for B2-high, plus a few C1 sparks, has been shown in that section. After it, only a broad slide on the proportional clauses, or a collapse of the task, can pull the score below 7 [reasoned].

  7. Rough arithmetic, assuming equal weights: one criterion dropping half a level, by both raters, costs about 5 points on the 450 scale. The NCLC 7 band is 39 points wide. From the middle of the band it takes about 4 such drops to fall to NCLC 6. That is the instrument's definition of "disaster" [reasoned].

  8. A and B are rated on separate grids, so each section needs its own box. Only register switching is proved by the two sections together [reasoned].

1. Sources used (and what each one gives)

The grid and the handbook carry a "no reproduction" notice. Below I paraphrase them in English rather than copying them.

2. The facts, checked against FRAME.md

2.1 Format

  • Expression orale lasts 15 minutes, with one examiner, face to face. Section A is a 5-minute formal role-play: get information about a practical situation by asking precise questions, while the examiner plays the advertiser. Section B is a 10-minute friendly role-play: present a document, argue, defend your view and react to the friend's counter-arguments [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024; CCI responsable handbook 2026]. Matches FRAME.

  • Before each section the candidate gets about 1 minute to read the subject, and the examiner checks that the task is understood. This part is not recorded [source: e-TEF Canada candidate manual 2016; CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexe 1]. FRAME doesn't mention it.

  • The Section A instruction says to ask about ten questions. The Section B instruction says to present the document, then try to convince the friend [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexes 2–3]. Matches FRAME.

  • Video administration: none of the sources I read mentions it. The handbook describes face to face only. I could not verify FRAME's "or by video".

2.2 What the examiner is told to do (this decides what the box must survive)

  • Section A [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024]:

    • The examiner opens with "Allô ?" and lets the candidate lead.

    • At first the examiner gives deliberately partial answers. The handbook's own example: to "how much is it?" the examiner says it depends, there are several packages. The aim is to see whether the candidate bounces back and gets unexpected answers clarified.

    • The examiner prepares unexpected elements ("imprévus") to test how the candidate reacts.

    • The examiner does not ask the candidate questions. Relaunches are statements ("special insurance is needed", "we have a promotion right now").

    • The communicative goal has three sub-criteria: relevance of the questioning, initiative / handling the unexpected, and quality of the exchanges.

    • The examiner cuts the call at 5 minutes.

    • Section B [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024]:

    • The examiner restates the roles: the candidate presents the document first, then tries to convince the friend, and the examiner warns that the friend will disagree.

    • The examiner opens with "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?".

    • If the candidate skips the presentation, the examiner steers back to it.

    • The examiner holds prepared counter-arguments, but should not shut the discussion down early with unanswerable objections. The examiner should push the candidate to develop arguments and to bring new ones, avoid prompts that pull the candidate into storytelling, and encourage good arguments.

    • Three sub-criteria: presenting the facts, quality of argument, quality of the exchanges.

    • The examiner cuts at 10 minutes.
    • In both sections the full time must be used so there is enough to rate [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024]. So the response does not end at the box. The rest of the section always happens [reasoned].

2.3 The grid (2025 version)

[source: CCI grid 2025; CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexes 5a and 6a]

Each section's grid has four rows:

Row Section A Section B
1 Ability to obtain information Ability to present and debate (Annexe 6a calls it "exposer et débattre")
2 Syntax Syntax
3 Lexis Lexis
4 Fluency and pronunciation ("Aisance à l'oral, élocution") Fluency and pronunciation

The columns run <A1, A1, A2, B1, B2, C1, C2. Below <A1 there is one tick box. Below every other level there are two tick boxes [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexe 5a, checked on the rendered page]. The live examiner starts filling the grid during the conversation, finishes the A grid during the 1-minute B preparation, and fills the B grid in the 1.5 minutes after B [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexe 1].

Annexe 6a links each row to CEFR scales and to evaluation goals [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024]:

  • Task A: covers the CEFR scales for conversation, obtaining goods and services, information exchange, cooperating, asking for clarification and turn-taking. Goals: gather relevant information; get information made precise or developed; respect the communication situation and turn-taking.

  • Task B: the same scales plus spoken production and thematic development. Goals: present a situation, take ownership of it and put it in context; develop ideas with examples, secondary arguments, details and precisions; respect the situation and turn-taking.

  • Syntax: covers the CEFR scales for monitoring and repair, general range, grammatical accuracy, flexibility, and coherence and cohesion. So connectors and linking are scored under Syntax.

  • Lexis: covers compensation, monitoring, vocabulary range, vocabulary control and flexibility.

  • Syntax and Lexis share these goals: everyday spoken use; reformulating and clarifying one's own turns when needed; adapting to the interlocutor. That last one is where register lives.

  • Fluency/pronunciation: covers phonological control and fluency. Goals: intelligibility, intonation, and degree of ease, spontaneity and effort to communicate.

Level descriptors, paraphrased (B1 / B2 / C1 are the ones that matter):

Row B1 B2 C1
Task A Satisfactory questioning. Gets some incomplete or ambiguous answers made precise. Appropriate questioning. Reacts with confidence to the answers, even in unforeseen situations. Exchanges follow on from each other. Complete and precise questioning. Intervenes aptly. Conversation is sustained.
Task B Simple, clear presentation with an effort to reformulate. Clear arguments, little developed. Intervenes regularly and justifies the view. Clear, detailed presentation. Arguments illustrated or detailed. Defends ideas clearly and with determination. Exchanges follow on. Developed, structured presentation. Varied, well-developed arguments. Intervenes aptly. Conversation is sustained.
Syntax Organised speech with a few common complex sentences. Errors frequent, overall meaning clear. Simple and common complex sentences used correctly. Errors don't cause misunderstandings. Wide variety of structures used appropriately. Errors rare.
Lexis Wider repertoire that adapts to the situation with circumlocution or borrowings from other languages. Fairly broad repertoire. Confusions and approximations don't hinder communication. Broad, well-controlled repertoire. Gaps covered without visible effort.
Fluency Frequent hesitations, but clear speech. Pronunciation errors can sometimes hinder. Fairly even tempo. Clear, natural intonation. Accent doesn't hinder. Natural and fluent overall. Intonation mostly suited to the situation.

The low end matters for the "don't" rules. A1/A2 descriptors include memorised and stereotyped phrases. In Task A, A1 is a conversation entirely dependent on the examiner and A2 is simple, very general questioning. In Task B, A1 is mere reading of the document and A2 is reading and paraphrase with simple, repetitive persuasion [source: CCI grid 2025].

2.4 Who scores, and how the ratings combine

  • Every session is recorded. Recordings go to CCI Paris for a second rating by its pool of raters. The handbook calls it the candidate's definitive evaluation. A missing or cut-off recording means no result at all [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024, p. 4 and Annexe 8].

  • CCI's results page says Expression orale is rated by two independent examiners, the test leader and a second examiner, from the recording. The raw results are then turned into a calibrated score. Re-marking cannot be requested [source: CCI results page]. The 2026 handbook confirms two different raters [source: CCI responsable handbook 2026].

  • How the two ratings combine is published only for the sister test TEF IRN. If the two raters agree or are on adjacent levels, the final result is the mean of the two raters' marks on each criterion. If they are more than two levels apart, a CCI pedagogical lead arbitrates [source: TEF IRN referential]. I assume TEF Canada works the same way [reasoned, not verified].

  • I found no published weights for the 8 criteria, or for A against B [reasoned: absence of evidence]. In the arithmetic below I assume equal weights and flag it.

  • One older study describes the earlier TEF/TEFAQ grid: 12 criteria (three communicative criteria per role-play and six linguistic ones), each on a 21-point scale aligned to the six CEFR levels. In that study the two examiners reached a consensus [source: Chénier 2021]. The three communicative criteria per section survive today as the handbook's sub-criteria, folded into one task row [reasoned].

2.5 Scales and the NCLC mapping (the key finding)

  • IRCC uses the certificate's "Équivalence ancien score" column. For Expression orale: NCLC 4 = 181–225, 5 = 226–270, 6 = 271–309, 7 = 310–348, 8 = 349–370, 9 = 371–392, 10 = 393–450 [source: IRCC language testing]. Matches FRAME.

  • On that same 0–450 scale, CCI's table puts B1 = NCLC 4–5 (181–270), B2 = NCLC 6–7 (271–348) and C1 = NCLC 8–10 (349–415) [source: CCI/AF Ottawa 2018 table, checked on the rendered page].

  • The main score on the certificate is out of 699. For Expression orale on tests from 11 Dec 2023: NCLC 6 = 422–455, NCLC 7 = 456–493, NCLC 8 = 494–517. Between Oct 2019 and Dec 2023 the bands were NCLC 6 = 400–449 and NCLC 7 = 450–499 [source: CCI NCLC table 2024]. On the /699 scale, B2 = 400–499 [source: TEF IRN results page shows the same 100-point bands, with B2 starting at 400; search summaries of CCI's "Correspondance TEF–CECR" PDF say 400–499 = B2. I could not open that PDF (404)].

  • CCI's sample certificate shows Expression orale 490/699 → B2 → 344/450 → NCLC 7, and on the same certificate Expression écrite 415/699 → B2 → 299/450 → NCLC 6 [source: CCI sample attestation]. So a B2 on the certificate can be either NCLC 6 or NCLC 7.

  • Where the 6/7 line falls inside B2:

    • On the 0–450 scale, the midpoint of 271–348 is 309.5, and the 6/7 cut is 310.
    • On the 2019–2023 /699 scale, the midpoint of 400–499 is 450, and the cut is 450.
    • On the current /699 scale, the cut is 456.

    In all three tables, the 6/7 line runs through the middle of B2 [reasoned: arithmetic on the sourced tables].

  • B1 and B2 each cover exactly two NCLC levels, and each grid column has two tick boxes. The simplest reading:

    • B1 low ≈ NCLC 4, B1 high ≈ NCLC 5.
    • B2 low ≈ NCLC 6, B2 high ≈ NCLC 7.
    • C1 spreads over NCLC 8–10.

    This is [reasoned]. CCI does not publish what the two boxes mean, and the score is "calibrated", not a plain average. With 16 ratings averaged, the result is near-continuous anyway. "B2-high" below is shorthand for "the combined rating sits in the upper half of B2".

2.6 Where the sources disagree with FRAME / the brief (flag for synthesis)

  1. "NCLC 7 / B2+". On the TEF's own tables, NCLC 7 is the upper half of B2. That is not the CEFR "plus level" B2+, which the CCLB study places at CLB 9 [source: CCLB alignment report, Table 6]. Min's intuition is right in spirit ("upper B2"). The label is loose.

  2. Lens 3's brief frames it as "B1/NCLC 6 vs B2/NCLC 7". On the TEF tables, B1 maps to NCLC 4–5, and NCLC 6 is already B2 [source: CCI/AF Ottawa 2018 table]. The real split is B2-barely vs B2-clearly (plus C1 sparks). B1 features are not enough even for NCLC 6.

  3. The CCLB's own CLB↔CEFR alignment is more lenient: CLB/NCLC 7 ≈ B1+ ("a very strong B1"), with the French-speaking panel leaning B2 [source: CCLB alignment report, pp. 15–18 and 29]. That is a descriptor-level alignment. What decides Min's score is the TEF's own score-to-NCLC conversion, which is stricter (upper B2) [reasoned].

  4. FRAME says "one examiner". That is true for the live part, but there are two raters, and the second is a CCI rater listening to audio only [source: CCI results page; CCI examiner handbook 2024].

3. The NCLC 7 benchmark and CEFR B2 alongside the grid

CLB 6 → CLB 7, speaking (paraphrased) [source: CLB 2012, pp. 54–57]:

Aspect CLB 6 CLB 7
Situations Some confidence in routine social situations; concrete information on familiar topics Some confidence in many daily situations; concrete and some abstract information
Interaction Asks clarifying questions to signal partial understanding Asks follow-up questions to keep the conversation going; confirms understanding by repeating or paraphrasing; holds the floor
Phone Short calls (strangers can be hard) Routine calls; clarifies and confirms
Persuading Gives reasons and predicts consequences Uses appropriate persuasive arguments
Opinions Agrees and disagrees Qualifies opinions; expresses reservations, possibilities and probabilities; needs little support
Grammar Some variety; difficulties sometimes impede Increasing variety; difficulties occasionally impede
Vocabulary Everyday range Expanding range, including idiomatic language
Register Some degrees of formality Adapts style and register to different audiences and situations

CEFR B1 → B2, spoken qualitative aspects (paraphrased) [source: CEFR 2001 Table 3, as reproduced in the CCLB alignment report]:

Aspect B1 B2
Range Enough to get by, with hesitation and circumlocution Enough for clear descriptions and viewpoints without much visible searching, using some complex sentence forms
Accuracy Routines and patterns for predictable situations Relatively high control; no errors that cause misunderstanding; corrects most mistakes
Fluency Planning and repair pauses are very evident in longer stretches Fairly even tempo; few noticeably long pauses
Interaction Repeats back to confirm understanding Takes and ends turns; helps the discussion along by confirming comprehension
Coherence A linear sequence of points A limited number of cohesive devices, giving clear, coherent discourse
Arguing Briefly gives reasons for opinions Develops a clear argument with supporting points and relevant examples; accounts for and sustains views; makes and responds to hypotheses (informal discussion)

The CEFR's convention for plus levels: a plus level is a strong performance at the level with elements of the next level [source: CCLB alignment report, p. 29: "B1+ has elements of B2, being a transition to B2"]. That is the best public model for what makes a rater tick the upper box of a column [reasoned].

Crosswalk [reasoned]: every CLB 7 marker above has a home on the TEF grid.

CLB 7 marker TEF grid home
Follow-up questions, paraphrase to confirm Task A: reacts, exchanges follow on
Persuasive arguments Task B: illustrated arguments
Qualifying, possibilities, hypotheses Syntax (conditional, concession) and Task B (determined but nuanced defence)
Register adaptation Task "respect of the situation" and Syntax/Lexis "adapt to the interlocutor"
Idiomatic language Lexis
Occasional impediment The B2 tolerance in Syntax and Fluency

4. Principles, derived

P1. The score is compensatory, so think in averages, not hurdles. The final result is a calibrated transform of per-criterion marks, averaged over two raters (sourced for TEF IRN, assumed for TEF Canada). Nothing published says a criterion has a floor. So a C1 on one row can make up for a B2-low on another [reasoned from §2.4]. The consequence: the box doesn't need every row at B2-high. It needs the average there, with margin.

P2. The target is "B2 met clearly, with C1 sparks". "Reaching B2" is only NCLC 6. From §2.5, NCLC 7 is the upper half of B2. From the plus-level convention (§3), the upper half of a level means the level is fully met and some features of the next level are present. So for each row, the box must show (a) every B2 clause, cleanly, and (b) at least one cheap C1 feature where one exists [reasoned].

P3. Three-quarters of the ratings are language ratings, and they are rated separately in each section. Six of the 8 rows are Syntax, Lexis and Fluency, each rated once for A and once for B [source: CCI grid 2025].

  • Language evidence in A does not fill B's grid, and vice versa.
  • Section A is mostly questions, so A's syntax and lexis must be shown inside questions. Indirect questions, conditionals, relative clauses, "if…" contingencies, and a contextualising sentence before a question carry this [reasoned].

P4. There are two kinds of descriptor clauses, and only one kind can be settled early.

  • Existential clauses are true once a single clean instance occurs, and they stay true:
    • gets an incomplete answer made precise;
    • reacts with confidence to an unexpected answer;
    • detailed, reformulated presentation;
    • illustrated argument;
    • defends a view against an objection;
    • common complex sentences;
    • broad repertoire (shown by precise words);
    • varied structures (C1);
    • structured presentation (C1);
    • varied arguments (C1).

  • Proportional clauses describe a rate across the whole section and can only be confirmed by not being falsified:

    • errors don't cause misunderstanding / are rare;
    • confusions don't hinder;
    • fairly even tempo, few long pauses;
    • intonation natural;
    • exchanges follow on / conversation sustained;
    • appropriate questioning overall.

    [reasoned from the grid wording, §2.3]

P5. Burden of proof: the box carries the burden on existential clauses. The rest only has to avoid rebutting the proportional ones.

  • Think of it as a trial. The candidate must produce evidence for each existential clause, and once produced it can't be withdrawn.

  • For proportional clauses, a good opening sets a presumption ("this speaker is accurate and fluent"). Later evidence can rebut it.

  • So the point (§6) is defined by discharging the burden. "Disaster" is defined as rebutting the presumption on several rows at once [reasoned].

P6. Build margin, because rater noise is about the same width as the target band.

  • Two raters are averaged "when identical or adjacent" (§2.4), so a one-level split between raters is treated as normal.

  • A rater's severity drifts over time about as much as severity differs between raters (median within/between ratio 0.97) [source: Chénier 2021].

  • The examiner's conduct can also sway the live grade [source: Ince 2021, abstract].

  • The NCLC 7 band is only half a CEFR level wide.

So a box aimed at the bottom of NCLC 7 is a coin flip. The box should aim for the top of the band (B2-high verging on C1-low) on every row the candidate controls [reasoned].

P7. Put the margin on the rows the box controls.

  • Controllable by preparation: Task A and Task B (which questions, which follow-up routines, the shape of the presentation, the arguments, the defence), plus range in Syntax and Lexis.

  • Only partly controllable: accuracy and fluency in the unscripted rest.

So the box should push Task A, Task B, syntax variety and lexical precision to C1-flavoured evidence. That buys room for fluency and accuracy to land at plain B2 [reasoned].

  • Worked example (equal weights; steps: B2-low = 7, B2-high = 8, C1-low = 9): tasks at 9, syntax and lexis at 8, fluency at 7 → average 8.0, solidly B2-high.

  • Same, but fluency at B1-high (6) → 7.75, borderline.

So the box can rescue a borderline-B2 speaker. It cannot rescue a B1 speaker [reasoned].

P8. The examiner's moves are known in advance, so script contingent replies to them. The protocol guarantees certain moves (§2.2):

  • a partial answer to the first price or package question;
  • statement-style relaunches and prepared surprises in A;
  • an objection after the presentation in B;
  • pushes to develop or add arguments.

Each move is an opportunity to discharge an existential clause. Turn each one into evidence with a fixed reply routine [reasoned]:

  • Partial answer → ask what it depends on, then ask for the difference. Discharges B1's "gets answers made precise".

  • Any answer → reformulate it ("so if I've understood, …") and adapt the next question or plan. Discharges B2's "reacts with confidence" and C1's "apt intervention"; it also matches CLB 7 "confirms by paraphrasing".

  • Objection → restate it, grant part of it, counter with a reason and a concrete fact or solution, then reaffirm. Discharges B2's "defends clearly and with determination". Granting part is a C1/C2 nuance spark.

  • "Develop that" → give an example. Discharges "illustrated arguments".

P9. The section's structure fixes the box's skeleton and sets a minimum length.

  • A has no presentation. The box is opening + chained questions. It needs at least one partial-answer chain and one reformulate-and-adapt chain, because those clauses can't be shown without the examiner's turns. Minimum: 2 chains + 2–3 anchored stand-alone questions [reasoned].

  • B has a fixed order: present first, then persuade, and the examiner objects. The box must reach at least the first objection and the reply to discharge "defends". Minimum: presentation → 2 illustrated arguments → objection → defence [reasoned].

P10. The second rater hears only audio, so every proof must be spoken and clearly marked. Half the final mark comes from someone who saw nothing. So:

  • reactions must be verbal ("ah, I see", "so if I've understood…");
  • structure must be signposted aloud ("first…, and the best part…");
  • the examiner's turns must be audibly answered.

Gestures, nods and eye contact count for nothing in the score, even though CLB lists non-verbal cues [reasoned from §2.4].

P11. Memorised-sounding speech is a low-level marker, so tie every scripted chunk to the live turn.

  • The grid uses memorised / stereotyped phrases to describe A1/A2 syntax.
  • The fluency row rates spontaneity.
  • Raters are trained on biases that can distort rating ("critères parasites") [source: CCI grid 2025; CCI examiner handbook 2024].

A recited script risks being heard as memorised. The defence is to anchor scripted lines to what the examiner just said (reformulation, "you mentioned…"), and to keep complex sentences of the everyday kind. The grid's own word for B2 complexity is "courantes", meaning common [reasoned].

P12. Some things are banned outright because the grid names them as low-level markers [source: CCI grid 2025; reasoning on the consequence]:

  • Any English word: borrowing from other languages is a B1 lexis marker.
  • Reading the ad aloud in B: A1/A2 presentation.

  • Bare generic questions ("how much? where? when?"): A2 "simple, very general questioning". Every question must carry a specific anchor from the ad or from the caller's situation.

  • Repeating the same argument: A2 "repetitive".

P13. Register is scored, and the A/B pair is itself a register-switching test.

  • Respecting the communication situation is a Task goal in both sections.
  • Adapting to the interlocutor is a Syntax/Lexis goal [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexe 6a].
  • Adapting register to audience is a CLB 7 marker that CLB 6 lacks [source: CLB 2012].

So the first sentence of each box must carry its register: in A, vous with polite conditionals and phone conventions; in B, tu with informal markers. Switching between the two can only be shown across both sections [reasoned].

P14. Self-correction is evidence for B2. A repaired slip is not a disaster. Syntax is mapped to the CEFR scale for monitoring and repair. B2 "can correct most mistakes" [source: CCI examiner handbook 2024, Annexe 6a; CEFR Table 3 via the CCLB alignment report]. A quickly corrected slip is neutral to positive [reasoned].

P15. An attempted structure that comes out wrong is negative evidence.

  • B2 syntax requires complex sentences used correctly.
  • B1 is frequent errors.

A subjunctive that comes out wrong counts against Min. A correct simple sentence costs nothing. So only put structures in the box that Min can say correctly about 9 times in 10 under pressure. Take the C1 sparks from safe sources: precise questions, reformulation, signposted structure, varied connectors, precise nouns and verbs, an idiom that is hard to get wrong. Avoid risky morphology [reasoned].

P16. One sentence can serve all four rows at once. The four rows of a section are rated on the same speech. One sentence can show task (an anchored follow-up), syntax (an indirect question with a conditional), lexis (a precise word not in the ad) and register (vous plus a polite conditional). Grading does not ask for separate evidence per row. This is the instrument's licence for a short box [reasoned].

5. The table: criterion by criterion

Read "6" as ≈ B2-low (B1-high at worst) and "7" as ≈ B2-high (B2 fully met plus a C1 spark).

  • E = existential, can be settled early.
  • P = proportional, needs time and is never locked.
  • Where = which section's grid it is proven on.
# Criterion (grid row → facet) What a 6 looks like What a 7 looks like Smallest piece of speech that proves the 7 E/P Where
1 Task A → relevance of questions Satisfactory but generic questions (price, place, date) Every question targets a specific element of the ad or the caller's situation; coverage heads toward complete (C1: complete and precise) 3–4 questions, each with one anchor ("does the price include the equipment and the insurance?") E (coverage builds over time) A
2 Task A → initiative / the unexpected Asks to clarify incomplete answers Reacts with confidence: restates the answer, then adapts the next question or plan, including after an unwelcome surprise One chain: [partial answer] → "it depends on what?" → [answer] → "so if I've understood, X; in that case, Y?" (about 25 words) E (the examiner triggers it; a generic routine covers any answer) A
3 Task A → quality of exchanges Mostly follows on; sometimes jumps list-style; examiner relaunches now and then Turns build on each other; Min leads; formal phone frame and turn-taking kept Two linked chains plus a phone opening that states the purpose Partly E; "follows on" is P A
4 Task B → presenting the facts Clear and simple; some reformulation; some details Clear and detailed, in own words, framed for the friend (why I'm telling you), structured (C1) About 50–70 words: hook tied to the friend + who / what / how a day goes / what's on offer / how to book, all reformulated and signposted E B
5 Task B → quality of argument Clear arguments, one reason each, little development Each argument illustrated (example, concrete detail, consequence); at least 2 different kinds (C1: varied) 2 × (claim + concrete illustration) ≈ 45–55 words E B
6 Task B → exchanges / defence Answers objections, but sometimes gives in or repeats Takes the objection on board, counters with a reason plus a concrete fact or solution, reaffirms; ideally grants a point and then counters (nuance spark) One full reply to the first objection (about 35–45 words); a short second reply when the friend pushes again shows "determined" (about 20 words) E (the examiner sets the timing; the first objection reliably follows the presentation) B
7 Syntax → range Mostly simple sentences; a few common complex ones Simple and common complex sentences used correctly; C1 spark = variety 3–4 correct complex sentences of different types per section (indirect question, if-clause, relative, purpose/concession clause, conditional of politeness or hypothesis) E A and B separately
8 Syntax → accuracy / control Errors fairly frequent; meaning clear Errors don't cause misunderstanding; slips get corrected No early proof. The box gives a clean anchor; the rest must not show frequent or meaning-breaking errors P A and B separately
9 Syntax → coherence / cohesion (scored under Syntax) Linear: and, but, because, then Linking of several logical types: contrast, concession, consequence, addition 3–4 different connectors across the box (e.g. so / on the other hand / even if / besides) E A and B (mostly B)
10 Lexis → range / precision Everyday words; reuses the ad's wording; some circumlocution Fairly broad and precise, beyond the ad (topic nouns, action verbs, evaluation words); in B, one idiom natural for the register About 5–8 precise words not in the ad per section, plus 1 informal idiom in B E A and B separately
11 Lexis → control / compensation Approximations; borrowings from English (a B1 marker) Confusions don't hinder; gaps covered smoothly Zero English in the box. After that, the rest must not keep reaching for English P A and B separately
12 Fluency → tempo / pauses Frequent hesitations; planning pauses audible Fairly even tempo; few long pauses; fillers in French No content proves it. The box must be sayable in breath-group chunks; its delivery anchors the impression; the rest must hold P A and B separately
13 Fluency → pronunciation / intonation Pronunciation errors sometimes hinder Clear, natural intonation; accent doesn't hinder First 2–3 sentences delivered clearly (rising question intonation, list contours); avoid meaning that depends on a minimal pair (e.g. dessus/dessous) Partly E; mostly P A and B separately
14 Register / situation (Task "respect the situation" + Syntax/Lexis "adapt to the interlocutor") Some formality adjustment; occasional tu/vous slips Consistent vous + polite conditionals + phone conventions in A; consistent tu + informal markers in B The first sentence of each section E in each section; the switch only exists across both Across both
15 Spontaneity (Fluency goal; memorised = A1/A2 marker) Speech sounds read or recited, or reacts mechanically Visibly responsive to the examiner's words Reformulating the examiner's own words at least once per section E A and B

6. "The point" in instrument terms

Definition. For a section, the point is the earliest moment when:

  • (a) every E item in rows 1–15 that belongs to that section has a clean instance on the recording;
  • (b) at least two C1 sparks have occurred on rows the box controls (rows 1, 4, 5, 6, 7, 9 or 10);
  • (c) nothing so far contradicts a P item.

After that moment, the projected average sits at the top half of the NCLC 7 band or above, and only a broad slide in the remaining minutes can pull it down [reasoned, from P1–P7].

Checklist for Point A (from rows 1–3, 7, 9–11, 13–15):

  • Phone opening in vous that states purpose and situation.
  • At least 4 questions, each with a specific anchor.
  • At least 1 partial-answer chain.
  • At least 1 reformulate-and-adapt chain.
  • At least 1 contingency question ("if X happens…?") or a confident reaction to a surprise.
  • At least 3 correct complex sentences of different types.
  • At least 2 connectors beyond and/but/because.
  • At least 5 precise words not in the ad.
  • Zero English.
  • All of it sayable at an even tempo.

Checklist for Point B (from rows 4–7, 9–10, 13–15):

  • Opening in tu that ties the ad to the friend.
  • Presentation reformulated, detailed and signposted; never read.
  • A turn-yielding question (a CEFR B2 interaction move, and it respects turn-taking).
  • At least 2 illustrated arguments of different kinds.
  • At least 1 complete objection defence (restate → grant/counter → concrete fact or solution → reaffirm).
  • Ideally a short second defence.
  • At least 3–4 complex sentence types, including a hypothesis.
  • At least 3 connector types, including a concession.
  • At least 6 precise words plus 1 informal idiom.
  • Zero English.

Can the point sit late, or be spread out? The instrument rates whole sections, so in principle the evidence can appear anywhere. In practice it should come early, for three reasons [reasoned]:

  1. The examiner cuts at 5 or 10 minutes, and an existential clause that never occurs is simply missing.
  2. The live examiner starts filling the grid during the interaction (§2.3).
  3. When the first objection comes is the examiner's choice. Getting the presentation and arguments out early pulls it forward.

How judgments anchor in time is Lens 2's question.

Does A's point help B? On the grid, mostly no. The B rows are filled from B's speech [source: CCI grid 2025, one grid per section]. Two exceptions [reasoned]:

  1. Through P1, a strong A raises the overall average, so B can be a little weaker.
  2. The live examiner is the same person, so impressions may carry over (Lens 2).

Design each box to be section-sufficient: it secures B2-high on its own four rows, without relying on the other section.

How long the box is next to the whole section [reasoned]:

  • In A, Min speaks perhaps 2–2.5 of the 5 minutes, roughly 250–350 words. A box of about 120–150 words is under half of that.

  • In B, Min speaks perhaps 6–7 minutes, roughly 600–800 words. A box of about 180–200 words is about a quarter.

So the unscripted rest is the larger sample for the proportional rows. The box locks the existential rows. It cannot lock accuracy and fluency.

7. "Disaster": what can and cannot pull the score below 7 after the point

Sensitivity arithmetic [reasoned; assumes equal weights for the 8 rows, two raters averaged, and a roughly linear conversion inside B2]:

  • 16 ratings are averaged. When one row drops one box (half a level) for both raters, the average falls by 2/16 of a box. That is about 1/8 × 39 ≈ 5 points on the 450 scale.

  • The NCLC 7 band is 310–348, 39 points wide.

Where the box leaves the projected score Box-drops needed to fall below 310
Bottom of the band (about 312) 1 drop
Middle (about 329) about 4 drops
Top (about 345) about 7 drops

This is why P6 and P7 aim the box at the top of the band. A "disaster" is then any combination worth 4 or more box-drops spread over the 16 ratings (or about 7 if the box sits at the top).

Disasters, with rough cost in box-drops [reasoned, using the grid descriptors]:

Event in the rest of the response Rows hit Rough cost
Fluency collapse: long silences, frequent restarts, through a whole section Fluency (that section), often Task "follows on" 2–4
The same collapse in both sections 4–6 → disaster on its own
Comprehension breakdown: repeatedly misunderstanding the examiner, off-target replies, exchanges no longer follow Task (toward A2), Fluency 3–5
A: Min stops leading and the examiner carries the call (the A1 descriptor) Task A 3+
B: Min gives in early ("ok, you're right, forget it"), or loops the same argument for minutes (A2 "repetitive") Task B 2–3
Errors become frequent and some cause misunderstanding (tenses or persons wrong throughout; malformed questions the examiner misreads) Syntax, possibly Lexis 2–4
Switching to English, or asking "how do you say…" repeatedly (the B1 borrowing marker) Lexis, Fluency 2–3
Register collapse: tu to the advertiser, or vous to the friend, throughout Task, Syntax/Lexis "adapt" 1–2

Not disasters (each costs 0–1 box-drop) [reasoned]:

  • A plainer second half that stays correct. B2 syntax covers simple and complex sentences used correctly.
  • A few slips, especially if corrected (P14).
  • One "sorry, could you repeat?" Asking for repetition is normal at B2; CEFR interaction lets B2 confirm comprehension.
  • One weak or short reply to an objection when others are fine.
  • A hesitation while searching for a word.

  • An approximate word that doesn't block meaning. B2 allows approximations that don't hinder, and CLB 7 allows difficulties that occasionally impede [source: CLB 2012].

  • Fewer complex sentences later on.

  • Losing the C1 sparks later. That costs about 1 drop on a task row and stays inside the margin.

An honest limit. If Min's unscripted French is B1 on fluency and accuracy, no box can lock NCLC 7. The box can turn "B2, but unproven" into "B2-high, proven". It cannot turn a B1 rest into B2 [reasoned, from P3, P4 and P7].

8. Rules a per-prompt analyst can run (instrument checklist)

  1. Anchor every A question to an ad element or to the caller's situation. Never ask a bare how much / where / when.

  2. Plan the partial-answer chain on the price or package question. The protocol makes "it depends" close to certain.

  3. Include one reformulate-and-adapt move written as a frame with a slot ("So if I've understood, [their point]. In that case, [adapted question]?"). Write the slot for the most likely answer.

  4. Include one contingency (weather, cancellation, medical condition, schedule clash). It shows handling of the unexpected and carries an if-clause.

  5. B presentation:

    • hook tied to the friend;
    • 4–5 ad facts in own words;
    • one appropriation line (why it fits us);
    • signposted;
    • ends on a turn-yielding question;
    • nothing read verbatim.
  6. Two arguments of different kinds, each with a concrete illustration.

  7. Predict the most likely objection for the ad (cost, fear or danger, effort, time, comfort, inexperience). Script restate → grant → counter with an ad fact or practical solution → reaffirm. Add a short second reply.

  8. Spread complex structures: at least 3 different types per section, all everyday ones, none that Min often gets wrong.

  9. Connectors: at least 3 logical types per section, including one concession in B.

  10. Lexis: count the precise words that are not in the ad (at least 5 in A, at least 6 in B, plus one idiom in B). Zero English.

  11. Register: the first sentence carries it; check every pronoun and verb form against vous (A) or tu (B).

  12. Audibility: reactions are said aloud, and structure is signposted aloud.
  13. Sayability: chunks that fit one breath, no tongue-twisters, no meaning that hangs on a minimal pair.
  14. Count the C1 sparks: at least 2 per section on controllable rows (precise or complete questioning, apt reformulation, structured presentation, varied arguments, granting a point then countering, structural variety).

9. Claims I'm least sure of

  1. That the two tick boxes mean the lower and upper half of the level, and that NCLC 7 ≈ upper B2. The second part rests on three sourced tables that agree on the midpoint. The first part is inference; CCI does not explain the boxes.

  2. Equal weights, and averaging the two raters, for TEF Canada. Averaging is published only for TEF IRN. I found no weights.

  3. The size of the rater noise. It is inferred from the "adjacent levels" rule and from Chénier's TEFAQ data (2011–2014, one centre). Current TEF Canada figures are not public.

  4. The disaster arithmetic. It depends on 1–3 and on a linear conversion inside B2.

  5. How raters weigh early against late evidence on proportional rows. The instrument rates whole sections; how people actually do it is Lens 2's question.

  6. Video administration is unverified (FRAME claims it; none of my sources mention it).

10. Test: what the instrument says the A-01 and B-03 boxes must contain

These are elements, not polished scripts. Word counts are Min's words only, rough.

A-01 — Paragliding introduction (formal phone call, vous)

# Element Rows proven ~Words
1 Phone opening: greeting, I'm calling about your ad for the paragliding introduction; since I've never flown, I'd like a few details before signing up. 14, 3, 7 (cause clause), 10 30
2 Anchored indirect question on the programme: Could you tell me how the day is organised, from the ground training to the mini-flights, and how long it lasts? 1, 7 (indirect question), 10 20
3 [examiner answers] → reformulate + adapt: So if I've understood, [their point]. And the mini-flights, are they in tandem with an instructor, or alone? 2, 3, 15, 10 (tandem, instructor) 20
4 Price with inclusions, anchored on "exceptional rates": …how much is it, and does that include the equipment and insurance? → [protocol: "it depends, several packages"] → It depends on what exactly? What's the difference between them? → [answer] → Then the [X] one would suit me better, since it's my first time. 1, 2, 7 (conditional), 9 40
5 Contingency: And if the weather is bad on the day, is the session postponed or refunded? 1, 2, 7 (if-clause), 10 (weather, postponed, refunded) 15
6 Requirements, precise: Are there conditions to take part, a minimum age, a weight limit, a medical certificate? (+ a slot for a confident reaction to a surprise: Ah, I didn't know that. In that case, would it be possible to…?) 1, 2 (C1: complete and precise) 15–25

Estimate:

  • Core (elements 1–5): about 120 words.
  • With element 6 for margin: about 145–150.

C1 sparks:

  • precision of questions in 2, 4 and 6;
  • the apt reformulation in 3;
  • coverage across programme, price, conditions and cancellation.

The rest of the call is ordinary anchored questions (what to wear, group size, booking dates) and a phone closing. It only has to stay correct, fluent and connected.

B-03 — Canoe-camping week (friend, tu)

# Element Rows proven ~Words
1 Hook in tu tied to the friend: You remember you said you needed a real break this summer? I've found exactly what we need. 14, 4 (appropriation), 7 (relative) 20
2 Presentation in own words, signposted: agency Vacances Nature, a week of canoe-camping; by day we'd paddle, at night we'd sleep in a tent, a comfortable one apparently; calm moments and some challenge; the region's best landscapes, fresh air; you book everything on their website. 4 (detailed, structured), 7 (conditional), 9, 10 60
3 Turn-yield: What do you think? 3/6 (turn-taking) 4
4 Argument 1 + illustration (switching off): It would do us good to disconnect, no screens, no emails; picture having your coffee by the lake at sunrise. 5, 10 (idiom-ready: "recharge our batteries") 25
5 Argument 2, a different kind (no hassle): And since the agency organises everything, we'd have nothing to plan, we'd just have to turn up. 5 (varied: C1 spark), 7, 9 20
6 [likely objection: never canoed / too tiring / tent uncomfortable] → restate + grant + counter with an ad fact + solution + reaffirm: I get it, you're worried it'll be too physical. But it's not a race: there are calm moments too, and if we're tired, we stop. Even if it's a bit of a challenge, that's exactly what makes it memorable. 6, 7 (if-clause, even if), 9 (concession), C1 nuance spark 45
7 [friend pushes again] → short determined reply + a new angle: Listen, give it a chance; if you really hate it, I'll never mention canoeing again, but I'm sure you'll love it. 6 (determined), 5 25

Estimate:

  • Core (elements 1–6): about 175 words.
  • With element 7 for margin: about 200.

C1 sparks:

  • structured presentation;
  • two different kinds of argument;
  • granting a point, then countering.

The risk is the French of element 6. "I get that you're worried it'll be too physical" pulls in subjunctives. Phrase it as two short clauses (I get it. You're worried it'll be too physical?) unless Min reliably gets que tu aies peur que ce soit right (P15).

What the test shows about the principles.

  • A needs fewer words than B. Its existential clauses are about reacting, and the examiner supplies the material.
  • B has to carry presentation, argument and defence before its point.
  • Neither box can be shorter than its skeleton (P9): about 100 words for A, about 160 for B.
  • Below that, at least one B2 clause is still undischarged, and that makes the rating B2-low at best, which is NCLC 6.
Lens 2 · How the examiner’s verdict forms and when it locks

Lens 2 — judge.md: how the examiner's verdict forms and when it locks

Question this lens answers: model the TEF Canada speaking examiner as a decision-maker. When does the level judgment form, what moves it, when is it effectively locked, what exactly is "the point" in decision terms, what is a "disaster", what does the examiner do after the point, and where in the response can the point sit?

Labels: [source: ...] = checked against a public document in this session unless marked "not re-checked"; [reasoned] = derived here. Grid descriptors are paraphrased in English, not copied.

Part 1. Facts the derivation stands on

F1. There are two judges, and the second one only hears audio. On site there is one examiner who plays the role and rates at the same time. Every test is recorded; the recordings go to the CCI Paris Île-de-France, whose pool of evaluators gives the candidate's "definitive" evaluation. A missing or truncated recording means no result. [source: CCI Paris IDF, Livret de l'examinateur TEF, épreuve d'expression orale (2024), pp. 4, 7, 26: lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/2024-livret-de-examinateur-tef-eo.pdf]. The publisher's TEF Canada results page describes the oral as assessed by two independent examiners, the session examiner and a second examiner working from the recording [source: lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/en/candidate/test-evaluation-francais/tef-canada/results]. How the two ratings are combined (average, central overrides, adjudication) is not public.

F2. The on-site examiner starts ticking during the section, and seals Section A within about a minute after it ends. During each section the examiner "participates in the exchange" and "begins to fill in the grid". The A grid is completed while the candidate is reading the B subject (about 1 minute, not recorded); the B grid is completed in about 1 min 30 after the candidate leaves [source: Livret 2024, pp. 8–9 and Annexe 1, p. 13]. The candidate gets about 1 minute with each subject before it starts [source: Livret 2024, Annexe 1].

F3. The grid: one per section, four criteria each, half-level boxes. Section A: (1) ability to obtain information, (2) syntax, (3) lexis, (4) ease/delivery (fluency, pronunciation, intonation). Section B: (1) ability to present and debate, then the same three language criteria. Each criterion runs <A1, A1, A2, B1, B2, C1, C2, and from A2 upward every level has two tick boxes [source: Livret 2024, Annexe 5a, grids for A and B, pp. 18–19]. So each rater makes eight ticks per candidate. (An older version of the TEF oral, 2010–2014, used two examiners in the room and twelve criteria scored 0–20 [source: Chénier, Étude longitudinale du niveau de sévérité d'examinateurs en FLE, UQAM thesis 2018, pp. 36–38: archipel.uqam.ca/12583]. The current procedure is the 2024 booklet's.)

F4. What separates B1, B2 and C1 on the TEF grid (paraphrased).

Criterion B1 B2 C1
A: obtain information satisfactory questions; asks for some incomplete or ambiguous information to be made precise appropriate questions; reacts with confidence to the answers, even when something unexpected comes up; exchanges are followed through complete, precise questions; well-judged interventions; the conversation is sustained
B: present and debate simple clear presentation with an effort to reformulate; arguments clear but little developed; intervenes regularly and justifies clear, detailed presentation; arguments illustrated or detailed; defends ideas clearly and with determination; exchanges followed through developed, structured presentation; varied, well-developed arguments; well-judged interventions; sustained conversation
