Syntax organised talk with some common complex sentences; errors frequent but meaning clear simple and common complex sentences used correctly; errors do not cause misunderstanding wide range of structures; errors rare and do not affect the exchange
Lexis broader repertoire, adapts using paraphrase or borrowings from other languages fairly broad repertoire; confusions or approximations do not hinder communication wide, well-controlled repertoire; gaps compensated without visible effort
Ease/delivery frequent hesitation, talk clear; pronunciation errors can sometimes hinder fairly regular flow; clear, natural intonation; accent does not hinder broadly natural and fluid

Below B1, two descriptors matter for "disaster": at A2 the A task shows "some requests for clarification or reformulation" and "exchanges poorly followed through", and A2 delivery "needs effort or requests for repetition"; at A1 the conversation "depends entirely on the examiner"; at A1–A2 the B presentation is reading or reading-plus-paraphrase [source: Livret 2024, Annexe 5a]. The grid description page says language competence is judged "in the service of a communicative intention" [source: Livret 2024, Annexe 6a, p. 22].

F5. Where NCLC 7 sits on TEF's own scale: the upper half of B2. IRCC speaking bands: NCLC 6 = 271–309, NCLC 7 = 310–348, NCLC 8 = 349–370 [source: canada.ca Express Entry language-test page, matches FRAME.md]. The CCI's own correspondence table for Expression orale puts B2 = 271–348 = NCLC 6 and 7, C1 = 349–415 = NCLC 8–10, B1 = NCLC 4–5 [source: CCI Paris IDF, Intervalles des scores … NCLC et CECR pour chaque épreuve du TEF Canada, distributed by AF Ottawa: af.ca/ottawa/wp-content/uploads/2018/08/Correspondance-Niveaux-NCLC-scores-TEF-CANADA.pdf]. On the /699 scale printed on current certificates, Expression orale NCLC 7 = 456–493 (from 11 Dec 2023; 450–499 before), inside the 400–499 B2 band [source: CCI Correspondance score TEF – niveau NCLC, lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/wp-content/uploads/2024/10/correspondance-tef-nclc.pdf; B2 = 400–499 from search summaries of the CCI CEFR table, not opened].

Where sources disagree with the request's wording: Min wrote "NCLC 7 / B2+". In CEFR terms the CCLB's alignment study puts NCLC 7 at B1+/B2 (researchers said B1+ for speaking; the panel median was B2; French-speaking panellists leaned B2) and puts B2+ at NCLC 9 [source: North & Piccardo, Aligning the CLB and the CEFR, CCLB 2018, Table 1 and pp. 15–18: language.ca/wp-content/uploads/2019/01/Aligning-the-CLB-and-CEFR.pdf]. But the TEF examiner does not rate in CCLB terms; she ticks TEF's grid, on which NCLC 7 is the top half of the B2 band. So the operational target is "TEF B2, upper half", not CEFR B2+.

F6. What the examiner is told to do with the candidate [source: Livret 2024, pp. 8–10]:

  • Section A: answer partially first (the booklet's own example: to "how much is it?" reply "it depends, we have several packages") so the candidate has to rebound and get things clarified; prepare unexpected elements to test reaction; relaunch a candidate who runs dry with statements, not questions (e.g. "you'll need special insurance", "we have a promotion at the moment"); never ask the candidate questions; close at 5 minutes ("I have another call").

  • Section B: tell the candidate beforehand "I won't agree; you'll have to find arguments"; use counter-arguments from a confidential list for that subject plus common stereotypes; do not shut the discussion early with unanswerable objections; leave the initiative to the candidate; push the candidate to develop arguments and to bring new ones ("the same argument often comes back"); do not object systematically; encourage a relevant argument; avoid "did you try it yourself?" questions that pull toward narration; if the candidate skips the presentation, steer back to it; close at 10 minutes.

  • Both: the full duration must be kept "so that there are enough relevant observables for the evaluation".

F7. What rater research says (the parts this lens uses).

  1. Initial impression then testing. The ACTFL OPI is built as warm-up (initial impression), then alternating level checks (find the floor the speaker sustains) and probes (find where performance breaks down, the ceiling). A "High" sublevel means the speaker functions much of the time at the next level but cannot sustain it; "Mid" may show partial ability at the next level [source: ACTFL, OPI Familiarization Guide 2024, pp. 4–7]. Raters of writing typically form an intuitive impression and then use the scale to articulate and justify it [source: Lumley 2005, Assessing Second Language Writing: The Rater's Perspective, not re-checked].

  2. No single feature drives the rating. In IELTS speaking, "no one measure drives the rating"; many features feed an overall impression; total amount of speech was the measure that differed most clearly across levels; higher scorers answer the question asked, develop the topic, and have less trouble and repair; complex structures cluster in higher bands [source: Seedhouse 2012, English Profile Journal 3(1), citing Brown 2006 and Lazaraton 1998].

  3. The interlocutor co-constructs the performance and raters react to that. Interviewers differ in how they elicit, which changes candidate performance and raters' perceptions [source: Brown 2003, Language Testing 20(1), abstract]. TEF examiners diverge in interpretation; the examiner's attitude can be perceived differently and affect the mark [source: Ince 2021, Mesure et évaluation en éducation 44(3), abstract via search; full text blocked].

  4. Rehearsed speech is discounted. Under ACTFL practice rehearsed material cannot count as evidence of level, and raters flag it; memorisation shows most in transcript patterns within and across candidates [source: Gates, Cox, Bell & Eggington 2020, Language Testing in Asia 10, abstract]. Raters can tell candidates who had seen the prompt from those who had not, and raters used to cram-prep populations do it better [source: Burton 2020, Language Assessment Quarterly 17(3), abstract].

  5. Order effects. Belief revision is sequential anchoring-and-adjustment; in short simple tasks, judging at the end favours primacy and judging step by step favours recency; a negative item moves belief by an amount proportional to the current belief (the higher the anchor, the bigger the drop), a positive item by an amount proportional to the room left above it [source: Hogarth & Einhorn 1992, Cognitive Psychology 24, as summarised in Wang, Zhang & Johnson 2000, Proc. Cognitive Science Society 22]. Anchors are adjusted insufficiently [source: Tversky & Kahneman 1974, Science 185, not re-checked].

  6. Fast impressions. Strangers' judgments from 30-second silent clips predict end-of-term teacher ratings [source: Ambady & Rosenthal 1993, JPSP 64(3), not re-checked]; listeners detect a foreign accent from very short stretches of speech [source: Flege 1984, JASA 76(3), not re-checked].

  7. Fluency is judged on pauses and speed, hardly on repairs. Pause and speed measures predicted perceived fluency; self-repairs contributed very little [source: Bosker et al. 2013, Language Testing 30(2), abstract].

  8. Halo and severity. Ratings on one criterion leak into others (halo) [source: Thorndike 1920; Myford & Wolfe 2003–04, Journal of Applied Measurement; not re-checked]. In 35 of 39 rater studies, the most and least severe raters differed by at least 1 logit (mean 2.6, about a fifth of the candidates' ability range); about a third of TEF/TEFAQ examiners at one centre drifted in severity over time [source: Chénier 2018 thesis, pp. 17–18 and abstract].

  9. Diagnosticity in ability judgments. For abilities, a high performance is more diagnostic than a low one, because able people sometimes perform badly but unable people rarely perform well [source: Skowronski & Carlston 1987, JPSP 52(4), not re-checked]. Slips (occasional, self-correctable) differ from errors (systematic) [source: Corder 1967, IRAL 5, not re-checked]; errors that break sentence-level meaning ("global") hurt communication far more than local ones such as agreement [source: Burt 1975, TESOL Quarterly 9, not re-checked].

Part 2. The examiner as a decision-maker: principles

P1. Design for the audio judge.

From F1: the rating that matters most is made by someone who hears only the recording, hears all of it, and hears many candidates who used the same prep books. Nods, smiles, gestures and a pleasant manner count for nothing with her. The on-site examiner is also a rater but is busy playing the role, keeping time and answering; raters who also interact tend to judge more globally (Cambridge gives its interlocutor one global mark and leaves analytic marks to a silent assessor) [source: Cambridge English speaking-test design, not re-checked]. Rule: every piece of evidence in the box must be audible and must make sense on the recording without seeing the room. Backchannels ("I see", "right") are the audible form of listening; they cost 1–2 words.

P2. The verdict is eight ticks; the 6/7 line runs through the middle of B2.

From F3 and F5: each rater ticks four criteria per section on half-level boxes, and TEF's B2 band holds both NCLC 6 and NCLC 7. The box-to-score conversion is not public, but the simplest reading of the CCI table is that the lower B2 box behaves like NCLC 6 and the upper B2 box like NCLC 7 [reasoned; least-sure claim 1]. What makes a rater pick the upper box of a level? The grid does not say; ACTFL's sublevel logic (F7.1) is the best available model: lower box = "meets B2, but I had doubts" or "B2 with B1 traces"; upper box = "B2 without doubt, and some C1 traces" [reasoned].

So the 6/7 decision is not "is this B2?" It is "is this B2 beyond reasonable doubt?" In burden-of-proof language: B2 shown on the balance of probabilities buys NCLC 6; B2 shown beyond reasonable doubt, with a glimpse of C1, buys NCLC 7. Ticks are averaged in some form, so a criterion that reaches C1-low can carry one that sits at B2-low.

P3. Each criterion has a "can" part and a "control" part, and they are proved in opposite ways.

Every B2 descriptor in F4 mixes two kinds of claim:

  • Capacity ("common complex sentences used correctly", "reacts with confidence to the answer", "arguments illustrated", "defends with determination"). One clear instance proves it, because a weaker speaker rarely produces it (F7.9).

  • Control ("errors do not cause misunderstanding", "fairly regular flow", "exchanges followed through"). No single instance proves it; it is presumed from a stretch of clean speech and can only be disproved, by a pattern.

Consequence: the box can prove the capacity half and start the control half. After the point, nothing new needs to be proved; the rest of the response can only fail to disprove. That asymmetry is what makes "a point" exist at all [reasoned].

P4. Evidence is a likelihood ratio between a 7 and a 6. Most words carry none.

Treat each observation the rater hears as having a likelihood ratio LR = P(heard | true level ≥ NCLC 7) / P(heard | NCLC 6). Only observations with LR well above 1 move the rater toward 7 [reasoned, standard Bayesian updating]. Three classes of words have LR ≈ 1 and are dead words for the point:

  1. Template phrases from prep books ("I'm calling about your ad", "I'd like some information", "what are the opening hours?"). Sixes memorise them as well as sevens do; raters hear them daily (F7.4).

  2. Words printed on the ad ("ground training", "mini-flights", "comfortably in a tent"). Anyone can read them off the page. Lexis is judged on words that are not on the ad.

  3. Generic questions with no link to the previous answer ("and what about the price?"). They show B1 "asks questions", not B2 "reacts to answers".

High-LR observations for the 6/7 decision (from F4): a correct complex sentence built on what the examiner just said; reformulating the examiner's answer and drawing a consequence; a follow-up that uses an answer to decide, choose or adapt; an argument with an example or a concrete consequence; a concede-then-counter reply to an objection; a precise word beyond the ad; a second defence after the friend pushes back again. Rule: dead words are allowed only where the task needs them (opening, register, naming the ad), and kept as short as possible.

P5. The authenticity gate: memorised evidence is worth nothing, so the proof must sit in contingent turns.

The box is written in English, translated, and learned. That is exactly what F7.4 says raters discount. Worse, a sentence clearly beyond the speaker's spontaneous level has an LR below 1 for credibility: it suggests a script. The only evidence a rater cannot attribute to memorisation is speech that depends on what the examiner just said. So:

  • Rule (contingency): the decisive evidence must be in Min's replies to the examiner's turns, not in Min's opening.

  • Rule (echo slot): each contingent turn opens with a short echo of the examiner's own words (3–8 words: "so it depends on the package…", "you're worried about sleeping in a tent…"), then a reaction, then the next move. The echo proves comprehension (audible listening), ties the exchange together ("followed through"), cannot have been memorised, and is linguistically safe because the words were just heard. The box writes the slot with a likely filler in brackets, so it reads as a script but works for any answer.

  • Rule (calibration): the box must be pitched at a level Min can approach in her own unscripted speech: clean B2 with one or two C1 traces, not a C1 showcase. Under F7.5 a high anchor makes later negative evidence hit harder (the drop scales with the anchor), and a visible gap between a polished box and a plainer remainder is itself evidence of a script. The best box is the one whose remainder sounds like the same speaker on a quieter day.

P6. One brilliant sentence is a glimpse; a level needs replication across turns ("3 + 2 rule").

ACTFL separates the floor (what is sustained) from the ceiling (what appears but breaks down) (F7.1). A single fine sentence tells the rater "this person can reach B2 sometimes", which is a lower-box profile. The floor is set by repetition in different turns. Rough arithmetic with Wald's sequential test [source: Wald 1945, Annals of Mathematical Statistics 16, not re-checked; numbers here are illustrative, reasoned]: start at even odds between 6 and 7; if each independent diagnostic moment has an LR of about 2.5–3, reaching about 95% (odds 19:1) takes ln 19 / ln 3 ≈ 2.7 to ln 19 / ln 2.5 ≈ 3.2 moments. Moments inside one turn are not independent (one good turn can be rehearsed or lucky), so count turns, not sentences.

Rule: the box contains at least three diagnostic moments in three different Min turns, and at least two of those turns are contingent on something the examiner said. The task frame (opening in A, presentation in B) is required on top and does not count toward the three unless it carries a diagnostic feature itself.

P7. Interaction criteria cannot be proved before the examiner speaks, so call the probe early.

The A task criterion at B2 is about reacting to answers, even unexpected ones; the B task criterion is about defending ideas. Neither can be shown before the examiner has answered or objected. So the point is never inside Min's first turn [reasoned from F4]. But the examiner's probes are predictable (F6), and Min can trigger them:

  • Section A: ask early the question the booklet itself scripts a partial answer for: price or package ("how much is it / what does it include?"). The examiner is told to answer "it depends, there are several packages". That hands Min the rebound test in turn 2 instead of minute 3.

  • Section B: after the presentation and one argument, hand the turn over ("what do you think?"). The examiner has promised to disagree and will object. Better still, mention the weak spot first (the tent, the effort, the cost) with a counter ready, so the first objection is one Min has prepared for. The rater still credits the reply as contingent if it echoes the friend's actual words.

This is game theory with a cooperative opponent: the examiner's job is to produce observables, not to make Min fail (F6: don't object systematically, encourage good arguments, keep options open). Making the examiner's job easy is the best reply.

P8. The interaction ladder: which follow-up move sits at which level.

From F4 the same move, "following up an answer", spans four rungs:

  • A2: "Sorry, can you repeat?" (Min needs the examiner to reformulate; more than once is A2 evidence).
  • B1: "Can you tell me more about X?" (asks for precision; satisfactory, not yet B2).

  • B2: uses the answer to react and steer: "Ah, so it depends on the package. In that case, which one would suit a complete beginner?" (confident reaction, chain kept).

  • C1 trace: reformulates to confirm and draws the implication: "If I understand correctly, the flights only happen after the ground training, so if the weather is bad that day, I'd lose both?" (precise, well-judged intervention).

Rule: every contingent turn in the box must be at the B2 or C1 rung. A box made of B1-rung follow-ups ("can you tell me more?") reaches B1 no matter how good the grammar is. The B equivalents: B1 = "but it's nice!" (justifies); B2 = concede, counter with a detail or example, keep going when pushed again; C1 trace = a second argument of a different kind (variety) or a signposted, structured presentation.

P9. When the verdict forms, and when it locks.

Putting F2, F7.1, F7.5 and F7.6 together [reasoned]:

  • 0–20 seconds: accent, rhythm and intelligibility give a delivery impression almost at once. The content of the box cannot change this, but the delivery of the first sentence can: open with a short sentence Min can say smoothly.

  • First minute of Min's speech: a working level hypothesis forms (the warm-up function in the OPI). The on-site examiner may already be ticking (F2).

  • Each contingent exchange: the hypothesis is tested. Each B2/C1-rung reply raises the odds; a B1-rung reply or a comprehension failure lowers them.

  • Lock, on site: for A, the grid is completed within about a minute after A ends; for B, within 1 min 30 after B. Psychologically it locks earlier, once the rater has a hypothesis she has seen confirmed two or three times and starts reading new evidence through it (anchoring, insufficient adjustment).

  • Lock, central rater: she hears the whole recording before ticking (end-of-sequence judgment), which with long material favours the anchor formed early, though a clear final impression can still pull (recency). Both ends matter; the beginning matters more.

"Locked" means: neutral evidence no longer moves the tick, and mildly negative evidence moves it within the same box. It never means immune. Only a pattern of B1-or-lower evidence, or something that re-labels the box as recited, can cross back over the line (Part 4).

P10. How later evidence moves a locked verdict.

Sort post-point evidence by its likelihood ratio against "≥ 7" [reasoned from P3, P4, F7.5, F7.7, F7.9]:

  • Neutral (LR ≈ 1): plain but correct sentences, simple questions, short polite turns. The B2 syntax descriptor explicitly includes simple sentences used correctly; plainness is not a B1 marker. No movement.

  • Mild (LR slightly below 1): a slip, a gender or agreement error, a self-correction, one search for a word, one weak reply to a hard objection. Sevens do all of these. Repairs barely touch perceived fluency (F7.7). A weak answer to a probe pitched above Min's level marks the ceiling, not a lower floor (F7.1). Movement within the box at most.

  • Strong (LR well below 1): things a 7 rarely does: the same basic error in turn after turn; errors that change meaning; an English word dropped in (borrowing is the B1 lexis marker); the examiner asking "sorry?" because of Min's French; long pauses inside clauses over a stretch; giving up under an objection; a scripted line that ignores what was just said. One of these is mild; a cluster of them is the disaster. With a high anchor each one hits harder (contrast effect), which is why P5's calibration rule matters.

P11. Turn length is itself a signal.

Total amount of speech is the measure that best separated levels in IELTS (F7.2), and the grid sets "exchanges are brief" against "followed through". A box made of eight-word turns reads as A2/B1 however correct it is; a 90-word monologue in Section A turns a phone call into a speech. Rule: each Min turn in the box is about 20–45 words, two or three clauses, then the floor goes back to the examiner [reasoned]. Minimum words is a property of the whole box, not a licence to clip each turn.

P12. Buy margin where content can buy it.

Rater severity spreads are large (F7.8): a severe rater can cost about half a level. Delivery (accent, rhythm) is the criterion the box can least control. The task criterion is the one the box controls most, since precision, structure and variety are content properties that survive translation. Rule: aim the task criterion at a C1 trace (precise, well-judged intervention in A; structured presentation and a second kind of argument in B), aim syntax and lexis at clean B2, and accept B2 on delivery. The average then lands in the upper B2 box with room for a severe rater.

Part 3. "The point", defined

Definition [reasoned]. The point is the end of the first Min turn after which all of the following are true on the recording:

  1. Frame done: register set (vous in A, tu in B), the ad identified, the task's mandatory part done (in B, the ad presented in Min's own words, not read).

  2. Capacity proved on every criterion Min can influence:

    • task: at least two B2-rung contingent moves (P8), at least one of them a reaction to a partial answer, an unexpected condition or an objection, plus one C1 trace;

    • syntax: at least two correct common complex sentences (relative clause, condition with if, a reason or purpose clause, a concession), at least one of them inside a contingent turn;

    • lexis: at least two precise words not on the ad, no word borrowed from English;

    • delivery: nothing in the box forces a long pause (no word Min cannot reliably say). 3. Replication: the "3 + 2 rule" is met: three diagnostic moments in three turns, two of them contingent (P6). 4. Control presumed: about 60–90 seconds of Min's speech with no strong negative evidence (P10). This usually comes free with conditions 1–3.

  3. Credibility intact: nothing in the box sounds recited against what the examiner said (echo slots used).

At that moment the rater's best estimate for every criterion is B2-upper or above, her uncertainty is small, and the remaining minutes can only add neutral or mild evidence unless there is a disaster.

Why nothing in it can be removed (the test analysts should apply to each element): remove the frame and the task criterion drops (in B, a missing presentation is redirected and costs the "presents" half of the criterion); remove a contingent turn and either "reacts even to the unexpected" or the 3 + 2 replication fails; remove the C1 trace and the profile reads B2-lower (NCLC 6); remove the complex sentences or the off-ad words and syntax or lexis has no capacity proof. If an element can be removed without breaking one of conditions 1–5, it is a dead word and must go.

Part 4. "Disaster", defined

A disaster is later performance that gives the rater pattern evidence that one criterion is B1 or lower, or that re-labels the box as recited. Concretely, any one of these, observed on the recording [reasoned from F4 and P10; thresholds are working estimates]:

# Disaster Observable threshold Criterion hit
D1 Comprehension breakdown caused by Min the examiner, in role, has to ask Min to repeat or clarify what she meant 2 or more times, or answers a different question because Min's was unclear delivery (A2: listener needs repetition), task
D2 Systematic basic errors the same basic error (present-tense conjugation, verb form after je, wrong auxiliary, persistent gender chaos) in most turns over a minute or more, or errors that change meaning more than once syntax (B1: errors frequent)
D3 Fluency collapse repeated long pauses inside clauses (about 3 seconds or more) over a stretch, many false starts, or switching to English delivery (B1/A2)
D4 Lexical borrowing English words dropped into French sentences more than once lexis (B1 marker)
D5 Task abandonment in A Min stops asking: the examiner has to relaunch twice in a row with no new question from Min, or Min ends the call well before 5 minutes task (A1: conversation depends on the examiner)
D6 Task abandonment in B Min gives in ("you're right, forget it"), repeats the same argument three or more times without development, or stops answering the objections' content task (B1: arguments undeveloped; A2: brief exchanges)
D7 Script exposure a learned line that ignores or contradicts what the examiner just said (asking about something already answered, thanking for information that was refused), more than once all criteria, retroactively: the box is reread as memorised
D8 Register collapse tu with the advertiser or vous with the friend, sustained task (situation not respected); minor alone, adds to any other cluster

Not a disaster (mild or neutral evidence under P10): the rest of the section in plainer but correct sentences; a few agreement or gender slips; self-corrections; one "sorry, could you say that again?"; a question the examiner had already answered, once; one weak or short reply to an objection; repeating one argument once; running dry 20–30 seconds before the end and closing politely; not convincing the friend (the grid rewards defending, not winning); a hard unexpected element Min handles imperfectly but in French and on topic.

Rule of thumb for analysts: after the point Min must sound like a solid NCLC 6 on a quiet day, not like a 5. A remainder at NCLC 6 (B2-lower) averaged with a box at B2-upper/C1-lower lands at 7; a remainder at NCLC 5 (B1) is disaster territory, because B1 descriptors are defined by exactly the negative patterns in D1–D6. No box can lock a 7 for a speaker whose unboxed French sits at NCLC 5; the box supplies the "beyond reasonable doubt" and the C1 trace that a 6 lacks.

Part 5. What the examiner does after the point, and what the box must survive

Inside the box window the examiner may: give a partial answer (likely, and wanted); give a full answer instead (then Min's echo slot still works: "so it's 150 dollars, equipment included. In that case…"); slip in an unexpected condition early (age, weight, medical certificate, deposit, full dates); in B, ask an information question as the friend ("where is it? how much?") before objecting. Every contingent turn in the box therefore has a slot and a fallback: if the examiner says something other than the bracketed guess, Min echoes what was actually said and continues with the same reaction frame.

After the point, Section A (about 3 more minutes): more partial answers; at least one prepared unexpected element; affirmative relaunches if Min pauses; adaptation of the answers to the candidate's level (F6: the examiner adapts to the candidate). Since a strong opening invites harder probes, expect the unexpected element to arrive here. Min must keep asking questions linked to the answers until the examiner's "I have another call". Silence or a list of generic questions is the main risk (D5).

After the point, Section B (about 6–7 more minutes): further objections drawn from the confidential list and from stereotypes; "develop that"; "is that your only argument?"; occasional agreement on a good point; an abrupt close at 10 minutes. The examiner is told not to accept the same argument again and again, so Min needs a reserve of two or three more arguments of different kinds outside the box. The main risks are D6 and D2/D3 under fatigue.

Probe escalation is good news. When the examiner raises the difficulty, it means the working hypothesis is high. A weak reply to a probe above Min's level fixes the ceiling; it does not move the floor (F7.1, P10).

Part 6. Where the point can sit

It should be early, and it cannot be first. Early, because the first minute anchors both raters (P9), the on-site examiner may already be ticking (F2), and the time left after the point is a buffer in which only disasters count. Not first, because the interaction evidence needs the examiner's turns (P7). The earliest feasible point is the end of Min's third or fourth turn: about 1:30–2:30 into Section A, about 2:30–3:30 into Section B [reasoned, at roughly 100–130 words a minute for Min plus the examiner's turns].

It can be spread over several turns; it must be. P6 requires three turns. Spreading further only delays it.

Section A's point helps Section B, but only indirectly. Formally, the on-site A grid is sealed before B begins (F2), and syntax, lexis and delivery are rated again in B (F3), so B must earn its own ticks. Informally: the same on-site examiner carries her A impression into B (halo across sections), and the central rater most likely hears A before B, so a clean A point sets a favourable anchor for B [reasoned]. And because one score covers both sections, a section that lands at C1-lower can carry one that lands at B2-lower. Each per-prompt box should still aim to reach B2-upper on its own section's grid, so that neither section depends on the other; the cross-section averaging is spare margin, not the plan.

Weight of the sections. Each grid has four criteria. If every tick counts equally, Section A's 5 minutes weigh as much as Section B's 10, so a minute of A is worth twice a minute of B, and a disaster in B is diluted in time but not in weight [reasoned; least-sure claim 3].

Part 7. Construction rules for the per-prompt analysts (mechanical)

  1. Frame, minimal. One short sentence that sets register and names the ad (and, in B, links to the shared situation in the prompt). Template wording is acceptable here only; keep it under about 20 words.

  2. Trigger the probe. A: the first or second question invites the scripted partial answer (price/package, what is included, "for beginners?"). B: presentation in Min's own words (no reading), one illustrated argument, then hand the turn to the friend, ideally after naming the weak spot Min has a counter for.

  3. Two or three contingent turns, each built as echo slot + reaction + next move, at the B2 or C1 rung (P8). In A: reformulate → consequence → precise question that a beginner/customer in Min's position would really need. In B: echo the objection → concede → counter with a detail or example → keep the plan alive (a proposal or a second, different kind of argument when pushed again).

  4. Place the language evidence inside the contingent turns: at least two correct common complex sentences overall, one of them contingent; at least two precise words not on the ad; no word Min might not know in French.

  5. One C1 trace on the task criterion, not in rare vocabulary: A = a reformulate-and-imply turn; B = a signposted presentation or a second argument of a different type.

  6. Turn length 20–45 words (P11); 4–5 Min turns in total.

  7. Calibration check: read the box aloud as Min would; any sentence she could not produce in a similar form without the script is too high and should be simplified (P5).

  8. Deletion test: delete each element in turn; if conditions 1–5 of Part 3 still hold, the element was dead and stays deleted.

  9. Stage directions show the examiner's likely move in brackets, with the slot filler as an example, e.g. [examiner: "it depends, we have several packages"].

Part 8. Claims I am least sure of

  1. Upper B2 box = NCLC 7. Inferred from the CCI table (B2 = NCLC 6–7) and the two boxes per level; the actual tick-to-score conversion is calibrated and unpublished. If the conversion is not linear, the 6/7 line might sit a little higher or lower inside B2. The direction of the advice (B2 beyond doubt plus a C1 trace) holds either way.

  2. How the on-site and central ratings combine. "Definitive evaluation" by the central pool suggests the central rating dominates; the candidate page suggests two independent ratings. Unknown whether the central rater sees the on-site grid.

  3. Equal weight of the two sections. Plausible from the grid layout, not documented.

  4. The 3 + 2 rule's numbers. The likelihood ratios are illustrative; the structure (replicate across turns, at least two contingent) is the robust part.

  5. How hard TEF raters discount rehearsed speech. The evidence is from ACTFL/OPIc and English tests (F7.4), not from the TEF. The TEF grid does tie memorised structures to A1, and central raters hearing the same prep-book lines daily is a reasoned, not documented, mechanism.

  6. Order effects in this setting. No TEF-specific study of primacy or anchoring was found; the timing in P9 combines the booklet's procedure with general findings.

  7. Disaster thresholds (2 repetition requests, 2 relaunches in a row, about 3-second pauses) are working estimates derived from the descriptors, not published cut-offs.

Part 9. Test: what this lens says the boxes for A-01 and B-03 must contain

A-01 — paragliding introduction, phone call, vous (about 120–150 words of Min's speech)

Turn Element (not a polished script) Why (principle) ≈ words
1 Greeting, calling about the paragliding introduction ad, would like some details before booking frame, register (Part 7.1); dead words kept minimal 18
2 Self-position + trigger: "I've never flown before" + price and what it includes sets up later precision; invites the scripted partial answer (P7) 18
[examiner: "it depends, we have several packages"]
3 Echo ("so it depends on the package") + B2 reaction + precise follow-up with a relative clause and a conditional: which package would you recommend for someone who has never done it, and what's the difference contingent turn 1, B2 rung (P8); complex sentence in a contingent turn 28
[examiner answers]
4 Reformulate-and-imply (C1 trace): "if I understand correctly, the mini-flights come after the ground training…" + hypothetical beyond the ad: if the weather is bad that day, can it be postponed or is the booking lost? contingent turn 2; condition + alternative; off-ad words (weather, postpone, cancel) 32
[examiner answers, possibly an unexpected condition: weight limit, medical certificate, insurance]
5 Confident reaction to the condition ("ah, good thing I asked") + adaptation + forward move: what do I need to bring so that I'm ready on the day / do I need to book a date now contingent turn 3; reacts to the unexpected (B2 core); purpose clause 30

Rough total: about 125 Min words (floor about 105 if turn 5 is merged into turn 4; safer up to 150). The point falls at the end of turn 5, roughly 1:45–2:15 into the call. After it, Min must keep asking answer-linked questions for about 3 minutes (D5 is the main risk).

B-03 — canoe-camping week, friend, tu (about 160–200 words of Min's speech)

Turn Element Why ≈ words
1 Hook tied to the prompt's situation: "you know how we wanted to go away together this summer? I think I've found exactly what we need" frame, register, shared plan; conditional/relative structure cheaply 20
2 Presentation in Min's own words, structured: an agency (Vacances Nature) runs a canoe-camping week; we'd paddle by day and sleep in a tent at night, in the most beautiful spots of the region; calm, but also some challenge; booking on their site "presents, reformulated, detailed" (B2); signposting = C1 trace; must not sound read 40
3 Argument 1, illustrated and aimed at the friend: you keep saying you need to disconnect; picture a week with no screens, just the lake and fresh air. Name the weak spot ("and yes, it's a tent, but…"), then hand over: "what do you think?" illustrated argument (B2); calls the probe (P7) 35
[friend objects, e.g. "sleeping in a tent? I'm not sporty enough"]
4 Echo the objection + concede + counter with a concrete detail + concession clause: I get that you're worried about [X], but it's designed for beginners and we'd go at our own pace; even if it's hard at times, that's exactly what makes it memorable contingent turn 1; "defends clearly"; concession; off-ad words (beginners, pace, memorable) 40
[friend pushes back again, e.g. cost, insects, weather]
5 Echo + a different kind of argument (relational or practical, = variety, C1 trace) + determination + concrete proposal: "I'd rather we do it together than alone; let's look at the site tonight and decide" contingent turn 2; "with determination" = keeps going when pushed again; subjunctive-friendly "I'd rather we…" 40

Rough total: about 175 Min words (floor about 150 if turns 1–2 are tightened; up to 200 if the examiner asks an information question first). The point falls at the end of turn 5, roughly 2:30–3:30 into the 10 minutes. After it, Min needs two or three further arguments of different kinds in reserve; the main risks are D6 (repeating or giving in) and fatigue-driven D2/D3.

What the test shows about the principles: both boxes hit the 3 + 2 rule only after two examiner turns, so neither can be shorter than about 100 (A) or 150 (B) Min words without dropping a contingent turn or the B presentation. Most of the word budget goes to contingent turns, not to the opening: the opening is the cheapest part to cut and the least diagnostic.

Lens 3 · The shortest speech that carries all the evidence

Lens 3: economy — the shortest speech that carries all the evidence

The question for this lens: given everything an examiner needs to hear before placing Min at NCLC 7 or above, what is the shortest speech that delivers all of it, said in French by a learner under exam pressure? The answer is a compression problem with a noisy channel. Every sentence costs words and carries some chance of coming out wrong. Evidence is worth different amounts depending on how it is said. Some evidence can be packed several pieces to a sentence; some cannot.

Labels: [source: …] means a public document says it. [reasoned] means it is derived here. Descriptors from the grid, the CLB and the CEFR are paraphrased in English, not quoted.

Part 1. Facts the derivation rests on

F1. The grid. The TEF speaking grid has four criteria per section. In Section A they are: ability to obtain information, syntax, lexis, and fluency/delivery. In Section B they are: ability to present and debate, syntax, lexis, and fluency/delivery. Each criterion is rated on columns from below A1 to C2. On the printed grid, every column from A2 to C2 has two tick boxes; below-A1 and A1 have one each. So each criterion is effectively rated on a 12-step scale with a lower and an upper box inside each level from A2 up. [source: CCI Paris IDF, Expression orale du TEF – grille d'évaluation, 2025, https://www.lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/wp-content/uploads/2025/03/2025-tef-grilleeo-confidentiel.pdf. The file is marked "confidentiel" but the publisher hosts it publicly.]

Paraphrased B1 / B2 / C1 descriptors [same source]:

Criterion B1 B2 C1
A: obtaining information Satisfactory questions; gets some incomplete or ambiguous information clarified and developed Appropriate questions; reacts confidently to the answers, even when they are unexpected; the exchange hangs together Complete, precise questions; well-judged interventions; the conversation is sustained
B: presenting and debating Simple, clear presentation with an effort to reformulate; arguments clear but thin; intervenes and justifies own view Clear, detailed presentation; arguments illustrated or detailed; defends ideas clearly and firmly; the exchange hangs together Developed, structured presentation; varied, well-developed arguments; well-judged interventions; sustained conversation
Syntax (both) Organised speech with some common complex sentences; frequent errors, but the general meaning is clear Simple and common complex sentences used correctly; errors do not cause misunderstanding Appropriate use of a wide variety of structures; rare errors that do not affect the exchange
Lexis (both) Wider repertoire, adapts by circumlocution or borrowing from other languages Fairly wide repertoire; confusions or approximations do not hinder communication Wide, well-controlled repertoire; gaps compensated without visible effort
Fluency (both) Frequent hesitations but clear; pronunciation sometimes hinders Fairly even pace; clear, natural intonation; accent does not hinder Natural and fluent overall; intonation mostly suited to the situation

F2. How Section A is run. The examiner's booklet splits "obtain information" into three sub-criteria: relevance of the questions, initiative and handling of the unexpected, and quality of the exchanges. The examiner is told to give partial answers first. The booklet's own example is a price question answered with "it depends, we have several packages", so that the candidate has to follow up. Examiners also prepare surprises, restart a stalled candidate with statements rather than questions, never ask the candidate questions, and close the call after 5 minutes. The sample Section A instruction tells candidates to ask about ten questions. [source: CCI Paris IDF, Livret de l'examinateur – épreuve d'expression orale, 2024, https://lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/2024-livret-de-examinateur-tef-eo.pdf, pp. 8–9 and Annexe 2]

F3. How Section B is run. Sub-criteria: presentation of the facts, quality of the argument, quality of the exchanges. Before starting, the examiner tells the candidate: present the document first, then convince me, and I will disagree. If the candidate skips the presentation, the examiner steers back to it. The examiner pushes for more development of thin arguments and asks for other arguments, because, the booklet notes, the same argument often keeps coming back. Examiners are told not to shut the discussion down early with unanswerable objections, and to avoid questions that pull the candidate into storytelling instead of arguing. Duration is 10 minutes. [source: examiner's booklet, pp. 9–10]

F4. Who decides. The examiner rates both sections on the spot. Every session is recorded, and the recording goes to the CCI's pool of raters for the definitive evaluation. The publisher's results page says speaking is assessed by the test leader and a second examiner using the recording, and that the final score takes all the criteria into account and is converted into a calibrated score. The conversion formula is not public. [source: examiner's booklet p. 4 and Annexe 8; https://lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/en/candidate/test-evaluation-francais/tef-canada/results] In practice, the verdict that counts comes from someone listening to audio of the whole section. [reasoned]

F5. Scores, NCLC and CEFR. This corrects part of the brief.

  • IRCC's Express Entry table for TEF Canada speaking: NCLC 7 = 310–348, NCLC 6 = 271–309. This matches FRAME.md. IRCC says to use the "old score equivalence" column. [source: https://www.canada.ca/en/immigration-refugees-citizenship/services/immigrate-canada/express-entry/documents/language-requirements/language-testing.html]

  • Since 11 Dec 2023, certificates also show a score out of 699. For speaking on that scale, NCLC 6 = 422–455 and NCLC 7 = 456–493. [source: CCI, Correspondance score TEF – niveau NCLC, https://www.lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/wp-content/uploads/2024/10/correspondance-tef-nclc.pdf] On the same scale, CEFR B2 = 400–499. [source: CCI chart Correspondance TEF CECR, indexed at lefrancaisdesaffaires.fr/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/Correspondance-TEF-CECR.pdf, now returning 404. The bands-of-100 layout is consistent with the next item.]

  • An older table on the 0–450 scale aligns speaking to CEFR as follows: B1 = NCLC 4–5, B2 = NCLC 6–7 (271–348), C1 = NCLC 8–10, C2 = NCLC 11–12. I read the merged-cell positions off the PDF layout. [source: 2018 table hosted by AF Ottawa, https://www.af.ca/ottawa/wp-content/uploads/2018/08/Correspondance-Niveaux-NCLC-scores-TEF-CANADA.pdf]

Consequence. On both scales, NCLC 6 is already inside B2, and NCLC 7 is the upper part of B2. The brief frames the 6/7 line as "B1 vs B2". On the TEF it is closer to lower B2 vs upper B2. Min's own wording, "NCLC 7 / B2+", is the accurate one. The B1-vs-B2 features still matter, but as the floor that must not be breached, not as the line itself.

F6. CLB 6 vs CLB 7 speaking (the Canadian benchmark NCLC mirrors), paraphrased [source: Canadian Language Benchmarks 2012, Speaking Benchmarks 6 and 7, https://www.canada.ca/content/dam/ircc/migration/ircc/english/pdf/pub/language-benchmarks.pdf]:

CLB 6 CLB 7
Hesitations a few few
Vocabulary everyday range expanding range of concrete and idiomatic language
Grammar developing control of more complex structures increasing variety, developing control of complex structures
Difficulties sometimes impede occasionally impede
Register some degree of formality adapts style and register to different audiences and situations
Content concrete information of personal relevance concrete and some abstract information

CLB 7 task indicators that map straight onto the TEF tasks: ask follow-up questions to keep the conversation going; confirm your own understanding by repeating or paraphrasing; clarify and confirm information on the phone; use persuasive arguments; use modals at the right level of politeness; express and qualify opinions, reservations and possibilities.

F7. CEFR B1 vs B2 (Companion Volume, paraphrased) [source: CEFR Companion Volume with new descriptors, 2018, scales pp. 56–162; mirror at https://cefr.schools.ac.cy/downloads/CEFR-Companion%20Volume%20with%20new%20descriptors%20-%202018-56-162.pdf]:

  • Asking for clarification. B1 asks someone to clarify or expand what they just said. B2 asks follow-up questions to check that they understood what was meant, and gets ambiguous points cleared up.

  • Obtaining goods and services. B1 handles travel-type transactions and asks about the difference between options. B2 states requirements and asks detailed questions about more complex services, and can negotiate.

  • Informal discussion. B1 gives brief comments on others' views. B2 accounts for and sustains opinions with relevant explanations, and makes and responds to hypotheses.

  • Putting a case. B1 gives brief reasons. B2 develops a clear argument with subsidiary points and relevant examples, and builds a chain of reasoning. The top of B2 develops it systematically with relevant supporting detail.

  • Grammatical accuracy. B2 makes no mistakes that cause misunderstanding, though it may use complex structures stiffly with some inaccuracy. The top of B2 shows good control: slips are rare and can often be corrected in retrospect.

  • Vocabulary range. B1 gets by with some circumlocution. B2 can vary its wording to avoid repetition.

  • Spoken fluency. B1 shows very evident pausing to plan and repair. B2 keeps a fairly even tempo with few noticeably long pauses.

F8. Research on prepared speech and on attention limits.

  • Raters spot rehearsed segments. In OPIc tests they found them through recurring wording and through jumps in articulation rate between rehearsed and spontaneous stretches. ACTFL does not count rehearsed material as evidence of a higher level. [source: Gates, Cox, Bell & Eggington 2020, Language Testing in Asia, https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40468-020-00110-5]

  • Raters could tell apart performances by candidates who had seen the prompt in advance. [source: "How scripted is this going to be?", Language Assessment Quarterly 17(3), 2020, https://doi.org/10.1080/15434303.2020.1754829]

  • Complexity, accuracy and fluency compete for limited attention (Skehan's trade-off hypothesis). Evidence for it is mixed, and the trade-offs shrink with proficiency and with repeated performance of the same task. [source: Michel, Révész, Shi & Lee 2019, summarising Skehan, https://discovery-pp.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10060845/1/Michel%2C%20Revesz%2C%20Shi%20%26%20Lee%20%282019%29.pdf; Sample & Michel 2014, https://eprints.lancs.ac.uk/id/eprint/71825/]

Part 2. Principles

P1. The target is "upper B2 on every criterion", not "B2".

From F1 and F5. NCLC 7 sits in the upper part of B2, and the grid has a lower and an upper box inside B2. The working reading is that NCLC 7 means the average of the ticks lands about on the upper B2 box. [reasoned; the conversion is not public, see Part 7] So the box has to do two things: (a) carry no B1 feature on any criterion; (b) on every criterion, meet the B2 descriptor in full and show at least a glimpse of the C1 descriptor.

The glimpses that buy the upper box are the cheap parts of C1: precise questions, one well-judged intervention, a structured presentation, a visible variety of structures, and lexis that is precise rather than rare. The expensive parts of C1 ("sustained", "complete", "constant") are not needed. [reasoned]

P2. Secure each of the eight ratings separately (minimax).

There are two grids of four criteria, filled in separately, combined by a formula that is not public (F1, F4). Under unknown weights, the only guarantee that holds whatever the weights are is that every rating clears the bar. Evidence given in Section A cannot fill in Section B's grid. A strong A can lift the total, so it partly offsets a weaker B, but you cannot count on that because you don't know by how much. So each section's box must be self-sufficient. [reasoned]

P3. The box can prove "can do". It cannot prove "keeps doing".

Most B2 descriptors are "can do" statements: appropriate questions, reacts to answers, common complex sentences used correctly, a fairly wide repertoire, arguments illustrated, ideas defended. A short stretch of speech can prove each of these. Two kinds of evidence need time:

  • Fluency is heard across the whole recording (F4). The box shows the delivery only for its own length.
  • "The exchange hangs together" needs a few linked turns, not one.

So the box is an opening book, as in chess: a prepared sequence that reaches a won position. After it, Min plays from principles, and the only way to lose is a blunder (Part 4). The section keeps running for its full 5 or 10 minutes whatever Min does (F2, F3). The box is not the whole response; it is the evidence packet at the front of it. [reasoned]

P4. What a piece of evidence is worth.

Model the value of each marker in the box as:

value = level signal × audibility × contingency × P(said correctly) − cost of an audible error × P(error)

  • Level signal: how strongly the feature separates upper B2 from lower B2. A cleft ("what I like is…"), dont, or a hypothetical si-clause scores high; "je voudrais" scores near zero, because it is an A2 politeness chunk.

  • Audibility: the rater only has audio (F4). A subjunctive that sounds the same as the indicative (que j'apporte, qu'il réserve) is worth zero.

  • Contingency: whether the sentence could only have been said after the examiner's last turn. Rehearsed-sounding material is discounted (F8). A memorised opener that every prepared candidate uses carries almost no information, however complex it is.

  • Error cost: an audible error in a complex structure is direct counter-evidence to the B2 syntax descriptor ("correctly"), so it costs more than the structure earns. [reasoned]

P5. Contingency: the most valuable sentence is a reaction.

A sentence that takes the examiner's last words and builds on them proves four things at once: Min listened (interaction), Min can build French on the fly (syntax not memorised), Min has words for the topic (lexis), and Min can do all this at speed (fluency). It cannot have been memorised. So:

  • Put the hard structures in the reactive turns, not in the opener.
  • Keep the opener short and natural. Its job is register and purpose, not proof. [reasoned from F2, F8]

P6. Game theory with the examiner: script the trap you know is coming.

The examiner's playbook is published (F2, F3):

  • In A, the price question gets a partial answer.
  • In B, the friend will disagree.
  • In B, a repeated argument gets probed.

Min can turn that predictability into scripted contingency. Ask the question whose partial answer you can predict, so the reaction can be prepared and still be a genuine reaction.

  • In A, the price question is the reliable trigger. The prepared follow-up turns the vague "it depends" into a precise question tied to Min's own constraint.

  • In B, the most predictable objections come from the ad itself: price, effort, comfort, time, "I've never done that". A canoe-camping week will draw "too tiring" or "sleeping in a tent is uncomfortable".

[reasoned]

P7. Follow-ups that work whatever the examiner says.

The box is a script, but the examiner's words are unknown. Two techniques keep the script honest:

  1. Questions at right angles to the answer. After "what happens if the weather is bad?", any answer allows "how much notice would you give me?". After any answer about content, "is that included in the price?" still works. The follow-up asks about a dimension (time, notice, cost, who, conditions, what if) that the first answer does not settle.

  2. Paraphrase only a gist you can predict. "So if I understand correctly, everything depends on the weather" is safe after a weather question, because every real answer has that gist. Never script a paraphrase of a price or schedule answer, because those details are unpredictable.

If an echo slot cannot be avoided, allow at most one, written as a stage direction ([repeat their key word]) and counted as 3 words. [reasoned]

P8. In French, compression and level point the same way.

Many B2 moves make French shorter:

Plainer French Upgraded French What happens
est-ce qu'il reste des places il en reste ? pronoun en, shorter
comment aller là-bas comment on y va ? pronoun y, shorter
quand on pagaie chaque jour en pagayant chaque jour gérondif replaces a clause
le matériel qu'on a besoin de (an error) le matériel dont on aura besoin dont, and the error is gone
s'il y a un problème avec la météo en cas de mauvais temps precise phrase, shorter

Rule: do substitution upgrades before addition upgrades. First make each needed sentence denser in level at the same length (a pronoun, a precise word, a gérondif). Only then add sentences for evidence that is still missing. [reasoned]

P9. How much one sentence can carry.

A sentence is sampled by all four criteria at once. One sentence can be a pragmatic move (task), a grammar sample (syntax), a word sample (lexis) and a delivery sample (fluency). Stacking across criteria is free. Stacking within one criterion is costly.

For example, "In that case, if the flight were cancelled because of the wind, how much notice would you give me?" gives a reaction (task), one si-hypothetical (syntax), annulé / vent / prévenir à l'avance (lexis) and formal vous (register) in 19 English words. That is four criteria and one hard structure.

Within syntax, here is a simple model [reasoned; the numbers are illustrative]:

  • Each marked structure succeeds with probability p, which falls by about 0.07 for each extra marked structure in the same sentence (planning load, F8).

  • A correct marker earns 1 unit; an audible error costs 2.

  • A sentence with k markers needs about 7 + 4k English words.

With p₁ = 0.95, value per word peaks at k = 2 (0.085 per word, against 0.077 at k = 1 and 0.068 at k = 3). With p₁ = 0.90, k = 1 and k = 2 tie and k = 3 falls clearly. Usable rules:

  • At most 2 marked structures per sentence, and at most 1 of them hard (subjunctive, si + imparfait + conditionnel, infinitif passé, au cas où + conditionnel, dont).

  • About 18 English words at most per sentence, so it comes out as one or two French breath groups.

  • Never two mood or tense agreements that depend on each other in one sentence. For example, je préférerais que vous m'appeliez pour que je ne fasse pas… stacks two subjunctives under a conditional.

P10. A practised self-repair turns an error into evidence.

The top of B2 is defined partly by slips that are rare and corrected after the fact (F7). A quick, practised repair, such as "si je viendrais — pardon, si je venais", costs 2–3 words and turns counter-evidence into an upper-B2 signal. So the box does not have to avoid every risky structure; Min needs a repair habit for the 3–4 structures in it. [reasoned from F7]

P11. Repeat only the evidence most likely to be challenged later.

Pressure makes the channel noisy. Repeat the evidence that the rest of the section is most likely to test, and nothing else:

  • A: two reactive turns, not one. One reaction can be luck or preparation. Two show the pattern the grid describes as confident reactions and an exchange that hangs together.

  • B: two objection replies, not one. One rebuttal can be prepared. A second shows the "firmly" in "defends firmly". It also means that one weak reply later in the section no longer reverses the rating.

  • Syntax: no repetition. Use one instance of each of four or five different structure types. Variety is itself the C1 glimpse (F1), and one instance of each type is enough proof.

[reasoned]

P12. Make each unit do two jobs.

  • A: stating a requirement and asking a hypothetical negotiating question fit in one turn ("I'm only free on weekends, and if I came with a friend, would you…"). That turn covers B2 goods and services, a si-clause and task initiative.

  • A: a paraphrase plus a link plus a new question ("So it all depends on the wind. In that case, …") gives the clarification check and the well-judged intervention in one turn.

  • B: an objection reply that brings in a new argument counts three times: defence, a new argument for variety, and a linked exchange. So 2 arguments plus 2 replies already give 3–4 distinct arguments.

  • B: the presentation can end on one judging clause ("what I like is that it mixes calm and challenge"). That clause structures the presentation and opens the case.

[reasoned]

P13. Precise words, not words from the ad.

Words printed on the ad prove nothing about range. Min is reading them. For each section, prepare two kinds of words:

  • Transaction words that every Section A prompt can use: tarif de groupe, formule, acompte, annulation, report, remboursement, créneau, la veille, à l'avance, sur place, inscription, prévenir.

  • Domain words for this ad only. For paragliding: moniteur, biplace / en tandem, vent, décollage, atterrissage, harnais, certificat médical, limite de poids.

Rules:

  • At least one precise word per turn, never one taken from the ad.

  • No clichés. Every prepared candidate says "inoubliable" and "sortir de sa zone de confort", so they carry almost no evidence.

  • No visible word-searching or English borrowing. Those are the B1 descriptor (F1). If a word is missing, slide to a plain general word (l'équipement) without pausing.

[reasoned from F1, F7]

P14. The "only here" test.

A question or argument that would make sense for any ad ("what are the hours?", "it'll be fun") is what a generic prep list produces, so it carries little evidence. One that only makes sense for this ad shows that Min understood the document and has topic-specific words. Examples: a weight limit for paragliding; "since it's a whole week and not a weekend, we'd really have time to switch off".

Rule: the box contains at least one only-here question (A), or one only-here argument (B). [reasoned; in information-theory terms, it is the surprising signal that carries the information]

P15. Register is free evidence.

  • A: vous, conditional politeness, one inversion or est-ce que question, a formal topic marker (en ce qui concerne).

  • B: tu, on, light informal markers (écoute, franchement, du coup, carrément).

These swap words rather than add them, and they directly show the CLB 7 line "adapts style and register to different audiences" (F6). Across both sections, the switch itself is evidence. [reasoned]

P16. Invest where preparation pays.

The criteria respond differently to preparation:

  • Lexis responds most: precise words can be chosen in advance.
  • Task comes next: precise questions and prepared reactions.
  • Syntax variety comes next.
  • Syntax accuracy responds less, because it depends on actual control.
  • Fluency responds least: it is Min's real skill, heard across the whole recording.

If the conversion even roughly averages the criteria (unknown, F4), the economical plan is to aim for touches of C1 on the criteria that preparation can move, as a buffer for a delivery that may sit at plain B2. [reasoned]

P17. Keep the scripted part at Min's natural pace.

Raters notice rehearsed stretches partly through jumps in articulation rate (F8). If the box comes out much faster or smoother than the rest of Min's speech, the box is discounted. That is the opposite of what we want. So:

  • short sentences;
  • natural pauses at clause boundaries;
  • deliver it at Min's real pace.

A box Min cannot say at an even pace is too long or too dense. That gives an independent check on P9. [reasoned]

P18. Don't spend the reserve, and don't invent facts.

  • The box takes the moves with the most evidence per word. The rest of the section still needs fuel: about 6–8 plain questions for A, and 2–3 extra arguments plus fallback replies for B. Running dry (silence, repeating questions, re-using the same argument) is the main real-world disaster (Part 4). So the box must not use up every good idea.

  • Never state ad facts that aren't printed ("everything is included"). The examiner knows the ad and can contradict them. Hedge instead: "I'd be surprised if it were more expensive than a hotel." A hedge also gives a subjunctive and the CLB 7 "qualify opinions" indicator for free.

[reasoned]

Part 3. The 6/7 line, criterion by criterion, and the cheapest proof

Criterion Lower B2 (about NCLC 6) Upper B2 (about NCLC 7) Cheapest proof in the box Approx. cost
A task Appropriate questions, some reactions Reacts confidently to partial answers twice; at least one precise question; one well-judged link Price trigger → prepared follow-up with a requirement; a paraphrase of a predictable gist followed by "in that case…" 2 reactive turns, ~45 words
B task Clear presentation; arguments with some detail; defends once Detailed, structured presentation; illustrated arguments of different kinds; holds the position through 2 objections Presentation of ~45 words with one judging clause; 2 illustrated arguments; 2 concede-and-counter replies ~120–150 words
Syntax Common complex sentences, some stiff or wrong Common complex sentences correct; visible variety 4–5 different marked types, one each, no more than 2 per sentence, practised repair free inside the turns above
Lexis Everyday range, some approximation Precise topic and transaction words; no circumlocution 1 precise word per turn, none from the ad, no clichés free (swapped in)
Fluency A few hesitations Few hesitations; even pace Short sentences said at natural pace cannot be bought; must not be harmed

[reasoned from F1, F6, F7]

Part 4. What "disaster" means, seen from this lens

After the point, the rest of the response is maintenance. Here is what can still pull a rating below the upper-B2 box, and what cannot. [reasoned from F1–F3]

Disasters (repeated or long enough to become the rater's picture of Min):

  • A: the questions run out, so the conversation depends on the examiner. That is the A1 descriptor for the task. Asking the same questions again, or ignoring answers.

  • B: giving in to the friend early; repeating one argument over and over (the booklet's named weakness); monologuing without answering what the friend said; reading the ad aloud.

  • Syntax: systematic basic errors, such as être / avoir forms, tense chaos, or gender errors that cause misunderstanding.

  • Lexis: switching to English, or long visible word-searching.

  • Fluency: long silences, strings of false starts.
  • Register: using the wrong register consistently, tu throughout A or vous throughout B.

Not disasters:

  • plainer sentences after the box;
  • re-using box structures;
  • occasional slips, especially if repaired;
  • one weak objection reply (the box already holds two strong ones);
  • simple questions after the box;
  • proposing a compromise late in B, since weighing alternatives is itself a B2 behaviour (F7).

The box is designed so that each item in the second list is outweighed by evidence already given, and so that the reserve (P18) prevents the first list.

Part 5. English wording that produces the right French structure

The box is written in English and translated later, so the English decides which French structure appears. The table gives English wording whose most natural French translation carries the target structure. "Risk" is the chance that a learner gets it wrong under pressure.

English wording Natural French Structure Heard on audio? Risk
I'd like to know whether… J'aimerais savoir si… conditional + indirect question yes low
Could you tell me how long…? Pourriez-vous me dire combien de temps…? polite conditional + indirect question yes low
It depends on what, exactly? Ça dépend de quoi, exactement ? reaction to a partial answer yes low
So if I understand correctly… Donc, si je comprends bien… paraphrase check yes low
In that case, … Dans ce cas, … link to the previous answer yes low
That's exactly why… Justement, c'est pour ça que… turning an objection around yes low
What I like is that… / What worries me is… Ce qui me plaît, c'est que… / Ce qui m'inquiète, c'est… cleft yes low
Are there any left? / How do we get there? Il en reste ? / Comment on y va ? en / y yes low
Is the equipment provided? Est-ce que l'équipement est fourni ? passive yes low
by paddling a bit every day en pagayant un peu chaque jour gérondif yes low
It's true that X, but… / even if… C'est vrai que X, mais… / même si… concession, indicative yes low
We've been saying for months that… Ça fait des mois qu'on dit que… ça fait… que + present yes low–med
the trip I told you about / the gear we'll need le voyage dont je t'ai parlé / le matériel dont on aura besoin dont yes med
If I came with a friend, would you…? Si je venais avec un ami, est-ce que vous… (feriez / auriez)? si + imparfait + conditionnel yes med
How about we…? Et si on (y allait / regardait…)? suggestion with imparfait yes med
When I get there, who do I ask for? Quand j'arriverai, je demande qui ? future after quand (an English speaker's trap) yes med
so that you can… (different subject) pour que vous puissiez… subjunctive, audible yes med
I understand that it scares you Je comprends que ça te fasse peur subjunctive, audible yes med
I'd be surprised if it were more expensive than a hotel Ça m'étonnerait que ce soit plus cher qu'un hôtel subjunctive + hedge yes med
unless the weather is bad à moins qu'il fasse mauvais subjunctive, audible yes med
after doing the ground training après avoir fait la formation au sol infinitif passé yes med–high
in case it rains au cas où il pleuvrait conditional after au cas où yes high

Wording to avoid (it loses the marker or invites an error):

  • so that I can… with the same subject comes out as pour pouvoir…: an infinitive, no subjunctive. For a subjunctive, the two subjects must differ.

  • Subjunctives of regular -er verbs in the je / tu / il / ils forms (apporte, réserve, regarde) can't be heard. Use être, avoir, faire, aller, pouvoir, savoir, vouloir, venir, prendre, or vous forms (-iez).

  • I'd like to know what… tempts the error j'aimerais savoir qu'est-ce que…. Prefer si / combien / comment / quand.

  • although produces bien que + subjunctive, which is risky and sounds memorised. Prefer même si unless it is well practised.

  • English idioms with no plain French equivalent push the translator toward rare French Min may stumble on.

  • Stock phrases (I'd like to…, it's a good idea, unforgettable) carry almost no evidence. Use them only as glue.

Suggestion: next to each box, outside it, keep a one-line structure note for each sentence (e.g. "S3 → si + imparfait + conditionnel"). The translator then keeps the structure the English was written to carry, and the box stays pure English.

Part 6. How to build a box: steps for a per-prompt analyst

  1. List the ad's printed facts and words. None of them count as lexical evidence. Note what the ad leaves unsaid: price details, conditions, who it is for, logistics. Those gaps are where the questions (A) or the illustrations (B) come from.

  2. Choose the triggers (P6):

    • A: the price question (partial answer guaranteed), plus one question whose gist you can predict (weather, conditions, level, safety).

    • B: the two objections the ad makes most likely, ranked. 3. Fill the unit list for the section (Part 7) and assign the units to turns. Put no more than 2 units in a turn. Put the hard structures in reactive turns (P5).

  3. Write each sentence in English using Part 5. Check the load: no more than 2 marked structures, no more than 1 hard, about 18 words at most (P9).

  4. Substitution pass (P8, P13, P15). Swap plain words for precise ones, nouns for pronouns, clauses for gérondifs, and add register markers. The word count should stay the same or drop.

  5. Coverage audit. Tick every unit in Part 7, plus:

    • at least 4 (A) or 5 (B) different marked structure types;
    • at least 4 (A) or 5 (B) precise words, none from the ad;
    • at least 1 only-here question or argument (P14);
    • at least 2 turns that react to the examiner;
    • register markers present.

  6. Deletion test (minimality). Remove each sentence in turn. If coverage still passes without it, delete it. Repeat until every remaining sentence is needed.

  7. Robustness test. For every examiner stage direction, imagine three plausible answers (helpful, partial, surprising). Min's next line must still make sense after all three (P7). If it does not, rewrite it at right angles to the answer.

  8. Say-aloud test (P17). Can it be said in French at Min's own pace, with no hesitation in the middle of a sentence? If not, split the sentence or drop one marker.

  9. Count Min's English words (contractions count as one word, stage directions do not count). Compare with the floor in Part 7. The French will run about 10–25% longer. [reasoned]

  10. Write down the reserve (P18): what Min says after the point. It is not part of the box, but without it the box can't hold.

Part 7. The word floor

Section A units (target: upper B2 on all four criteria):

# Unit Grid or benchmark basis
A1 Opening and purpose, vous CLB 7 opens a formal call
A2 One stated requirement (Min's level, availability or company) CEFR B2 goods and services
A3–A5 Three precise questions, at least one only-here task relevance and precision
A6 Follow-up to the partial answer task: reacts confidently, handles the unexpected
A7 Paraphrase of a predictable gist CEFR B2 clarification; CLB 7
A8 One well-judged link ("in that case…") C1 glimpse
A9 Hypothetical or negotiating question B2 goods and services; si-clause

Plus 4 or more structure types and 4 or more precise words, carried inside these units. A6, A7 and A8 must each come after an examiner turn, so the minimum is 4 of Min's turns (5 is safer).

Minimum packing gives turns of about 28 + 8 + 18 + 9 + 25 words. The theoretical floor for Section A is about 75–80 English words. The robust version is about 90–105 (it adds a brief reaction word in each turn, the requirement stated in full, and an indirect question). Below about 75 words, a unit has to go. The cheapest to drop is A8, the C1 glimpse. The price of dropping it: the task rating rests on plain B2 and nothing pushes it toward the upper box. [reasoned]

Section B units:

# Unit Grid or benchmark basis
B1 Return the greeting and open —
B2 Presentation: who, what, how, booking; reworded, with one judging clause B2 clear and detailed; C1 structured
B3 Argument 1, aimed at the friend, plus an illustration (scenario or comparison) B2 illustrated
B4 Argument 2 of a different kind, ideally only-here C1 varied
B5 Reply 1: echo the objection, concede, bring in a new argument defends; exchange hangs together
B6 Reply 2: concede or hedge, then a counter-proposal or hypothetical defends firmly; B2 makes and answers hypotheses

Plus 5 or more structure types, 5 or more precise words, and informal register markers.

Minimum packing: about 3 + 42 + 22 + 15 + 30 + 18 words. The theoretical floor for Section B is about 120–130 English words. The robust version is about 140–160. Below about 120 words, the cheapest unit to drop is B4. Reply 1 still brings in a new argument (P12), so variety survives, just barely. [reasoned]

Timing. Assume about 110–130 French words a minute for a prepared learner [reasoned] and short examiner turns.

  • A: the point falls around 1:15–1:45 into the 5 minutes.
  • B: the point falls around 2:00–2:45 into the 10 minutes.
  • Together the two boxes come to about 200–265 English words. Everything after them is maintenance.

Part 8. Test: what the lens says A-01 and B-03 must contain

These are lists of elements with rough wording to test the principles. They are not final scripts. Counts are Min's English words.

A-01: paragliding introduction (Section A)

The ad prints: ground training, practical trials, a series of mini-flights, a phone number, exceptional rates.

[examiner: "Hello?"]

  1. Opening + requirement + only-here question (A1, A2, A3). "Hello, I'm calling about your introduction to paragliding. I've never flown before, so I'd like to know whether the mini-flights are done alone or in tandem with an instructor." (29 words. Markers: n'ai jamais volé; j'aimerais savoir si; en biplace / moniteur.)

[examiner answers]

  1. The price trigger (A4). "OK, that's reassuring. And regarding the rates, how much is the introduction?" (12 words. Markers: c'est rassurant; en ce qui concerne.)

[examiner: partial answer, "it depends, we have several packages"]

  1. Reaction to the partial answer + requirement + hypothetical negotiation (A6, A9). "It depends on what, exactly? I'm only free on weekends, and if I came with a friend, would you give us a group rate?" (24 words. Markers: ça dépend de quoi; si je venais… vous nous feriez; tarif de groupe.)

[examiner answers]

  1. Question with a predictable gist (A5). "Perfect. And what happens if there's too much wind on the day?" (12 words. Markers: qu'est-ce qui se passe; vent; le jour J.)

[examiner: depends on the conditions / postponed]

  1. Paraphrase + link + subjunctive (A7, A8). "So if I understand correctly, everything depends on the weather. In that case, could you call me the day before, so that I don't drive out there for nothing?" (29 words. Markers: si je comprends bien; dans ce cas; pourriez-vous; la veille; pour que je ne fasse pas la route pour rien.)

Total about 106 words (robust version). The floor version is about 80: drop "OK, that's reassuring", "Perfect", "before", the weekend requirement and the indirect question.

Structure types present: passé composé with negation, indirect question, si + imparfait + conditionnel, polite conditional, subjunctive (fasse), passive. Precise words: biplace / moniteur, tarif de groupe, vent, la veille, faire la route.

The reserve for after the point: medical certificate, weight limit, what to wear or bring, the ground-training content, deposit and cancellation, how many mini-flights, where the meeting point is.

B-03: canoe-camping week (Section B)

[friend: "Hi! How are you?"]

  1. Greeting + presentation with a judging clause (B1, B2). "Great, and you? Listen, I saw an ad from a travel agency, Vacances Nature. They're organizing a week of canoe-camping: we'd paddle during the day and sleep in a tent at night. What I like is that it mixes calm and real challenge, in the region's most beautiful landscapes. You book online." (52 words. Markers: conditional on pagaierait / on dormirait; cleft "ce qui me plaît, c'est que"; reworded rather than read.)

  2. Argument 1, aimed at the friend, plus illustration (B3). "We've been saying for ages that we should go away together, and this is perfect for us: no screens, no traffic, just the lake, and dinner by the fire." (29 words. Markers: ça fait des mois qu'on dit qu'on devrait; concrete scenario.)

  3. Argument 2, only-here (B4). "And since it's a whole week, not just a weekend, we'd actually have time to switch off." (17 words. Markers: comme causal; on aurait; décrocher.)

[friend objects, most likely: "it'll be too tiring, I'm not sporty"]

  1. Reply 1: echo + concession + new argument (B5). "I understand that it scares you, but the ad also talks about calm moments: we'd go at our own pace. And by paddling a little every day, you'd get stronger without even noticing." (33 words. Markers: que ça te fasse peur, an audible subjunctive; à notre rythme; gérondif; the fitness argument is new.)

[friend objects again, likely: price or comfort]

  1. Reply 2: hedge + counter-proposal (B6). "Honestly, I'd be surprised if it were more expensive than a hotel. How about we look at the website together tonight, and then you decide?" (24 words. Markers: ça m'étonnerait que ce soit; et si on regardait.) If the second objection is not about price, swap the hedge for the matching one, e.g. comfort: "even if it's a tent, the ad says you sleep comfortably", and keep the "how about we…" close.

Total about 155 words (robust version). The floor version is about 125: drop unit 3 and shorten unit 5 to the "how about we…" line.

Structure types present: conditional, cleft, ça fait… que, causal comme, two audible subjunctives in separate sentences, gérondif, et si + imparfait. Precise words: décrocher, à notre rythme, pagayer, le feu de camp. Register: tu, on, écoute, franchement.

The reserve for after the point: safety (guides, routes), the "first time in a canoe" objection, insects or weather, splitting costs, a shorter alternative if the week is too long.

Part 9. The claims I am least sure of

  1. That NCLC 7 equals "the upper B2 box". I inferred this from the grid's two boxes per level and from both score tables placing NCLC 7 in the upper part of B2. The way the eight ticks become a calibrated score is not public. If the formula weights the task criteria or Section B more heavily, the most efficient allocation of words changes; the minimax rule (P2) does not.

  2. How the definitive rater forms the rating. If raters rate the typical level heard across the recording rather than the best evidence shown, the box's power to lock the score is weaker, and "not a disaster" demands more of the rest. Lens 2 should settle this.

  3. The numbers in the packing model (P9) are illustrative. The rule it yields (no more than 2 markers per sentence, no more than 1 hard) is robust across plausible values, but the exact optimum is not measured.

  4. That TEF raters reward contingency and discount rehearsed speech. This comes from general rater research (OPIc, ACTFL, LAQ 2020), not from TEF-specific evidence.

  5. The word floors (about 80 for A, about 125 for B) come from a count of evidence units, not from rated samples. The robust figures (about 100 and about 150) are the ones to plan with.

  6. Score scales. FRAME.md's 0–450 bands are right for IRCC. Certificates since December 2023 also show a 0–699 score, on which speaking NCLC 7 is 456–493. The CCI chart linking 0–699 to CEFR (B2 = 400–499) is cited from an indexed copy that now returns 404.
