Section A · the call to the business

A-01

PARAGLIDING

Outdoor & adventure · Active adventure & leisure · est. #1 of 78
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement for an introduction to paragliding and you are interested. You call to get more information.

PARAGLIDING

On the way to the clouds…

Discover new sensations in paragliding!

On the program of your introduction to paragliding:

- Ground training

- Practical trials

- Series of mini-flights

BOOK AT

450-629-6997

Exceptional rates!

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité pour une initiation au parapente et vous êtes intéressé(e). Vous téléphonez pour avoir plus d’informations.

PARAPENTE

En route pour les nuages…

Découvrez de nouvelles sensations en parapente !

Au programme de votre initiation au parapente :

- Formation au sol

- Essais pratiques

- Séries de mini-vols

RÉSERVEZ AU

450-629-6997

Tarifs exceptionnels !

The point

The point falls at the end of your fifth turn (the mini-flights question), about 1:25 after the examiner's "Hello?". That is about 58 seconds of your own speech (101 words x 0.58) plus five short examiner replies and the hand-overs.

The box101 words

[examiner: "Hello?"]

Good morning, I'd like to know how much your introduction to paragliding costs.

[examiner: "It depends, we have several packages."]

In that case, which package would you recommend for a complete beginner who's only free on weekends?

[examiner: "For beginners, our one-day introduction, Saturdays or Sundays."]

Noted. Besides, if there were too much wind to take off, what would you do?

[examiner: "We'd postpone the flights to another date."]

So if I understand correctly, it all depends on the wind. Since I live an hour away by car, how far in advance would you warn me?

[examiner: "We'd call you the evening before."]

Okay. And during the mini-flights, will I be alone up there? Even if they're short, I'd like an instructor to be with me, at least the first time.

Why this, and why nothing less

By the end of your fifth turn the recording holds every kind of evidence the grid asks for at upper B2, in turns that only make sense after what the advertiser just said, so a rater cannot write it off as memorised.

Task (obtaining information). Your opening question is the one the examiner's booklet scripts a partial answer for (price), so the "it depends" comes in their first reply. Your second turn takes their own noun ("which package") and turns the vague answer into a precise request tied to your situation (beginner, weekends only). That is the B2 rung: you react to an answer and steer. Your fourth turn restates the gist of their wind answer and draws a consequence for you (you live an hour away, so how much notice will they give?). That is the C1 glimpse: a well-judged question that combines their answer with your situation. Your third and fifth questions only make sense for paragliding (too much wind to take off; alone up there or with an instructor).

Syntax. A "marked structure" is a grammar form that only stronger speakers use reliably. You produce five different ones: an indirect question with inversion, combien coûte votre initiation (turn 1); a relative clause with ne... que (turn 2); si + imperfect + conditional and trop de... pour + infinitive (turn 3); an audible subjunctive, soit (turn 5). The future and the polite conditionals are not counted. No sentence holds more than one hard one. The links cover four relations: consequence (dans ce cas, donc), addition (par ailleurs), cause (comme) and concession (même si).

Lexis. Six precise words, none printed on the ad and all produced by the English itself: débutant, vent, décoller, à l'avance, prévenir, moniteur. No clichés.

Delivery and register. No sentence runs past about 18 French words; no word is rare. Vous and votre from the first sentence, polite conditionals throughout.

Replication and control. Clear evidence lands in four separate turns, and two of them (turns 2 and 4) are replies to what the examiner said. On the shared conversion (seconds = English words x 0.58), the box gives about 58 seconds of clean speech, the floor for the rater to presume control. After that only a pattern of trouble can take it away.

Why nothing can go. The box sits at the time floor (about 100 words), so a word with no evidence would be swapped for one with evidence, not deleted.

  • Turn 1 names the ad, sets vous, and asks the probe. Without the price question, no partial answer comes.
  • "Which package" is the audible uptake on "it depends". "Who's only free on weekends" carries the ne... que, the only solid structure in that reply turn.
  • "Noted" and "Okay" are one word each. Without them your next question ignores their reply, and that is what a recited list sounds like.
  • "Besides" is the addition link, and "even if" is the concession link. Without both you are down to two relations.
  • Turn 3 is the only-here question, the hard hypothetical, and the question whose answer turn 4 restates.
  • In turn 4, the restatement is the "reword" half of the C1 move, and "since I live an hour away" is the "consequence" half. Remove either and the move drops to plain B2.
  • Turn 5 carries the second only-here question, the subjunctive and the concession, and takes your speech from about 42 to about 58 seconds.

What the next cut would cost. If you stop after turn 4 (72 words, about 42 seconds), every capacity is proved once, but the box ends before the point, because control is not yet presumed. If you cut the "since" clause, the task rating rests on plain B2, the coin-flip zone between 6 and 7.

What the box assumes. It assumes the playbook's partial answer to price (Working has fallbacks) and that you can say the five turns at your normal pace. After the point you keep asking linked questions for three more minutes (the reserve in Working). You need to sound like a solid NCLC 6 on a quiet day: plain, correct sentences, no long silences, no English.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it counts as vocabulary evidence): parapente, initiation, en route pour les nuages, découvrez de nouvelles sensations, au programme, formation au sol, essais pratiques, séries de mini-vols, réservez au, tarifs exceptionnels, the phone number. Note that route is printed ("en route"), so une heure de route would reuse an ad word. The box uses à une heure de voiture instead.

What it leaves unsaid: the price and what it covers (gear, number of flights, insurance); how long the introduction lasts (one day, a weekend, several sessions) and whether the three stages are on the same day; who it is for (minimum age, weight range, fitness, medical certificate); what happens with bad weather, which matters more for paragliding than for almost any other activity because wind decides whether you can take off at all; whether the "mini-flights" are solo (in real schools they usually are: short, low glides on a gentle slope with the instructor guiding by radio) or in tandem; group size; where the site is, access and parking; how to book (deposit, cancellation, postponement); how long the "exceptional rates" last.

Who is on the other end: someone at a small paragliding school (reception or an instructor), answering a beginner's call. Formal register.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad:

  • Partial answer: price. "It depends, we have several packages" (the booklet's own example), possibly with "and there's a promotion right now" to echo "exceptional rates".
  • Unexpected elements they are likely to have ready: a weight range (for example 40 to 100 kg), a minimum age, a medical certificate, sessions cancelled or moved for wind, a non-refundable deposit, a need to be fit enough to walk up the slope carrying the wing, sturdy boots required, dates full until next month, weekday-only sessions.
  • Section A has no objections as such, but the examiner relaunches with statements ("you need to book two weeks ahead") and keeps answers short to see whether you follow up.

Step 2. The hard part

The ad is thin: a three-item programme and "exceptional rates". Every obvious question (price, length, equipment, location) fits any ad, so it carries no "only here" credit. The only-here material has to come from what paragliding actually involves: wind, taking off, landing, flying alone or with an instructor, weight limits.

The hardest condition here is the C1 glimpse, because it has to be reactive and safe at the same time. You can only restate a gist you can predict, and for paragliding one gist is close to certain: any answer to a wind question admits that the flights depend on the wind on the day. So the wind question is the keystone. It gives the only-here question (turn 3) and a predictable gist for the restatement (turn 4).

The second binding condition is control (condition 4). The reactive core (frame, partial answer, wind question, restatement plus consequence) fits in about 72 English words, which is about 42 seconds on the shared conversion (English words x 0.58). That is short of the floor of about 100 words (58 seconds) a rater needs to presume control. So the fifth turn is there for time, and it must earn that time with evidence: a second only-here question, the box's only audible subjunctive and its concession link.

Precise words this topic offers, none on the ad: moniteur, vent, décoller, atterrir, voile, casque, poids, biplace, pente école, radio, reporter, acompte, prévenir à l'avance.

Step 3. Designs compared

A. Price-then-wind chain, five turns (chosen). 101 words, point at about 1:25. Turn 1 is the probe, turn 2 reacts to the partial answer, turn 3 asks the only-here hypothetical, turn 4 restates and draws a consequence, turn 5 adds the second only-here question, the concession and the subjunctive. Each examiner reply is followed by a line that still works whatever they say (see the reply table). All the hard grammar sits in turns 2 to 5, none in the opener.

B. Self-portrait opener (unconventional). About 115 words, point at about 1:50. Turn 1 front-loads your situation: never flown, a bit scared of heights, an hour away, weekends only. Every later question then becomes only-here, and the examiner's reassuring reply ("don't worry, the mini-flights are very low") becomes the gist to restate. Rejected for three reasons. The evidence lands in the opener, which raters discount as rehearsed. A 35-word opener sounds like a monologue. And the reassurance gist is less certain than the wind gist: they may say "you'll be with an instructor" or "it's very safe" or "the slope is gentle", so a scripted restatement ("so I'd stay close to the ground") can come out wrong.

C. Two probes in one question (unconventional). About 95 words, point at about 1:30. "How much is it, and would I be refunded if the wind stopped the flights?" The aim is a partial answer on both halves at once, so one reply turn covers two chains. Rejected because the playbook says partial: the examiner answers one half and leaves the other hanging, so you spend a turn re-asking anyway. It also puts the hard hypothetical into the opener, the turn raters believe least.

D. Restate the ad, not the examiner (unconventional). About 100 words. The C1 move comes from the printed programme order: "If I understand correctly, the flights come last, after the ground training. So if the wind rose that day, would I still do the ground training?" It is perfectly safe because the ad prints the order. Rejected as the main move because it is not a reply to anything the examiner said, so it does not count toward the two reactive turns. On the grid, paraphrasing the document is also closer to the A2 habit than to "reformulates to check". Kept as a fallback (see the reply table).

E. Four-turn minimum. 72 words, about 42 seconds of your speech. Design A without turn 5. Every capacity is proved once, but condition 4 fails, so the box ends before the point and the point does not come sooner. Rejected. This is "the next cut" in Why.

Why A wins: it has the fewest words that meet all five conditions, every hard structure sits in a reactive turn, and the two predictions it rests on (a partial answer to price, a wind answer that concedes weather dependence) are the two most certain moves in this call.

Step 4. Structure note for each sentence (the French the English is written to carry)

Turn English Target French Structures and words
1 Good morning, I'd like to know how much your introduction to paragliding costs. Bonjour, j'aimerais savoir combien coûte votre initiation au parapente. One sentence: frame and probe together. Vous through votre; conditional + indirect question with inversion (combien coûte votre initiation; combien votre initiation coûte is also correct). "How much", not "what", so there's no qu'est-ce que trap. "Good morning", not "Hello", so the French is Bonjour, never Salut.
2 In that case, which package would you recommend for a complete beginner who's only free on weekends? Dans ce cas, quel forfait vous me conseilleriez pour un parfait débutant (une parfaite débutante) qui n'est libre que le week-end ? Consequence link plus the examiner's own noun (two audible uptakes); polite conditional; relative qui; restriction ne... que. Two marked, none hard. Say their noun with your own quel / quelle: quel forfait, quelle formule, quelle option. The agreement is with the noun you say, so there's no lequel risk.
3a Noted. C'est noté. Uptake.
3b Besides, if there were too much wind to take off, what would you do? Par ailleurs, s'il y avait trop de vent pour décoller, qu'est-ce que vous feriez ? Addition link; si + imperfect + conditional (hard); trop de... pour + infinitive. Irregular conditional feriez. "What would you do" is a direct question, so qu'est-ce que is right, and it avoids the qu'est-ce qui / que choice of "what would happen". Only here: vent, décoller.
4a So if I understand correctly, it all depends on the wind. Donc, si je comprends bien, tout dépend du vent. Restatement of a gist your own question made near certain; dépendre de + du.
4b Since I live an hour away by car, how far in advance would you warn me? Comme j'habite à une heure de voiture, vous me préviendriez combien de temps à l'avance ? Cause link; conditional préviendriez; à l'avance. "Warn", not "let me know", so the English itself produces prévenir. If préviendriez is not automatic for you, vous m'avertiriez is the other natural rendering.
5a Okay. And during the mini-flights, will I be alone up there? D'accord. Et pendant les mini-vols, je serai seul(e) là-haut ? Simple future (not counted as marked); là-haut is everyday and not counted. Mini-vols is an ad word, used only as the anchor.
5b Even if they're short, I'd like an instructor to be with me, at least the first time. Même s'ils sont courts, j'aimerais qu'un moniteur soit avec moi, au moins la première fois. Concession link; audible subjunctive soit after aimer que (hard); au moins; moniteur. "They're short" comes from the ad's "mini", so no new fact is claimed. It goes at the front because after "with me" the pronoun would point to the instructors. "An instructor to be with me" is what produces que... soit; "to have an instructor" would give avoir and no subjunctive.

Translator notes (each only chooses between natural renderings of the English):

  • "Complete beginner": parfait débutant or débutant complet. Both carry débutant.
  • "Since": comme, not puisque. The examiner doesn't already know the reason, so puisque would sound off.
  • "Besides": par ailleurs (or en plus), not aussi, which means "so" at the start of a sentence.
  • "Noted": c'est noté.
  • "Instructor": moniteur, the usual word at a paragliding school (instructeur is also correct and also precise).

Substitution pass (same length, denser): the opener was merged into one sentence, "I'd like to know how much your introduction to paragliding costs". That kept the frame and probe, dropped au sujet de, and saved 4 words, which went to evidence in turn 5. "What would you advise" became "which package would you recommend" (the examiner's noun as uptake). "Let me know" became "warn me" (the English now produces prévenir). "What happens if it's windy" became "if there were too much wind to take off, what would you do" (hard hypothetical, trop de... pour, décoller). "An hour's drive" became "an hour away by car" (route is on the ad). "Will an instructor fly with me" became "will I be alone up there? Even if they're short, I'd like an instructor to be with me" (second only-here question, concession, audible subjunctive). "That day" was cut from turn 3, because nothing depended on it.

Step 5. Deletion test

The box is at the time floor (101 words, about 58 seconds; the floor is about 100). A piece that carries no evidence is swapped for one that does, not deleted, because deleting it ends the box before the point.

Piece removed What breaks
"Good morning" Frame: an abrupt start to a formal call, and it takes 2 words of the time floor.
"your introduction to paragliding" Frame: the ad is never named. Votre goes too.
"I'd like to know how much ... costs" No partial answer arrives, so turn 2 has nothing to react to (condition 2, task). The indirect question goes too.
"In that case" One of the two uptakes on "it depends", and one consequence link. "Which package" alone would still be an uptake, but you'd be down to cause, addition and concession for links, with no consequence link until turn 4's "so". Kept for the floor and for the clearest uptake.
"which package" (as "what") The noun uptake goes. Standard 10 is still met by "in that case", but the turn loses an audible piece taken from the reply. Same length either way, so the noun stays.
"for a complete beginner" The request has no anchor in your situation. Débutant goes.
"who's only free on weekends" Ne... que and the relative go, so turn 2 has no solid marked structure (condition 2 wants one inside a reply turn). The advice question also turns generic.
"Noted" Turn 3 ignores the recommendation it follows, which is how a recited list sounds (condition 5).
"Besides" The addition link goes. Three relations would remain (consequence, cause, concession), but you'd be below the floor, so it stays.
Turn 3 question You lose the only-here question, the hard hypothetical, and the answer turn 4 restates. Conditions 2 and 3 fail.
"So if I understand correctly, it all depends on the wind" The "reword" half of the C1 move goes, and the task tops out at B2.
"Since I live an hour away by car" The notice question becomes generic. The judged consequence (C1) and the cause link go.
"how far in advance would you warn me" Turn 4 has no question, so the exchange stops. Prévenir and à l'avance go too.
"Okay" Turn 5 ignores the answer about notice (condition 5).
"will I be alone up there" The second only-here question goes, and speech falls to about 55 seconds (95 words), below the floor.
"Even if they're short" The concession link goes (four relations down to three). Speech drops to about 56 seconds, below the floor.
"I'd like an instructor to be with me" The only audible subjunctive and moniteur go. Variety drops and condition 4 fails.
"at least the first time" Speech drops to about 56 seconds, below the floor. It also turns the wish into a reasonable request from a beginner. It is the first piece to swap if you find something with more evidence.

Step 6. Examiner-reply test (three replies per turn; your next line still has to work)

After Helpful Partial Surprising Your line
"Hello?" "Paragliding school, good morning." "Hello?" "Air Libre, can I help you?" Turn 1 works after all three.
Turn 1 (price) "180 dollars for the day, everything included." "It depends, we have several packages." "It depends on the season." / "It depends, but you must weigh 40 to 100 kilos." Partial: as scripted, with their noun (quelle formule, quel forfait, quelle option). If they named no noun (season): "In that case, what would you recommend for a complete beginner who's only free on weekends?" Helpful: "180 dollars, very well. Is that suitable for a complete beginner who's only free on weekends?" (Est-ce que ça convient à...; keeps ne... que). Weight: "Ah, that's fine for me. In that case, which package...?"
Turn 2 (advice) "The one-day introduction, Saturdays, 190 dollars." "It depends on your goals." "Our introductions only run on weekdays." Helpful and partial: "Noted." works. Surprising: "Ah, that's a problem, because I work during the week. Could you make an exception on a Saturday?" Then turn 3 when the topic closes. Handling this kind of unexpected answer is B2 evidence in itself.
Turn 3 (wind) "We'd postpone to another date at no cost." "It depends, we decide on the morning." "That rarely happens, our site is sheltered." Helpful: as scripted. Partial: "So if I understand correctly, it all depends on the wind. On the morning itself? Since I live an hour away by car, could you warn me the evening before?" Surprising: "Ah, that's reassuring. Even so, since I live an hour away by car, how far in advance would you warn me if it happened?" (quand même). Deposit kept: "So if I understand correctly, I'd lose my deposit even if the wind is to blame?" Then the notice question.
Turn 3, notice already given "We'd postpone and call you the evening before." Don't ask what was just answered. Say: "So if I understand correctly, it all depends on the wind. Since I live an hour away by car, what if the wind changed that morning?" (Comme j'habite à une heure de voiture, et si le vent changeait le matin même ?) This keeps the restatement, the cause link and a hypothetical.
Turn 4 (notice) "The evening before, by phone." "As soon as we know." "We can't always tell; sometimes we decide on site." "Okay" works after all three. For the surprising one, add first: "So I could drive an hour for nothing. Could I at least call you before I leave?" Then turn 5.
Already answered If an earlier reply already said the mini-flights are solo with radio guidance, swap turn 5 for: "And for the mini-flights, how high would I go? Even if they're short, I'd like an instructor to be close by, at least the first time." Same subjunctive, same concession, same length.

Lines to drill as frames, not fixed sentences (standard 13): in turn 2, "in that case, which [their noun] would you recommend..." (repeat the noun they said; with no noun, "what would you recommend"). In turn 4, "so if I understand correctly, it all depends on [the wind / the weather / their key word]". Your own question makes the wind gist near certain, but if they say "the weather" or "the conditions", repeat their word.

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6: "This was a prepared list: price, wind, notice, alone or not. Only two turns build on what I said, and 'it all depends on the wind' just repeats the premise of the candidate's own question, so the reformulation is cheap. I heard under a minute of speech and no unexpected answer being handled. The grammar is conditionals everywhere, a stock si clause and a stock j'aimerais que. Correct common complex sentences is lower B2."

How the box answers it:

  • The reformulation. The C1 credit doesn't rest on the restatement alone. It rests on what you do with it in the same breath: their answer plus your hour's drive gives a question about notice. You couldn't ask it before hearing the wind answer, and it isn't a generic follow-up. Fix made: the cause clause sits in the same turn as the restatement, so the restatement is used right away. When their answer differs, the fallbacks restate what they actually said ("on the morning itself?", "I'd lose my deposit even if the wind is to blame?", "what if the wind changed that morning?").
  • Only two reactive turns. Two is the replication rule (P6). Turn 2 now takes their noun as well as "in that case", and the other two questions open with an uptake. Turn 2 reacts to a partial answer, which the grid puts beside unexpected answers ("reacts with confidence, even to unexpected ones"). Where the unexpected element lands is the examiner's choice, and the reply table handles each likely one at the same rung.
  • Under a minute. On the shared conversion the box gives about 58 seconds, the agreed floor. Fix made: turn 5 carries a question, a concession and the subjunctive sentence, not a single question (which left about 42 seconds).
  • Grammar. There are five different marked types, not just conditionals: an inverted indirect question, ne... que, trop de... pour + infinitive, a si hypothetical about the one real risk of this activity, and an audible subjunctive. The links run through four relations, including a concession. Range is what separates the upper B2 box from the lower one.
  • After the fixes, the case fails on every count it relies on. Rerunning the deletion test confirms that every piece holds a condition or the floor.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

Every sentence is one or two breath groups of about 18 French words at most, and each pause falls at a comma. The sentences to drill are s'il y avait trop de vent pour décoller (smooth once practised), vous me préviendriez (or vous m'avertiriez), and même s'ils sont courts, j'aimerais qu'un moniteur soit avec moi (three short groups, no hard sounds). No sentence asks for two mood choices that depend on each other. The conditional j'aimerais is a politeness choice, and soit is the only form that depends on another.

Step 9. Count and timing (shared conversion)

  • Your words in the box: 101 English. Seconds of your speech = 101 x 0.58 = about 58 seconds (French about 106 words at 120 a minute, plus 10% for pauses). That is at the floor of about 100 words.
  • The clock starts at the examiner's "Hello?". The examiner's five short replies take about 12 seconds, and the hand-overs about 10 to 15 more.
  • So the point falls at about 1:20 to 1:30, roughly 1:25 into the five minutes. That leaves about 3:35 of buffer, where only a pattern of trouble counts.

Reserve (after the point; not part of the box)

  1. Follow up on their answer about flying alone: "And how high do the mini-flights go? Do you guide us by radio?"
  2. "Is there a weight or age limit?"
  3. "Is the equipment provided, the wing and the helmet? What kind of shoes should I wear?"
  4. "How many students are there per instructor?"
  5. "Do I need insurance or a medical certificate?"
  6. "Where exactly is the take-off site, and is there parking?"
  7. "Your ad mentions exceptional rates. Until when are they valid?"
  8. "To book, do I have to pay a deposit? And if I had to cancel, would I be refunded?"
  9. If the call runs dry, use design D's move: "If I understand correctly, the flights come last, after the ground training. So if the wind rose that day, would I still do the ground training?"
  10. Closing, only when the examiner signals the end: "Thank you, that's very clear. I'll check my dates and call you back to book."

After review

  • Review cut 1 (merge the opener): applied. Turn 1 is now one sentence of 13 words. Because the box sits at the time floor (standard 5), the 4 words saved went to evidence (the concession in turn 5), not to a shorter box.
  • Review suggestion 1 (concession instead of "at least the first time"): applied in part. "Even if they're short" now opens the last sentence. "At least the first time" stays, because cutting it would leave 96 words (about 56 seconds), below the floor. Under standard 6, comme (cause) already counts, so the concession is a fourth relation and gives margin.
  • Review suggestion 2 (turn 4 when the wind answer already gives the notice): applied. A new row in the reply table uses "what if the wind changed that morning?".
  • Review suggestion 3 (lexis list): applied. Conseiller, libre, dépendre de and à une heure de voiture are no longer counted. The list is now six precise words: débutant, vent, décoller, à l'avance, prévenir, moniteur (là-haut dropped after the final audit).
  • Across the chunk 1 and standard 5 (time floor): applied. 101 words x 0.58 = about 58 seconds. The deletion test now swaps rather than deletes, and Point, Why and Step 9 use this conversion.
  • Across the chunk 2 and standard 6 (links): checked. Consequence (dans ce cas, donc), addition (par ailleurs), cause (comme) and now concession (même si). The si clause is counted only as syntax, and the topic marker au sujet de is gone.
  • Across the chunk 3, 7 and standard 10 (uptake on "it depends"): applied. Turn 2 keeps "In that case" and now says the noun ("which package"). Working says to repeat their noun and gives the no-noun fallback.
  • Across the chunk 4 and standard 11 (one-sentence opener): applied (see cut 1).
  • Across the chunk 5 and standard 13 (lines that depend on the examiner): applied. There's a frames note under the reply table for turn 2's noun and turn 4's restatement.
  • Across the chunk 6, standards 12 and 19 (precise words): applied (see suggestion 3). Words the examiner supplies (forfait, reporter) are not counted.
  • Standard 1 (audible subjunctive): checked. Soit is audible, and "an instructor to be with me" produces que... soit on its own.
  • Standard 2 (no indirect "what"): checked. The only indirect question is "how much". "Which package" and "what would you do" are direct.
  • Standard 7 (timing basis): applied. The point is timed from the examiner's "Hello?", at about 1:25.
  • Standard 8 (translator notes): applied. The list is under Step 4.
  • Standard 9 (facts not on the ad): checked. "An hour away by car" is your own situation, "they're short" comes from the ad's "mini", and everything else is asked as a question.
  • Standard 14 (format): checked. The box opens with [examiner: "Hello?"].
  • Standard 15 (English makes its own French): applied. "Let me know" became "warn me" so the English produces prévenir. The Step 4 French matches each English line.
  • Standards 3, 4, 16, 17, 18 (Section B only): not applicable.
  • Final audit (A-01): applied. Là-haut is no longer counted as precise (six real words remain, above the five section C needs, so the box is unchanged). The structure count is now five marked types (future and polite conditionals not counted), the longest sentence is about 18 French words, and cutting "will I be alone up there" leaves 95 words, about 55 seconds.

A-02

DANSE ACADÉMIE

Courses, lessons & workshops · Courses & lessons · est. #12 of 78
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement for dance classes and you are interested. You phone to get more information.

DANSE ACADÉMIE

Do you want to learn to dance? To improve your skills?

Come and enroll at our school Danse Académie!

Different types of dances

Flexible Schedules

Themed evenings

Warm atmosphere!

Information and registration:

www.danseacademie.org

Tel: 00810 321 11

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité pour des cours de danse et vous êtes intéressé(e). Vous téléphonez pour avoir plus d’informations.

DANSE ACADÉMIE

Vous souhaitez apprendre à danser ? Vous perfectionner ?

Venez vous inscrire dans notre école Danse Académie !

Différents types de danses

Horaires Flexibles

Soirées à thèmes

Ambiance chaleureuse !

Renseignements et inscriptions :

www.danseacademie.org

Tél : 00810 321 11

The point

The point falls at the end of your fourth turn, the question about the themed evenings. That is about 1:15 after the examiner's "Hello?" (about 58 seconds of your own speech on the standard conversion, plus three short examiner replies).

The box100 words

[examiner: "Hello?"]

Hello, I'm calling to find out how much your dance classes cost for a complete beginner.

[examiner: "It depends, we have several packages."]

In that case, which package would you recommend? I work nights every other week. So if I paid for a whole term, I'd miss half the classes.

[examiner: "Then take our ten-class card. You come whenever you want."]

So if I understand correctly, I could change slots from week to week. In that case, for couple dances, would you need me to come with my own partner?

[examiner: "No, don't worry. Partners rotate during the class."]

That's reassuring, because I don't know anyone who dances. Besides, the themed evenings, could I go even if I'm only just starting? Or are they for advanced dancers?

Why this, and why nothing less

Task (obtaining information). You ask the price in your first sentence, so the booklet's scripted partial answer comes in the examiner's first reply. Your second turn takes their noun ("package"), states a real constraint (nights every other week) and turns it into a hypothetical cost ("I'd miss half the classes"). That is the B2 rung: you react and steer instead of asking for "more details". It also makes their next answer predictable: a school that prints "flexible schedules" answers that with its flexible option. Your third turn is the C1 glimpse. You reword that answer ("I could change slots from week to week"), then "In that case" marks the consequence: if your group changes every week, who do you dance with in couple dances? That question only exists for this ad. Your fourth turn takes up their answer with a reason ("because I don't know anyone who dances") and asks a two-sided question about the themed evenings. Three replies in three turns, each tied to what was just said.

Syntax. Each reply carries a different complex structure. Turn two has if + past + conditional (si je payais..., je manquerais...). Turn three has a subjunctive you can hear (vous auriez besoin que je vienne). Turn four has a negative pronoun with a relative clause (je ne connais personne qui danse), plus y and a concession (même si je débute à peine). The opener adds an indirect question. Links of three relations: consequence (dans ce cas, donc), addition (par ailleurs), concession (même si).

Lexis. These precise words come out of the English on their own (or with a note choosing between natural renderings) and are not on the ad: grand débutant, une semaine sur deux, trimestre, créneau, d'une semaine à l'autre, cavalier / cavalière, confirmés. That is seven, with at least one in every turn.

Delivery. No word you might freeze on. The longest sentence (turn three, about 17 French words) breaks at its commas.

Why nothing can go.

  • Without the price question, the "it depends" comes later, and so does everything built on it.
  • "For a complete beginner" stops your first question from being the grid's A2 example ("simple, very general questions").
  • "In that case, which package" is the audible uptake on the partial answer. Without it, turn two drops to the B1 rung.
  • "Nights every other week" is why you would miss half the classes, so the hypothetical follows. It is also why the examiner offers the flexible card that your reword repeats.
  • The reword plus "In that case" is the C1 glimpse. "Would you need me to come" carries the subjunctive. "My own" carries the inference (no steady partner).
  • "That's reassuring, because..." shows you heard the answer. Without it, turn four jumps topic and sounds recited.
  • "Besides" is the third link relation. The themed-evenings question is the third reply and carries the concession.

What the next cut would cost. The box sits on the time floor: 100 words is about 58 seconds of your speech. Below that, cutting does not bring the point earlier. The box just ends before the point. The cheapest piece is the last question ("Or are they for advanced dancers?"). It is partly there for time, but it also adds confirmés and makes the question two-sided. Drop it and you are at 94 words (about 55 seconds), so "surely not below 7" becomes "probably 7". Any further saving has to be swapped for evidence, not taken.

What the box assumes. After the point you keep asking questions tied to the answers for three more minutes: trial class, registration fee, make-up classes, what to wear, booking, location. They can be plain, but they must be correct and linked to what you hear. The box cannot lift a remainder at NCLC 5. It needs you to sound like a solid NCLC 6 on a quiet day. Learn turns two and three as frames, not fixed sentences: repeat the examiner's noun, and reword whatever flexibility they actually offered. When the reply differs from the bracketed one, use the fallbacks in Working.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (no lexis credit for any of it): learn to dance (apprendre à danser), improve (se perfectionner), enrol (s'inscrire, inscriptions), school (école), different types of dance (danses), flexible schedules (horaires flexibles), themed evenings (soirées à thème), warm atmosphere (ambiance chaleureuse), information (renseignements), website, phone number.

What it leaves unsaid: price and packages (per class, card, term, year), registration fee, commitment length, which dances, levels and how you get placed, whether you need a partner, group size, teachers, trial class, what "flexible" means in practice (choose once, or change every week; booking ahead; make-up classes), what the themed evenings are, whether they cost extra, who can go, dress and shoes, location, start dates, medical certificate.

Who is on the other end: the school's receptionist or manager, formal register, vous.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad:

  • Partial answer: the price ("it depends, we have several packages"). This is the booklet's own example, and this ad prints no price at all.
  • Surprises they may have ready:
    • beginner groups are full (waiting list);
    • despite "flexible schedules", packages are yearly with a fixed slot;
    • a registration fee, or classes booked 48 hours ahead;
    • couple dances register in pairs, or too few men or women, so people alone wait;
    • themed evenings cost extra or are for members;
    • a free trial class, or a promotion this month.
  • Relaunch statements if you stall: "We also have private lessons", "Our gala is in June".

Step 2. The hard part

The ad is all slogans, so you cannot predict what the examiner will say about anything except the price ("it depends"). The C1 glimpse needs a reword of the examiner's answer, and P13 says to reword only a gist you can predict. So the hardest conditions here are the C1 glimpse and credibility (conditions 2 and 5) together.

The answer is to make the gist predictable. Your second turn gives a concrete constraint and its cost ("nights every other week... I'd miss half the classes"). A school that advertises flexible schedules answers that with its flexible option, and you reword that. The only-here question comes from the one thing specific to dance: couple dances need a partner, and changing slots every week breaks that. "In that case" makes the consequence audible.

The second hard part is time. The evidence fits in about 80 words, but condition 4 (standard 5) puts the floor near 100 to 104 words. So the extra words have to be evidence too: a reason, a link, a complete question.

Precise words this topic offers that are not on the ad: créneau, cavalier / cavalière, confirmés, grand débutant, cours d'essai, frais d'inscription, rattraper un cours, chaussures de danse, tenue, trimestre / session, une semaine sur deux.

Step 3. Designs compared

  1. The steered reword (chosen; the unconventional mechanism). Price, then the partial answer. Then a reaction that states your constraint as a hypothetical cost, so the examiner's next answer has a gist you can predict. Then a reword plus a dance-specific consequence, then a third reply. 100 words, point about 1:15. Your reasoning shapes the examiner's second answer, so your reword of it is safe and still clearly a reply.
  2. The flexible-schedule opener. The first question asks what "flexible schedules" means for someone who works nights. Rejected:
    • the answer can't be predicted, so the next line becomes a slot;
    • the price chain moves to turn three, pushing the point to about 1:45;
    • "what it means" invites the indirect "what" error (standard 2).
  3. The wedding deadline (a self-description that makes every question only-here). Rejected:
    • about 20 words in the rehearsed opener;
    • it invites answers about private lessons and wedding packages the box can't foresee;
    • it removes the partner question, since you'd already have one;
    • a story told in turn one sounds prepared.
  4. Call the surprise. Ask the partner question first, where a dance school's surprise probably sits. Rejected. The reply can go either way, so the prepared reaction is a coin flip, and the price question still has to come later (one more turn).
  5. Three turns only (turns one to three, 72 words, about 42 seconds). Rejected. One link relation, under the time floor, and replication that leans on the rehearsed opener.

Winner: design 1. It reaches all five conditions at the time floor, and every examiner turn has either a reply you can predict or a fallback that keeps your next line valid.

Step 4. Structure notes (sentence by sentence)

  • S1 "Hello, I'm calling to find out how much your dance classes cost for a complete beginner." → Bonjour, je vous appelle pour savoir combien coûtent vos cours de danse pour un grand débutant (or une grande débutante). Vous, plus an indirect question with inversion. Frame and trigger in one sentence.
  • S2 "In that case, which package would you recommend?" → Dans ce cas, quelle formule me conseilleriez-vous ? Consequence link plus the examiner's noun (uptake); conditional.
  • S3 "I work nights every other week." → Je travaille de nuit une semaine sur deux.
  • S4 "So if I paid for a whole term, I'd miss half the classes." → Donc si je payais un trimestre entier, je manquerais la moitié des cours. If + past + conditional, the hard structure of this turn.
  • S5 "So if I understand correctly, I could change slots from week to week." → Donc, si je comprends bien, je pourrais changer de créneau d'une semaine à l'autre. Reword check; conditional; changer de with no article.
  • S6 "In that case, for couple dances, would you need me to come with my own partner?" → Dans ce cas, pour les danses de couple, vous auriez besoin que je vienne avec mon propre cavalier ? Consequence link. "Need me to" can only come out as avoir besoin que + subjunctive, and vienne is audible (not viens).
  • S7 "That's reassuring, because I don't know anyone who dances." → C'est rassurant, parce que je ne connais personne qui danse. Uptake with a reason; ne... personne plus a relative clause.
  • S8 "Besides, the themed evenings, could I go even if I'm only just starting?" → Par ailleurs, les soirées à thème, je pourrais y aller même si je débute à peine ? Addition link, topic placed first (ordinary spoken French), y, concession même si + indicative.
  • S9 "Or are they for advanced dancers?" → Ou c'est plutôt pour les danseurs confirmés ?

Structure types: indirect question; if + past + conditional; audible subjunctive; negative pronoun with a relative clause; y; concession clause; conditional reword. Link relations (standard 6): consequence (dans ce cas, donc), addition (par ailleurs), concession (même si). Parce que and ou are not counted. The si clauses count as syntax, not links.

Translator notes (each one chooses between natural renderings of the English):

  • complete beginner: grand débutant / grande débutante
  • package: the examiner's own noun (formule or forfait, with quelle or quel to match)
  • term: trimestre (or session with a Canadian examiner)
  • slot: créneau
  • partner: cavalier if you dance with a man, cavalière if with a woman (partenaire if unsure)
  • only just starting: je débute à peine
  • advanced dancers: danseurs confirmés

Precise words counted (standards 12 and 19): grand débutant, une semaine sur deux, trimestre, créneau, d'une semaine à l'autre, cavalier / cavalière, confirmés. Not counted:

  • formule (the examiner's noun);
  • rassurant (an everyday reaction word);
  • danseurs (a noun made from the printed danser);
  • danses de couple (leans on the printed danses);
  • conseiller, de nuit, manquer, débuter (everyday words).

Step 5. Deletion test (re-run on the revised box)

Piece What breaks without it
"Hello" Opening of the call, frame (condition 1)
"I'm calling to find out how much your dance classes cost" No ad identified and no guaranteed partial answer in turn 2 (conditions 1, 2)
"for a complete beginner" The opening question becomes the grid's A2 question, the first thing both raters hear; grand débutant lost
"In that case, which package" No audible uptake on the partial answer (standard 10); turn 2 drops to the B1 rung
"would you recommend" No question; turn 2 stops steering
"I work nights every other week" "Half the classes" has no reason; the examiner may not offer the flexible card, and the turn 3 reword breaks (condition 5); une semaine sur deux lost
"So if I paid for a whole term, I'd miss half the classes" No hypothetical (P17b); the hard structure in a reply is lost
"So if I understand correctly, I could change slots from week to week" No reword, so no C1 glimpse (condition 2)
"In that case" (turn 3) The consequence is no longer heard; the turn reads as reword then new topic (B2 rung, not C1)
"for couple dances" The only-here question becomes generic
"would you need me to come with my own partner" No audible subjunctive; no consequence drawn
"my own" The inference (changing groups means no steady partner) is not heard
"That's reassuring" Turn 4 ignores the answer and sounds recited (condition 5)
"because I don't know anyone who dances" The uptake becomes a token; ne... personne qui lost; box drops to 93 words, under the floor
"Besides" Only two link relations (standard 6)
"the themed evenings, could I go even if I'm only just starting?" Third reply lost (condition 3 has no spare); concession lost
"Or are they for advanced dancers?" Confirmés lost; the box drops to 94 words, under the floor
"Or do you pair people up?" (tried in the revision) Nothing broke: the box stays at 100 words (58 seconds) without it, so it was cut

Step 6. The three-replies test

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
After T1 "15 euros a class, or 120 for ten classes." "It depends, we have several packages." (box) "It depends on the dance." / "First you take a level test." Helpful: turn 2 as written ("which package..."). Dance: "Ah, it depends on the dance. In that case, for salsa, for example, which package would you recommend? I work nights every other week..." Level test: "I see. And after the test, which package would you recommend? I work nights every other week..."
After T2 "The ten-class card, 130 euros, valid three months." "We have something flexible, yes." "All our packages are yearly, with a fixed slot." Helpful and partial: turn 3 as written. Fixed slot: "Ah, so in spite of the flexible schedules, I'd keep the same slot all year. In that case, if I missed a class because of work, could I make it up another day?" (rattraper un cours)
After T3 "No, don't worry. Partners rotate." (box) "It's better to come with someone, but not required." "Yes, couple dances are by pairs." / "We're short of men, there's a waiting list." Helpful: turn 4. Partial: "Good to know, because I don't know anyone who dances. Besides..." Pairs: "Ah, that's a problem, because I don't know anyone who dances. In that case, could the themed evenings be a way to meet a partner, even if I'm only just starting?" Waiting list: "Ah, I didn't expect that. Roughly how long is the wait? And in the meantime, could I go to the themed evenings?"

Lines that depend on what the examiner chose (standard 13), drilled as frames:

  • Turn 2: "In that case, which [their noun] would you recommend?"
  • Turn 3: "So if I understand correctly, I could [the flexibility they described]. In that case, for couple dances..."

Every fallback keeps the same structures (dans ce cas, the reword, même si), so the evidence holds whatever the reply.

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their case for a 6:

  1. The opener is formulaic.
  2. The constraint in turn 2 could be prepared.
  3. "Si je comprends bien" is a prep-book phrase, and what follows sounds like a change of topic.
  4. The themed evenings are lifted from the ad.
  5. The sample is about a minute.
  6. Some of the words are B1 or come from the ad.

How the box answers:

  • (1) Granted. The opener carries no weight.
  • (2) It answers "it depends" with the examiner's own noun, and the hypothetical follows from packages. Even if prepared, it sits in a reply, and the next turn proves listening.
  • (3) Fixed after review. "In that case" now marks the partner question as a consequence of the reword, so the C1 move is audible.
  • (4) The ad words get no credit. The credit is the level question, confirmés and the concession.
  • (5) Conditions 1 to 3 are met in three replies, and the box now reaches the time floor on the standard conversion.
  • (6) Fixed. Horaires décalés (reused the printed horaires) is gone, and the lexis list holds only real precise words.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

Longest sentence: S6, about 17 French words, with breaks at the commas and the subjunctive near the end. S3 and S4 were split, so each is under 14 French words. No sentence has more than two marked structures or more than one hard one. Words to practise until they come out automatically: conseilleriez-vous, manquerais, vienne, créneau, cavalier / cavalière.

Step 9. Count and timing

100 English words. On the standard conversion (x 0.58), that is about 58 seconds of your speech, the floor in standard 5. The clock starts at the examiner's "Hello?". With three examiner replies of about 4 to 5 seconds each, the point falls at about 1:15.

Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

  • Is there a trial class, and is it free?
  • Is there a registration fee on top of the card?
  • How long is the card valid, and what happens to unused classes?
  • Do I need to book a class in advance, and up to when?
  • Which dances would you suggest for a complete beginner?
  • How many people per group?
  • What should I wear, and do I need dance shoes?
  • Do the themed evenings cost extra, and could I bring a friend who isn't enrolled?
  • Where are you, and is there parking or a metro nearby?
  • Can I enrol on site, or only on the website?
  • When does the next beginners' group start?
  • Close: thank them, say you'll enrol online this week.

After review

  • Review fix 1 (par contre hid the consequence): applied. Turn 3 now says "In that case", and turn 4 opens with "Besides" so there are still three link relations.
  • Review cuts (none; opener could merge): applied standard 11. The opener is one sentence, and the 3 words saved went to evidence, since the box is at the floor.
  • Suggestion 1 ("I work shifts"): applied as "I work nights every other week" (une semaine sur deux). It uses no ad word, and it makes "half the classes" follow. I chose it over "my schedule changes every week", which would duplicate the turn 3 reword.
  • Suggestion 2 (the subjunctive did not come from the English): applied. The English is now "would you need me to come", which can only come out as que je vienne.
  • Suggestion 3 (horaires décalés reused an ad word): applied. The phrase is gone and the lexis list is cleaned.
  • Suggestion 4 (format): applied; the box opens with [examiner: "Hello?"].
  • Across the chunk 1 and standard 5 (time floor): applied. 93 words were 54 seconds. I added evidence (a reason for the uptake, a precise constraint, a two-sided question) and reached 106. The deletion test then showed "Or do you pair people up?" was not needed above the floor, so it was cut. 100 words is about 58 seconds.
  • Across the chunk 2 and standard 6 (links): applied. Consequence, addition and concession; parce que and the si clauses are not counted.
  • Across the chunk 3, 7 and standard 10 (uptake, say the noun): already met ("In that case, which package"). Kept.
  • Across the chunk 5 and standard 13 (lines that depend on the examiner's choice): applied. Frames are in Step 6.
  • Across the chunk 6 and standards 12, 19 (precise words): applied. The list now has seven real ones (after the final audit), and the excluded words are named.
  • Standard 1 (audible subjunctive): applied, as in suggestion 2.
  • Standard 2 (no indirect "what"): checked; the box has none.
  • Standard 7 (timing basis): applied; Point and Step 9 count from "Hello?".
  • Standards 8 and 15 (the English makes its own French): applied. "For advanced dancers" no longer claims réservées à, and "That's reassuring" is noted as c'est rassurant. Translator notes only choose between natural renderings.
  • Standard 9 (facts not on the ad): checked. Nothing about the school is asserted; the only invented facts are about you.
  • Standards 3, 4, 16, 17, 18: Section B only, not applicable.
  • Final audit (rassurant and danseurs counted as precise): applied. Both were dropped from the lexis lists, and Why now lists confirmés alone. Seven real precise words remain, above section C's five, so the box is unchanged.
