A-03

THE MEDIEVAL CASTLE OF VALLON

Trips, tours & stays · Tours & guided visits · est. #17 of 78
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement for a tour and you are interested. You are calling to get more information.

THE MEDIEVAL CASTLE OF VALLON

An exceptional panorama between mountains and rivers:

Come visit the medieval castle of Vallon!

Guided tours

Workshops for adults and children (by reservation)

On-site dining

Souvenir shop

More information at 07 98 76 54 32

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité pour une visite et vous êtes intéressé(e). Vous téléphonez pour avoir plus d’informations.

LE CHATEAU MEDIEVAL DE VALLON

Un panorama exceptionnel entre montagnes et rivières :

Venez visiter le château médiéval de Vallon !

Visites guidées

Ateliers adultes et enfants (sur réservation)

Restauration sur place

Boutique souvenirs

Plus de renseignements au 07 98 76 54 32

The point

The point falls at the end of your fourth turn (the weather question), about 1:20 after the examiner's "Hello?". By then you have spoken about 61 seconds (105 English words on the shared conversion) and the examiner has had four short turns.

The box105 words

[examiner: "Hello?"]

Hello. I'd like to visit the castle of Vallon on Saturday with my son, who is eight and loves knights. Regarding your workshops for adults and children, are they done together or separately?

[examiner: "It depends on the workshop. Some are for families, others for children only."]

So he could do some without me. Which workshop would you recommend, so that he's busy during my guided tour?

[examiner: "The knights' workshop, from age six. It lasts an hour."]

The knights' workshop? That's exactly the kind of thing he dreams of. Is there still a slot on Saturday afternoon, or is it already full?

[examiner: "Almost full. There are only two places left."]

Then I'll book right away, since there are so few left. However, if the weather were bad that day, could I postpone my booking without a fee?

Why this, and why nothing less

This box gives a rater everything a 6 lacks in the first 80 seconds of the call, and after the opening every piece of evidence is a reply to something the examiner just said.

Task (obtaining information). Your first question is one only this ad allows. The ad says "workshops for adults and children", and you ask whether that means together or apart. It is a precise question about the document, and it invites the partial answer the examiner is told to give. Your son's age and "loves knights" do two jobs: they are the B2 move "states a requirement", and they close the two easy partial answers to your next question ("it depends on his age", "it depends what he likes"), so the examiner most likely names a workshop, which turn 3 is written for. Turn 2 rewords the "it depends" into what it means for you ("he could do some without me") and turns it into a plan that joins two things on the ad (his workshop while you take the tour). That is the C1 glimpse: a well-judged intervention, not a request for more detail. Turn 3 repeats the examiner's noun, reacts, and with "or is it already full?" invites the obvious surprise. Turn 4 takes the examiner's own words ("so few left") and adds a what-if. That makes three replies, where two are the minimum.

Syntax. One of each, all audible: a relative clause (qui a huit ans), a fronted topic picked up by a pronoun (En ce qui concerne vos ateliers..., est-ce qu'ils...), en with certains, pour que + subjunctive (soit), dont (le genre de chose dont il rêve), and si + imperfect + conditional. The three hard ones sit in three different replies, one per sentence. Links of three relations: consequence (donc, alors), cause (puisque), contrast (par contre).

Lexis. Precise words the English produces on its own: chevaliers, créneau, complet, reporter, sans frais, with séparément and s'il faisait mauvais as everyday support. None is printed on the ad, and none is a cliché.

Delivery. No sentence has more than three breath groups. Drill two things: dont il rêve (never que il rêve) and conseilleriez.

Register. vous, conditionals and en ce qui concerne, at no extra cost.

Why nothing can go. The box is 105 English words, about 61 seconds of your speech on the shared conversion, just over the 100-word floor for "control presumed". Under that floor a cut does not bring the point earlier; the box just ends before the point. Each piece also has its own job. The first sentence names the ad and the day. The age and "loves knights" keep turns 2 and 3 valid. The together-or-separately question is the only-here probe. "He could do some without me" is the reword that makes turn 2 a C1 glimpse, and the purpose clause carries the subjunctive and the plan. The echo and "the kind of thing he dreams of" make turn 3 a reaction and carry dont. "Or is it already full?" makes the examiner's next move predictable. "Since there are so few left" is the audible uptake and the cause link. The weather question carries the hypothetical, the contrast link, reporter and sans frais.

The next cut. The only piece above the floor is "without a fee" (3 words). Cutting it leaves 102 words but only four real precise words. Any bigger cut takes you under a minute of speech and removes a structure, a link or a reply.

What it assumes. That the examiner splits the workshops (some family, some children only) and names one; Working gives the fallbacks and the frames ("repeat their noun"). And that after the point you keep asking plain, correct questions tied to the answers for the remaining 3 minutes 40 (price, lunch, access, the view), at the same even pace as the box.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it counts as vocabulary evidence): château médiéval, Vallon, panorama exceptionnel, montagnes, rivières, venez visiter, visites guidées, ateliers adultes et enfants, sur réservation, restauration sur place, boutique souvenirs, renseignements. Sur place and réservation, two stock transaction words for most Section A calls, are printed here, so they are dead for this prompt.

What it leaves unsaid: every price (entry, guided tour, workshops, any family or combined ticket); opening days, hours and season; when the guided tours run, how long, in which languages, group size; what the workshops are (themes, ages, length, whether parents take part); how far ahead to book and whether places run out; what "on-site dining" is (restaurant, snack bar, picnic area), whether it needs booking, children's menu; access (car, parking, shuttle, public transport, the walk up); accessibility (stairs, towers, strollers, reduced mobility); weather (the panorama and the ramparts are outdoors); cancellation, postponing, refunds, fees.

One real ambiguity in the wording: "Ateliers adultes et enfants" can mean workshops that adults and children do together, or two separate kinds of workshop. This is the only-here hook the box uses.

Who is on the other end: the castle's reception or ticket desk, which takes bookings for the tour and the workshops.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad:

  • Partial answers (playbook: partial first), most likely on the first one or two questions, whatever they are: "it depends on the workshop", "it depends on the period", "it depends on the age". On price: "it depends, there are several formulas" (entry only, entry plus tour, plus workshop).
  • Surprises this ad makes easy: an age floor, or a parent must stay; the Saturday session is almost full or full; workshops must be booked 48 hours ahead or online only; guided tours only at fixed times or only in French; the keep or a tower closed for restoration; the restaurant must be booked or is closed some days; the path up is steep, no strollers; parking is far, shuttle; no refunds; a medieval festival that weekend at a different price.
  • Relaunches (statements, not questions): "we also have a family pass", "the restaurant serves local dishes", "there's a medieval festival next month".

Step 2. The hard part

The hardest condition here is the lexis and only-here half of condition 2. The ad is a list of four generic tourist services. Every candidate asks price, hours and how to get there, and the ad has already used up réservation and sur place. A box built from the obvious questions would be all prep-list (P3) and would only show B1 "satisfactory questions". The only-here material comes from (a) the ambiguity in "adults and children" and (b) joining two services the ad lists separately (a workshop for the child while the parent takes the guided tour). Precise words not on the ad: chevaliers, créneau, complet, reporter, sans frais; for the reserve remparts, donjon, la vue, marches, poussette, tir à l'arc, blason, se déguiser, menu enfant, pique-nique, stationnement, navette, acompte. One trap: English "tour" must never become French tour (a tower, or a turn); it is always la visite guidée.

The second difficulty is condition 4 (control presumed). On the shared conversion (seconds = English words x 0.58), the evidence alone fits in about 75 English words, which is about 44 seconds of speech. The floor of about 100 words (58 seconds) therefore sets the length here, not the evidence. A box at the floor swaps dead words for evidence instead of deleting them.

Step 3. Designs compared

Design Idea Words Point Holds against the playbook? Verdict
1. Price first "How much for a family?" then the scripted "it depends, several formulas", then "is the tour included?", reword, what-if about 100 turn 4 partial answer near certain; but rewording a price is unpredictable (P13), every prepared candidate runs this chain, and there is no only-here question until turn 3 or 4 rejected
2. Access and the view opener mentions a parent who has trouble with stairs; "can he get up to see the panorama?"; adapt to "stairs only" about 100 turn 4 the reply splits three ways (lift, stairs only, partly), so the script breaks often; needs donjon, à mobilité réduite, words that can cause a pause rejected, kept for the reserve
3. Two probes at once one turn: "could he do a workshop alone while I take the tour, and how much would that come to?" about 95 turn 3 or 4 whichever half the examiner answers, you follow up the other, so turn 2 always works; but asking for the missing half is the B1 rung ("asks for answers to be made precise"), and two-question turns sound listed rejected, the workshop-plus-tour plan kept
4. Ad-ambiguity probe plus a self-description that pre-empts the partial answers (chosen) turn 1 gives the child's age and interest and asks whether "adults and children" means together or apart; turn 2 rewords the "it depends" into a plan; turn 3 repeats their noun and invites the scarcity surprise; turn 4 takes it up and adds a what-if 105 turn 4, about 1:20 the first partial answer comes from a question only this ad allows; the age and "loves knights" close the two escape routes for the second answer; "or is it already full?" makes the third reply predictable chosen

Why design 4 wins: it is the only one where the first question is already only-here and also triggers the partial answer, so the frame and the probe share one turn. The self-description is the unconventional part. It costs 8 words and decides the examiner's next two answers in your favour: the examiner cannot answer "it depends on his age" or "it depends what he likes" to turn 2, because you have already said both. The obvious recommendation is then a knights' workshop, which turn 3 repeats. Turn 3 then asks for the surprise you are ready for, and turn 4 takes it up.

Step 4. Structure note per sentence (the French the English produces)

# English French Structures Risk
1a Hello. Bonjour. frame none
1b I'd like to visit the castle of Vallon on Saturday with my son, who is eight and loves knights. J'aimerais visiter le château de Vallon samedi avec mon fils, qui a huit ans et qui adore les chevaliers. polite conditional; relative qui low (a huit ans, never est huit ans)
1c Regarding your workshops for adults and children, are they done together or separately? En ce qui concerne vos ateliers pour adultes et enfants, est-ce qu'ils se font ensemble ou séparément ? fronted topic picked up by ils (the topic marker itself is register, not a link) low
2a So he could do some without me. Donc il pourrait en faire certains sans moi. donc (consequence, joins to the examiner's turn); en + certains; conditional of inference low
2b Which workshop would you recommend, so that he's busy during my guided tour? Quel atelier vous me conseilleriez, pour qu'il soit occupé pendant ma visite guidée ? the examiner's noun (atelier); polite conditional; pour que + audible subjunctive (soit; the purpose subject is your son, not you, so pour pouvoir / pour être cannot replace it) medium (drill conseilleriez)
3a The knights' workshop? That's exactly the kind of thing he dreams of. L'atelier des chevaliers ? C'est exactement le genre de chose dont il rêve. echo of their noun; dont (rêver de forces it) medium (dont, not que)
3b Is there still a slot on Saturday afternoon, or is it already full? Est-ce qu'il reste encore un créneau samedi après-midi, ou c'est déjà complet ? il reste; alternative question low
4a Then I'll book right away, since there are so few left. Alors je réserve tout de suite, puisqu'il en reste si peu. alors (consequence); puisque (cause); en with a quantity low
4b However, if the weather were bad that day, could I postpone my booking without a fee? Par contre, s'il faisait mauvais ce jour-là, est-ce que je pourrais reporter ma réservation sans frais ? par contre (contrast); si + imperfect + conditional medium

Load check: no sentence has more than two marked structures or more than one hard one (2b subjunctive, 3a dont, 4b si clause, each in a different reply). Longest English sentence 19 words (1b), with natural pauses at the commas.

Links (standard 6): consequence (donc 2a, alors 4a), cause (puisque 4a), contrast (par contre 4b). Three relations. Not counted: en ce qui concerne (topic marker), pendant (a preposition before a noun), the si clause (syntax).

Precise words (standards 12 and 19): chevaliers, créneau, complet, reporter, sans frais. Everyday support, not counted: séparément, s'il faisait mauvais. Not counted: conseiller, tout de suite, occupé (everyday), atelier, visite guidée, réservation (on the ad).

Translator notes (standard 8; each only chooses between natural renderings):

  • "Which workshop would you recommend" : Quel atelier vous me conseilleriez or me conseilleriez-vous; keep the conditional and atelier.
  • "the kind of thing he dreams of" : le genre de chose dont il rêve.
  • "so that he's busy during my guided tour" : pour qu'il soit occupé pendant ma visite guidée.
  • "slot" : créneau (not place).
  • "since there are so few left" : puisqu'il en reste si peu (rather than puisqu'il reste peu de places).
  • "postpone" : reporter. "without a fee" : sans frais.
  • "guided tour" : visite guidée, never tour.

Substitution pass (same length, denser):

  • "I'm calling about the castle of Vallon. I'd like to come on Saturday" became "I'd like to visit the castle of Vallon on Saturday": one purpose sentence, 6 words fewer.
  • "with my seven-year-old son" became "my son, who is eight": a relative clause, and eight clears more age floors than seven.
  • "In your workshops, do parents and children work together?" became "Regarding your workshops..., are they done together or separately?": fronted topic and séparément, fewer words.
  • "Which ones could he do alone?" became "So he could do some without me" + "Which workshop would you recommend, so that...": the reword with en ... certains, the examiner's noun and the subjunctive.
  • "Since it's by reservation, are there still places?" became "Is there still a slot..., or is it already full?": ad words out, créneau and complet in, the scarcity surprise invited.
  • After review: "That's exactly what he'd love" (an indirect "what", and its French was ce qu'il adorerait, not the ce qui lui plairait noted) became "That's exactly the kind of thing he dreams of" (dont). "Then I'll book right away" gained "since there are so few left" (uptake plus cause link). "Move my booking to Sunday" became "postpone my booking" (reporter, works after any day), and "without a fee" adds sans frais.

Step 5. Deletion test (re-run after review)

Conditions: (1) frame, (2) capacities, (3) replication, (4) control presumed (floor about 100 English words, 58 seconds), (5) credibility. The box is 105 words, so only "without a fee" sits above the floor; every other cut also fails condition 4.

Piece removed What breaks
"Hello." 1: no greeting on a formal call
"I'd like to visit the castle of Vallon on Saturday" 1: ad and purpose not identified (the examiner only says "Allô ?"); "Saturday" returns in turn 3
"with my son, who is eight" 5: turn 2 likely draws "it depends on his age", which turn 2's line would ignore; 2: the stated requirement goes
"and loves knights" 5: turn 3's echo and "dreams of" lose their anchor; turn 2 can draw "it depends what he likes"; 2: chevaliers goes
"Regarding your workshops..., together or separately?" 2: no only-here question, no partial-answer trigger
"So he could do some without me." 2: turn 2 drops from reword-and-consequence (C1 glimpse) to a plain follow-up; the donc link and en go
"Which workshop would you recommend" 2: no steer, the reword hangs
"so that he's busy during my guided tour" 2: the subjunctive and the plan that joins two services go; the question becomes B1 "can you recommend one?"
"The knights' workshop?" 5: without the echo, the next sentence can be heard as recited
"That's exactly the kind of thing he dreams of." 2 and 3: turn 3 becomes a generic availability question; dont goes
"Is there still a slot on Saturday afternoon" 3: nothing for turn 4 to react to; créneau goes
"or is it already full?" 4: under the floor; the examiner's reply becomes less predictable; complet goes
"Then I'll book right away" 5: turn 4 would ignore "only two places left"; 3: turn 4 is then not a reply
"since there are so few left" 2: links fall to two relations (consequence, contrast); the audible uptake of the examiner's words goes
"However, if the weather were bad that day, could I postpone my booking" 2: the hypothetical, the contrast link and reporter go; 4: 92 words, about 53 seconds
"without a fee" (above the floor) 2: real precise words fall to four (chevaliers, créneau, complet, reporter), under "about five". Kept

Step 6. Examiner-reply test (three replies per examiner turn)

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
Opening "Castle of Vallon, hello, how can I help?" "Allô ?" "Reception, one moment... yes?" turn 1 works after all three
After turn 1 "Some are for families, others for children only." "It depends on the workshop." "They're all done together, parents with children." box line after the first two. Fallback for the third: "Ah, so we'd do it together. In that case, does the guided tour come before or after, so that he can rest between the two?" (keeps an audible subjunctive, pour qu'il puisse, with a subject that is not you)
After turn 2 "The knights' workshop, from six, one hour." "There are several: calligraphy, archery, coats of arms." "Under-tens must be with an adult." box line after the first. Frame for the second: repeat their noun for the one closest to knights ("Archery? That's exactly the kind of thing he dreams of."). Fallback for the third: "Ah, so I'd have to stay with him. In that case, we'll do it together and I'll take the tour afterwards. Is there still a slot on Saturday afternoon?"
After turn 3 "Yes, there's room." "Almost: only two places left." / "You have to book 48 hours ahead." "Saturday is full." box line after "only two left". Frame after "there's room": keep the cause link with your own reason ("Then I'll book right away, since Saturday suits us best", puisque samedi nous arrange). After "48 hours ahead": "Then I'll book today, since..." Fallback for "full": "That's a pity. In that case, is there a slot on Sunday?" then the weather sentence unchanged ("that day" fits any day)

Every fallback keeps the same frame (their noun or a reword, a link, one structure), so a switch does not cost the evidence. The two lines that depend on what the examiner chooses (the workshop's name in turn 3, "so few left" in turn 4) are drilled as frames, "repeat their noun" and "since + what they just said", not as fixed sentences (standard 13).

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6: "Turn 1 is a prepared speech with a staged family. 'He could do some without me' just repeats the examiner. The workshop-while-touring plan existed before the examiner spoke, so the subjunctive is a set piece. The reaction in turn 3 is stock enthusiasm. 'If the weather is bad, can I postpone?' is in every prep book. The examiner never really surprised the candidate. Appropriate questions, reacts: B2, lower box."

How the box answers it:

  • "Repeats the examiner": turn 2 draws a consequence the examiner did not say (children-only means you are free), then joins it to a second service on the ad. That is the C1 "well-judged intervention", and it only fits after "some are for children only".
  • "Set piece": what a rater hears as recited is a line that ignores the answer (D7). Every box line fits its stage direction, and the fallbacks keep the fit when the examiner goes elsewhere.
  • "Stock enthusiasm": the echo repeats the examiner's own noun, and "the kind of thing he dreams of" ties back to "loves knights" two turns earlier, with dont. That cross-turn tie is the "exchange holds together" evidence a 6 rarely produces.
  • "Never surprised": the box asks for the surprise ("or is it already full?") and takes it up in the next breath with the examiner's own words ("since there are so few left"). Turn 2 also answers the scripted partial answer.
  • "Prep-book contingency": granted for the content, but the si + imperfect + conditional comes in a reply, after a contrast link, with a negotiating detail (sans frais), and it is the third hard structure in a third turn. That is variety, not one lucky sentence.

After the review changes the deletion test was run again (Step 5); nothing new is dead.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

Every French sentence splits at commas into at most three breath groups: J'aimerais visiter le château de Vallon samedi avec mon fils, / qui a huit ans / et qui adore les chevaliers. and Par contre, / s'il faisait mauvais ce jour-là, / est-ce que je pourrais reporter ma réservation sans frais ? Tongue risks: conseilleriez (five syllables; if it comes out as conseillez, keep going), dont il rêve (say it as one group), huit ans (liaison), puisqu'il en reste si peu (one group). No word in the box needs a search.

Step 9. Count and timing (shared conversion, standards 5 and 7)

  • English: 33 + 20 + 25 + 27 = 105 words.
  • Your speech: 105 x 0.58 = about 61 seconds (French about 110 words at 120 a minute, plus 10% for pauses).
  • Examiner: "Hello?" plus three replies, with turn gaps, about 18 to 20 seconds.
  • Clock from the examiner's "Hello?": the point falls at the end of turn 4, about 1:20 into Section A. Condition 4 is met (about 60 seconds), with 5 words over the 100-word floor.

Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

  1. Price, expecting the scripted partial: "How much would that come to, the workshop and the guided tour?" then "In that case, if I took both, would there be a family rate?"
  2. Lunch: "Is the restaurant open on Saturday lunchtime, and should I book a table?"; "Is there a children's menu, or could we bring a picnic?"
  3. The view: "From where do you see the panorama best? Can you go up onto the ramparts with a child?"
  4. Access: "How do you get there without a car?"; "Is there parking near the entrance, or is it a long walk up?"
  5. The tour itself: "How long does the guided tour last, and is it only in French?"
  6. The workshop: "Does he need to bring anything, or is everything provided?"; "Can he take home the things he makes?"
  7. Booking details: "Can I pay on the day, or do you need a deposit?"; "How much notice do you need if we cancel?"
  8. Relaunches: if the examiner mentions a festival or a pass, react ("A festival? In that case, would the prices be different that weekend?").

After review

  • Review suggestion 1 (turn 4 should stand after any day): applied. "On Saturday... to Sunday" became "that day... postpone my booking" (reporter); the fallback after "full" is now one line.
  • Review suggestion 2 (drill the echo as a frame): applied in Step 6 and the Step 6 note ("repeat their noun").
  • Review suggestion 3 (topic marker is not a link): applied. Why and Working now count three relations and leave en ce qui concerne out.
  • Standard 1 (audible subjunctive): checked at review; corrected after the final audit (last line below).
  • Standard 2 (no indirect "what"): applied. "That's exactly what he'd love" became "That's exactly the kind of thing he dreams of" (dont).
  • Standard 5 (time floor): applied. 95 words (55 s) was under the floor; the review version had 106 words (105 after the final audit), about 61 s. The added words carry evidence: the uptake and cause link (6), dont (4 net), sans frais (3).
  • Standard 6 (links): applied. pendant ce temps (time) and the topic marker no longer count; "since there are so few left" adds cause, so the box has consequence, cause and contrast.
  • Standard 7 (one timing basis): applied. Clock from "Hello?"; the point is at about 1:20.
  • Standard 8 (translator notes): applied. List added in Step 4.
  • Standard 9 (facts not on the ad): checked. Nothing about the castle is stated as fact; "so few left" repeats the examiner.
  • Standard 10 (uptake, say the noun): applied. "Which one" became "Which workshop"; turn 2 already opens on a reword with donc.
  • Standard 11 (one-sentence opener): checked. The purpose sentence already names the ad.
  • Standard 12 and 19 (precise words): applied. conseiller, tout de suite, pendant ce temps dropped from the lists; real ones are chevaliers, créneau, complet, reporter, sans frais.
  • Standard 13 (lines that depend on the examiner's choice): applied. Frames for the turn 3 echo and the turn 4 "since" clause in Step 6.
  • Standard 14 (format): checked. The box opens with [examiner: "Hello?"].
  • Standard 15 (the English makes its own French): applied. Turn 3's French now matches its English (dont il rêve); the old note ce qui lui plairait did not follow from "what he'd love".
  • Standards 3, 4, 16, 17, 18: Section B only, not applicable.
  • Final audit (standard 1, section E): applied. "so that I can take the guided tour meanwhile" could come out as pour pouvoir, with no subjunctive. It is now "so that he's busy during my guided tour" (pour qu'il soit occupé, audible soit, purpose subject your son). The turn-1 fallback now uses pour qu'il puisse. Box 105 words, about 61 seconds; Step 5 timing for the weather cut corrected to about 53 seconds.

A-06

Walking Club

Clubs, volunteering & paid missions · Clubs & associations · est. #30 of 78
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement and you are interested. You are calling to get more information.

Walking Club

Walking Club

Come walk with us!

Varied routes depending on the seasons

Gentle hikes in the city and in the countryside

Discovery of the local heritage

Information:

www.clubdemarche.org

or at 0801 12 12 451

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette annonce et vous êtes intéressé(e). Vous téléphonez pour avoir plus d’informations.

CLUB DE MARCHE

Club de Marche

Venez marcher avec nous !

Circuits variés selon les saisons

Randonnées douces en ville en campagne

Découverte du patrimoine local

Renseignements :

www.clubdemarche.org

ou au 0801 12 12 451

The point

The point falls at the end of your fifth turn, the heritage question, about 1:15 to 1:25 after the examiner's opening "Hello?". By then you have spoken for about 58 seconds (100 English words on the standard conversion), two of your turns are replies built on the examiner's answers, and one of them rewords an answer and draws a consequence from it.

The box100 words

[examiner: "Hello?"]

Hello, how much is the membership for the Walking Club?

[examiner: "It depends, we have several formulas."]

I'd like to try one outing before committing for the year, to see whether I can keep up the pace. Which formula would you recommend?

[examiner: "Come to a first outing for free, then decide."]

All right. In the city, I can get around by bus. For the countryside, on the other hand, how do you get there without a car?

[examiner: "We carpool from a meeting point in town."]

So if I understand correctly, I'd depend on the drivers. In that case, if there were no seats left, would I miss the outing?

[examiner: "No, don't worry, we always find a solution."]

Good to know. Besides, for the heritage you talk about, does someone explain its history?

Why this, and why nothing less

This ad is thin and harmless: no price, no dates, no risk. It invites the generic call (when, where, how much), which the grid calls A2 ("simple, very general questions"). The box gets past that by making every question come either from the examiner's last answer or from something only this ad says.

Task (obtaining information). Price is the question the examiner's booklet scripts a partial answer for, so "it depends" comes in their first reply and your second turn is already a reaction. It sits on the B2 rung: you hand them your criterion (one trial before paying for a year, to check the pace) and ask them to choose, using their own noun ("which formula"), so the recording shows you took something from their answer. Turn 4 is the C1 glimpse: you reword their answer (carpooling means you depend on the drivers) and draw a consequence they did not say (no seat, no outing). Two questions only make sense for this ad: the city-versus-countryside question, built on the ad's own split and your lack of a car, and the heritage question. Three turns carry clear evidence, two of them replies.

Syntax. Five different marked structures (grammar forms that only stronger speakers use reliably), one of each: avant de + infinitive, an indirect question with si, the pronoun y, si + imperfect + conditional, and dont. No sentence holds more than one hard one. Links of three different relations: contrast (par contre), consequence (donc, dans ce cas) and addition (par ailleurs).

Lexis. Five precise words you choose, none printed on the ad: adhésion, sortie, s'engager, suivre le rythme, conducteurs. A 6 tends to reach these by a detour ("the people with cars", "to see if I'm fast enough").

Delivery and register. Short sentences of one or two breath groups, no rare word. Vous and polite conditionals from turn 2 on (recommanderiez, dépendrais).

Why nothing can go. The box is at the time floor: 100 English words is about 58 seconds of your speech, the least that satisfies "about 60 to 90 seconds". Below that, cutting doesn't bring the point earlier; the box just ends before it. So every piece either carries evidence or holds the floor, and most do both. Without the price question there is no partial-answer chain. Without "before committing for the year", turn 2 no longer answers their "several formulas". Without "to see whether I can keep up the pace" you lose the only indirect question inside a reply and rythme. Without "Which formula", turn 2 has no audible uptake. "All right" and "Good to know" stop turns 3 and 5 sounding recited after answers you could not know in advance. The city sentence is what par contre contrasts with. In turn 4 the reword is the C1 glimpse and the if-clause is the consequence. Turn 5 brings dont, the addition link and the second only-here question.

The next cut. Turns 1 to 4 alone are 85 words, about 49 seconds: every capacity is shown, but the box ends about 10 seconds before the point, and it loses dont and the third link relation. Any cut below 100 words has the same problem.

What the box assumes. That the price answer is partial (the booklet's own example), and that the transport answer falls in one of four kinds (carpool, it changes per outing, you need a car, take the bus or train). Working has the turn-4 line for each, as a frame: repeat their noun, then "so I'd depend on...". After the point you need three more minutes of plain, correct questions: distance and climbing, which days, equipment, winter and summer, insurance, booking. Plain is fine. Running dry is not.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (no lexis credit for any of it): club de marche, venez marcher avec nous, circuits variés selon les saisons, randonnées douces, en ville, en campagne, découverte du patrimoine local, renseignements, a website and a phone number. In English: walk, club, routes, varied, seasons, gentle hikes, city, countryside, discovery, local heritage, information.

What it leaves unsaid: price and how it is charged (annual membership, per outing, couple or family rate); whether you can try before joining; days, times and frequency (weekday mornings would shut out anyone who works); distance, duration, climbing and pace ("gentle" is undefined); where you meet and how you reach the countryside; who leads (volunteer, qualified guide) and who explains the heritage; whether museum or monument entries cost extra; what changes with the seasons (mud, early dark, heat, snow); equipment; insurance and medical certificate; booking and cancellation; group size; dogs, children.

Who is on the other end: a volunteer or the club secretary of a local association. Friendly, informal knowledge, likely to answer in "we" (on fait, on se retrouve).

The examiner's predictable moves:

  • Partial answer: almost certainly to the price question ("ça dépend, il y a plusieurs formules"), possibly also to "where do you meet" ("ça dépend de la sortie") and "how often" ("ça dépend de la saison", since the ad prints seasons).
  • Likely unexpected elements (imprévus) for this ad: outings on weekday mornings only; membership required after the first outing for insurance; a medical certificate; summer outings longer than "gentle" suggests (15 to 20 km); a fuel contribution for carpooling; booking online by a deadline; group full or a waiting list; paid entry for heritage visits; no dogs.
  • Relaunch statements if you run dry: "nous avons aussi des sorties en soirée l'été", "il faut prévoir de bonnes chaussures".
  • Objections (Section B) don't apply here. Their Section A cousins are the unexpected conditions above.

Step 2. The hard part

The hard condition here is the task criterion's top half: an only-here question and a C1 glimpse. The ad is short, cheap and safe, so the questions it invites are the generic list every prepared candidate brings. Two features make a question only-here: the city-and-countryside split (which raises transport, because the countryside is where buses stop) and the heritage line (which raises who explains it). The seasons line is the third, kept in reserve.

The C1 glimpse (reword plus consequence) needs a gist you can predict. Transport to the countryside has one: a French walking club almost always answers "covoiturage". Your own fact (no car) turns that answer into a consequence the examiner did not state: you would depend on the drivers, so a full car means a missed outing.

Precise words this topic offers that are not on the ad: sortie, adhésion, cotisation, adhérent, bénévole, accompagnateur, rythme, dénivelé, sentier, boucle, point de rendez-vous, point de départ, covoiturage, conducteur, chaussures de randonnée, bâtons de marche, imperméable, gourde, boueux, se déplacer, s'engager, l'histoire des lieux.

Step 3. Designs compared

Timings below use the standard conversion: seconds of your speech = English words x 0.58.

  1. Price chain, countryside inference, heritage close (chosen). Five turns, 100 words, about 58 seconds. Price in turn 1 pulls the scripted partial answer into the examiner's first reply. Turn 2 steers it, using their noun. Turn 3 is the only-here transport question. Turn 4 rewords the carpool answer and draws the risk. Turn 5 adds dont, the addition link and the heritage question. Reaches all five conditions around 1:15 to 1:25.
  2. Newcomer profile up front (unconventional). Turn 1: "I've just moved here, I don't have a car and I'd like to discover the region. How much is the membership?" Every later question then refers back to the profile, so each is only-here. Rejected: it adds about 15 words to the opener, the one turn raters discount as preparable, and a three-fact self-introduction sounds like a script. The same anchoring comes cheaper when each fact appears in the turn that uses it (no car in turn 3).
  3. Seasons first, C1 glimpse early (unconventional order). Turn 1 asks whether outings continue in winter; the examiner says "it depends on the weather"; turn 2 rewords ("so an outing can be cancelled at the last minute") and asks how members are told. Price comes third, as a reaction. Rejected: the weather gist is less predictable than it looks (many clubs say "on sort par tous les temps"), so the reword sits on the shakiest answer, and the scripted partial answer arrives later.
  4. Heritage first, price as a reaction (unconventional). Turn 1 asks who explains the heritage; turn 2 asks whether visits are included in the membership, which pulls "it depends" as a contingent question; turn 3 asks how much a month if you came every week. Rejected: the C1 reword would have to fall on a price answer, which can't be scripted (P13), so it needs a sixth turn; and a "no guided visits" answer breaks turn 2.
  5. Two probes at once. Turn 1 asks price and trial together. Rejected: on the phone the examiner answers one of them, and a turn with two questions sounds like reading a list.
  6. Design 1 without turn 5 (85 words). Shows every capacity at the end of turn 4, but on about 49 seconds of your speech, under the time floor, and without dont or a third link relation. Rejected on condition 4.

Design 1 wins on words to the point because every turn after the opener carries at least two kinds of evidence, and it holds up best because each reactive line is written either at right angles to the answer (turns 3 and 5) or on a gist with four listed variants (turn 4).

Step 4. Structure notes, sentence by sentence

Turn English French the English is written to carry Structure and job
1 Hello, how much is the membership for the Walking Club? Bonjour, combien coûte l'adhésion au Club de marche ? One-sentence opener: the ad named and the price probe. Frame only; c'est combien, l'adhésion is also fine, nothing here is counted.
2a I'd like to try one outing before committing for the year, to see whether I can keep up the pace. J'aimerais essayer une sortie avant de m'engager pour l'année, pour voir si je peux suivre le rythme. avant de + infinitive; indirect question with si. Two marked, none hard. Pause at the comma.
2b Which formula would you recommend? Quelle formule vous me recommanderiez ? Uptake: their noun. Polite conditional. Say the noun they used (formule, tarif, option); never laquelle / lequel.
3a All right. D'accord. Acknowledges any answer.
3b In the city, I can get around by bus. En ville, je peux me déplacer en bus. Sets up the contrast (se déplacer is everyday, not counted).
3c For the countryside, on the other hand, how do you get there without a car? Par contre, pour la campagne, comment est-ce que vous y allez, sans voiture ? Contrast link par contre; pronoun y. Only-here.
4a So if I understand correctly, I'd depend on the drivers. Donc, si je comprends bien, je dépendrais des conducteurs. Reword (consequence link donc); conditional; dépendre de, conducteurs. Frame: repeat their transport noun, then "so I'd depend on...".
4b In that case, if there were no seats left, would I miss the outing? Dans ce cas, s'il n'y avait plus de place, est-ce que je raterais la sortie ? Consequence link dans ce cas; si + imperfect + conditional (hard). Rehearse the repair "s'il n'y aurait, pardon, s'il n'y avait".
5a Good to know. C'est bon à savoir. Acknowledges any answer.
5b Besides, for the heritage you talk about, does someone explain its history? Par ailleurs, pour le patrimoine dont vous parlez, est-ce que quelqu'un explique son histoire ? Addition link par ailleurs; dont (hard). "Talk about" is chosen so the French gives dont vous parlez, not que vous mentionnez. Pause after parlez.

Translator notes (each only picks between natural renderings of the English):

  • "membership": l'adhésion (not l'abonnement).
  • "outing": une sortie.
  • "keep up the pace": suivre le rythme.
  • "get around by bus": me déplacer en bus.
  • "drivers": les conducteurs.
  • "Besides": par ailleurs (en plus is also fine; both are addition).

Substitution pass (same length, denser): "take the bus" became "get around by bus" (se déplacer); "the others" became "the drivers" (conducteurs); "the heritage you mention" became "the heritage you talk about" (dont); "keep up" became "keep up the pace" (rythme). After review: "What would you advise?" became "Which formula would you recommend?" (audible uptake); "Regarding" became "Besides" (a topic marker is not a link; addition is); the two opener sentences became one.

Step 5. Deletion test

The box is at the time floor (100 English words, about 58 seconds), so a piece stays if it carries evidence; a piece that carried nothing would be swapped for evidence, not deleted.

Piece What breaks without it Verdict
"Hello" The call opens without the greeting a phone call to a business requires; the task row includes respecting the situation. stays
"how much is the membership" Condition 2: no scripted partial answer, so no partial-answer chain. stays
"for the Walking Club" Condition 1: the ad is never named. stays
"I'd like to try one outing" Turn 2 shrinks to "Which formula would you recommend?", the B1 rung (asks, doesn't steer). stays
"before committing for the year" Turn 2 no longer answers "several formulas" (the annual commitment is the formula dimension); loses avant de + infinitive and s'engager. stays
"to see whether I can keep up the pace" Loses the only indirect question inside a reply and rythme; syntax falls to four types with no spare; box falls to 91 words, under the floor. stays
"Which formula would you recommend?" No audible uptake of "it depends" (standard 10); turn 2 becomes a statement the examiner can answer with "yes". stays
"All right" Turn 3 ignores their answer (condition 5). stays
"In the city, I can get around by bus" par contre has nothing to contrast with, so the contrast link goes; the only-here reasoning (bus works in town, not in the country) becomes implicit. stays
"For the countryside... without a car?" No only-here question; turn 4 has no material. stays
"So if I understand correctly, I'd depend on the drivers" Turn 4 becomes a plain hypothetical (B2 rung): no C1 glimpse. stays
"In that case, if there were no seats left, would I miss the outing?" No consequence, no hard structure in a reply. stays
"Good to know" Turn 5 ignores their answer (condition 5). stays
"Besides" Links fall to two relations (contrast, consequence). stays
"for the heritage you talk about, does someone explain its history?" Condition 4: the box falls to 89 words, about 52 seconds; loses dont and the second only-here question. stays

Tried and dropped: "I've just moved to the area" (nothing later used it); "I'd like to know" in turn 1 (the indirect question sits in turn 2, where it counts); "I'm calling about" (merged into the price question); "in the ad" in turn 5 (dont vous parlez already points at the ad); "Regarding" (topic marker, not a link); "the history of the places" shortened to "its history" (lieux is everyday; 2 words over the floor went).

Step 6. Reply test (three plausible replies to each examiner turn)

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
After the price question "It's 30 euros a year." "It depends, we have several formulas." (boxed) "We're full, there's a waiting list." Boxed line after the partial, with their noun (formula, rate, option). After a figure: "Thirty euros a year, all right. Before committing for the year, I'd like to try one outing, to see whether I can keep up the pace. Would that be possible?" After a waiting list: "A waiting list? In that case, could I try one outing while I wait, to see whether I can keep up the pace?"
After "Which formula would you recommend?" "Come to a first outing for free, then decide." (boxed) "It depends how often you want to come." "You'll need a medical certificate first." Boxed "All right" works after the helpful one. After the partial: "About once a week, I think. All right..." then turn 3. After the certificate: "A medical certificate, all right. Can my own doctor sign it?" then turn 3 from "In the city".
After the transport question "We carpool from a meeting point in town." (boxed) "It depends on the outing." "You really need a car for the countryside." Boxed turn 4 after carpool (or a club minibus: "I'd depend on the minibus", same second sentence). After "it depends": "So if I understand correctly, the meeting point changes each time. In that case, if it were far from a bus stop, could someone pick me up?" After "you need a car": "So if I understand correctly, without a car I'd be limited to the city walks. In that case, if I only did those, would the membership be cheaper?" After "take the regional train": "So if I understand correctly, the transport is up to each person. In that case, if my train were late, would the group wait for me?"
After "would I miss the outing?" "No, don't worry, we always find a solution." (boxed) "That rarely happens." "Seats are booked online, first come, first served." "Good to know. Besides..." fits all three; turn 5 is at right angles to transport, so it stands after any of them.

Every turn-4 variant keeps the same frame (reword, dans ce cas, si + imperfect + conditional), so you rehearse one frame and four short gists, not four scripts. Drill turns 2 and 4 as frames ("repeat their noun"; "so I'd depend on..."), never as fixed sentences.

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6: "Turn 1 is the most generic question there is. Turn 2 is the stock 'can I try first?' that fits any club or class. 'Si je comprends bien... dans ce cas' is a prep-book formula. Turns 3 and 5 are new questions with a token acknowledgement, so this is a list, not a conversation. The words are everyday: bus, car, seats, outing. Two showpiece structures, the rest simple. Appropriate questions, adequate reactions: lower B2."

How the box answers it:

  • Turn 2 now takes their noun ("which formula"), so it is audibly a reply to "it depends", and it gives them your criterion (one trial before a year's commitment, checking the pace) so they can choose: the B2 rung, "uses the answer to steer".
  • The formula in turn 4 is the frame; what follows it cannot be prepared without the examiner's word covoiturage: je dépendrais des conducteurs and the missed-outing risk are an inference from their answer plus your situation. That is the C1 move "draws a consequence".
  • Turn 3 shows you worked out from the ad's own split (city and countryside) where your problem is, and turn 4 builds on its answer: a chain of two.
  • Five different structure types, one each, two of them hard (si-hypothetical in a reply, dont), plus three link relations (par contre, donc / dans ce cas, par ailleurs).
  • Lexis: adhésion, sortie, s'engager, suivre le rythme, conducteurs, each where a 6 would use a detour.

Fixes made after the case: "take the bus" became "get around by bus" and "the others" became "the drivers" (the everyday-words charge); turn 5 was rewritten to carry dont (the "two showpieces" charge); after review, turn 2 took the examiner's noun (the "stock question" charge). The deletion test was run again on the fixed box (table above): nothing further goes.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

  • Longest sentences: 2a (17 French words, two breath groups split at pour voir si) and 5b (14 French words, split after parlez). Both say at a natural pace with one pause each.
  • Hard spots: 4b (the si + conditional trap, s'il n'y aurait) and 5b (que vous parlez instead of dont). Rehearse a one-word repair for each.
  • No rare or tongue-twisting words: conducteurs, rythme, déplacer, patrimoine are all common.

Step 9. Count and timing (standard conversion)

  • Your English words: 100. Seconds of your speech = 100 x 0.58 = about 58 seconds (French words = English x 1.05, 120 French words a minute, plus 10% for pauses).
  • Examiner, from "Hello?": four short answers, about 13 seconds, plus about 9 turn changes of roughly a second.
  • The point: end of turn 5, about 1:15 to 1:25 after "Hello?", inside the 1:15 to 2:15 window the principles expect.
  • Turns 1 to 4 alone: 85 words, about 49 seconds, under the floor.

Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

  • "Gentle": how many kilometres, how much climbing (dénivelé), how long an outing lasts.
  • Days and times; anything at weekends if they say weekday mornings.
  • Seasons: in winter, when the paths are muddy and it gets dark early, do you stay in town; in summer, do you leave earlier because of the heat.
  • Equipment: walking shoes, poles, rain gear; is anything lent.
  • Who leads (volunteer, qualified guide); group size.
  • Insurance: included in the membership; medical certificate.
  • Booking: how far in advance; what if you cancel the day before.
  • Heritage visits: is the entry to museums or monuments included.
  • "If I came with a friend to the trial outing, would that be possible?"
  • Close: "I'll sign up on the website, thank you for your help."

After review

  • Required fix 1 (no uptake in turn 2): applied. "What would you advise?" became "Which formula would you recommend?" (their noun; quelle formule, no laquelle).
  • Cut 1 ("in the ad"): applied.
  • Cut 2 ("Regarding"): applied, swapped for "Besides" as suggestion 1 and standard 6 ask (addition link, no word cost).
  • Cut 3 (opener): applied, now "Hello, how much is the membership for the Walking Club?".
  • Suggestion 1 (links counted twice): applied. Links are now contrast, consequence and addition; the si clause counts only as syntax.
  • Suggestion 2 ("depend on the others"): rebutted. It is not true after a bus or train answer either, so the fallbacks are still needed, and it would drop conducteurs, one of the five precise words. Kept "the drivers" for the most likely answer, with the frame noted under standard 13.
  • Suggestion 3 (one ratio): applied through standard 5.
  • Own trim: "the history of the places" became "its history" (2 words over the floor that carried no evidence).
  • Standard 1 (audible subjunctives): no subjunctive is counted; nothing to change.
  • Standard 2 (indirect "what"): checked; "What would you advise?" is gone and no indirect "what" remains.
  • Standards 3, 4, 16, 17, 18: Section B only; not applicable.
  • Standard 5 (time floor): applied. 100 words = about 58 seconds; turns 1 to 4 = 85 words = about 49 seconds; timing restated in Point, Why and Working.
  • Standard 6 (links): applied, as above.
  • Standard 7 (timing basis): applied; the clock starts at "Hello?".
  • Standard 8 (lexis the English doesn't produce): applied; translator notes added in step 4.
  • Standard 9 (facts not on the ad): checked; the box states no fact about the club, only questions and facts about you.
  • Standard 10 (uptake on "it depends"): applied, as required fix 1.
  • Standard 11 (one-sentence opener): applied, as cut 3.
  • Standard 12 (precise means precise): applied. The list is now five: adhésion, sortie, s'engager, suivre le rythme, conducteurs; se déplacer, place, rater, l'histoire des lieux are no longer counted.
  • Standard 13 (lines that depend on the examiner): applied; turns 2 and 4 are given as frames in steps 4 and 6.
  • Standard 14 (format): applied; the box opens with [examiner: "Hello?"].
  • Standard 15 (the English makes its own French): checked; step 4's French now matches the English (recommanderiez, son histoire, est-ce que je raterais).
  • Standard 19 (count only your own words): applied; formule (theirs) and words printed on the ad are not counted.
  • Final audit: applied both items. se déplacer dropped from the lexis lists as everyday (five real words remain, enough for condition 2, so no swap in the box); the turn-5 deletion row now says 89 words, about 52 seconds.

A-04

Faites la fête

Events, parties & shows · Events & parties · est. #29 of 78
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement for an event-planning agency and you are interested. You call to get more information.

Faites la fête

Unforgettable parties, personalised and tailored to your preferences:

With the professionals of…

Faites la fête

*Personalised decorations

*Buffets and varied drinks

*For young and old

*Music of your choice

*Afternoon or evening

*In a venue or at home

Call us on 07 98 76 54 32

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité pour une agence évenementielle et vous êtes intéressé(e). Vous téléphonez pour avoir plus d’informations.

FAITES LA FÊTE

Des fêtes inoubliables, personnalisées et adaptées à vos préférences :

Avec les professionnels de…

Faites la fête

*Décorations personnalisées

*Buffets et boissons variées

*Pour les petits et les grands

*Musique de votre choix

*Après-midi ou soirée

*En salle ou à domicile

Appelez-nous au 07 98 76 54 32

The point

The point falls at the end of your fourth turn, the one that ends "is it you who take care of it?". Counting from the examiner's "Hello?", that is about 1:15 into Section A: about 59 seconds of your speech on the shared conversion (101 English words x 0.58) plus about 15 seconds of examiner turns.

The box101 words

[examiner: "Hello?"]

Hello, I'm calling about a surprise party for my parents, with about fifty guests. How much would that cost?

[examiner: "It depends, we have several packages."]

So there's no fixed price. If we held it at their place rather than renting a hall, would it work out cheaper?

[examiner: "Yes, a little, you'd save on renting the hall."]

Either way, we'll do it at their place. Since it's a surprise, could your team set everything up while we take them out to lunch?

[examiner: "Of course. We'd need about three hours to set up."]

Then we'd need you to be able to get in during lunch. My sister has the keys, she could let you in. And the tidying up afterwards, is it you who take care of it?

Why this, and why nothing less

The box is built around one fact about you: you are planning a surprise party for your parents, about fifty guests. You say it in your first sentence, and it makes every later question one that only fits this ad and this situation. A list of generic questions can't produce it.

Task (obtaining information). You ask the price straight away, because the examiner's booklet scripts a partial answer to it ("it depends, we have several packages"). Turn 2 is the B2 reaction to that partial answer: it rewords the gist ("so there's no fixed price", true of every "it depends") and steers with a hypothetical that uses the ad's own choice between a hall and home. Turn 3 decides ("either way, we'll do it at their place"), which fits whatever they said, then asks a question only a surprise party at home can raise. Turn 4 is the C1 glimpse: it draws a consequence from the examiner's yes (if they set up while your parents are at lunch, they need a way in) and solves it before being asked, then hands the floor back with a new question. That is three replies to the examiner: turn 2 at the B2 rung, turn 4 at C1, and turn 3 a decision that holds after any answer.

Syntax. Four marked structure types, all in replies: if + past + conditional (si on l'organisait chez eux... est-ce que ça reviendrait moins cher ?), plutôt que de + infinitive (plutôt que de louer une salle), an audible subjunctive that the two subjects in "we'd need you to" force (il faudrait que vous puissiez entrer), and a cleft with en (c'est vous qui vous en occupez ?). On top, a time clause (pendant qu'on les emmène déjeuner). Links of three relations: consequence (donc, alors), cause (comme), concession (de toute façon).

Lexis. Five precise words, none on the ad, each produced by the English: une cinquantaine d'invités, louer, revenir moins cher, installer, le rangement.

Delivery. No sentence is over 17 English words, none has more than one hard structure, and each breaks at a clause boundary.

Time. 101 English words is about 59 seconds of your speech on the shared conversion; the floor for condition 4 is about 100 words (58 seconds). The box sits at that floor, so the point can't come earlier by cutting: a further cut only makes the box end before the floor and pushes the point into turn 5.

Why nothing can go. The opener is the price trigger plus the facts the examiner needs to answer it (the examiner never asks you questions, so the guest count must come from you). Turn 2 is the reply to the partial answer and the only si hypothetical. "Either way" keeps turn 3 from sounding recited after an answer you could not predict, and makes the venue a decision; without it "take them out" doesn't follow. Turn 3's question carries the time clause, the cause link and the only-here logistics, and creates what turn 4 reacts to. Turn 4 is the C1 glimpse, the subjunctive and the cleft. Without turn 4 you have about 40 seconds of speech and no C1 glimpse.

The next cut. Any further cut takes the box under about 100 words and moves the point into turn 5. Dropping the sister sentence also takes the C1 glimpse with it (turn 4 becomes a check followed by a topic change, the B1/B2 rung). Dropping the cleft question leaves three structure types, one short of the bar.

What the box assumes about the rest. About three and three quarter minutes remain. You need six to eight more plain, correct questions tied to your party (deposit, cancellation, allergies, children, music and neighbours, rain), said at the same pace. The reserve in Working lists them. The box does not survive running dry, the same error in every turn, or switching to English.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (no lexical credit for any of it): fêtes inoubliables, personnalisées, adaptées à vos préférences, professionnels, décorations personnalisées, buffets, boissons variées, pour les petits et les grands, musique de votre choix, après-midi ou soirée, en salle ou à domicile, a phone number.

What it leaves unsaid: any price, and whether it is per person, per package or per hour; packages and what each includes; minimum or maximum guests; deposit, payment, cancellation and postponement; how far in advance to book; whether "en salle" means they supply the hall or you do; travel fees for a home party; setup time, access, tidying up and waste; staff (servers, a DJ, someone for the children); special diets and allergies; alcohol; noise limits and end time; plan B for rain at a garden party; tasting before the day.

Who is on the other end: a salesperson at a small events agency. Natural partial answers: "it depends on the package", "it depends on the number of guests", "it depends on the options". Natural unexpected elements (the examiner prepares some): a 30 percent deposit, a minimum number of guests for home parties, a travel fee outside the city, music must stop at 10 or 11 pm at home, some dates fully booked, setup the day before, an extra charge for setup or tidying up.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad.

  • Partial answer: almost certainly to the price question; the booklet's own wording ("it depends, we have several packages") fits this ad exactly.
  • Surprise: most likely attached to "à domicile" (fees, noise, space, minimum guests) or to dates.
  • Relaunches if you stall are statements ("we also have a children's entertainer", "we have a promotion this month").

Step 2. The hard part

The hard part is lexis and the only-here condition together. The ad already prints the obvious domain words (decorations, buffet, drinks, music, afternoon, evening, venue, home), so the natural questions ("what kind of music?", "what's on the buffet?") recycle the ad's words and earn nothing. The second difficulty is a C1 glimpse whose reword you can predict: prices and schedules can't be reworded safely (P13).

The only-here material comes from your own situation. A surprise party at your parents' home creates problems no other ad raises: the team must set up while your parents are out, someone must let them in, and the choice between a hall and home is a real decision with a price consequence. Those problems produce the precise words: une cinquantaine d'invités, louer, revenir moins cher, installer, le rangement.

The predictable-consequence problem is solved by logic instead of guessing: if the agency says yes to setting up during lunch, they must be able to get in while your parents are out. That holds whatever number of hours they give, so turn 4 can draw it and solve it (the C1 move) without depending on a figure.

Step 3. Designs compared

Design Idea Words to point How it holds up Verdict
A. Conventional price chain Opener, price, "it depends on what, exactly?", package, "what's included?", "if it rains?" about 115 high Rejected. Every question fits any event ad (fails only-here); the C1 glimpse needs an extra turn; lexis is transaction words only.
B. The surprise frame (chosen) One self-description in the opener (surprise party for your parents, 50 guests) makes every later question only-here; the partial price answer is reworded and steered with the ad's own hall-or-home choice; the surprise creates a logistics question whose consequence is guaranteed by logic, which turn 4 draws and solves 104 high (every reply line is at right angles to the answer or rests on that logic) Chosen.
C. Two-probe opener Open with a stack of constraints (ages 4 to 88, at home, music for both, neighbours complain after 10) so the examiner answers part and you react to whichever part they pick about 95 low Rejected. The examiner picks which constraint to answer, so your next line can't be scripted; a 40-word opener full of constraints sounds recited (P4); it spends half the reserve in the first turn.
D. Examiner carries the evidence Yes/no transaction chain (deposit? refundable if we postpone? if it rains?) where the examiner's conditions give you something to react to about 100 very high Rejected. Generic: works for any Section A ad, so no only-here question and no domain words.
E. Three-turn cut of B B without turn 4 66 high Rejected. No C1 glimpse, one B2 reply, three structure types, about 40 seconds of speech: fails replication (P6) and condition 4.

Why B beats A on words to point: in A the reply to "it depends" is a B1/B2 move ("depends on what?") and you need a further turn for the C1 glimpse. In B the self-description pays three times (the price question is answerable, the hall-or-home hypothetical has a reason, the surprise logistics set up turn 4's consequence).

Step 4. Structure note for each sentence (the French the English is written to produce)

# English Target French What it carries
1a Hello, I'm calling about a surprise party for my parents, with about fifty guests. Bonjour, je vous appelle au sujet d'une fête surprise pour mes parents, avec une cinquantaine d'invités. Frame in one sentence (STANDARDS 11): register, purpose, the stated requirement (CEFR B2 "states requirements"). Lexis: une cinquantaine d'invités. Sets up every later only-here question.
1b How much would that cost? Ça coûterait combien ? Polite conditional. The trigger for the scripted partial answer.
2a So there's no fixed price. Donc il n'y a pas de prix fixe. Uptake on the partial answer (STANDARDS 10): a reword true after any "it depends". Consequence link donc.
2b If we held it at their place rather than renting a hall, would it work out cheaper? Si on l'organisait chez eux plutôt que de louer une salle, est-ce que ça reviendrait moins cher ? Hard structure: si + imparfait + conditionnel. Second marked item: plutôt que de + infinitive. Lexis: louer, revenir moins cher. The B2 steer, using the ad's own hall-or-home choice (only-here).
3a Either way, we'll do it at their place. De toute façon, on la fera chez eux. Concession link that fits any answer. Future + object pronoun. Turns the venue from hypothesis into decision.
3b Since it's a surprise, could your team set everything up while we take them out to lunch? Comme c'est une surprise, est-ce que votre équipe pourrait tout installer pendant qu'on les emmène déjeuner ? Cause link (comme), time clause (pendant que) with pronoun + motion verb + infinitive, polite conditional. Lexis: installer. Only-here (surprise + "à domicile").
4a Then we'd need you to be able to get in during lunch. Alors il faudrait que vous puissiez entrer pendant le déjeuner. Hard structure: audible subjunctive (puissiez), forced by the two subjects ("we" need "you" to). "During lunch" is a noun phrase, so no second mood choice (P8). The consequence drawn from the examiner's yes: first half of the C1 glimpse. Consequence link alors.
4b My sister has the keys, she could let you in. Ma sœur a les clés, elle pourrait vous ouvrir. Second half of the C1 glimpse: you solve the problem before being asked. Your own fact, not an ad fact.
4c And the tidying up afterwards, is it you who take care of it? Et le rangement après la fête, c'est vous qui vous en occupez ? Dislocation + cleft + en: one medium marked item. Lexis: le rangement. Hands the floor back.

Structure types (one list, counted once each): si + imparfait + conditionnel (2b); plutôt que de + infinitive (2b); audible subjunctive (4a); cleft with en (4c). Also present, not counted as types: time clause with pendant que (3b), future with object pronoun (3a). The comme clause is counted as a cause link, not as a structure type (STANDARDS 6: nothing counted twice). Every counted structure sits in a reply turn; no sentence has more than one hard structure or two dependent mood choices.

Links (STANDARDS 6, connectors joining clauses or turns): consequence (donc 2a, alors 4a), cause (comme 3b), concession (de toute façon 3a). Three relations. "And", "if" and "rather than" are not counted.

Precise words (STANDARDS 8, 12, 19), only those the English produces on its own and you choose: une cinquantaine d'invités, louer, revenir moins cher, installer, le rangement. Not counted: prix fixe (a reword of the examiner's answer), everyday words (équipe, déjeuner, clés, s'occuper de, vous ouvrir), and salle (printed on the ad).

Substitution pass (same length, denser).

  • "about fifty guests" over "fifty people": une cinquantaine d'invités, a precise word plus a marked noun form, at the same length.
  • "would it work out cheaper" over "would it be cheaper": revenir moins cher instead of être moins cher.
  • "rather than renting a hall" over "rather than in a hall": same length, adds louer and plutôt que de + infinitive.
  • "we'd need you to be able to get in during lunch" over "you would need to be able to get in before they're back": the two subjects force the subjunctive, and "during lunch" avoids avant qu'ils rentrent (a second mood choice). One word shorter.
  • "Then" in 4a over a second "So": alors instead of a second donc.
  • "is it you who take care of it" over "do you do the tidying up too": same length, adds the cleft and en.

Step 5. Deletion test (re-run after review)

The box is at the time floor (101 English words = about 59 seconds; the floor is about 100 words), so a piece that carries no evidence would be swapped for evidence, not deleted (STANDARDS 5). Each piece below was removed in turn.

Piece removed What breaks
"roughly" (cut after review) Nothing: "would that cost" already carries the polite conditional, and the partial answer comes from the examiner's playbook. Cut, 1 word.
"I saw your ad" (merged after review) Nothing once "I'm calling about a surprise party" carries the purpose and names the service the agency sells (STANDARDS 11). Merged, 3 words saved.
1a whole The price question has nothing to price (the examiner never asks you questions), the later "their place", "surprise" and "them" have no referent, and every only-here question loses its basis.
1a "fortieth wedding anniversary" (cut after the audit) Nothing: no capacity rested on it, and without it the box is 101 words, still at the floor of about 100. Cut, 3 words.
1a "with about fifty guests" The partial answer turns into "it depends how many people", the examiner can't ask, so you'd spend a turn supplying it; you also lose une cinquantaine d'invités.
1b price question The scripted partial answer does not come, so turn 2 has nothing to react to (condition 2: a reaction to a partial answer).
2a "So there's no fixed price" The audible reaction to the partial answer goes (STANDARDS 10), and turn 2 becomes a fresh question.
2b The B2 reply to the partial answer, the only si hypothetical, plutôt que de + infinitive and the only-here use of the hall-or-home choice all go (conditions 2 and 3).
2b "rather than renting a hall" "cheaper" loses its reference point on the recording (cheaper than what?), and louer and plutôt que de go.
3a "Either way, we'll do it at their place" Turn 3 ignores an answer you could not predict (condition 5 at risk if it held a condition), the concession link goes (links down to two relations), and the venue stays hypothetical, so "take them out" no longer follows.
3b The cause link and the only-here logistics go, and turn 4 has nothing to draw a consequence from.
4a "Then we'd need you to be able to get in during lunch" (changed after review) The consequence half of the C1 glimpse and the only subjunctive go (syntax down to three types).
4b "My sister has the keys, she could let you in" Turn 4 becomes a check followed by a topic change, the B1/B2 rung: the C1 glimpse goes.
4c Syntax falls to three types; the turn ends on a statement and the examiner has to relaunch (a mild sign of dependence).
All of turn 4 Design E: no C1 glimpse, about 40 seconds of speech. Conditions 2, 3 and 4 fail.

Step 6. How each line holds up (three replies per examiner turn)

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
Opening "Hello?" (box) "Faites la fête, hello!" "Faites la fête, Julie speaking, I'm listening." Turn 1 fits all three.
After turn 1 (price) "About 40 dollars a person, buffet and decorations included." "It depends, we have several packages." (box) "Saturdays in June are almost full; when is it?" After partial: box. After helpful: reword the unit, never the figure: "So it's per person. If we held it at their place rather than renting a hall, would that bring it down?" After surprising: "It would be the last Saturday in June. And if we held it at their place rather than renting a hall, would it work out cheaper?" The si hypothetical survives every case. Frame: if they name something ("packages", "options", "the number of guests"), say their noun: "So it depends on the package. If we held it..."
After turn 2 (home vs hall) "Yes, you'd save on renting the hall." (box) "Not necessarily, at home we bring all the equipment." "At home, the music has to stop at 10 pm." "Either way" fits all three: it is written at right angles to the answer. If the surprise is a minimum number of guests above fifty, replace 3a with "We'll be about fifty, so would that be a problem?" and keep 3b for the next turn.
After turn 3 (setup during lunch) "Of course, we'd need about three hours." (box) "That's possible, but setup is charged separately." "We usually set up the day before." 4a rests on logic, not on their words: if they set up while your parents are out, they need to get in while your parents are out. It fits helpful and partial without echoing any figure. After "the day before": "Then they would see everything. If my sister invited them to her place that evening, could you set up then?" (a reply with a si clause; the subjunctive moves to the reserve).

Frame for any answer not in the table (STANDARDS 13): repeat their key noun, then the same frame ("So it's X. Either way... / Then we'd need you to...") and continue with the next scripted question, which never depends on a figure or a schedule.

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6. "Turn 1 is a prepared self-introduction. Turn 2's si + imparfait is the structure every prep course drills, and 'so there's no fixed price' is a stock reword. 'Either way' in turn 3 is a filler that would follow any answer, so it proves nothing. That leaves one real reaction, turn 4. The vocabulary is mostly everyday (party, guests, team, lunch, keys, sister). I have heard lower B2, not upper B2."

How the box answers it.

  • Turn 2: the reword is general, but the hypothetical uses the ad's own choice between a hall and home and your own situation; a drilled sentence doesn't come with that content. "Rather than renting a hall" (plutôt que de louer une salle) adds a verb not on the ad and a second structure at the same length. It is the B2 reaction to the partial answer, and the box claims no more for it.
  • Turn 3: "Either way" is not offered as task evidence. It is there so the line is not recited against the answer (condition 5) and it carries the concession link. The evidence in turn 3 is the question, which only a surprise party at home can ask.
  • Turn 4 is a reply the rater can't call prepared: it draws a consequence from the examiner's yes, solves it before being asked (the C1 glimpse), and carries the box's only subjunctive, forced by two subjects. With turn 2, that is two replies at the B2 rung or above, one of them C1.
  • Vocabulary: the rater counts the plain words; the grid's B2 asks for a "fairly wide repertoire", and the box gives five precise words not on the ad (une cinquantaine d'invités, louer, revenir moins cher, installer, le rangement).

What the rater can still say: delivery. If you speak the box faster or smoother than the rest, it reads as recited. That is fixed by saying it at your own pace with pauses at the commas, not by the script.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

Longest sentences in French: 2b (17 words, breaks after salle), 1a (16 words, breaks after parents), 3b (16 words, breaks after surprise). Every sentence has at most one hard structure and breaks at a clause boundary. Spots to practise: si on l'organisait (the elided l'), il faudrait que vous puissiez (keep puissiez, not pouvez), and c'est vous qui vous en occupez (the verb agrees with vous, not s'en occupe). Repair habit if a slip comes: "si on l'organiserait... pardon, si on l'organisait" (a quick self-repair counts for upper B2, not against).

Step 9. Count and time (shared conversion, STANDARDS 5 and 7)

  • English words in the box: 101 (turns of 19, 22, 25 and 35).
  • French words: 101 x 1.05 = about 106.
  • Your speech: 101 x 0.58 = about 59 seconds (120 French words a minute plus 10 percent for pauses). This meets the floor of condition 4 (about 100 English words, 58 seconds), with nothing to spare.
  • Examiner turns: "Hello?" plus three short replies, about 15 seconds with the gaps.
  • The clock starts at the examiner's "Hello?". The point lands at about 1:15, with about three and three quarter minutes of the call left.

Reserve (after the point; not part of the box)

  1. Deposit: how much, and is it refundable if you have to postpone (l'acompte, remboursable si on reporte).
  2. Allergies: one nephew is allergic to nuts; can the buffet be made without them, and are there vegetarian dishes.
  3. Children: guests from 4 to 88; is there someone to keep the little ones busy (un animateur pour les enfants).
  4. Music and neighbours: who chooses, a DJ or your playlist, until what time, a volume the grandparents can bear.
  5. Rain plan for a garden party: a tent, or room inside (en cas de pluie).
  6. Deadline for the final number of guests, and what happens if a few cancel at the last minute.
  7. Tables, chairs, plates: supplied or not.
  8. How far in advance to book for late June; could they send a quote by email (un devis).
  9. Closing: "I'll talk to my sister and call you back before Friday. Thank you for your help."

Translator notes

Each note only chooses between natural renderings of the English (STANDARDS 8, 15).

  • "I'm calling about" → je vous appelle au sujet d' (or pour).
  • "about fifty guests" → une cinquantaine d'invités (not environ cinquante).
  • "work out cheaper" → revenir moins cher (not être moins cher).
  • "Since it's a surprise" → comme at the front (puisque is also fine).
  • "we'd need you to be able to" → il faudrait que vous puissiez (on aurait besoin que vous puissiez is also fine; both are subjunctive).
  • "let you in" → vous ouvrir (or vous faire entrer).
  • "is it you who take care of it" → c'est vous qui vous en occupez (the English verb agrees with "you", as the French must).

After review

  • Review fix 1 (turn 4 French not forced): applied. 4a is now "Then we'd need you to be able to get in during lunch." Two subjects force il faudrait que vous puissiez; "during lunch" (a noun phrase) was chosen over the suggested "while they're out" so there is no second mood choice and no second pendant que clause after 3b. avant leur retour is out of the lexis list.
  • Review fix 2 (C1 credited to the wrong turn): applied. Turn 2 is now described as the B2 reaction to the partial answer (reword plus steer); turn 4 is the C1 glimpse (consequence of the examiner's yes, solved before being asked). Why, structure notes, deletion rows 2a, 4a and 4b, design table and sceptic section changed to match.
  • Cut 1 ("roughly"): applied, 1 word.

  • Suggestion 2 (lexis list): applied; prix fixe and avant leur retour dropped, six genuine words remain.
  • Suggestion 3 ("fortieth wedding anniversary"): applied; its stated job is now time (the box is at the floor) and believability, not lexis.

Cutting "roughly", merging the opener and the shorter 4a took the box from 109 to 104; the 3 words in "fortieth wedding anniversary" are kept rather than cut because without them the box is 101 words and ends under 60 seconds.
  • Across the chunk 2 (rung labels): applied through fix 2.
  • Across the chunk 3 (counted subjunctive needs two subjects): applied in 4a.
  • Across the chunk 4 (one structure list): applied. Four types counted once each (si hypothetical, plutôt que de + infinitive, audible subjunctive, cleft with en); the comme clause counts as a link only, pendant que and the future are listed but not counted.
  • Across the chunk 5 (lexis hygiene): applied with suggestion 2.

  • STANDARDS 1 (audible subjunctive): applied; puissiez is the only subjunctive counted, and it is forced.
  • STANDARDS 2 (no indirect "what"): checked; the box has none.
  • STANDARDS 3, 4, 16, 17, 18: Section B only; not applicable.
  • STANDARDS 6 (links): applied. Recounted as consequence (donc, alors), cause (comme) and concession (de toute façon, which French grammar lists with en tout cas and quoi qu'il en soit as concession markers). "And", "if", "rather than" and "while" are no longer counted.
  • STANDARDS 7 (clock from "Hello?"): applied in Point and step 9.

  • STANDARDS 9 (facts not on the ad): checked; nothing about the agency is stated as fact. "My sister has the keys" and "we'll do it at their place" are your own facts and decisions.

  • Final audit (after the first review): all three items applied.
