A-12

JOYEUSEVUE.COM

Buying & renting · Online commercial services · est. #54 of 78
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement and you are interested. You phone to get more information.

JOYEUSEVUE.COM

JOYEUSEVUE.COM

Buy your glasses online!

Prescription glasses or sunglasses

Order your glasses in 4 steps:

1. Choose your frame + your lenses

2. Send your prescription

3. Pay in complete security

4. Receive your glasses

Quality at the best price… Satisfied or your money back… Free delivery

More info:

820 12 12 546

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité et vous êtes intéressé(e). Vous téléphonez pour avoir plus d’informations.

JOYEUSEVUE.COM

JOYEUSEVUE.COM

Achetez vos lunettes en ligne !

Lunettes de vue ou lunettes solaires

Commandez vos lunettes en 4 étapes :

1. Choisissez votre monture + vos verres

2. Envoyez votre ordonnance

3. Réglez en toute sécurité

4. Recevez vos lunettes

La qualité au meilleur prix… Satisfait ou remboursé… Livraison offerte

Plus d’infos :

820 12 12 546

The point

The point falls at the end of your fourth turn, the question about who pays for new lenses if you get the measurement wrong. On the standard conversion that is about 60 seconds of your speech (105 English words x 0.58) plus about 20 seconds of examiner replies, so about 1:20 after the examiner's "Hello?".

The box105 words

[examiner: "Hello?"]

Hello, I'm very short-sighted, and I'm calling to find out how much a complete pair of glasses would cost me.

[examiner: "It depends on the frame and the lenses you choose."]

So for me it depends mostly on the lenses, and mine are always thick. Do you have thinner ones, and how much more would they cost?

[examiner: "Yes, thinner lenses cost thirty euros more."]

That's still reasonable. What worries me, though, is that everything is done remotely. Since the distance between my pupils isn't on my prescription, who measures it?

[examiner: "You can measure it yourself with our online tool."]

So I'd do it myself, with the tool you told me about. In that case, if I got it wrong by a few millimetres, would the new lenses be at your expense?

Why this, and why nothing less

This ad is a shop with four printed steps, and every candidate asks the same four things: price, delivery time, returns, payment. Those questions are on the ad already, so they prove nothing. The one thing the ad hides is the real problem with buying glasses online: nobody takes your measurements. Step 2 says "send your prescription", but a prescription usually doesn't give the distance between your pupils, and lenses centred in the wrong place give headaches. Your box walks the examiner from the scripted price trap straight to that gap, so by the fourth turn the rater has heard a customer who understood how this service works, not a list.

Criterion by criterion:

  • Task. Turn 2 reacts to the examiner's planned partial answer ("it depends") by rewording it with the examiner's own noun (the lenses) and a nuance you draw from your own eyes (for me, mostly), then ties it to you (thick lenses). Turn 3 reacts to the price, then asks the only-here question. Turn 4 is the C1 glimpse: it rewords the answer (so I'd do it myself), draws the consequence (I might get it wrong) and asks a hypothetical about who pays. That is two reactive turns at the B2 rung, one of them to a partial answer, plus one C1 move.
  • Syntax. Four marked structure types (grammar forms only stronger speakers use reliably), at most one hard one per sentence: a conditional inside an indirect question (turn 1), a cleft, "what worries me is" (turn 3), dont (turn 4) and si + imperfect + conditional (turn 4). En and les miens (turn 2) are extra, not counted. Links of three relations: consequence (so, in that case), contrast (though) and cause (since).
  • Lexis. Not on the ad, and produced by the English itself: myope, épais, à distance, pupilles, se tromper de quelques millimètres, à votre charge (with l'écart for "distance" as a translator note).
  • Delivery. No word you can't say at speed; every sentence breaks into one or two breath groups.
  • Register. Vous from the first sentence (je vous appelle), conditionals in every turn.

Why nothing can go. The box is at the time floor: 105 words is about 61 seconds of your speech, and condition 4 of the point needs about 60. So any cut would not bring the point earlier; it would push it into turn 5. Each piece also carries evidence:

  • "Very short-sighted" gives myope, steers the partial answer, and is the reason turn 2 asks for thinner lenses.
  • The price question gets the booklet's partial answer, so turn 2 has something planned to react to.
  • Turn 2 is the only reaction to the partial answer, and it keeps after the price instead of dropping it.
  • "That's still reasonable" is what stops turn 3 sounding like a recited next line after a price you asked for. It also gives "though" something to contrast with.
  • "Everything is done remotely" is the inference that turns the pupil question into a judgement, and gives à distance.
  • "Since it isn't on my prescription" blocks the easy answer "it's on your prescription", which would end the chain before turn 4.
  • Turn 4 holds the C1 move, the only hypothetical, and the dont. Because "the tool you told me about" uses the examiner's own words, it is the clearest sign that the box isn't recited.

If the examiner's replies differ from the stage directions, use the fallback lines in Working. Each fallback still gives four structure types: the conditional indirect question, the cleft, the si clause, plus dont (method, app or average) or ce que (the optician fallback).

What the box assumes about the rest: about three and a half more minutes of plain, correct questions that follow the answers (reserve list in Working), one reaction to the examiner's planned surprise (most likely a condition on "satisfied or refunded" or on who pays for remakes), and no long silences.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (no lexical value): lunettes, en ligne, lunettes de vue, lunettes solaires, commandez, 4 étapes, choisissez, monture, verres, envoyez, ordonnance, réglez, en toute sécurité, recevez, qualité, meilleur prix, satisfait ou remboursé, livraison offerte, plus d'infos, the phone number. So monture, verres, ordonnance, rembourser, livraison carry nothing as vocabulary. Verres and ordonnance are still needed to talk, they just don't count.

What it leaves unsaid:

  • Price: frame price vs lens price; options (thinner lenses, anti-glare, blue-light filter, tint, progressive lenses); strong prescriptions.
  • Step 2: format (photo, scan, original), prescription age, foreign prescriptions, and above all the measurements a prescription doesn't give (distance between the pupils, fitting height for progressives).
  • Fit: no try-on, no optician to adjust the arms or nose pads.
  • "Satisfied or refunded": lenses are made to measure, so what exactly is refunded, how many days, who pays return postage, what if the error is the customer's.
  • "Free delivery": how long (manufacturing plus shipping), abroad, minimum order, tracking.
  • Payment: in instalments, insurance receipt, warranty against breakage and scratches.

Who is on the other end: a customer-service agent of an online optician. Formal, vous.

Predictable moves:

  • Partial answer: to "how much?", "it depends on the frame and the lenses you choose" (the ad's step 1 literally says frame + lenses, and the booklet scripts exactly this).
  • Unexpected elements likely in the examiner's notes: thinner lenses or anti-glare cost extra; "satisfied or refunded" covers the frame only, or only within 14 days, or not customer measurement errors; prescription must be under one or three years old; delivery takes three weeks; free delivery only above a minimum; a current promotion (second pair for one euro).
  • Relaunch statements if you go quiet: "we also do prescription sunglasses", "we have a virtual try-on".

Step 2. The hard part

The hard condition here is the only-here question plus precise words. The ad is a generic e-commerce page, so a prep list writes the call for you (price, delivery, refund, payment) and none of it is anchored. The only-here material comes from the gap between "send your prescription" and what a pair of glasses actually needs: the pupil distance and the centring of the lenses. It gives precise words that are not on the ad (pupilles, à distance, se tromper de quelques millimètres) and a natural hypothetical (what if I measure wrong?). A second source is your own eyes: being very short-sighted means thick lenses, so the price chain has a real follow-up.

Step 3. Designs compared

Design Shape Words to point Holds up? Verdict
A. Price trap, then the measurement gap (chosen) price, partial answer, thinner lenses, pupil distance, reword + who pays 105 Partial answer is near certain; turn 3 is at right angles to any price reply; turn 4 has fallbacks Best words-to-point, every turn anchored
B. Refund paradox first open with "how can you refund lenses made to my prescription?", react to the catch about 95 The examiner may simply say "full refund within 30 days", and the scripted reaction to a catch then fails Strong first question but the reply is a coin toss; no booklet-guaranteed partial answer
C. Two probes at once turn 1 asks price and how to send the prescription; turn 2 reacts to both about 85 Examiners usually answer one question; turn 2 then reacts to something not said, and the price thread is dropped Fewer words but weak follow-up to the partial answer, and below the time floor
D. Prescription sunglasses (reads the ad's "or" as "both") "can I have sunglasses made to my prescription?" then tint and price about 100 Fine, but the answer is usually just "yes, with a surcharge"; little to reword Only-here but shallow; kept for the reserve
E. Self-description opener ("very short-sighted, always bought from an optician") making every later question personal long opener, then three short questions about 110 Opener carries proof that raters discount as rehearsed Kept only the useful part (very short-sighted) in design A's turn 1

The unconventional parts kept in A: (1) the self-description in turn 1 shapes the partial answer and makes turn 2 personal; (2) "since it isn't on my prescription" closes off the examiner's easy answer so the reply to turn 3 is predictable, which is what lets turn 4 reword it safely; (3) turn 4's dont is built on the examiner's own words ("the tool you told me about"), so the hardest structure in the box is also the most obviously unrehearsed.

Step 4. Structure notes (French the English is written to carry)

Turn / sentence Target French Structures Precise words
T1 Bonjour, je suis très myope, et je vous appelle pour savoir combien me coûterait une paire de lunettes complète. frame and vous; conditional inside an indirect question (counted) myope
T2 s1 Donc pour moi, ça dépend surtout des verres, et les miens sont toujours épais. reword with the examiner's noun, framed as your own inference; possessive pronoun (extra) épais
T2 s2 Vous en avez des plus fins, et ils coûteraient combien de plus ? en + comparative (extra); conditional none counted
T3 s1 Ça reste raisonnable. rester + adjective, reaction none
T3 s2 Ce qui m'inquiète, par contre, c'est que tout se fait à distance. cleft (counted); se fasse may come out, not counted à distance
T3 s3 Puisque l'écart entre mes pupilles n'est pas sur mon ordonnance, qui le mesure ? cause link (puisque, counted as a link only); object pronoun le pupilles (l'écart by note)
T4 s1 Donc je le ferais moi-même, avec l'outil dont vous m'avez parlé. reword; dont (counted) none (outil is the examiner's word)
T4 s2 Dans ce cas, si je me trompais de quelques millimètres, les nouveaux verres seraient à votre charge ? si + imperfect + conditional (counted); consequence link se tromper de quelques millimètres, à votre charge

Load check: no sentence has more than two marked structures or more than one hard one. Longest English sentences are 20 words (T4 s2) and 14 words (T3 s3); T4 s2's French is 17 words in two breath groups.

Substitution pass done: "how much more" kept as combien de plus; "do you have thinner ones" chosen over "do you offer thinner lenses" to get en; "the tool you told me about" replaces "your tool" to get dont; "at your expense" replaces "would you pay for" to get à votre charge.

Translator notes (each chooses between natural renderings of the English):

  • "distance between my pupils": l'écart entre mes pupilles rather than la distance entre mes pupilles.
  • "Since": puisque (not comme).
  • "at your expense": à votre charge (or à vos frais; both count).
  • "I'm calling to find out": je vous appelle pour savoir.
  • "everything is done remotely": tout se fait à distance; se fasse is fine too, but it is not counted.

Step 5. Deletion test (rerun on the new opener and turn 4)

The box is at the time floor (105 words, about 61 seconds), so a piece that carries no evidence is swapped for evidence, not cut.

Piece Removed, what breaks
T1 "Hello" Register and the opening of the call (condition 1)
T1 "I'm very short-sighted" T2's thick lenses lose their reason; myope lost; the partial answer is less steered
T1 "I'm calling to find out how much ... would cost me" Purpose (condition 1), vous, the counted conditional indirect question, and the booklet's partial answer (condition 2, task)
T1 "of glasses" The only words that name the product on the recording now that "your ad" is merged away (condition 1)
T2 "So for me it depends mostly on the lenses" The uptake on the partial answer (STANDARDS 10); the rater hears a new question, not a reaction
T2 "and mine are always thick" épais lost, and the box falls under the time floor (condition 4)
T2 "how much more would they cost" The price thread is dropped after the partial answer, a B1-rung follow-up
T3 "That's still reasonable" The price you asked for gets no reaction (condition 5); "though" contrasts with nothing
T3 "everything is done remotely" The pupil question loses its reasoning; à distance and the counted cleft lost
T3 "since ... isn't on my prescription" Examiner can answer "it's on your prescription", T4's reword then fails; cause link lost (only two link relations left)
T3 "who measures it" No only-here question (P11)
T4 "So I'd do it myself" No reword, so no C1 move
T4 "with the tool you told me about" dont lost (syntax falls to three counted types) and the clearest uptake of the examiner's words
T4 "In that case" Consequence link and the box under the time floor; it stays because the box is at the floor
T4 "by a few millimetres" se tromper de quelques millimètres lost; the risk becomes vague
T4 "would the new lenses be at your expense" No hypothetical, no si clause, à votre charge lost

Step 6. Reply test (three replies per examiner turn)

After Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
T1 "Frames from 29 euros, lenses from 40" "It depends on the frame and lenses" (box) "It depends on your prescription" T2 as written works for all three; for the first, say "Lenses from 40, I see" first, then "So for me it depends mostly on the lenses"
T2 "Yes, 30 euros more" (box) "It depends on your correction, between 20 and 80" "They're included" / "We can't do thinner lenses that strong" Box line for the first two; "That's good to know" for included; for "can't", "I'm at minus six, would that still be possible?" then T3 from "What worries me"
T3 "Measure it yourself with our online tool" (box) "It's easy, there's a method on our site" "Any optician will measure it for free" / "We use an average" Repeat their noun: "the method (the app, the ruler) you told me about". Optician: "So I'd still need an optician, which is what I wanted to avoid. In that case, if the measurement were wrong, would the new lenses be at your expense?" (ce que je voulais éviter). Average: "So you'd use the average you told me about, not my own measurement. In that case, if the lenses were badly centred, would the new ones be at your expense?"
Any turn The examiner drops a surprise (delivery only above 50 euros, three weeks' wait) React in one sentence first ("Three weeks, that's long but fine"), then continue the box line

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Best case for a 6: "Four questions in a minute. Turn 2 reacts to the answer every examiner gives, which every prepared candidate rehearses. The pupil question is clearly prepared. One minute of speech is too little to judge accuracy." Answer: (1) turn 2 is not the stock "depends on what, exactly?"; it rewords with the examiner's noun and a nuance, links it to the candidate's own eyes, and pursues the price, which a prepared B1 does not do. (2) The pupil question is prepared, but it is set up by the candidate's own reasoning (everything remote, not on the prescription), and turn 4 builds on the examiner's reply with dont and a hypothetical, which cannot be recited. (3) Accuracy is presumed from about 60 seconds of clean speech with four counted structure types and no errors; the rest of the call can only disprove it with a pattern. Fixes applied after this pass: "That's still reasonable" makes turn 3 a reaction, and "with the tool you told me about" makes turn 4 visibly depend on the reply. Deletion test rerun: both stay.

Step 8. Say-aloud

Every French sentence above can be said in one or two breath groups. Practise three chunks until automatic: combien me coûterait, l'écart entre mes pupilles, l'outil dont vous m'avez parlé.

Step 9. Count and timing

105 English words. Standard conversion: 105 x 0.58 = about 61 seconds of your speech (about 110 French words at 120 a minute, plus 10% for pauses). With "Hello?" and four short replies (about 20 seconds), the point falls at about 1:20 after the examiner's "Hello?".

Reserve (after the point, not in the box)

  • React to the answer on who pays for remakes (likely the examiner's planned surprise).
  • How long between sending the prescription and receiving the glasses?
  • Is anti-glare included, or extra?
  • Can the sunglasses be made to my prescription, and do tinted lenses cost more?
  • How old can my prescription be?
  • If the arms or nose pads need adjusting, can any optician do it, and would that be at my cost?
  • "Satisfied or refunded": how many days, and who pays the return postage?
  • Is there a guarantee against breakage or scratches?
  • Can I pay in several instalments?
  • Can you give me a detailed receipt for my insurance?
  • Close: thank them, say you'll measure and order this week.

After review

  • Required fix 1 ("the tool you mentioned" doesn't force dont): applied, now "the tool you told me about" (dont vous m'avez parlé); the "method" fallback uses "told me about" too. This adds 2 words, not 1.
  • Cuts: none proposed, none made (box at the time floor).
  • Suggestion 1 (split turn 3): applied, full stop after "remotely".
  • Suggestion 2 ("In that case" after "So"): kept. It is the consequence link in the C1 turn, and the box is at the floor, where STANDARDS 5 says swap, not cut.
  • Suggestion 3 (one timing figure): applied, about 60 seconds on the standard conversion everywhere.
  • Suggestion 4 (structures in fallbacks): applied, and improved. Each fallback now keeps four counted types: dont in the method and average fallbacks, and ce que ("which is what I wanted to avoid") in the optician fallback. Puisque is counted as a link, not as syntax (STANDARDS 6).
  • STANDARDS 1 (audible subjunctive): the optional se fasse is no longer counted; the cleft is counted alone.
  • STANDARDS 2 (indirect "what"): checked; "what worries me" is a cleft, not an indirect question.
  • STANDARDS 5 (time floor): 105 words after the audit fix, about 61 seconds; Point and Working state it on this basis.
  • STANDARDS 6 (links): three relations, consequence, contrast and cause; the si clause is counted only as syntax.
  • STANDARDS 7 and 14 (timing basis, format): the box now opens with [examiner: "Hello?"] and timing runs from it.
  • STANDARDS 8 and 15 (lexis from the English, translator notes): added the Translator notes list; l'écart and puisque are notes, not counted on their own.
  • STANDARDS 9 (facts not on the ad): checked. "Everything is done remotely" restates the ad's printed steps (order online, send, receive). The other statements are about you.
  • STANDARDS 10 (uptake on "it depends"): checked; turn 2 rewords and repeats the examiner's noun "lenses".
  • STANDARDS 11 (one-sentence opener): applied. "I'm calling about your ad" is merged into the purpose sentence; the 2 words saved went to "of glasses", which names the product.
  • STANDARDS 12 and 19 (precise words): plus fins, outil (the examiner's word) and raisonnable dropped from the counted lists; six counted remain.
  • STANDARDS 13 (lines that echo the examiner): Working says to repeat their noun (tool, method, app, ruler), with frames for the optician and average replies.
  • STANDARDS 3, 4, 16, 17, 18: Section B only, not applicable.
  • Audit (STANDARDS 9, "mostly on the lenses" was an unhedged claim about how the shop prices): applied, now "So for me it depends mostly on the lenses", your inference from your own eyes (+2 words, 105, about 61 seconds); Words, Point, Why and Working updated.

A-09

TRANQUILLE AUTOMOBILE AT HOME

Services at home · At-home services · est. #53 of 78
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement for a car maintenance service and you are interested. You phone to get more information.

TRANQUILLE AUTOMOBILE AT HOME

For the maintenance of your car

Tranquille Automobile at home

Takes care of everything!

- Repair

- Spare parts

- Paint touch-ups

- Bodywork

- Annual maintenance

We lend you a car during the repairs!

Call us at 07 98 76 54 32

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité pour un service d’entretien de voitures et vous êtes intéressé(e). Vous téléphonez pour avoir plus d’informations.

TRANQUILLE AUTOMOBILE A DOMICILE

Pour l’entretien de votre voiture

Tranquille Automobile à domicile

S’occupe de tout !

- Réparation

- Pièces de rechange

- Retouches peintures

- Carrosserie

- Entretien annuel

Nous vous prêtons une voiture pendant les réparations !

Appelez-nous au 07 98 76 54 32

The point

The point falls at the end of your fourth turn, the question about being there or leaving the keys with the caretaker. Counted from the examiner's "Hello?", that is about 1:20 into Section A (1:15 to 1:30): 100 English words, about 58 seconds of your speech on the standard conversion, plus three short answers.

The box100 words

[examiner: "Hello?"]

Hello, my brakes are making a noise. I'm calling to find out whether your mechanic could work in my building's underground car park.

[examiner: "For the brakes, yes, that's no problem."]

So if I understand correctly, he can do the brakes on site. Since my car stays here, I won't even need the loan car your ad talks about. Regarding the price, how much should I expect?

[examiner: "It depends on the parts. He has to see the car."]

So he'd give me a quote first? On the other hand, if I turned it down, would I still pay his travel costs?

[examiner: "The quote is free, but travel costs thirty dollars."]

I see. Does he need me to be there, or can I leave the keys with the caretaker?

Why this, and why nothing less

By the end of your fourth turn every capacity on the Section A grid has been shown, the hard structures sit in your replies rather than your opener, and 100 English words (about 58 seconds of your speech on the standard conversion) are on the recording.

Task (obtaining information). Your opener states a real problem (noisy brakes) and a real constraint (an underground car park), so every later question is anchored in your situation, not in a prep list. Turn 2 is the C1 glimpse: you reword the examiner's yes and draw a consequence that only this ad allows. The ad says the service comes to you and also lends you a car during repairs. If the brakes are done in your car park, the car never leaves, so you won't need the loan car. A weaker speaker repeats the answer; you reason with it, and "since" and "even" make the reasoning audible. Turn 2 then asks the price, which the booklet says to answer partially. Turn 3 takes up that partial answer (so the price comes with a quote?) and steers to a contingency (if you refuse the quote, do you still pay the travel costs?). That is the B2 rung twice and the C1 rung once, in two different replies.

Syntax. Four marked structures (grammar forms that only stronger speakers use reliably), one of each: a conditional indirect question (turn 1), dont (turn 2), si + imperfect + conditional (turn 3), and an audible subjunctive, il a besoin que je sois (turn 4). The three hard ones sit in three different replies, so none depends on the rehearsable opener. Links of three relations: consequence (donc), cause (puisque), contrast (par contre). Register: vous, pourrait, paierais, en ce qui concerne.

Lexis. Six precise words that are not on the ad, each produced by the English itself: freins, parking souterrain, sur place, devis, frais de déplacement, concierge. You say devis before the examiner does. No clichés, nothing borrowed from English.

Delivery. Every sentence is under 18 English words and none holds a word a learner trips on. 58 seconds of clean speech is the floor at which the rater presumes control.

Why nothing can go. The box sits at the time floor (about 100 words), so a cut does not move the point earlier; it ends the box before the point. Piece by piece:

  • The greeting and "I'm calling to find out" set vous and the purpose; "your mechanic" identifies the service you are calling.
  • "My brakes are making a noise" anchors turns 2 and 3; without it the reword has nothing to reword and the price question is generic.
  • The car park question produces the answer the C1 consequence is built on.
  • In turn 2, the reword alone is the B1 rung and the consequence alone sounds prepared. "Since my car stays here" is the only cause link, "your ad talks about" is the dont, and the price question is the trigger for the partial answer.
  • In turn 3, the check question is the uptake on the partial answer and brings in devis; "on the other hand" is the only contrast link; the hypothetical is the steer and a hard structure.
  • Turn 4 is the third evidence moment and the only subjunctive; "I see" is the only sign that you heard the fee answer.

The next cut. Anything you take out now takes you under the time floor. The piece with the least evidence is the caretaker clause (9 words): without it you lose concierge and about 5 seconds, and the box ends before the point. Taking out turn 4 also costs the subjunctive and the third evidence moment, which leaves the syntax at three types.

What the box assumes. That the examiner's first answer is close to "yes, the brakes can be done there"; Working gives a line for each other answer, and each keeps the loan car consequence, the cause link and dont. That after the box you keep asking plain, correct questions tied to the answers until the examiner ends the call, handle the prepared surprise in French, and sound like a solid NCLC 6 on a quiet day. No box can lift a remainder at NCLC 5.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it counts as vocabulary evidence): maintenance of your car, at home (à domicile), takes care of everything (s'occupe de tout), repair, spare parts (pièces de rechange), paint touch-ups (retouches peinture), bodywork (carrosserie), annual maintenance (entretien annuel), "we lend you a car during the repairs" (nous vous prêtons une voiture), a phone number. So réparation, entretien, carrosserie, pièces, peinture, prêter, domicile, voiture prove nothing about your range.

What it leaves unsaid:

  • Price: labour, parts, whether there is a quote, whether the trip is charged (frais de déplacement).
  • Where the work is actually done. This is the ad's internal tension: the service comes to your home, yet it lends you a car "during the repairs". If the mechanic works at your place, why would you need a car? So either some jobs (bodywork, paint) go to a workshop, or "at home" means they collect the car.
  • Space and access: driveway, street, underground car park, building rules.
  • Area served, how soon they can come (slot, delay), how long a job takes.
  • Which cars (make, age, electric), original or generic parts, warranty.
  • The loan car's conditions: deposit, fuel, insurance and deductible, mileage, licence, availability.
  • Whether you must be present, keys, payment, cancellation, what the annual maintenance package includes.

Who is on the other end: a small mobile mechanic business, a receptionist or the owner. Formal vous.

The examiner's predictable moves:

  • Partial answer: price. For a car repair it is close to certain: "it depends on the parts, he has to see the car, he'll give you a quote".
  • Surprises they are likely to have ready (A): a travel fee or a limited area; "we can't work in underground car parks"; bodywork and paint only at the workshop; the loan car only for repairs over a day, or subject to availability, or against a deposit, fuel at your expense; fully booked for two weeks; no electric cars; a promotion on annual maintenance.
  • Relaunch statements if you run dry: "we also have an annual maintenance package", "there's a promotion this month".
  • Objections (B): not part of Section A; the examiner does not ask you questions.

Step 2. The hard part

Price is easy here: the partial answer is guaranteed and the follow-up can be prepared. The hard conditions are the only-here question and the C1 glimpse. Most questions for this ad would fit any garage (price, delay, warranty) or any at-home service (travel fee, do I need to be there). The one thing only this ad offers is the tension between "at home" and "we lend you a car during the repairs". That tension is where the C1 reword-and-consequence comes from. Asked straight in the opener ("why lend a car if you come to me?"), it is non-reactive, rehearsable and a little confrontational. It has to come out of an answer. Your own constraint, an underground car park, is what produces that answer: the examiner says the brakes can be done there, and you draw the consequence that the loan car becomes unnecessary.

Precise words this topic offers (none on the ad, everyday words left out): freins, plaquettes, disques, vidange, rayure, portière, devis, main-d'œuvre, frais de déplacement, sur place, atelier, parking souterrain, concierge, caution, franchise, kilométrage, garantie, pièces d'origine, créneau.

Step 3. Designs compared

On the standard time floor (STANDARDS item 5) every design needs about 100 English words before its point, so the comparison is about which design reaches all five conditions inside those 100 words and which holds when the examiner answers differently.

Design Idea Turns to point Weakness Verdict
A. Price first Opener + brakes + price; partial answer; quote hypothetical; then loan car question and a consequence 5 (about 120 words) The only-here move comes last and depends on the least predictable answer (loan car conditions); point later rejected
B. Self-description that creates the only-here answer Opener states the brakes and the underground car park; the examiner's yes becomes the raw material of the C1 consequence; price trigger in the same turn 4 (100 words) Depends on the first answer being yes (the step 6 lines keep the move in every branch) chosen
C. The ad's tension in the opener "You come to the home but also lend a car: which repairs are done at my place, which at your workshop?" 4 The inference is spent in a non-reactive, rehearsable turn; the reply that follows is only B2 rejected
D. Two probes in one turn "How much would brakes cost, and can he work in an underground car park?" The examiner answers one half; you bring back the other with "regarding the car park" 4 If the examiner answers both, your "regarding" line ignores what was said (D7); two questions at once sounds like a list rejected
E. The examiner's turn carries the words Ask what annual maintenance includes; pick up one item from the examiner's list 4 or 5 Words taken from the examiner are not your lexis (STANDARDS item 19); the list is unpredictable rejected
F. Pull the surprise into the box Ask the loan car's conditions early so the prepared surprise lands inside the box, where your reaction is ready 4 or 5 The gist is the least predictable on this ad (deposit? fuel? availability? long repairs only?), so the scripted reaction has four branches rejected for the box; first move of the reserve

B wins: all five conditions by turn 4 at the 100-word floor, the C1 glimpse sits in a reply and is only-here, and every examiner turn either has a predictable gist or is followed by a line at right angles to it. B also sets up F: saying you won't need the loan car invites the examiner to state its conditions, the most likely place for the prepared surprise, after the point.

Step 4. Structure note, sentence by sentence

The French is the target the English is written to produce, not part of the box. "Marked" = a grammar form that only stronger speakers use reliably. No sentence carries more than two marked structures or more than one hard one.

# English (box) Target French Structure Job
T1 S1 Hello, my brakes are making a noise. Bonjour, mes freins font du bruit. none the anchor for turns 2 and 3; freins
T1 S2 I'm calling to find out whether your mechanic could work in my building's underground car park. Je vous appelle pour savoir si votre mécanicien pourrait travailler dans le parking souterrain de mon immeuble. conditional + indirect question with si (low) purpose and vous in one sentence; your constraint; the question whose answer feeds the C1 move; parking souterrain
T2 S1 So if I understand correctly, he can do the brakes on site. Donc, si je comprends bien, il peut faire les freins sur place. reword check (low); donc = consequence link reaction to the yes; sur place
T2 S2 Since my car stays here, I won't even need the loan car your ad talks about. Puisque ma voiture reste ici, je n'aurai même pas besoin de la voiture de prêt dont parle votre annonce. puisque = cause link; future + ne... même pas; dont (med) the C1 consequence, only-here. "Talks about" forces dont; "mentions" would give que votre annonce mentionne.
T2 S3 Regarding the price, how much should I expect? En ce qui concerne le prix, il faut compter combien ? formal topic marker (register, not a link) the trigger for the scripted partial answer
T3 S1 So he'd give me a quote first? Donc il me ferait d'abord un devis ? uptake on the partial answer: donc + a reword put as a check you name devis before the examiner does; gives "it" in S2 something to point to
T3 S2 On the other hand, if I turned it down, would I still pay his travel costs? Par contre, si je le refusais, est-ce que je paierais quand même les frais de déplacement ? par contre = contrast link; si + imparfait + conditionnel (med); pronoun le (low) the steer and the contingency; frais de déplacement
T4 S1 I see. Je vois. none acknowledges the fee answer, whatever it was
T4 S2 Does he need me to be there, or can I leave the keys with the caretaker? Est-ce qu'il a besoin que je sois là, ou est-ce que je peux laisser les clés au concierge ? avoir besoin que + subjonctif (sois, audible, med) third evidence moment; concierge. Two subjects (he / me) force the que clause.

Translator notes (each one only chooses between natural renderings of the English):

  • "Since" → puisque (or comme); both are cause links.
  • "On the other hand" → par contre (or en revanche).
  • "how much should I expect" → il faut compter combien (or combien je dois prévoir).
  • "travel costs" → les frais de déplacement.
  • "the caretaker" → le concierge (or le gardien).
  • "your ad talks about" → dont parle votre annonce (or dont votre annonce parle).

Inventory at the point.

  • Marked structure types (four, one of each): conditional indirect question (T1), dont (T2), si + imperfect + conditional (T3), audible subjunctive que je sois (T4). The three hard ones are in three different replies.
  • Links (three relations, STANDARDS item 6): consequence (donc, T2 and T3), cause (puisque, T2), contrast (par contre, T3). The si clause is counted as syntax only, and en ce qui concerne as register only.
  • Precise words not on the ad, chosen by you (six): freins, parking souterrain, sur place, devis, frais de déplacement, concierge. Not counted: mécanicien, immeuble, clés (everyday), voiture de prêt (the ad prints prêtons).
  • Register: vous, pourrait, paierais, en ce qui concerne.

Substitution pass (same length or shorter, denser):

  • "I'm calling about Tranquille Automobile. ... I'd like to know whether" became "I'm calling to find out whether" (one sentence for purpose and vous, 5 words saved).
  • "In that case" became "Since my car stays here" (+2 words: a cause link, and the reasoning behind the C1 consequence said out loud).
  • "And if I turned it down" became "On the other hand, if I turned it down" (+3 words: the contrast link).
  • "Okay, so first he gives me a quote" became "So he'd give me a quote first?" (a check instead of a claim; devis still comes from you).
  • "pay for his trip" became "pay his travel costs" (same length; frais de déplacement, the precise term, and no risk of voyage).
  • "Do I need to be there while he works" became "Does he need me to be there" (two subjects force the audible subjunctive).

Step 5. Deletion test (rerun after review)

The box is at the time floor (100 words, 58 seconds), so a piece that carries nothing is swapped for evidence rather than deleted; above the floor it goes. Two pieces went in this pass: "Okay" (T3) and "while he works" (T4) carried no structure, link or precise word.

Piece removed What breaks Stays?
T1 S1 "Hello, my brakes are making a noise." The examiner's yes, the turn-2 reword and the price question lose their object; freins gone; under the floor stays
T1 S2 car park question Turn 2 has no answer to build on: no C1 glimpse, no only-here move (condition 2); the indirect question goes stays
clause "in my building's underground car park" "Could he work at my place?" is answered by the ad itself; parking souterrain gone; turn 4's caretaker comes from nowhere stays
T2 S1 reword The consequence floats with no audible base and sounds prepared; the C1 rung needs reword plus consequence stays
T2 S2 consequence No C1 glimpse, no only-here move, no cause link, no dont stays
clause "Since my car stays here" Links fall to two relations (condition 2 under item 6); the reasoning behind "won't even need" is no longer said stays
clause "your ad talks about" Dont goes; syntax falls to three types stays
T2 S3 price question No partial answer, so nothing to react to (condition 2, task) stays
T3 S1 "So he'd give me a quote first?" No audible uptake on the partial answer (item 10); after "it depends on the parts", "it" in S2 has nothing to point to; devis would then come from the examiner stays
"On the other hand" Links fall to two relations stays
T3 S2 hypothetical Turn 3 becomes a check only, the B1 rung; si + imperfect + conditional and frais de déplacement gone stays
T4 S1 "I see" The question jumps past the fee answer (condition 5 at the margin) and the box falls under the floor (98 words) stays
T4 S2 whole question No subjunctive (three types left), no third evidence moment (condition 3), 84 words stays
clause "or can I leave the keys with the caretaker" Concierge gone, 91 words, under the floor; the question can only be answered yes or no stays, the weakest keep
"Okay" (T3, before review) nothing; 1 word above the floor cut
"while he works" (T4, before review) nothing counted (a time clause is not on the link list); 3 words above the floor cut

Step 6. The reply test (three plausible answers per examiner turn)

E1, after the car park question (the least predictable turn in the box; the question invites a surprise). Frame for turn 2 (STANDARDS item 13): repeat what they said can or can't be done, then draw the loan car consequence from it. Never repeat a detail they didn't say.

Examiner says Your next line
Helpful: "Yes for the brakes; bodywork and paint go to our workshop." Box line as written. The consequence fits even better (the car only leaves for bodywork).
Partial: "It depends on the repair." "So it depends on the repair. For the brakes, since my car would stay here, I wouldn't even need the loan car your ad talks about? Regarding the price..."
Surprising: "No, we can't work in underground car parks." "Ah, I see. Since the car would go to your workshop, that's when I'd use the loan car your ad talks about? Regarding the price..." (a reaction to an unexpected condition, which is stronger evidence still)
Surprising: "We come and collect the car from your home." "Ah, so 'at home' means you collect the car. Since it leaves, that's when I'd use the loan car your ad talks about? Regarding the price..."

In every branch the loan car consequence, the cause link and dont survive, so the C1 glimpse and the only-here move do not depend on the examiner's yes.

E2, after the price question (the booklet's scripted partial answer). Frame for turn 3: "so" + their gist as a check, then "on the other hand" + the refusal hypothetical.

Examiner says Your next line
Partial (box): "It depends on the parts. He has to see the car." Box line.
Partial with the noun: "It depends. He'll give you a quote." "So the quote comes first? On the other hand, if I turned it down, would I still pay his travel costs?" (devis is then theirs, so the lexis count is five, still enough)
Helpful: "Between 150 and 300 dollars, depending on the discs." "So the exact price comes after he sees the car? On the other hand, if I turned the repair down, would I still pay his travel costs?"
Surprising: "Pads are 120, plus a 40 dollar travel fee." "So forty dollars just for him to come? On the other hand, if I turned the repair down, would I still pay them?"
Picks up the loan car instead: "Exactly, the loan car is only for workshop jobs." "That makes sense. Regarding the price, how much should I expect for the brakes?" then turn 3 as written

The hypothetical (refuse, still pay?) is at right angles to every price answer: no answer about price settles who pays for a visit you turn down.

E3, after the travel costs question. Turn 4 is at right angles: "I see" fits free, charged or deducted, and "does he need me to be there" is a dimension no fee answer settles.

Examiner says Your next line
Helpful: "The quote is free, but travel costs thirty dollars." Box line.
Partial: "That depends on the distance." "I see. I live downtown, by the way. Does he need me to be there, or can I leave the keys with the caretaker?"
Surprising: "No, it's all free if you sign up for annual maintenance." "I see, I'll think about it. Does he need me to be there, or can I leave the keys with the caretaker?" (the package goes on the reserve list)

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6.

  1. The opener is prepared; its structure (pour savoir si) is prep-book material and proves nothing.
  2. The loan car inference is the obvious question this ad raises; a coached candidate could have it ready, and "he can do the brakes on site" just repeats the examiner's yes.
  3. The travel costs question and the "be there" question fit any at-home service.
  4. Nothing unexpected has been handled yet; the grid's B2 says "even unexpected answers".
  5. Only four structure types: one slip and syntax falls to three.

How the box answers it.

  1. Agreed, and the box does not lean on the opener: the three hard structures (dont, si + imperfect + conditional, subjunctive) sit in three different replies.
  2. The consequence exists only after the examiner's answer and combines it with the document; "since my car stays here" and "even" say the reasoning out loud. In the other branches (step 6) the line changes and the consequence still arrives, so it is not a recited sentence.
  3. Turn 3 is built on the examiner's answer: it opens with "so" and their gist as a check, and le points to the quote. Turn 4 is right-angle and is not counted as a reaction; it is the third evidence moment and the subjunctive.
  4. The partial price answer is the booklet's own unexpected-answer probe, and E1 is an answer you do not control (yes, depends, no, collect: all handled). The prepared surprise proper comes after the point; turn 2's loan car remark tends to draw it there, and the reserve has the reaction ready.
  5. True at the margin. A fifth type would cost words above the floor for evidence the condition doesn't ask for. The protection is the practised repair (si je refuserais, pardon, si je refusais), which turns a slip into upper-B2 evidence, and the remainder will reuse the same structures.

Rerun of step 5 after this step: nothing added, so nothing new to test.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

Every French sentence is one or two breath groups with the pause at a clause boundary: after Bonjour, after pour savoir si, after si je comprends bien, after Puisque ma voiture reste ici, after Par contre, si je le refusais, after que je sois là. Words to practise once: souterrain, paierais (two syllables, said pè-rè), déplacement, concierge. The longest sentence (T2 S2, about 19 French words) splits naturally after ici.

Step 9. Count and timing (STANDARDS items 5 and 7)

  • Your words: T1 23, T2 36, T3 23, T4 18 = 100 English words.
  • Standard conversion: 100 × 1.05 = 105 French words at 120 a minute, plus 10% for pauses = 58 seconds of your speech (100 × 0.58).
  • The examiner: "Hello?" and three short answers, about 15 to 20 seconds, plus turn gaps of a few seconds.
  • The clock starts at the examiner's "Hello?". The point: end of turn 4, about 1:20 into Section A (1:15 to 1:30), leaving about 3:40 in which only a disaster counts.

Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

Plain, correct questions tied to the last answer. Reusing the box's structures is fine.

  1. Loan car conditions: "Regarding the loan car, is there a deposit? And who insures it?" (caution, assurance). If the surprise lands here (availability, only for long repairs), react to it: "And if no car were free that day, could we postpone?" (reporter).
  2. Slot: "When could he come? I work during the day. Would Saturday morning be possible?" (créneau)
  3. Duration: "How long does a brake job take, roughly?"
  4. Warranty: "Are the parts and the labour guaranteed, and for how long?" (garantie, main-d'œuvre)
  5. Paint: "While he's there, could he look at a scratch on my door?" (rayure, portière)
  6. Annual maintenance: "Is the annual maintenance a package? Would it include the brakes?" (forfait)
  7. Payment and cancellation: "Can I pay by card on site? And how much notice do you need if I cancel?" (prévenir à l'avance, la veille)
  8. Close: "Very good. Could you confirm the appointment by text message? Thank you, goodbye."

After review

Review A2, A-09:

  • Fix 1 (subjunctive not forced): applied. "Does he need me to be there" (two subjects) forces il a besoin que je sois. "While he works" then went in the deletion test (nothing counted, above the floor).
  • Cut 1 ("have been making a noise for two weeks"): applied. Ça fait... que is gone; four types remain, the three hard ones in replies.
  • Cut 2 ("roughly"): applied.
  • Suggestion 1 (check instead of claim after "it depends"): applied in the box itself: "So he'd give me a quote first?" With the examiner's line now "He has to see the car", you also name devis first.
  • Across the chunk, item 1 (one time floor): applied through STANDARDS item 5; the Point, Why and step 9 use 100 × 0.58 = 58 seconds.
  • Across the chunk, items 3 to 5 (forced structures, one structure list, one lexis list): applied; pendant que is no longer counted anywhere, voiture de prêt stays out, everyday words left the counts.

STANDARDS:

  • Item 1: the only subjunctive is now forced and audible (sois).
  • Item 5: the box is at the floor (100 words, 58 seconds). "Okay" and "while he works" were cut as dead words above it; nothing else can go without ending the box before the point.
  • Item 6: the old links did not qualify (quand même joins no clauses; pendant que and d'abord are not on the list), so the box had one relation. Applied: "Since my car stays here" (cause, replacing "In that case") and "On the other hand" (contrast). Three relations: consequence, cause, contrast.
  • Item 7: timing now starts at the examiner's "Hello?".
  • Item 8: translator notes added in step 4.
  • Item 10: turn 3 opens with "so" and a reword of their gist as a check.
  • Item 11: applied. The opener's purpose and vous are one sentence ("I'm calling to find out whether..."); the ad's name went, since "your mechanic" in a call the advertiser answers identifies the service, and the name carried no evidence.
  • Item 12 and 19: precise-word lists hold only freins, parking souterrain, sur place, devis, frais de déplacement, concierge; il faut compter, faire du bruit, immeuble left the counts.
  • Item 13: frames for turns 2 and 3 added at the head of the step 6 tables.
  • Item 14: the box opens with [examiner: "Hello?"].
  • Item 15: "pay for his trip" (risk of voyage) became "pay his travel costs"; "talks about" keeps forcing dont.
  • Items 2 and 9 checked: no indirect "what"; no unhedged fact the ad doesn't print ("since my car stays here" follows from the examiner's answer). Items 3, 4, 16, 17 and 18 are Section B only.
↑ top

Section B · convincing the friend

B-21

VOLUNTEERS FOR SENIORS

Volunteering & charity · Volunteering/solidarity · est. #5 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement for an association. You talk about it to a friend who is looking for an activity.

VOLUNTEERS FOR SENIORS

VOLUNTEERS WANTED

The association "Leisure for Seniors" is looking for volunteers of all ages to lead:

reading workshops

computer workshops

gardening activities

card games, chess, scrabble...

Give a little of your time, give joy and join us.

Contact us at 0556768840

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité pour une association. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) qui cherche une activité.

BÉNÉVOLES POUR SENIORS

RECHERCHE BÉNÉVOLES

L'association « Loisirs pour les Seniors » recherche des bénévoles de tout âge pour animer :

des ateliers de lecture

des ateliers d'informatique

des activités de jardinage

des jeux de carte, d'échec, de scrabble...

Donnez un peu de votre temps, donnez de la joie et rejoignez-nous.

Contactez-nous au 0556768840

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, your reply to the friend's second objection (the line ending "you'd see for yourself"). On the standard conversion that is about 74 seconds of your speech (128 English words x 0.58) plus about 12 seconds of examiner turns, so about 1 minute 25 seconds into Section B, counted from the examiner's "Hi! How are you?".

The box128 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Fine! Listen, you've been looking for an activity for weeks, and I've found you one. An association for retirees, Leisure for Seniors, needs volunteers to run their activities. Some are quiet, like reading or chess, others more practical, like gardening or computers. With computers, you could just show them how to video call their grandchildren. For someone isolated, that's huge. I know you: you're going to tell me you don't have time.

[examiner: "Well, it's true! I work all week, I'm exhausted."]

I get that you're swamped. But that's exactly why it would take your mind off things. Besides, they say it themselves: a little of your time, not all your weekends.

[examiner: "Maybe, but I wouldn't know what to say to old people."]

Even if you don't know what to say, by playing cards you end up chatting. If you came with me just once, you'd see for yourself.

Why this, and why nothing less

This ad has one weak spot printed on it: "give a little of your time". A friend told about volunteering says "I don't have time" first. The box spends twelve words naming that objection, which turns the examiner's first move into one you have prepared for, and then uses the ad's own words against it. The ad also has a strength: four different activities, and the games among them answer the "I wouldn't know what to talk about" worry. The box is built on those two facts.

Task (present and debate). The presentation is tied to the friend (you've been looking, I found you one), gives the ad's facts in your own words, and regroups the four activities into two kinds (quiet, practical) instead of reading the list. That regrouping is the structure a C1 presentation has, and it is your one C1 glimpse on the task (one is enough). The first argument is illustrated and only fits this ad: video calls with grandchildren are what a computer workshop for retirees is really for, and "someone isolated" names the stakes. Then two objection replies, each opening on the friend's own complaint. Reply 1 rewords it ("swamped"), turns it around into a different kind of argument (good for you, after turn 1's good for them) and quotes the ad back ("a little of your time, not all your weekends"), which no prepared template can do. Reply 2 concedes, counters with something only this ad offers (the cards do the talking), and pushes on with a proposal.

Syntax. Seven different marked structures (grammar forms only stronger speakers use reliably), one of each: ça fait des semaines que + present, je t'en ai trouvé une, conditional + indirect question (tu pourrais leur montrer comment), an audible subjunctive (que tu sois), a cleft (c'est justement pour ça que), a gérondif (en jouant) and si tu venais..., tu verrais. The two hard ones are in replies, each alone in its sentence. Links of three kinds: consequence (c'est pour ça que), addition (en plus), concession (même si).

Lexis. Six precise words you choose and the ad doesn't print: retirees, grandchildren, video call, isolated, swamped, take your mind off things. Informal markers: listen, I know you, that's huge.

Delivery. Every sentence is under 18 English words, said at your normal pace, in one or two breath groups. Practise two chunks in turn 1 as fixed pieces (je t'en ai trouvé une, quelqu'un d'isolé): a slip in the opener is the first thing both raters hear.

Why nothing can go. Cut the opening link and the presentation is no longer about the friend. Cut the regrouping and nothing is left to present but the association's name, and the C1 glimpse goes with it. Cut the video call line and there is no illustrated argument; cut "that's huge" and it becomes a description, not a claim. Cut "I know you..." and the first objection is a lottery, so your reply can't be prepared and still fit. In reply 1, the first sentence rewords the friend and carries the subjunctive, the second carries the cleft and the new argument, the third is the concrete counter; without the ad quote the reply is "but it'd be nice" (B1). In reply 2, the cards line answers what the friend said and carries the concession and the gérondif; the si line is the second hard structure and the proposal that keeps your plan alive when pushed. Without it the turn is 15 words.

The next cut. The cheapest pieces left are "and I've found you one" (5 words) and the ad quote (14 words). The first costs the link from the friend's need to the ad, so the presentation stops being about the friend. The second leaves reply 1 with a stock argument and no concrete counter, below the B2 rung. Either one breaks a condition of the point, so both stay.

What the box assumes. After the point you keep answering what the friend says in plain correct French for about eight more minutes, with two or three further arguments (see the reserve). You don't need to win: the grid rewards defending, not convincing.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (carries no vocabulary evidence): volunteers (bénévoles), seniors, association "Leisure for Seniors" (Loisirs pour les Seniors), of all ages (de tout âge), to lead (animer), reading workshops, computer workshops, gardening activities, card games, chess, scrabble, give a little of your time, give joy, join us, contact us, a phone number.

What it leaves unsaid: where (a centre, a retirement home, people's homes?), how often and how long, which days, whether you choose your activity, whether there is any training, whether you need a skill (can you "lead" a computer workshop if you are no expert?), whether you can try once before committing, any background check, transport. It is unpaid by definition. These gaps are where the friend's objections come from, and where you must not invent facts (P18): you can hedge or quote the ad, not state a schedule.

Who is on the other end: a friend who "is looking for an activity". That one fact matters: the friend wants something for themselves (a hobby, a way to fill free time), and the ad offers unpaid work for other people. The gap between those two is the whole debate.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad:

  • Opens with "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?" and lets you present. May interrupt the presentation with an information question ("where is it?", "is it every week?").
  • Objections from the ad's own content: time ("a little of your time" is printed, so "I don't have time" is close to certain); competence ("lead a workshop? I'm not a teacher / I'm no computer expert"); commitment ("once I sign up I'll have to go every week").
  • Objections from stereotypes: old people are boring or sad, retirement homes are depressing; "I wouldn't know what to say to them"; "I'm not patient"; volunteering is working for free; "I wanted an activity for me, with people my age".
  • Pushback on a thin answer: "Yes, but still, Saturday is my only day off", "Is that your only argument?".

Step 2. The hard part

Hardest condition: credibility and the C1 glimpse on the task (conditions 2 and 5). This ad is a bare list. The natural presentation is to read the four activities out, which is the A1 / A2 habit the grid names. And the natural arguments ("it's good to help", "they're alone", "it makes you feel useful") are the ones every candidate brings to every volunteering prompt, so they read as prep-list content and buy nothing above B2. The only-here material has to come from the specific activities, not from volunteering in general.

Where the only-here argument comes from:

  • Computer workshops for retirees: the real use is calling grandchildren by video (also booking a doctor's appointment online, online banking). That is a concrete illustration nobody can give for a canoe trip.
  • Card games, chess, scrabble: the game does the talking, so "I wouldn't know what to say to them" has an answer that only this ad allows.
  • Chess and gardening: retirees are often better at these than a younger friend, which turns the stereotype (old people need entertaining) upside down: they will teach you.
  • Four activities: any "I'm not good at X" has the counter "then take Y".
  • The printed slogan "a little of your time": the ad itself answers the time objection, so you can quote it without inventing a fact.

Precise words this topic offers (not on the ad): retraités, isolé(e), petits-enfants, appel vidéo / appeler en vidéo, prendre rendez-vous en ligne, débordé(e), changer les idées, potager, mettre les mains dans la terre, une partie de cartes, battre quelqu'un aux échecs, donner des leçons, s'engager, un créneau, à l'essai, par toi-même.

Step 3. Designs compared

Design 1, conventional (rejected). Present, give two prepared arguments (useful to others; good for you), hand over with "What do you think?", then answer whatever comes. About 165 words. Weak on two counts: the two arguments sit in the presentation, the most rehearsable part, so they are the evidence a rater discounts first (F6); and the first objection is unknown, so the first reply can only be a generic frame. More words, less certainty.

Design 2, name the weak spot (chosen). Present, give one only-here illustrated argument, then hand over by naming the time objection ("I know you: you're going to tell me you don't have time"). The ad prints "a little of your time", so this is the objection the examiner is most likely to raise anyway; naming it costs 12 words and makes reply 1 preparable while it still reacts (it rewords "I work all week" as "swamped" and quotes the ad back). Reply 2 is written for the most likely stereotype objection and built from a frame (concession on the friend's words + game-based counter + universal si proposal) that survives the other likely objections. 140 words as first written, 128 after review.

Design 3, the menu handover (rejected as the main line, kept as a fallback). End the presentation with "Which one would tempt you?". The four activities mean any refusal ("I'm no good at computers") has a ready counter ("then the garden"). Very sturdy against "I can't do X" objections, but it invites an information-style turn ("none of them" or "where is it?") that delays the first real objection, so the point moves later, and "none, it's not for me" gives you nothing specific to echo.

Design 4, self-description first (rejected). Open with "I've signed up for the computer workshop, come and do the garden" so that every objection gets "but you'd be with me". Strong on persuasion, but the examiner can then ask "so what did they tell you? when is it?" and you must either invent the association's schedule (P18) or admit you don't know, which undercuts the presentation. The "with me" idea survives in the last line of the box at no cost.

Design 5, two probes at once (rejected). Hand over with "I know you: no time, and old people aren't your thing, right?". Makes both objections predictable, so both replies are prepared. Costs about 6 more words, and the examiner may bundle both into one turn (forcing one long reply that monologues) or pick a third from the confidential list anyway. It also takes the friend's role away from the examiner, which can sound staged (D7). Naming one objection, the one the ad itself invites, gets most of the gain with less of the risk.

Winner: Design 2. Fewest words, the first objection is close to certain, and every reply is anchored in something only this ad has (the slogan, the games).

Step 4. Structure note for each sentence

Turn 1 (presentation, argument, hand-over)

  • T1.1 "Fine! Listen, you've been looking for an activity for weeks, and I've found you one." Oui, ça va ! Écoute, ça fait des semaines que tu cherches une activité, et je t'en ai trouvé une. Ça fait... que + present (an English speaker's trap: not j'ai cherché; tu cherches depuis des semaines is the same structure), and en with a number plus two object pronouns in order (t'en). Two marked, none hard. Ties the ad to the friend's stated need (the prompt says the friend is looking for an activity).
  • T1.2 "An association for retirees, Leisure for Seniors, needs volunteers to run their activities." Une association pour retraités, « Loisirs pour les Seniors », cherche des bénévoles pour animer leurs activités. Plain; identifies the ad. Retraités is not printed (the ad says seniors).
  • T1.3 "Some are quiet, like reading or chess, others more practical, like gardening or computers." Certaines sont calmes, comme la lecture ou les échecs, d'autres plus pratiques, comme le jardinage ou l'informatique. Pronoun contrast certaines... d'autres. The regrouping that makes the presentation structured instead of read: the box's one C1 glimpse on the task.
  • T1.4 "With computers, you could just show them how to video call their grandchildren." En informatique, tu pourrais juste leur montrer comment appeler leurs petits-enfants en vidéo. Conditional + indirect question with comment, leur before the infinitive. Two marked, none hard. "Could", not "would have to": an example, not a claim about what the association requires (P18).
  • T1.5 "For someone isolated, that's huge." Pour quelqu'un d'isolé, c'est énorme. Quelqu'un de + adjective (learners drop the de), informal c'est énorme. Turns the example into a claim.
  • T1.6 "I know you: you're going to tell me you don't have time." Je te connais, tu vas me dire que t'as pas le temps. Near future + reported speech; informal dropped ne. Names the weak spot and is the hand-over (no question after it).

Turn 2 (reply 1)

  • T2.1 "I get that you're swamped." Je comprends que tu sois débordé(e). Audible subjunctive (sois, hard), alone in its sentence. Rewords the friend's "I work all week, I'm exhausted"; débordé / débordée sound the same, so the friend's gender costs nothing.
  • T2.2 "But that's exactly why it would take your mind off things." Mais c'est justement pour ça que ça te changerait les idées. Cleft c'est... pour ça que (consequence link) + conditional. Two marked, none hard. The turn-around and a new kind of argument (good for you).
  • T2.3 "Besides, they say it themselves: a little of your time, not all your weekends." En plus, ils le disent eux-mêmes : un peu de ton temps, pas tous tes week-ends. Neuter le, eux-mêmes, addition link en plus. Quoting the ad shows you understood it, and it is the concrete counter without inventing a schedule ("not all your weekends" glosses "a little"; it states no fact).

Turn 3 (reply 2)

  • T3.1 "Even if you don't know what to say, by playing cards you end up chatting." Même si tu sais pas quoi dire, en jouant aux cartes, tu finis par discuter. Concession même si + indicative, gérondif (same subject as the main verb, so no dangling gérondif), finir par + infinitive. Two marked, none hard. Opens on the friend's own words.
  • T3.2 "If you came with me just once, you'd see for yourself." Si tu venais avec moi juste une fois, tu verrais par toi-même. Si + imperfect + conditional (hard, alone in its sentence). A proposal that fits after any objection.

Coverage audit.

  • Frame: tu throughout, one-word greeting, ad named, presented in your own words (T1.1 to T1.3).
  • Task: detailed, structured presentation (T1.1 to T1.3); illustrated, only-here argument (T1.4 to T1.5); two objection replies at the B2 rung, each opening on the friend's words (T2, T3); C1 glimpse: the regrouped presentation (T1.3). Arguments of three kinds: useful to someone isolated (turn 1), good for you (T2.2), the game does the talking (T3.1).
  • Syntax: 7 types (ça fait que, en, conditional + indirect question, audible subjunctive, cleft, gérondif, si hypothesis), both hard ones in replies and each alone in its sentence, at most two marked per sentence. Links, three relations: consequence (c'est pour ça que), addition (en plus), concession (même si). The si clause is counted as syntax only.
  • Lexis, six precise words you choose and the ad doesn't print: retraités, petits-enfants, appeler en vidéo, isolé, débordé, changer les idées. Informal: écoute, je te connais, c'est énorme, t'as pas.
  • Only-here: T1.4 (video calls with grandchildren), T2.3 (the slogan quoted back), T3.1 (cards carry the talk).
  • Replication: evidence in three turns, two of them replies.
  • Time: 128 English words x 0.58 = about 74 seconds of your speech, above the 58-second floor, so dead words go rather than get swapped.

Translator notes (each only chooses between natural renderings of the English)

  • "you've been looking for weeks": ça fait des semaines que tu cherches (or tu cherches depuis des semaines); never a past tense.
  • "I've found you one": je t'en ai trouvé une.
  • "For someone isolated": pour quelqu'un d'isolé (rather than pour une personne isolée).
  • "swamped": débordé(e).
  • "that's exactly why": c'est justement pour ça que.
  • "take your mind off things": changer les idées.
  • "you end up chatting": tu finis par discuter.

Step 5. Deletion test (rerun after review)

Piece Conditions still all hold without it? What breaks
T1.1 "Fine!" No the greeting goes unanswered; one word is the standard
T1.1 "you've been looking for an activity for weeks" No presentation no longer tied to the friend (P17b); loses ça fait que
T1.1 "and I've found you one" No the jump from the friend's need to the ad has no link, so the presentation loses its "why I'm telling you"; loses en
T1.2 No the ad is never identified (condition 1)
T1.3 No nothing of the ad's content is presented; the C1 glimpse goes
T1.4 No no illustrated argument (condition 2), no only-here argument
T1.5 No T1.4 becomes a description; a rater hears no claim
T1.6 No the first objection is unknown, so T2 can no longer be prepared and still fit (condition 5 at risk)
T2.1 (changed: now its own sentence) No reply 1 no longer opens on the friend's words; loses the only subjunctive
T2.2 (changed: now carries the cleft) No loses the consequence link, the cleft and the turn-around; T2.3 alone is a quote with no claim attached
T2.3 No reply 1 drops to "but it'd take your mind off things", a stock argument with no concrete counter (B1: "but it's nice!")
T3.1 (changed: "end up chatting") No reply 2 no longer answers what the friend said (D6); loses the concession and the gérondif, and the concession is the only one in the box
T3.2 No loses the second hard structure and the proposal that keeps the plan alive when pushed (judge rule 3: concede, counter, keep the plan alive); reply 2 would be 15 words
Cut by review: the chess line ("And honestly, at chess, they'll be the ones giving you lessons") Yes nothing: the C1 glimpse is the regrouped presentation, the cleft now sits in T2.2, informal register is in turn 1; moved to the reserve
Cut earlier: "young or not so young" in T1.2 Yes nothing; the age fact can come back after the point
Cut earlier: a gardening illustration in T2 Yes nothing the box needs; in the reserve
Clause test: "just" in T1.4, "just once" in T3.2 one word each "just" in T1.4 keeps the example modest (no invented requirement); "just once" makes the proposal small enough to accept, which is its point

Step 6. Reply test (three plausible examiner replies per turn)

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
E1 greeting "Hi! How are you?" "Hey!" "Hi! I'm so bored, I still haven't found anything to do." T1 works after all three; after the third, start "Fine! Listen, that's exactly it: ..." and go on from "you've been looking".
E2 after "I know you..." "Well, it's true! I work all week, I'm exhausted." (box) "Hmm... maybe." "No, I have time. It's old people, that's not my thing." Helpful and partial: T2 as written ("swamped" fits any version of busy). Surprising: skip to the T3 frame now ("Even if old people aren't your thing, by playing cards you end up chatting"), keep T2 for when time comes up, which it will.
E2, information question instead "Where is it? Is it every week?" "It doesn't say, but there's a number, I'll call them. And I know you: you're going to tell me you don't have time." (no invented schedule)
E3 second objection "Maybe, but I wouldn't know what to say to old people." (box) "Still... I don't know." "I'm not a teacher, I've never led a workshop." / "It's not even paid." / "Retirement homes are depressing." T3.1 keeps its frame and swaps in the friend's words after "even if": "Even if you've never led a workshop, by playing cards with them, you end up leading it." / "Even if it's not paid, you're looking for an activity, not a job." / "Even if it can be sad sometimes, by playing cards you end up laughing." T3.2 fits after all of them.
E3, the same objection again "Yes, but Saturday is my only day off." "Even if it's your only day off, an hour of cards isn't a job." Then T3.2.

Frames for lines that depend on what the friend chose (standard 13). T2.1: put the friend's complaint into je comprends que tu sois + adjective (débordé, crevé, occupé). T3.1: "even if" + the friend's own words, then "by playing cards you end up" + a verb that answers them. T3.2 is at right angles to every objection (P13): it proposes a trial, which settles no specific worry and so fits after any of them.

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6: "The response is short and tidy. The presentation is a paraphrased list. 'I know you, you'll tell me you don't have time' is a persuasion trick from a prep book, and so are 'I understand that..., but...' and 'if you came with me you'd see'. The arguments are one sentence each. Eighty seconds isn't enough to judge control. This is a well-prepared B2 candidate, lower box."

How the box answers it:

  • Templates attached to unpredictable content. Each reply opens on words that only fit what the friend just said: "swamped" rewords "I work all week"; "even if you don't know what to say" echoes the second objection.
  • The ad used against the objection. T2.3 quotes the ad's own slogan back at the friend. A generic rehearsed line can't do that, and it shows the document was understood, not just read.
  • Three kinds of argument in about 75 seconds: useful to someone isolated (turn 1), good for you (T2.2), the game does the talking (T3.1). With the regrouped presentation, that covers both C1 routes P7 names; one is required.
  • The regrouping (quiet / practical) is reorganisation, which B2 "clear, detailed" doesn't require and C1 "structured" does.
  • Lexis is precise rather than rare on purpose (P15): débordé, changer les idées, quelqu'un d'isolé, appeler en vidéo are the choices a 6 tends to replace with occupé, c'est bien, seul.
  • Control is not claimed by the box (P2); its 74 seconds start it, and the remaining eight minutes can only fail to disprove it.

Fix made after this pass (first version): "For them, that's huge" became "For someone isolated, that's huge": it makes the social argument explicit and adds a precise word. Deletion test rerun: no piece became removable.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

Longest French sentences: T1.1 (about 19 French words), T3.1 (about 15), T2.3 (about 15). Each breaks at a comma or colon into two breath groups. No sentence needs a word a B2 learner may not have (débordé is the least common; très occupé is the fallback). The two hard structures are alone in their sentences. Watch points: je t'en ai trouvé une (pronoun order; practise it as one chunk), quelqu'un d'isolé (keep the d'), sois not es, venais / verrais not viendrais. A quick self-repair on any of these ("si tu viendrais... pardon, si tu venais") is evidence for upper B2, not against it. T3.1 no longer needs t'en rendre compte, a reflexive learners often get wrong with tu.

Step 9. Count and timing (standard conversion)

  • Your words in the box: 128 English, so about 134 French words (x 1.05).
  • Seconds of your speech: 128 x 0.58 = about 74 seconds (120 French words a minute plus 10 percent for pauses). Above the 58-second floor.
  • Examiner: greeting about 2 seconds, two objections about 4 to 5 seconds each: about 12 seconds.
  • The point: end of your third turn, about 1 minute 25 seconds after the examiner's "Hi! How are you?". It is earlier than P17's general estimate (2:00 to 3:30) because the hand-over names the first objection, so no time goes on information questions. If the examiner interrupts the presentation with a question, add 15 to 20 seconds.

Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

  • Reciprocity (cut from the box by review): "And honestly, at chess, they'll be the ones giving you lessons" (c'est eux qui vont te donner des leçons).
  • Logistics without invented facts: "It doesn't say where or when; I'll call the number this week and ask whether you can try once."
  • Hedge, for when the friend pushes on commitment: "I'd be surprised if it were every day" (Ça m'étonnerait que ce soit tous les jours; soit is audible).
  • Gardening as rest: "Getting your hands in the soil after a week at work is real rest" (Mettre les mains dans la terre...).
  • Meeting people: the ad says volunteers of all ages, so you meet other volunteers too, not only retirees.
  • CV: "It looks good on a CV, especially if you lead a computer workshop" (ça fait bien sur un CV).
  • Choice: "If computers aren't your thing, take reading: you love books."
  • "It's not paid": "You're looking for an activity, not a job; and you pay for the gym."
  • "It's depressing": "They're not patients, it's called Leisure for Seniors: they want to do things."
  • Structures kept back: dont ("That's exactly the kind of help they need": c'est exactement le genre d'aide dont ils ont besoin); "How about we go together on Saturday?" (Et si on y allait ensemble samedi ?).
  • Compromise near the end: "Try one session; if you don't like it, you stop, and I'll stop asking."

After review

  • Review fix 1 (three marked structures in one sentence): applied. T2.1 is now split in two: "I get that you're swamped." (subjunctive alone) and "But that's exactly why it would take your mind off things." (cleft + conditional, c'est justement pour ça que). Same words; the English and its French now agree, and the cleft is counted.
  • Review fix 2 (next-cut paragraph and padded lexis): applied. "The next cut" now names the pieces actually left; the lexis list is the six words retirees, grandchildren, video call, isolated, swamped, take your mind off things.
  • Review cut 1 (the chess line, 11 words): applied. Standard 16 and P7 accept the regrouped presentation as the one C1 glimpse; the cleft moved to T2.2; the line is in the reserve.
  • Suggestion 1 (t'en rendre compte): applied as "by playing cards you end up chatting" (tu finis par discuter): no reflexive with tu, one word shorter, still a gérondif.
  • Suggestion 2 ("take your mind off things" is stock): applied. Counted as a precise word, not as only-here; the only-here list is T1.4, T2.3, T3.1.
  • Suggestion 3 (practise the opener's chunks): applied, in Why (Delivery) and Step 8.
  • Across the chunk 5 (lexis counts) and 7 (timing): applied; timing restated on the standard conversion.
  • Across the chunk 6 (unprinted facts): checked. "Could" keeps T1.4 an example; "not all your weekends" glosses the printed "a little of your time"; "for weeks" is about the friend, not the ad. Nothing changed.
  • Standard 1 (audible subjunctive): checked. "I get that you're swamped" gives que tu sois, audible.
  • Standard 2 (indirect "what"): rebutted for T3.1. "You don't know what to say" gives tu sais pas quoi dire, an infinitive chunk with no finite clause, so qu'est-ce que has no slot; and it is the friend's own words, which standard 17 asks the reply to open on.
  • Standard 3 (hand-over): checked. T1.6 names the weak spot and stops; no question after it.
  • Standard 4 (concession and grants): checked. "Even if" in T3.1 is the concession; the grant in T2.1 stays because it carries the subjunctive and the reword of the friend.
  • Standard 5 (time floor): checked. 128 x 0.58 = about 74 seconds, above the floor, so the dead words were cut, not swapped.
  • Standard 6 (links): applied. Links recounted as consequence (c'est pour ça que), addition (en plus), concession (même si); si and justement on its own are no longer listed as links.
  • Standard 7 (one timing basis): applied. The point is timed from "Hi! How are you?".
  • Standard 8 (translator notes): applied. A "Translator notes" list is in Step 4; each note chooses between natural renderings.
  • Standard 9 (facts not on the ad): checked, as above.
  • Standards 12 and 19 (precise words): applied. Weekends, give you lessons and see for yourself are no longer counted.
  • Standard 13 (lines that depend on the examiner): applied. Frames for T2.1 and T3.1 are in Step 6.
  • Standard 14 (format): checked. The box opens with [examiner: "Hi! How are you?"].
