  • Standard 15 (the English makes its own French): applied for T2.2 (fix 1) and T3.1 (suggestion 1); the other lines checked, and "you've been looking for weeks" gives a present with ça fait... que or depuis either way.
  • Standard 16 (one C1 glimpse): applied through the chess cut.
  • Standard 17 (replies open on the friend's words): checked. T2.1 rewords "I work all week"; T3.1 echoes "I wouldn't know what to say".
  • Standard 18 (one-word greeting): checked. "Fine!".
  • Standards 10 and 11: Section A only, not applicable.
  • Final audit (T3.2 turn length): applied. Without T3.2 reply 2 is 15 words, not 14; Why and the Step 5 row now say 15 and no longer claim it falls below P14's range. T3.2 stays for the second hard structure and the proposal.
↑ top
B-03

CANOE-CAMPING TRIP

Travel & stays · Travel/getaway · est. #1 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this travel agency advertisement. You are talking about it to a friend with whom you want to go on vacation.

CANOE-CAMPING TRIP

THE TRAVEL AGENCY VACANCES NATURE IS ORGANIZING A WEEK OF « CANOE-CAMPING »

Paddle by day and sleep comfortably in a tent at night.

Experience moments of calm, but also of challenge and adventure! Discover the most beautiful landscapes of the region and fill up on fresh air!

All reservations possible at: www.vacancesnature.ca

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité d’agence de voyage. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) avec qui vous voulez partir en vacances.

SÉJOUR CANOT CAMPING

L'AGENCE DE VOYAGE VACANCES NATURE ORGANISE UNE SEMAINE EN « CANOT-CAMPING »

Pagayez de jour et dormez confortablement dans une tente le soir.

Vivez des instants de calme, mais aussi de dépassement et d'aventure ! Découvrez les plus beaux paysages de la région et faites le plein d'air pur !

Toutes les réservations possibles sur : www.vacancesnature.ca

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, your second objection reply ("I'd row for both of us"). That is about 1:30 after the examiner's "Hi! How are you?": about 74 seconds of your speech on the standard conversion, plus about 15 seconds of examiner turns and changeovers.

The box128 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Great! I've found our vacation. It's a week of canoe-camping organized by an agency, Vacances Nature. We paddle during the day, and in the evening we probably pitch the tent on the shore. What I like is that it suits both of us: you want rest; me, I need to move. You're going to tell me that a tent isn't your thing.

[examiner: "Ha! Comfortably, in a tent? I really doubt it."]

I'm not surprised you're skeptical. But with a good inflatable mattress and a warm sleeping bag, you sleep well. Besides, after a whole day in the canoe, you'll fall asleep in two minutes.

[examiner: "A whole day in a canoe? I'm not sporty, I'll be exhausted!"]

Not sporty? That's exactly why it's perfect: unlike a hike, it's the canoe that carries the bags, not your back. And even if you were exhausted, I'd row for both of us.

Why this, and why nothing less

Three turns are enough because you choose both objections yourself, so both replies can be prepared and still react to what the friend said.

How it works. Your first turn presents the ad in your own words, ties it to the two of you, and ends by naming the objection the ad itself invites (it promises you'll sleep "comfortably" in a tent): a tent isn't your friend's thing. The examiner is told to leave you the initiative and to disagree, so they will almost always take it. Your reply ends with a line that invites the next one ("after a whole day in the canoe, you'll fall asleep in two minutes"), and the natural comeback is "I'll be exhausted". Your second reply opens on the friend's words, gives the one argument only a canoe trip allows, and reuses their word in a hypothetical.

Criterion by criterion.

  • Present and debate: a presentation in your own words with a clear order (what it is, how a day goes, why it suits the two of you, what you expect to hear back), then two objection replies, each opening on the friend's words and each with a concrete counter, the second pushed further. Arguments of four kinds (it suits both of us, gear, a comparison only a canoe allows, a commitment) give the one C1 feature this criterion needs, without extra words.
  • Syntax: five different types of marked structure (grammar forms that only stronger speakers use reliably): a participle used as a passive (organisée par), clefts (ce qui me plaît, c'est que; c'est le canot qui, counted once), a stressed pronoun put up front (moi, j'ai besoin de bouger), an audible subjunctive (ça m'étonne pas que tu sois sceptique), and même si + imperfect + conditional. The two hard ones sit in your two replies. Links of three kinds, counted the strict way: en plus (addition), c'est justement pour ça que (consequence), même si (concession).
  • Lexis: six precise words you choose that the ad doesn't print (planter la tente, la rive, matelas gonflable, sac de couchage, randonnée, ramer), plus sceptique and the informal c'est pas ton truc. Épuisé or crevé doesn't count: the friend says it first.
  • Delivery: no sentence over 18 English words, each breaks at its commas, and every verb form is easy (je ramerais, not je pagaierais).

Replication: three turns carry evidence, two of them replies to the friend. Time: 128 English words is about 74 seconds of your speech, above the 60-second floor.

Why nothing can go. "Great" answers the greeting; skipping it sounds recited. "I've found our vacation" ties the ad to your shared plan. The agency sentence identifies the ad. The day-and-evening sentence makes the presentation detailed, and "probably" hedges the one unprinted fact in it. The "what I like" sentence is the cleft, the link to the two of you and your first argument at once. The tent line makes the first objection predictable. In reply one, the opener grants the point and carries the subjunctive, the gear sentence is the concrete counter, and the canoe-day line adds a second reason, the addition link and the call for the second objection. In reply two, "not sporty?" makes it audibly a reply, "that's exactly why" is the consequence link, the hike comparison is the only-here argument, and the "even if" sentence carries the hard structure, the concession and the determination the grid asks for.

The next cut. The cheapest piece to lose is the canoe-day line (14 words). Without it the second objection is no longer called (four prepared second replies instead of one) and the addition link goes. Cutting the tent line does the same to the first objection. Any other cut removes a condition.

What the box assumes. That the examiner takes the tent (if not, the fallbacks in Working keep the same frames: a grant with an audible subjunctive, then "even if" + imperfect + conditional). That after the point you keep answering in plain, correct French for eight minutes, with fresh arguments in reserve (price, switching off, landscapes, booking) and never the same argument three times. And that you say the two hard structures cleanly; a quick self-repair is fine.

The working for this prompt

1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it counts as vocabulary evidence). The agency Vacances Nature; a week; canoe-camping (canot-camping); paddle by day (pagayez de jour); sleep comfortably in a tent in the evening (dormez confortablement dans une tente le soir); calm, but also challenge and adventure (calme, dépassement, aventure); the most beautiful landscapes of the region (paysages, région); fresh air (faites le plein d'air pur); book on the website. Every content word on the ad is topic vocabulary, so the topic's obvious words are already used up: pagayer, tente, dormir, calme, aventure, paysages, air pur, canot. Your precise words have to come from what the ad leaves out.

What it leaves unsaid. Price and what it includes (canoes, tents, sleeping gear, food); guided or not; level needed; dates; which region; lakes or river; distance per day; portages (carrying the canoe between lakes); showers and toilets; phone network; safety (life jackets, swimming); weather; insects; group size; cancellation. Inventing any of these is a risk (P18, standard 9): the examiner knows the ad. The box states one of them, where and how you camp ("we probably pitch the tent on the shore"), and hedges it with "probably". Gear is offered as something you bring ("with a good mattress"), not as something the agency provides. "I'd row for both of us" is a hypothetical, not a claim about the trip. If the friend asks "we have to pitch it ourselves?": J'imagine que oui, c'est du canot-camping. Et à deux, ça prend dix minutes. Nothing new is invented.

Who is on the other end. A friend you already want to go on vacation with (the prompt says so). That gives the hook for free: "I've found our vacation" ties the ad to an open plan the two of you share, which is what "present the document to a friend" asks for. Register: tu, on, informal markers.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad.

  • Opening: "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?" (booklet). Then lets you present.

  • The Section B analogue of the partial answer is the first objection. The examiner holds a confidential list for this subject plus stereotypes, and is told to leave you the initiative, push thin arguments, ask for new ones, and not shut the discussion with unanswerable objections.

  • Objections the ad invites from its own content, ranked by how likely they are to come first:

    1. Comfort: tent, sleeping on the ground, no shower, cold nights. The ad's "comfortably in a tent" practically prints the objection. Very likely in the list.

    2. Effort: paddling every day, "I'm not sporty", sore shoulders. The ad's "dépassement" (pushing yourself) invites it, and so does your own "you want rest" (a fallback row for effort coming first is in section 6).

    3. Safety: capsizing, "I can't swim", bears, storms.

    4. Weather: "and if it rains all week?"
    5. Insects: mosquitoes and black flies (a Canadian stereotype).
    6. Time: "a whole week? I don't have that many days off."
    7. Price: not printed, so "it must cost a fortune" or a plain "how much?" as an information question.
    8. Boredom: "a week of water and trees, no wifi, I'll be bored; I'd rather visit a city." - Surprises the examiner may have ready: an information question in role ("where is it? how much?"), a counter-proposal ("why not a hotel by a lake?"), a personal fact ("I hurt my back last year"), or "is that your only argument?".

2. The hard part of this prompt

  • Detailed presentation without inventing facts. The ad is four lines. A "detailed" presentation can only reword what is printed and add the link to the two of you. The detail comes from structure (what it is, how a day runs, why it suits us), not from new facts.

  • Precise words off the ad. All the easy topic words are printed. The off-ad words have to come from the gaps: the camp (planter la tente, la rive), the gear (matelas gonflable, sac de couchage), the comparison with hiking (randonnée), the paddling done by someone else (ramer pour nous deux).

  • The only-here argument. Most arguments for this ad (nature, disconnecting, adventure, a change of scene) fit any outdoor trip. The one that only works for canoe-camping: on a canoe trip the boat carries the bags, not your back, so it is easier than a hiking trip with tents. It is also the best counter to the most likely second objection (effort), which is why the box keeps it for the reply instead of spending it in the presentation.

  • Which objection comes first. Eight are plausible. Without steering, the first reply cannot be prepared with confidence. That is the reason for the design below.

  • Links counted the strict way. Under standard 6 only clause-joining connectors of five relations count, so contrairement à (a preposition before a noun) and en gros (a summary marker) do not. The box needs three relations among addition, consequence, concession, contrast and cause, from words already there or swapped in at no cost.

3. Designs compared

D1. The textbook skeleton. Greeting, presentation, two illustrated arguments (disconnecting from screens; the canoe carries the bags), "are you in?", then two objection replies. About 160 words, point about 1:50 on the standard basis. Weak on the examiner's side: the first objection could be any of eight, so you need five or six prepared first replies; the only-here argument is spent before the effort objection arrives, so when it does arrive you repeat yourself (the booklet's named weakness); and the first turn is a 90-word monologue.

D2. Call both objections (chosen). The presentation ends by naming the objection the ad invites (the tent). Reply 1 grants it, counters with gear, and adds a line that invites the second objection (effort). Reply 2 opens on the friend's words, turns the objection round with the only-here argument and reuses the friend's word in a hypothetical. 128 words, point about 1:30. The first objection is steered (the examiner is told to leave you the initiative, and you hand over a ready-made objection that is surely on their list); the second is invited by your own line. Both replies are prepared and both are still answers to what was just said.

D3. Two weak spots at once. "You'll tell me a tent and paddling all day aren't your thing." The examiner's reply will touch one or both, and reply 1 must answer both in one turn (about 50 words, a speech). Then the second objection is entirely the examiner's choice (price, safety, time), so reply 2 is the least prepared turn in the box. About 130 words, same length as D2 and less predictable. Rejected.

D4. Presentation only, all arguments in the replies. First turn about 45 words, ending "are you in?". About 110 words, still above the 100-word time floor, but P17b wants the presentation to say why it fits the two of you, and the first objection is unsteered. The saving is about 18 words and the cost is the predictability of both replies. Rejected.

D5. The friend's self-description first. "You keep saying you're sick of screens and work." Every later argument becomes friend-specific ("out there there's no network, you'd have to switch off"). Unconventional and strong for the task criterion, but it costs about 15 words, the examiner can deny the premise ("not at all, I love my phone"), and the "no network" claim is an unprinted fact. A light version survives in the box: "you want rest; me, I need to move" maps the ad's calm-and-challenge onto the two of you, and denying it still leaves the ad's own promise standing.

D6. Irony on the ad's own word. "I know, 'comfortably' and 'tent' in the same sentence make you laugh." It calls the same objection as D2's tent line and shows you read the ad critically, but it quotes ad words (no lexis credit), costs four more words, and loses the informal idiom "c'est pas ton truc". Kept as a reserve line, not in the box.

Design Min's words Point (standard basis) First objection prepared? Second objection prepared? Only-here argument
D1 skeleton ~160 ~1:50 no (8 possible) no spent early
D2 call both 128 ~1:30 yes (steered) yes (invited) in reply 2
D3 two at once ~130 ~1:30 yes no in reply 1
D4 bare presentation ~110 ~1:20 no no in a reply, if it fits
D5 friend profile ~145 ~1:40 partly no "no network" (unprinted)

Why D2 wins. It is not the shortest (D4 is), but D4 only reaches the point if the examiner happens to pick objections you prepared. D2 reaches it against an examiner who behaves as the booklet says, and every word in it carries either evidence or the steering that makes the next reply predictable.

4. Structure note for each sentence

Turn 1 (presentation, after "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?")

  • T1.1 "Great! I've found our vacation." → Super ! J'ai trouvé nos vacances. One-word greeting, then straight to the ad; nos vacances ties it to the shared plan.

  • T1.2 "It's a week of canoe-camping organized by an agency, Vacances Nature." → C'est une semaine de canot-camping organisée par une agence, Vacances Nature. Participle used as a passive (organisée par, agreement with semaine, silent). The ad's active sentence turned round: rewording, not reading. Use canot, the ad's word.

  • T1.3 "We paddle during the day, and in the evening we probably pitch the tent on the shore." → La journée, on pagaie, et le soir, on plante sûrement la tente sur la rive. On for "we"; fronted time phrases; hedge sûrement; off-ad: planter la tente, la rive.

  • T1.4 "What I like is that it suits both of us: you want rest; me, I need to move." → Ce qui me plaît, c'est que ça nous va à tous les deux : tu veux du repos ; moi, j'ai besoin de bouger. Pseudo-cleft (ce qui... c'est que) + stressed pronoun put up front (moi, je...), which the English "me, I" produces on its own. Two marked structures, neither hard. First sentence with tu.

  • T1.5 "You're going to tell me that a tent isn't your thing." → Tu vas me dire que la tente, c'est pas ton truc. Near future + informal idiom c'est pas ton truc (spoken ne dropped, normal with a friend). The hand-over: no question after it (standard 3).

Turn 2 (reply 1, comfort)

  • T2.1 "I'm not surprised you're skeptical." → Ça m'étonne pas que tu sois sceptique. Étonner que forces the subjunctive, and sois is audible (not es). Hard structure 1. Rewords the friend's "I doubt it". Sceptique has one form for both genders.

  • T2.2 "But with a good inflatable mattress and a warm sleeping bag, you sleep well." → Mais avec un bon matelas gonflable et un sac de couchage bien chaud, on dort bien. The concrete counter and two precise words. Gear you bring, so no unprinted fact about the agency.

  • T2.3 "Besides, after a whole day in the canoe, you'll fall asleep in two minutes." → En plus, après une journée entière en canot, tu vas t'endormir en deux minutes. Addition link (en plus); reflexive verb in the near future. A second reason, and the bait for the effort objection.

Turn 3 (reply 2, effort)

  • T3.1 "Not sporty? That's exactly why it's perfect: unlike a hike, it's the canoe that carries the bags, not your back." → Pas sportif ? C'est justement pour ça que c'est parfait : contrairement à une randonnée, c'est le canot qui porte les sacs, pas ton dos. Opens on the friend's words (repeat their adjective in the form they used: sportif or sportive). Consequence link (c'est justement pour ça que); cleft c'est... qui. The only-here argument.

  • T3.2 "And even if you were exhausted, I'd row for both of us." → Et même si t'étais épuisé, je ramerais pour nous deux. Même si + imperfect + conditional: hard structure 2 and the concession link. Frame: put the friend's own word after t'étais (épuisé or crevé; both sound the same in either gender). Ramer comes straight from "row".

Translator notes (each only chooses between natural renderings of the English):

  • "a tent isn't your thing": la tente, c'est pas ton truc.
  • "on the shore": sur la rive (rather than au bord de l'eau).
  • "probably" in T1.3: sûrement.
  • "That's exactly why": c'est justement pour ça que (rather than c'est exactement pour ça que).
  • "a hike": une randonnée (or une rando).
  • "exhausted" in T3.2: the friend's own word.

Inventory.

  • Marked structure types (5, the two hard ones in replies): participle passive; clefts (ce qui... c'est que and c'est... qui, one family, counted once); stressed pronoun put up front (moi, je); audible subjunctive after étonner que; même si + imperfect + conditional. Five is the Section B minimum, so none of these can go.

  • Links under standard 6 (3 relations): en plus (addition), c'est justement pour ça que (consequence), même si (concession). Not counted: mais, et, contrairement à, the hypothetical's si (counted as syntax).

  • Precise words you choose, not on the ad (6): planter la tente, la rive, matelas gonflable, sac de couchage, randonnée, ramer; plus sceptique (an evaluative word, borderline). Not counted: épuisé / crevé (the friend says it first), se reposer, bouger, s'endormir, sacs, dos (everyday).

  • Informal markers: c'est pas ton truc, dropped ne, t'étais.

  • English words: none.

Substitution pass (same length, denser). "I think I've found" became "I've found". "You sleep almost like in a bed" became "you sleep well". "And I know you:" before the tent line went. "Believe me" went from T2.3. After review: "paddle" in T3.2 became "row" (same length, produces ramer); "That's exactly the point" became "That's exactly why it's perfect" (produces a consequence link instead of a calque); "And" at the start of T2.3 became "Besides" (an addition link at no cost); "I get that you're not convinced" became "I'm not surprised you're skeptical" (one word shorter, and étonner que forces an audible subjunctive); "by the water" became "on the shore" (la rive, a precise word at the same length).

5. Deletion test (rerun on the revised box)

Conditions: C1 frame done, C2 every capacity proved, C3 replication, C4 control presumed, C5 credibility intact. The box is at 128 words, above the 100-word floor of standard 5, so a dead word is deleted, not swapped.

Piece Removed, what breaks Verdict
"Great!" Ignoring "tu vas bien ?" makes the first line sound launched from a script (C5). One word. stays
"I've found our vacation" The presentation is no longer tied to the friend and the shared plan the prompt sets up (C1, presenting to this friend). stays
T1.2 agency sentence The ad is not identified: who organizes it, what, how long (C1). Also the participle passive (C2 syntax, one of five types). stays
T1.3 day and evening The presentation becomes one sentence: B1 "simple presentation", not "detailed" (C2 task). Loses planter la tente and la rive (lexis drops to 4). stays
"probably" (new) The camp location becomes an unhedged unprinted fact (standard 9), a target for the friend. One word. stays
"on the shore" (new wording) Without it, la rive goes and lexis drops to 5; the sentence still stands, so this is the thinnest piece in T1.3. stays (lexis margin)
T1.4 "what I like..." Loses the stressed pronoun (syntax drops to 4 types; the cleft family survives in T3.1), the link to the two of you that P17b requires, the first argument, and the first tu. stays
T1.4 second half "you want rest; me, I need to move" "It suits both of us" without the illustration is a bare claim (B1 "arguments clear but little developed"), and moi, je goes (syntax drops to 4 types). stays
T1.5 tent line The first objection is no longer called; any of eight can come, and T2 is no longer prepared (C5 at risk). Loses the informal idiom. stays
T2.1 "I'm not surprised you're skeptical" (new wording) Reply 1 no longer opens on the friend's words (standard 17) and loses the only mood marker in the box (hard structure 1; syntax would fall to four types, one below the Section B minimum). stays
T2.2 gear sentence Reply 1 has no concrete counter, so the comfort objection is unanswered (C2 task: illustrated). Loses two precise words. stays
T2.3 canoe-day line The second objection is no longer invited; T3 loses its predictability; the addition link goes (links fall to 2). stays (the next cut, see Why)
"Besides" (new) Links fall to 2 relations, below the 3 that C2 asks for. stays
"whole" in T2.3 The line invites the effort objection less clearly. One word. stays
"Not sporty?" (new) Reply 2 no longer opens on the friend's words (standard 17); it sounds like a new prepared argument (C5). stays
"That's exactly why it's perfect" (new wording) Loses the consequence link (links fall to 2) and the turn that makes the objection the reason. stays
T3.1 hike comparison No only-here argument in the box (C2 task); loses randonnée and the c'est... qui cleft. stays
"not your back" The comparison stops being an answer to "I'm not sporty" and becomes a fact about luggage. stays
T3.2 "even if..." Loses hard structure 2 and the concession link (no concession left: the B requirement fails), ramer, and the commitment that shows "defends firmly". stays
Whole of turn 3 Only one objection reply: P6 replication fails, "defends firmly" unproved. stays

Went at this round: "Listen" (tu is set in T1.4 within about 15 seconds, and standard 18 wants the greeting followed straight by the ad) and "Basically" (once T1.3 says sûrement and la rive, it no longer sounds like the ad's own line, so the summary marker carries nothing). Earlier rounds: "I think", "And I know you", "almost like in a bed", "believe me", "really".

6. Reply-variation test (three plausible examiner replies per turn)

Examiner turn 1, the greeting.

  • Helpful: "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?" → "Great!" fits.
  • Partial: "Salut !" → Salut ! instead of Super !, then the same turn.
  • Surprising: "Salut... bof, je suis crevé(e), le boulot..." → Oh non ! Justement, j'ai trouvé nos vacances. The rest of T1 is unchanged and the tiredness becomes an extra hook.

Examiner turn 2, after the tent line. The frame for every version: open on the friend's word, grant it with an audible subjunctive (ça m'étonne pas que / je comprends que + sois, fasse, paraisse, puisse), then a concrete counter.

  • Helpful (box): "Ha ! Confortablement, dans une tente ? J'en doute." → T2 as written.

  • Partial: "Bof, je sais pas..." or "Mouais, c'est pas trop mon style." → T2 as written: ça m'étonne pas que tu sois sceptique fits any doubt, and the tent is the topic you just named.

  • Surprising, six versions:

    • Effort first ("Te reposer ? Pagayer toute la journée, c'est pas du repos !"): this is the row the review asked for. Reply 2 moves up and becomes reply 1: Ça m'étonne pas que ça te paraisse fatigant. Mais justement : contrairement à une randonnée, c'est le canot qui porte les sacs, pas ton dos. Et même si t'étais épuisé, je ramerais pour nous deux. The tent reply (T2) follows as reply 2 when the tent comes up, opened on the friend's word (La tente ? Avec un bon matelas gonflable...). If the tent never comes up, reply 2 is the safety or price fallback below.

    • Time: "La tente, ça va, mais une semaine ? J'ai pas autant de congés." → Je comprends que ça te paraisse long, mais en un week-end, on n'a même pas le temps de décrocher. Une semaine, c'est ce qu'il nous faut pour vraiment changer d'air.

    • Price or "c'est combien ?": → Aucune idée, c'est pas sur la pub, mais ça m'étonnerait que ce soit plus cher qu'une semaine à l'hôtel, puisqu'on dort sous la tente. On peut regarder sur leur site ce soir.

    • Safety, "je nage mal / et si on chavire ?": → Je comprends que ça te fasse peur, mais ça m'étonnerait qu'on puisse partir sans gilet de sauvetage. (puisse is audible; laisse would not be.)

    • Insects: "Et les moustiques ?" → Les moustiques, c'est vrai qu'il y en aura, mais avec un bon chasse-moustiques et des manches longues, ils te laissent tranquille. Then T2.3 as written.

    • Counter-proposal: "Pourquoi pas un hôtel au bord d'un lac ?" → Un hôtel ? Parce qu'à l'hôtel, on regarde le lac ; là, on est dessus toute la semaine. Then T2.3.

    • "On doit monter la tente nous-mêmes ?" → J'imagine que oui, c'est du canot-camping. Et à deux, ça prend dix minutes.

Examiner turn 3, after the canoe-day line. The frame: open on the friend's word, a link that turns it round (justement, c'est pour ça que), a concrete counter, then même si + imperfect + conditional.

  • Helpful (box): "Une journée entière en canot ? Je suis pas sportif, je vais être épuisé !" → T3 as written, echoing their adjective in T3.1 and their word for tired in T3.2.

  • Partial, the same objection pushed: "Même avec un matelas, une semaine sans douche, non." → Sans douche ? Justement : on est au bord de l'eau toute la semaine, alors une baignade chaque soir, c'est ta douche. Et même si la première nuit était dure, au bout de deux jours, tu n'y penserais plus.

  • Surprising:

    • Rain: "Et s'il pleut toute la semaine ?" → Toute la semaine, ça m'étonnerait. Et même s'il pleuvait deux jours, on jouerait aux cartes sous la tente. (Avoid au cas où + conditional here: high risk.)

    • "C'est ton seul argument ?" → Non : contrairement à une randonnée, c'est le canot qui porte les sacs, pas ton dos. Et même si t'étais épuisé, je ramerais pour nous deux. T3 survives almost word for word.

    • Price or time at this point → the turn-2 fallbacks above, opened on the friend's word.

Every fallback keeps one hard structure in each reply (an audible subjunctive in reply 1, même si + imperfect + conditional in reply 2), so the syntax count does not depend on which objection comes.

7. The sceptical rater

A central rater, listening to the recording and wanting to give a 6, makes this case:

  1. "The presentation is thin and half of it is the ad: paddle, tent, calm." Answer: the ad has four lines and the box covers organizer, length, activity, the day-and-evening rhythm and the calm-and-challenge mix, all turned round (active to passive, imperative to on, ad nouns to se reposer / bouger), with an added, hedged detail (on plante sûrement la tente sur la rive), and tied to the two of you. What it leaves out (landscapes, booking) is in the reserve.

  2. "The candidate steered the friend's objections; this is a script." Answer: anticipating the objection is itself an argumentation skill, and the rater hears a friend who raises the obvious worry and a speaker who answers it. Both replies open on the friend's words (sceptique rewords "j'en doute"; pas sportif ? repeats it), and T3.2 puts the friend's own word inside a hypothetical, which cannot be recited without hearing it.

  3. "Je comprends que + subjunctive is a prep-book chunk." The box now uses ça m'étonne pas que tu sois sceptique, which is less of a stock phrase and forces the audible form; and the second hard structure (même si + imperfect + conditional) sits in the least predictable sentence of the box, built on the friend's word.

  4. "Only three turns; the exchange hasn't been shown to hold together." That clause is control, not capacity: it is presumed from linked turns and can only be disproved later. Three linked turns, each picking up the last line, is the most the box can show; the remaining eight minutes carry it.

  5. "Camping words are a prep list: matelas, sac de couchage." They are precise, topic-bound and not clichés; with planter la tente, la rive, randonnée, ramer and the idiom c'est pas ton truc the range is wider than lower B2's "everyday repertoire". The count is held to words you choose (standard 19), so the margin is real: six.

  6. "The second reply is a joke, not an argument." T3.1 is the argument (the canoe carries the load); T3.2 is the commitment that keeps the plan alive. Together they are the "defends clearly and with determination" the B2 column names.

After the fixes, the rater's best remaining case is item 4, which no box can answer. The deletion test above was rerun on the revised wording.

8. Say-aloud test

Each French sentence above splits into breath groups at its commas, the longest being T3.1 (Pas sportif ? / C'est justement pour ça que c'est parfait : / contrairement à une randonnée, / c'est le canot qui porte les sacs, pas ton dos) and T1.4 (three groups). Watch points: canot-camping (say canot as the ad does); ça m'étonne pas que tu sois, never que tu es (repair at once if it slips); même si t'étais, never même si tu serais (repair: même si tu serais, pardon, même si t'étais...); je ramerais is regular and easy. No sentence needs a word you might have to search for.

9. Count and timing (standard conversion)

Min's words in the box: 128 English words (T1 63, T2 33, T3 32). On the shared conversion (French words = English x 1.05; 120 French words a minute; plus 10% for pauses), that is 128 x 0.58 = about 74 seconds of your speech, above the 60-second floor (about 104 English words). The clock starts at the examiner's "Hi! How are you?". Examiner turns: the greeting (about 2 s) and two objections (about 5 s each), plus about 3 s of changeovers. The point (end of T3.2) falls at about 1:30 into Section B, leaving about eight and a half minutes after it.

10. Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

  • Close the loop and keep the initiative: Allez, on regarde leur site ce soir et on réserve ?
  • Switching off: Une semaine entière, pas un week-end : on aurait enfin le temps de décrocher du boulot.
  • The landscapes in your own words: se réveiller au bord d'un lac, voir le coucher du soleil depuis le canot.
  • Price hedge: Ça m'étonnerait que ce soit plus cher qu'une semaine à l'hôtel, vu qu'on dort sous la tente.
  • A canoe reaches places with no road: pas de foule, juste nous et l'eau (hedge it: sûrement).
  • Something for each of you (from T1.4): mornings of calm for the friend, a bit of effort for you.
  • Irony on the ad's word (D6), if the examiner quotes "confortablement" again.
  • The fallback replies in section 6 for effort first, safety, insects, rain, time and price.
  • If pushed hard late on, a compromise is fine (asking the agency about beginners), but never "you're right, forget it".

After review

  • Required fix 1 ("paddle" seeds pagaierais; lexis list padded): applied. "I'd row for both of us" (je ramerais), same length. Lexis list now counts only words you choose: six (planter la tente, la rive, matelas gonflable, sac de couchage, randonnée, ramer), with épuisé / crevé marked as the friend's word, not counted.

  • Cuts: none were asked for. Two dead words went anyway under standard 5 (box above the floor): "Listen" and "Basically".

  • Suggestion 1 (unprinted facts in "we pitch the tent by the water"): applied. Hedged with "probably" (sûrement); the answer to "we pitch it ourselves?" is in section 6 and adds no new fact. The Dissection line now says what the box states and how it is hedged.

  • Suggestion 2 (no row for effort coming first): applied. Section 6 has the row, with ça m'étonne pas que ça te paraisse fatigant opening the moved-up reply.

  • Suggestion 3 ("That's exactly the point" as a calque): applied by changing the English, not a note: "That's exactly why it's perfect" (c'est justement pour ça que), which also supplies the consequence link.

  • Across the chunk 2 (English relying on a note) and standard 15: applied. "row" replaces "paddle"; every translator note now only chooses between natural renderings.

  • Across the chunk 5 and standards 12 and 19 (honest lexis counts): applied, as in fix 1.

  • Across the chunk 6 and standard 9 (unprinted facts): applied, as in suggestion 1.

  • Across the chunk 7 and standards 5 and 7 (timing): applied. Point restated on the shared conversion from "Hi! How are you?": 128 x 0.58 = about 74 s of speech, point at about 1:30.

  • Standard 1 (audible subjunctive): applied. "I get that you're not convinced" (which can come out as an indicative "realize that") became "I'm not surprised you're skeptical": étonner que forces sois.

  • Standard 2 (no indirect "what"): checked, nothing to change. "What I like is that" is a pseudo-cleft (ce qui me plaît), not an indirect question.

  • Standard 3 (hand-over): checked. The tent line is the hand-over, no question after it.

  • Standard 4 (grants and reaffirms): checked. The concession is même si in reply 2; the grant in T2.1 stays because it carries the only audible subjunctive and the reword of the friend's doubt.

  • Standard 6 (what counts as a link): applied. Under the strict count the old box had only même si (and arguably justement); contrairement à and en gros do not count. "And" became "Besides" (en plus, addition) and the new "That's exactly why" gives consequence: three relations, one a concession, no extra words.

  • Standard 8 (translator notes): applied. A short "Translator notes" list is in section 4.

  • Standard 13 (lines echoing the examiner): applied. Section 4 gives the frames: repeat their adjective in T3.1, their word for tired in T3.2.

  • Standard 14 (format): checked, the box opens with [examiner: "Hi! How are you?"].

  • Standard 16 (one C1 glimpse is enough): checked. No words are spent on a glimpse alone; the varied arguments come from replies that are needed anyway.

  • Standard 17 (replies open on the friend's words): applied. Reply 1 opens on a reword of "I doubt it" (sceptique); reply 2 now opens "Not sporty?".

  • Standard 18 (one-word greeting, then the ad): applied. "Listen" cut; "Great!" then the ad.

  • Standards 10 and 11: Section A only, not applicable.
  • Final audit (English alone gave four structure types; the toi / moi contrast existed only through a translator note): applied as a swap, no added words. "you want to rest, I need to move" became "you want rest; me, I need to move", which produces moi, j'ai besoin de bouger on its own. The translator note is gone, and Why and Working now count the two clefts as one family: five types (participle passive, clefts, stressed pronoun up front, audible subjunctive, même si + imperfect + conditional). Still 128 words, about 74 s, point about 1:30.
↑ top
B-05

THEATRE WORKSHOP

Workshops & courses · Creative-workshop · est. #12 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement for a theatre workshop. You are telling a friend about it who is looking for a creative activity for their free afternoons.

THEATRE WORKSHOP

We are looking for a participant for a

THEATRE WORKSHOP

- Perform in front of an audience

- Practise different techniques: singing, dancing, etc.

- Develop creativity, listening

- Meet professional actors

Come and create a show!

Further information and registration on our website

www.onimprovise.com

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité pour un atelier de théâtre. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) qui cherche une activité créative pour ses après-midis libres.

ATELIER THÉÂTRE

Nous cherchons participant pour un

ATELIER THÉÂTRE

- Jouer devant un public

- Pratiquer différentes techniques : chant, danse, etc.

- Développer la créativité, l'écoute

- Rencontrer des comédiens professionnels

Venez créer un spectacle !

Renseignement complémentaire et inscription sur notre site

www.onimprovise.com

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, your second reply to an objection. On the standard basis (123 English words x 0.58 = about 71 seconds of your speech, plus about 13 seconds of the friend's three short turns) that is about 1:25 into Section B, counted from "Hi! How are you?".

The box123 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Great! You told me you were looking for something creative for your afternoons? I found a theatre workshop that's totally your thing. You work on your voice and your body, and you rub shoulders with real actors. What I like is that there's a goal: putting on a show. Imagine yourself on stage, with your family in the front row!

[examiner: "On stage? No way, I'm far too shy for that."]

It's normal that the stage scares you. Even the pros get stage fright. But since their website is called On improvise, it must be mostly improv. So no text to learn by heart: you just bounce off the others' ideas.

[examiner: "Maybe, but I can't sing, and dancing is even worse."]

Nobody's asking you to sing well: it's a workshop, not an audition. Even if you don't like it, at least you'll have tried.

Why this, and why nothing less

This box gives a Section B rater the three things they must hear before a 6 stops being an option: the ad presented in your own words for this friend, two replies that start from what the friend said, and the same level of French in both replies.

Task (present and debate). Turn 1 ties the ad to your friend (they told you they wanted something creative), rewords the ad instead of reading it (singing and dancing become voice and body, "meet" becomes rub shoulders with, "perform in front of an audience" becomes the stage and the front row), and gives a first argument with an illustration: a goal, putting on a show, then a picture of the friend on stage. The picture is also bait: it puts the ad's weak spot, the stage, in front of a friend told to disagree. Turn 2 picks up the friend's word (the stage), grants the fear, and counters with an argument only this ad allows: the website is called "On improvise", so it must be mostly improv, so no lines to learn, you bounce off the others (the ad's "listening", reworded). That inference, said with its reason and hedged with "it must be", is your one C1 glimpse: an argument of a different kind, drawn from reading the document closely. Turn 3 picks up the friend's verb (sing), reframes the whole thing (a workshop, not an audition) and holds the position with a concession. Three arguments of three kinds: motivation, ease, low risk.

Syntax. Seven different marked structures (grammar forms that only stronger speakers use reliably), never more than two in a sentence and only one hard: reported speech with the imperfect (tu m'as dit que tu cherchais), a cleft (ce qui me plaît, c'est que), an audible subjunctive (c'est normal que la scène te fasse peur), a cause clause, then devoir for a guess (comme leur site s'appelle..., ça doit être: it must be), a noun plus à plus infinitive (pas de texte à apprendre), personne ne te demande de, and même si with a futur antérieur (au moins tu auras essayé). Three kinds of link that join clauses: comme (cause), donc (consequence), même si (concession). Most sit in the replies, which cannot be rehearsed.

Lexis. Eight precise words that the ad does not print and that you choose: côtoyer, monter (un spectacle), la scène, le premier rang, le trac, par cœur, rebondir, une audition. Informal register: carrément ton truc, les pros, tu throughout.

Delivery. No sentence is over 18 English words, each breaks at a clause boundary, and no word needs searching for.

Replication. Three turns of clear evidence, two of them replies.

Time. About 71 seconds of your speech, above the 58-second floor.

Why nothing can go. Without the reported-speech opener the presentation is not for this friend. Without voice and body, or the actors, it is one fact, not "detailed". Without the cleft sentence argument 1 has no content; without the front-row picture it has no illustration and the first objection stops being predictable. In turn 2, the first sentence is the echo, the grant and the subjunctive; "even the pros get stage fright" is the only place for le trac; the website sentence is the only-here argument, the C1 glimpse and the cause link; "no text to learn" carries donc; "bounce off the others' ideas" is what answers shyness rather than memory. Turn 3 is the second reply, which is what "defends firmly" means: the echo, the counter, and the only concession.

The next cut. The cheapest is "with your family in the front row" (7 words). It costs le premier rang and weakens the bait, so the first objection becomes less certain. Below that, every cut removes a reply, a fact, a link or the only-here argument.

What the box assumes. The friend opens with the scripted greeting and raises the stage first. If not, the replies in Working swap order or use a fallback (one turn, about 15 seconds). After the point you need two or three more arguments of new kinds (the reserve), plain correct French, and no giving in. Winning the friend over is not required.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it counts as vocabulary evidence): atelier théâtre, nous cherchons participant, jouer devant un public, pratiquer différentes techniques, chant, danse, développer la créativité, l'écoute, rencontrer des comédiens professionnels, venez créer un spectacle, renseignement, inscription, notre site, and the site name "onimprovise". Saying these back in order is the A1/A2 habit of reading the document.

What it leaves unsaid: price, days and times (the friend has free afternoons, the ad gives no schedule), how long the workshop runs, where it is, level required (beginners or not), group size, whether there is a selection ("we are looking for a participant" sounds like a casting call), how much of it is singing and dancing, when the show is, whether you must attend every session. Min cannot fill any of these with facts (P18); they are where the friend's objections and Min's hedges come from.

Who is on the other end: a friend (tu) who is looking for a creative activity for free afternoons. That one line is the only fit the prompt gives, so the presentation must hook onto it.

The examiner's predictable moves:

  • Opens with "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?" (scripted in the playbook).
  • Ranked objections from the ad's own content and common stereotypes:
    1. Stage fright, shyness ("perform in front of an audience" is printed; the classic stereotype about theatre).
    2. "I can't sing / dance" (printed on the ad).
    3. "I'm not creative / I have no talent" (the ad says "develop creativity").
    4. Price or time (not printed, so the friend can ask; Min must not invent an answer).
    5. "Professional actors will find me ridiculous."
    6. "Improv is even worse, I'd have nothing to say" (a likely push back after any improv argument).
    7. "Theatre is for kids / I'm too old" or "I'd rather do something calm, alone, like painting."
  • Pushes a thin argument for development and asks for new ones; does not accept the same argument twice.
  • May ask an information question as a friend ("how much is it?", "when is it?") before objecting.

Step 2. The hard part

  1. Lexis. The ad prints almost every obvious theatre word (jouer, public, chant, danse, comédiens, spectacle, créativité, écoute). The precise words have to come from around the ad: la scène, le trac, le premier rang, par cœur, rebondir, une audition, monter (un spectacle), côtoyer. L'impro does not count: it comes from the printed site name.

  2. The only-here argument. Generic theatre arguments ("it gives you confidence", "you'll meet people") fit any workshop. Two things fit only this ad: the site name "on improvise" (so the workshop is very likely improv, which changes what stage fright means: no lines to forget) and the wording "we are looking for a participant", which makes it sound selective (so "a workshop, not an audition" is a reframe worth having ready; the rater only hears it as a general reframe, since the box never quotes that wording).

  3. The presentation without reading. The ad is a list of four infinitives. Read out, it is A1/A2. Each line has to be reworded (voice and body, rub shoulders with, putting on a show, on stage) and reordered around the friend.

  4. The second reply. The first objection is very predictable; the second is not. So the second reply opens on a frame that takes the friend's own verb ("Nobody's asking you to sing well / dance well / be funny") and continues the same way.

Step 3. Designs compared

Design Shape Words to point Holds up when the friend goes off script? Verdict
D1 Conventional presentation, argument 1 with illustration, argument 2 (improv), question naming the weak spot, reply 1, reply 2 about 150 yes the only-here argument is spent before any objection, so reply 1 needs a new one; about 25 more words for the same evidence
D2 Bait (chosen) presentation ending in a picture of the friend on stage; improv inference used as the counter in reply 1; reply 2 = echo, reframe, concession 123 yes, with two swappable replies (below) each unit does two jobs: the illustration triggers the objection, the only-here argument is the counter
D3 Joint venture Min opens "I'm signing up and I want you with me", every argument is "we" about 135 weaker: the friend can say "then go alone", and the task is to sell it to the friend rejected
D4 Pre-empt Min raises stage fright in turn 1 and answers it before the friend speaks about 105 no the answer is no longer a reply (P4), and the examiner then picks an objection you did not prepare
D5 Scarcity "they're looking for one participant, so hurry" about 120 no rests on a reading of the singular that is probably a typo; the friend can say "then it's taken"; kept in reserve
D6 Two turns presentation plus one long reply with two arguments about 95 yes one reply is a glimpse, not a level (P6); fails replication and sits under the time floor

D2 wins on both counts: fewest words that still give three evidence turns with two replies, and the first objection is steered rather than hoped for.

Swappable replies. Reply 1 (stage module) answers fear of being watched; reply 2 (ability module) answers "I'm not good enough" in any form. If the friend raises ability first, say the ability module's first sentence, then the website sentence and "So no text to learn by heart..." as its counter; the stage module waits for the stage objection. The even-if sentence fits after anything.

Step 4. Structure notes, sentence by sentence (French target)

# English French target Structure carried
1.1 Great! Super ! answers the greeting; register
1.2 You told me you were looking for something creative for your afternoons? Tu m'as dit que tu cherchais quelque chose de créatif pour tes après-midi ? reported speech with imperfect; quelque chose de + adjective; sets tu
1.3 I found a theatre workshop that's totally your thing. J'ai trouvé un atelier de théâtre qui est carrément ton truc. informal expression
1.4 You work on your voice and your body, and you rub shoulders with real actors. Tu travailles ta voix et ton corps, et tu côtoies de vrais comédiens. côtoyer; de + plural adjective
1.5 What I like is that there's a goal: putting on a show. Ce qui me plaît, c'est qu'il y a un objectif : monter un spectacle. cleft; monter un spectacle
1.6 Imagine yourself on stage, with your family in the front row! Imagine-toi sur scène, avec ta famille au premier rang ! pronominal imperative; scène, premier rang
2.1 It's normal that the stage scares you. C'est normal que la scène te fasse peur. audible subjunctive after c'est normal que (fasse); echo of the friend's "stage"
2.2 Even the pros get stage fright. Même les pros ont le trac. le trac; les pros (informal)
2.3 But since their website is called On improvise, it must be mostly improv. Mais comme leur site s'appelle « On improvise », ça doit être surtout de l'impro. cause link (comme); devoir for a guess (ça doit être) as the hedge
2.4 So no text to learn by heart: you just bounce off the others' ideas. Donc pas de texte à apprendre par cœur : tu rebondis juste sur les idées des autres. consequence link (donc); noun + à + infinitive; rebondir
3.1 Nobody's asking you to sing well: it's a workshop, not an audition. Personne ne te demande de bien chanter : c'est un atelier, pas une audition. personne ne + demander de; echo of the friend's verb; no gender agreement
3.2 Even if you don't like it, at least you'll have tried. Même si ça ne te plaît pas, au moins tu auras essayé. concession link (même si); futur antérieur

Load check: no sentence has more than two marked structures; the only hard one (subjunctive) stands alone in 2.1.

Translator notes (choices between natural renderings, never overruling the English):

  • 1.3 "totally your thing": carrément ton truc (or tout à fait ton truc); both are the informal expression.
  • 1.4 "rub shoulders with": côtoyer, the plain French for it; avoid rencontrer, which is the ad's word.
  • 2.1 "It's normal that": c'est normal que + subjunctive (fasse), not "c'est normal si".
  • 2.3 "since": comme at the head of the sentence (puisque is also fine); both are cause links.
  • 3.2 "you don't like it": ça ne te plaît pas or tu n'aimes pas; même si stays either way.

Substitution pass (same length, denser): "you meet real actors" became "you rub shoulders with real actors" (côtoyer instead of the printed rencontrer); "it's mostly improv" became "it must be mostly improv" (hedge, devoir, no invented fact); "judging by the name of their website" became "since their website is called On improvise" (same 8 words, now a cause link, and the rater hears the name the inference rests on); "you just react to what the others say" became "you just bounce off the others' ideas" (one word fewer, rebondir instead of an indirect "what").

Step 5. Deletion test (rerun after review; the box is above the 104-word time floor, so dead words go)

Piece Without it Verdict
1.1 "Great!" the turn ignores the friend's "how are you?" stays (1 word)
1.2 "you told me you were looking..." the presentation is not tied to the friend (P17b); lose reported speech; tu not yet set stays
1.3 "I found a theatre workshop..." the ad is never named; lose the one informal expression stays
1.4 voice and body presentation loses its reword of the techniques; one fact left stays
1.4 rub shoulders with real actors lose a fact of the ad and côtoyer; "even the pros" loses its anchor stays
1.4 "Basically" (old) only a second glimpse (structured presentation), which standard 16 says not to pay for; tu and the informal register are set elsewhere cut after review
1.5 cleft, "putting on a show" argument 1 has no content; lose the cleft and monter stays
1.5 "concrete" (old) an everyday adjective; nothing breaks cut after review
1.6 front-row picture no illustration for argument 1; the first objection is no longer steered; lose scène and premier rang stays; the next cut (see Why)
2.1 "It's normal that the stage scares you" no echo of the friend (standard 17), no grant, no subjunctive stays
2.2 "Even the pros get stage fright" lose le trac, the one word a reply to stage fright calls for; the strict lexis count falls to seven, and to five if a rater discounts monter and par cœur stays
2.3 website sentence lose the only-here argument, the C1 glimpse and the cause link; the improv claim would have no stated reason stays
2.4 "no text to learn by heart" the improv point has no consequence; lose donc (a link) and par cœur stays
2.4 "bounce off the others' ideas" the reply answers forgetting lines, not being watched; lose rebondir and the reword of the ad's "listening" stays
3.1 "Nobody's asking you to sing well" the reply no longer opens on the friend's words (standard 17) stays
3.1 "a workshop, not an audition" the second reply has an echo but no counter, the B1 rung ("but it's fun!"); lose audition stays
3.2 even-if sentence lose the only concession link (needed in B) and the futur antérieur; the reply ends without holding the position stays
3.x "How about we sign up together online?" (old) registration is printed; et si + imperfect not needed for the five-type minimum; shyness already answered in turn 2 cut after review

Step 6. Three-replies test (each examiner turn: helpful, partial, surprising)

Examiner turn Reply type Likely words Your next line still fits?
greeting all "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?" (scripted) yes
after turn 1 helpful "On stage? No way, I'm far too shy." yes, as written
after turn 1 partial "Hmm, theatre... I don't know if it's for me." yes, with the frame "It's normal that [their worry] scares you": "It's normal that it scares you a bit" (que ça te fasse un peu peur); rest unchanged
after turn 1 surprising "I can't sing at all." swap: 3.1 with their verb, then 2.3 and 2.4 as the counter; the stage module waits for the stage objection
after turn 1 surprising "How much is it? When is it?" not on the ad: "It doesn't say, but I'd be surprised if it cost more than a yoga class. We'll check on their website." (ça m'étonnerait que ce soit plus cher qu'un cours de yoga). The objection follows, then turn 2; the point moves one turn later
after turn 2 helpful "Maybe, but I can't sing, and dancing is even worse." yes, as written
after turn 2 partial "Improv? That's even worse, I'd have nothing to say." frame with their idea: "Nobody's asking you to be funny: it's a workshop, not an audition." (drôle does not change for gender); 3.2 unchanged
after turn 2 surprising "And the price? I don't have the money." "It's true that the price isn't on the ad, but we can check their website together. Even if it's too expensive, at least we'll know." (concession kept)
after turn 2 surprising "And it takes too much time." "But you told me your afternoons were free! Even if you only go once, at least you'll have tried."
after turn 2 surprising "They're looking for one participant. Maybe they choose." "Even if they choose, it's a workshop: they're looking for someone who wants to learn, not a star."

The pattern: each reply's first sentence repeats the friend's noun or verb inside a fixed frame ("It's normal that ... scares you", "Nobody's asking you to ... well"); 3.2 works after anything.

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6: "The presentation is four facts and a slogan. Both replies answer the two most predictable objections for a theatre ad, so they were prepared, and 'au moins tu auras essayé' is a prep-book line. The improv claim is invented: the ad says singing and dancing. Nothing about when, where or how much, so the presentation is not 'detailed'. The second reply is short."

How the box answers it:

  • Four facts is all the ad prints, and the box rewords them around the friend's wish; inventing a schedule or a price would be worse (the examiner knows the ad). Gaps are handled when the friend asks (fallback above).

  • Prepared for the likely objection is not the same as recited: each reply opens on the friend's own word (the stage, sing), and the content comes from this ad (the site name). A rater can tell a recited line only when it does not fit what was said, and the three-replies test covers that.

  • The improv point is an inference with its reason said out loud ("since their website is called On improvise") and a hedge ("it must be"). If the friend says "it's mostly singing", answer "Even better, you'll do a bit of everything".

  • The second reply is short by design: echo, counter, concession in 23 words is the B2 rung for a second push; length would add no new evidence.

  • The case then rests only on "it was prepared", which F6 discounts only when the line ignores the examiner. It fails.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

  • 1.5 was first "What I like is that it's not just exercises" (c'est que ce n'est pas que des exercices): three que in a row, a stumble risk. Rewritten; now "c'est qu'il y a un objectif : monter un spectacle".

  • 2.3 and 2.4 were one sentence of about 26 French words with three marked structures; split in two.

  • All other sentences are under 15 French words or break at a colon where a natural pause goes.
  • Watch: côtoies (say it [kotwa]); rebondis sur (no liaison problem); "On improvise" said as the site name, with a small pause before it.

Step 9. Count and timing (standard basis)

  • 123 English words (60, 40, 23).

  • Your speech: 123 x 0.58 = about 71 seconds (French about 129 words at 120 a minute, plus 10% for pauses). Above the 58-second floor.

  • Friend's turns: greeting about 2 seconds, two objections about 4 seconds each, plus turn gaps: about 13 seconds.

  • Clock from "Hi! How are you?": the point falls at the end of turn 3, about 1:25 into Section B, leaving about eight and a half minutes of buffer.

Reserve (after the point; not part of the box)

  • Proposal (cut from the box, first thing after it): "How about we sign up together online?" (Et si on s'inscrivait ensemble en ligne ?)

  • Transfer: speaking in front of people helps at work or in interviews (prendre la parole en réunion).

  • Social: a group that laughs together, people you would never meet otherwise (une troupe).
  • "I'm not creative": the ad says develop, so it is trained like a muscle; nobody arrives creative.
  • "The actors will judge me": they are there to guide you, and they were beginners once.
  • "I'd rather paint, alone and calm": painting is alone; here you get out of the house and you can still paint at weekends.
  • "I'm too old / it's for kids": nothing on the ad gives an age, and everyone starts at zero in improv.
  • Price or schedule: hedge and check together (ça m'étonnerait que ce soit plus cher qu'un cours de yoga).
  • Scarcity (D5, only if numbers come up): "they're looking for one participant, so if you want the spot, don't wait".
  • Closing: "Et si on allait à la première séance ensemble ? Tu décides après."

After review

  • Required fix 1 (reply 2 did not echo the friend): applied. "Nobody's asking you to sing well", with "be funny" as the frame for an improv push; no gender agreement left on the friend.

  • Required fix 2 (Why link list and the "participant" claim): applied. Links are now comme (cause), donc (consequence), même si (concession); mais and en gros no longer counted; vu was replaced by comme because a preposition before a noun is not a link (standard 6). "A workshop, not an audition" is described as a general reframe; the registration claim is gone.

  • Cut 1 ("Hi!", "thanks"): applied; "Great!" alone (also standard 18).

  • Cut 2 ("at the end, the group puts on a show"): applied as "putting on a show"; the unprinted "at the end, the group" is gone.

  • Cut 3 ("How about we sign up together online?"): applied; moved to the reserve.

  • Suggestion 1 (improv push as likely as the singing push): taken; "be funny" frame in step 6.
  • Suggestion 2 (cut "that's totally your thing"): not taken; it is the box's only informal expression (condition 2, lexis).

  • Standard 1: "I understand that the stage scares you" could come out with the indicative (je comprends que... te fait peur); changed to "It's normal that the stage scares you" (c'est normal que + fasse, audible). Same 7 words.

  • Standard 2: "react to what the others say" replaced by "bounce off the others' ideas" (no indirect "what"; adds rebondir).

  • Standard 4: one concession (même si) kept; "Even the pros get stage fright" kept for le trac and the logic of "normal".
  • Standard 5: 123 words, about 71 seconds, above the floor; dead words deleted, not swapped.
  • Standard 6: link count corrected as above.
  • Standard 7: timing stated from "Hi! How are you?".
  • Standard 8: translator notes added; each only chooses between natural renderings.
  • Standard 9: the improv inference keeps its hedge and now says its reason; the unprinted "at the end, the group" is cut.

  • Standard 12 and 19: lexis list reduced to words you choose that the ad does not print; l'impro, voix, corps, objectif and the friend's timide removed.

  • Standard 13: frames for the echo lines written in step 6.

  • Standard 16: "Basically" cut as a second glimpse; the website inference is the one C1 glimpse.
  • Standard 17: both replies now open on the friend's word (the stage; sing).

  • Standards 3, 10, 11, 14, 15, 18: checked; 3 (no question after the bait), 14 (opening line) and 18 (one-word greeting) hold; 10 and 11 are Section A only; 15 holds (every line's English produces the French in step 4).

  • Final audit: applied. "The next cut" now says 7 words; "epistemic devoir" replaced by "devoir for a guess (ça doit être: it must be)" in Why and step 4.
