B-22

THE KEYS TO THE GARDEN

Earn money · Earn-money · est. #8 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You read this job offer in the newspaper. You talk about it to a friend who is looking for work.

THE KEYS TO THE GARDEN

Offer your services on Les Clefs du Jardin.com

We are looking for people to carry out small gardening jobs at private homes:

- Clean and mow the lawns

- Remove the weeds

- Maintain the balconies

- Prune the trees and shrubs

- Water regularly…

Contact us quickly at 0323 43 4334 or on lesclefsdujardin.com

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette offre d’emploi dans le journal. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) qui cherche du travail.

LES CLEFS DU JARDIN

Proposez vos services sur Les Clefs du Jardin.com

Nous recherchons des personnes pour effectuer de petits travaux de jardinage chez des particuliers :

- Nettoyer et tondre les gazons

- Enlever les mauvaises herbes

- Entretenir les balcons

- Tailler les arbres et arbustes

- Arroser régulièrement…

Contactez-nous vite au 0323 43 4334 ou sur lesclefsdujardin.com

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, the reply to the friend's second objection (about the pay). That is about 1:20 into Section B, counted from the friend's "Hi! How are you?", after about 68 seconds of your own speech.

The box118 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Great! I saw an ad that made me think of you. It's a website, Les Clefs du Jardin, where you sign up to look after people's gardens. Basically, some jobs come back every week, like mowing, others now and then, like the hedges. So you'd have loyal clients, and a steady income. I know, you're going to tell me you've never gardened.

[examiner: "Well, yes! I know nothing about gardening."]

It's true, you don't know much about it. But people aren't looking for a landscaper, they just want you to be reliable. Besides, you used to mow your parents' lawn every summer!

[examiner: "Maybe. But how much does it pay?"]

The ad doesn't say. But even with a low hourly rate, if you had five loyal clients a week, you'd make quite a bit.

Why this, and why nothing less

The ad is a list of five chores. The cheapest way to fail this prompt is to read that list back to the friend, which is the grid's A1/A2 description for Section B ("reads the document"). So your first turn sorts the chores instead: some come back every week (mowing), others only now and then (the hedges). Sorting them yourself is the structured presentation the grid puts at C1: the box's one C1 glimpse on the task. It also gives you the first argument: weekly jobs mean loyal clients and a steady income. That argument only works for this ad, unlike "you'll be outdoors".

Then you name the weak spot ("you're going to tell me you've never gardened"). That sentence is the hand-over. "I know nothing about it" is on every objection list for a job offer, so the first objection is one you have a counter for.

Criterion by criterion, at the point:

  • Task (present and debate). A presentation in your own words, tied to the friend, with a structure the ad doesn't have. Two objection replies, each opening on the friend's words and then countering: "it's true, you don't know much about it, but..." and "the ad doesn't say, but even with a low hourly rate...". Arguments: two kinds plus a callback. Steady income (turn 1); reliability matters more than skill, with a fact from the friend's own life (reply 1); then reply 2 brings the loyal-clients argument back against the pay worry. The callback adds no variety, but it shows the exchange holds together.
  • Syntax. Eight different marked structures (forms that only stronger speakers use reliably), at most two per sentence: faire + infinitive (m'a fait penser à toi), certains... d'autres, a conditional, a reported near future (tu vas me dire que), the pronoun y, a subjunctive the ear can hear (que tu sois), the habitual imperfect (tu tondais, which "used to" pulls in), and si + imperfect + conditional. The two hard ones sit in your replies, where they can't have been memorised against what the friend just said. Links of three kinds: consequence (donc), concession (c'est vrai... mais), addition (en plus).
  • Lexis. Eight precise words that the English produces and the ad doesn't print: s'inscrire, les haies, des clients fidèles, un revenu stable, un paysagiste, fiable, la pelouse, un tarif horaire, plus the informal en gros, pas grand-chose, pas mal. (La tonte doesn't count: it comes from the ad's tondre.)
  • Delivery. The one trap is les haies: no liaison ("lé-è", not "lé-zè").

Why nothing can go. Without the sorting sentence the presentation becomes a list, the C1 glimpse goes, and the first argument has no premise. Without "So you'd have loyal clients, and a steady income" you hand over before arguing anything, and the consequence link goes. Without "I know, you're going to tell me..." the first objection is a lottery and reply 1 can't be prepared. Without the concession in reply 1 there is no concession link in the box (Section B needs one). Without the lawn, reply 1 is a reason with no example, the B1 line ("arguments clear but thin"), and the addition link goes. Without "the ad doesn't say" you dodge a direct question. Without "even with a low hourly rate" the counter answers a worry you never granted. Without the si sentence reply 2 has no counter, and with one reply "defends firmly" is unproven.

The next cut would be "people aren't looking for a landscaper, they just want you to be reliable" shortened to "all the clients want is for you to be reliable" (3 words saved). It keeps the subjunctive but loses paysagiste and the "not a pro, just reliable" contrast that answers this exact objection. Below that, a whole reply goes, and the box nears the time floor (about 104 English words, 60 seconds).

What the box assumes about the rest. Over eight minutes remain. You need two or three more arguments of new kinds (the reserve), plain correct French, and no giving in. One weak answer later doesn't undo the box. Bringing "loyal clients" back a third time would, a little, because the examiner is told to push a returning argument.

The working for this prompt

Step 1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it earns lexical credit): the name Les Clefs du Jardin, "offer your services", small gardening jobs at private homes, a list of five chores (clean and mow lawns, remove weeds, maintain balconies, prune trees and shrubs, water regularly), "contact us quickly", a phone number and the website.

What it leaves unsaid: the pay (hourly or per job), the status (self-employed through a platform, or employed), hours and flexibility, who supplies the tools, whether experience is needed, travel between homes, what happens in winter, any commission the site takes, insurance. The friend will reach for these gaps, and the first one any job-seeker reaches for is the pay.

Who is on the other end: a friend who is looking for work (task wording). Nothing says what kind of work. So the friend's likely stance is "this is not a real job / not for me".

The examiner's predictable moves (Section B playbook): "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?", then the friend waits for the presentation (and steers back to it if you skip it), then objects from a confidential list plus stereotypes, pushes thin arguments, and asks for new ones when an argument comes back. Likely objections, ranked by my estimate:

  1. "I know nothing about gardening / I've never done it." (lack of experience, the standard objection to any job offer)
  2. "How much does it pay? It must pay badly." (the ad's biggest gap)
  3. "It's tiring, on your knees all day, in the sun or the rain."
  4. "It's not a real job, it's not stable, and what about winter?"
  5. Less likely: no tools, no car, working at strangers' homes, allergies, "it's not my field / I studied for something else", "a website, is it a scam?".

Stereotypes the ad invites: gardening as low-paid manual work; online gig platforms as precarious.

Step 2. The hard part

The hardest condition here is condition 1 in its B form: present the ad in your own words, not read it. The ad is almost nothing but a list of verbs. Any presentation that walks through the list ("you clean and mow lawns, you remove weeds, you maintain balconies...") is reading with light rewording, which the grid places at A1/A2 for Section B. The fix has to come from reorganising the list, not paraphrasing it.

The second hard part is the only-here argument. The generic arguments for this ad ("you'll be outdoors", "it's flexible", "it's money") fit every outdoor job. The only-here material sits in two places: the frequency of the chores (watering and mowing come back every week, so clients come back every week), and the ad's wording "offer your services" (a platform where you sell your own time).

Precise words this topic offers (not on the ad): la pelouse, les haies, la tondeuse, le sécateur, le tuyau d'arrosage, un paysagiste, s'inscrire, un tarif horaire, des clients fidèles, un revenu stable, un complément de revenu, fiable, à ton rythme, le bouche-à-oreille, les voisins, des personnes âgées, se mettre à genoux, le mal de dos.

Step 3. Designs compared

Design 1, the standard skeleton (rejected). Return the greeting, walk through the five chores in your own words, give two arguments (outdoors, flexible hours), ask "what do you think?", then answer two objections. About 175 words. Weak on three counts: the walk-through sits close to reading; both arguments fit any outdoor job, so there is no only-here argument; and "what do you think?" leaves the first objection to chance, so reply 1 can't be prepared.

Design 2, the frequency frame plus a called probe (chosen). Sort the chores into weekly jobs and occasional big jobs. That sorting is the structured presentation (C1 on the task criterion) and it is also the premise of argument 1 (weekly jobs, so loyal clients, so steady income). Then name the weak spot yourself ("you're going to tell me you've never gardened"), so the first objection is the one you have a counter for. Reply 1: concede, counter with "they want reliable, not a pro", illustrate with the friend's own lawn. Reply 2 (to the pay question, the next most likely push): admit the ad doesn't say, concede a low rate, counter with a si hypothetical that reuses your own "loyal clients" frame. 118 words, three turns. The thread "every week, loyal clients" runs through all three turns, which makes the exchange hold together without any extra sentence.

Design 3, ask the friend first (rejected). After a short presentation, ask "what kind of job are you looking for?", then reframe whatever the answer is as "this is for while you look" (a reply at right angles to any answer). It adds a genuinely reactive turn early, but the friend's answer is unpredictable, the examiner is told to object rather than tell stories, and "defends firmly" still needs two objection replies after it. Four turns, about 165 words, point near 2:30. Kept as a fallback line ("it's for while you look").

Design 4, the honest gap inside the presentation (rejected). Say in turn 1 that the ad doesn't give the pay and infer that you set your own rates ("offer your services"). It pre-empts pay, so the second objection becomes harder to predict (tiring, winter, tools), and it moves the best reactive material into the scripted turn, where it can't prove anything (P4). Same length, less evidence where it counts.

Design 5, two turns only (rejected). Turn 1 plus reply 1, about 100 words. One reply can't show "defends firmly", and 55 seconds of speech is too short for control to be presumed. Fails conditions 3 and 4.

Why Design 2 wins: it is the shortest that reaches all five conditions, and its two most uncertain moments (what the friend objects to first and second) are the two most predictable objections for a job offer, one of which you trigger yourself.

Step 4. Structure notes, sentence by sentence

Marked structures are counted per sentence (at most two, at most one hard). Links are counted only where a connector joins clauses or turns (STANDARDS 6).

# English (box) French the English produces Structures Precise words / register / links
S1 Great! I saw an ad that made me think of you. Super ! J'ai vu une annonce qui m'a fait penser à toi. relative qui + causative faire + infinitive tu register set by toi
S2 It's a website, Les Clefs du Jardin, where you sign up to look after people's gardens. C'est un site, Les Clefs du Jardin, où tu t'inscris pour t'occuper du jardin des gens. relative où, pour + infinitive s'inscrire; "people's gardens" rewords chez des particuliers
S3 Basically, some jobs come back every week, like mowing, others now and then, like the hedges. En gros, certains travaux reviennent chaque semaine, comme la tonte, d'autres de temps en temps, comme les haies. certains... d'autres (indefinite pronoun contrast) en gros (informal), les haies (h aspiré, no liaison); la tonte not counted (from the ad's tondre)
S4 So you'd have loyal clients, and a steady income. Donc tu aurais des clients fidèles, et un revenu stable. conditional link: consequence (donc, joins S3 and S4); des clients fidèles, un revenu stable
S5 I know, you're going to tell me you've never gardened. Je sais, tu vas me dire que tu n'as jamais jardiné. near future + reported speech, ne... jamais the hand-over; no indirect "what"
S6 It's true, you don't know much about it. C'est vrai, tu n'y connais pas grand-chose. pronoun y link: concession (c'est vrai... mais, with S7); pas grand-chose (informal)
S7 But people aren't looking for a landscaper, they just want you to be reliable. Mais les gens ne cherchent pas un paysagiste, ils veulent juste que tu sois fiable. vouloir que + audible subjunctive sois (hard) un paysagiste, fiable
S8 Besides, you used to mow your parents' lawn every summer! En plus, tu tondais la pelouse de tes parents tous les étés ! habitual imperfect, seeded by "used to" (against the passé composé of S5) link: addition (en plus); la pelouse
S9 The ad doesn't say. L'annonce ne le dit pas. neuter le (not counted) opens on the friend's question
S10 But even with a low hourly rate, if you had five loyal clients a week, you'd make quite a bit. Mais même avec un petit tarif horaire, si tu avais cinq clients fidèles par semaine, tu gagnerais pas mal. si + imperfect + conditional (hard); même avec grants the worry (a preposition before a noun, so not counted as a link) un tarif horaire, pas mal (informal); callback to clients fidèles

Counted: eight marked structure types (faire + infinitive, certains... d'autres, conditional, reported near future, y, subjunctive, habitual imperfect, si hypothetical); three link relations (consequence, concession, addition); eight precise words (s'inscrire, les haies, des clients fidèles, un revenu stable, un paysagiste, fiable, la pelouse, un tarif horaire) plus three informal expressions.

Translator notes (each one only chooses between natural renderings of the English):

  • "Basically" is en gros (not fondamentalement).
  • "lawn" is la pelouse (not le gazon, which the ad prints).
  • "you don't know much about it" is tu n'y connais pas grand-chose.
  • "loyal clients" is des clients fidèles; "steady income" is un revenu stable (not régulier, which echoes the ad).
  • "a low hourly rate" is un petit tarif horaire; "quite a bit" is pas mal.
  • "The ad doesn't say" is L'annonce ne le dit pas.
  • ne cherchent pas un paysagiste (not de) is right here because it contrasts one kind of person with another.
  • No adjective in the box agrees with the friend's gender (fiable is the same for both), so the French works whether the examiner plays un ami or une amie.

Substitution pass (same length, denser), applied before the final count:

  • "connects people who have a garden with people who can look after it" became "where you sign up to look after people's gardens": shorter, and it adds the "how" (s'inscrire).

  • "regular clients" became "loyal clients" (fidèles, not a word on the ad).

  • Reply 2's first draft ("since you offer your services, you probably set your rates; if you tried it for a month, you'd see if it's worth it", 29 words) became the current 24: one sentence now grants the worry, carries the si hypothetical and answers the pay question head on instead of guessing about rates.

  • After review: the 28-word sorting sentence became 16 words (certains... d'autres), "That's the good part for you:" became the link "So", "And" became "Besides" (addition link), "mowed" became "used to mow" (imperfect), "it would add up" became "you'd make quite a bit" (tu gagnerais pas mal), the greeting became one word.

Step 5. Deletion test

Each piece removed alone; "breaks" names the condition of the point (section C) or the standard that no longer holds. Re-run after review on every changed piece. The box (118 words, 68 seconds) is above the time floor (about 104 words), so dead words go.

Piece Removed, what breaks Verdict
"Great!" The friend's "how are you?" goes unanswered; the opening sounds launched, not spoken (condition 5). One word (STANDARDS 18). stays
"Hi!" before it, "Listen," Nothing. cut
"I saw an ad that made me think of you." No reason for telling the friend (presentation not tied to them, condition 1); the causative goes. stays
"...in the paper", "...right away" Nothing. cut
S2, the website sentence The ad is never named or explained; the "how" (sign up) goes (condition 1). stays
S3, the sorting sentence The presentation shrinks to one line, not "detailed"; the C1 glimpse goes; S4 loses its premise (condition 2, task). stays
S3 "like mowing" "Weekly jobs" becomes abstract and the friend can't picture them; S8's lawn loses its echo. stays
S3 "like the hedges" The second group has no example, so the sorting sounds unfinished; les haies goes. stays
S3 "only" (in the reviewer's version) Nothing: certains... d'autres already carries the contrast. cut
S3 "two kinds of jobs", "watering" Nothing the 16-word version doesn't carry; "watering" is the ad's arroser. cut
S4 "So" The consequence link goes, leaving two link relations (condition 2, STANDARDS 6). stays
S4 "you'd have loyal clients" No argument before the hand-over; reply 2's callback has nothing to call back to; the conditional goes. stays
S4 "and a steady income" The argument loses its payoff for a job-seeker (loyal clients, so what?); un revenu stable goes. stays
S4 "That's the good part for you:", "quickly" Nothing: "So" links better and "you'd" already speaks to the friend; "quickly" was an unhedged extra claim. cut
S5, the named weak spot The first objection becomes a lottery; reply 1 can't be prepared and risks ignoring what was said (condition 5). stays
"...in your life", "honestly" Nothing. cut
S6, "It's true, you don't know much about it." No concession link anywhere (STANDARDS 4 and 6); reply 1 no longer opens on the friend's words (STANDARDS 17). stays
S7, not a landscaper, just reliable Reply 1 has no counter; the subjunctive goes. stays
S7 "people aren't looking for a landscaper" Could shrink to "all the clients want is for you to be reliable" (3 words saved) but loses paysagiste and the "not a pro" contrast that answers this exact objection. Kept as the next cut. stays
S8, the parents' lawn Reply 1 becomes a reason with no example (B1 "clear but thin"); the imperfect and the addition link go. stays
S8 "used to" (2 words) "you mowed" lets the translator choose tu as tondu, and the imperfect counted in the box disappears (STANDARDS 15). stays
S9, "The ad doesn't say." You ignore a direct question; the reply sounds like a prepared speech (condition 5). stays
S10 "even with a low hourly rate" The counter answers a worry you never granted; tarif horaire goes. It carries the logic of the next clause (STANDARDS 4). stays
S10 "if you had five loyal clients a week, you'd make quite a bit" Reply 2 has no counter, so "defends firmly" is unproven; the si hypothetical goes (conditions 2 and 3). stays

Step 6. Reply test (three plausible replies per examiner turn)

Examiner turn 1, the greeting.

  • Helpful: "Salut ! Ça va ?" Your "Great!" fits.
  • Partial: "Coucou !" Still fits.

  • Surprising: "Bof, pas terrible, je cherche toujours du boulot." Swap "Great!" for "Oh, then I saw an ad that made me think of you" (Ah, justement, j'ai vu une annonce...). The rest of turn 1 is unchanged.

  • If the friend cuts in during the presentation ("ah bon, c'est quoi comme travail ?"), S3 is the natural answer; carry on.

Examiner turn 2, after "you're going to tell me you've never gardened".

  • Helpful (the box): "Ben oui, je n'y connais rien !" Reply 1 fits.
  • Partial: "Oui... je ne sais même pas reconnaître une mauvaise herbe." Same gist; reply 1 fits as written.

  • Surprising, a different objection: "Non, ça va. Mais c'est payé combien ?" Give reply 2 now. When the friend pushes again, open with "And don't worry about never having gardened:" and give reply 1's S7 and S8. The point then falls one turn later, same words.

  • Surprising, tiring or weather: "Et puis c'est fatigant, à genoux toute la journée !" Fallback: "It's true it's physical. But they're small jobs, a few hours at someone's place, not eight hours in a warehouse. And since you offer your own services, you decide how many clients you take." (concession, contrast, cleft optional: c'est toi qui décides combien de clients tu prends).

Examiner turn 3, after reply 1.

  • Helpful (the box): "Peut-être. Mais c'est payé combien ?" Reply 2 fits.

  • Partial: "Ouais... mais ça doit être mal payé." Reply 2 fits exactly ("The ad doesn't say. But even with a low hourly rate...").

  • Surprising, stability: "Ce n'est pas un vrai travail, et l'hiver, je fais quoi ?" Fallback: "It's true it's not for life. But it's for while you look for something in your field, and if you had five loyal clients by spring, you'd already have a steady income." (keeps the si hypothetical and the callback).

  • Surprising, tiring: use the "small jobs, you decide how many clients" fallback above, then "And if you tried it for a month, you'd see if it's worth it." (si tu essayais un mois, tu verrais si ça vaut le coup), which fits after any objection.

  • Surprising, "your parents' lawn? They live in a flat!": laugh it off: "OK, but you water your plants every day, it's the same idea." Then carry on.

  • Surprising, a challenge to the sorting: "How do you know it's every week?" Answer from the chore, not the company: "Grass grows; in summer you can't leave a lawn two weeks." (l'herbe pousse ; en été, on ne peut pas laisser une pelouse deux semaines.) Nothing about the site is invented.

Frames for lines that depend on the friend's words (STANDARDS 13): reply 1 opens by rewording whatever "I don't know how" they said (c'est vrai, tu n'y connais pas grand-chose / c'est vrai, tu n'en as jamais fait); reply 2 opens by answering their actual question or worry in one clause (l'annonce ne le dit pas for pay; c'est vrai que c'est physique for effort), then the counter.

Rule behind the fallbacks: the reply's first clause echoes whatever the friend actually said; the counter comes from the two only-here facts (weekly work, you sell your own services), which answer pay, effort, time and stability alike.

Step 7. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6: "The first turn was a 62-word block, smooth and tidy, the kind candidates rehearse during the reading minute. The candidate then invited the 'never gardened' objection, so reply 1 was prepared too. The only truly unplanned moment was the pay question, and the first draft of the answer was a guess ('you probably set your rates') plus a stock 'try it and see'. Two short replies, one of them evasive, is the bottom of 'defends', not 'firmly'. The structures are standard frames."

How the box answers it (after the fix):

  • The presentation is allowed to be prepared; what the rater scores there is structure, and sorting the chores by frequency is structure the ad doesn't contain. The level claim does not rest on turn 1.

  • Reply 1 was invited, but it echoes the friend's actual words, brings a new kind of argument (reliability over skill) and a fact from the friend's own life. That is the B2 rung (concede, counter, illustrate) whatever its origin.

  • The fix: the guess about rates and the stock trial line were replaced. Reply 2 now admits the gap, grants the friend's worry (low hourly rate) and counters with a hypothetical built on your own turn-1 frame (five loyal clients a week). A callback to your own earlier argument, made against a new objection, can't be a stock line. (After review it is no longer counted as a C1 feature: the examiner pushes on returning arguments. The C1 glimpse is the sorting.)

  • "Firmly": the position holds across two different pushes without giving ground on the conclusion.

  • Re-run of step 5 after the fix: every piece of reply 2 still breaks something when removed (see the table), and reply 2 went from 29 words to 24.

Second line of attack, delivery: "les zhaies". Practise les | haies with no liaison. If it slips, a quick self repair (les haies, pardon) is upper-B2 evidence, not a fault.

Step 8. Say-aloud test

  • S3 (16 English words) has three short breath groups: En gros, certains travaux reviennent chaque semaine | comme la tonte | d'autres de temps en temps, comme les haies.

  • S10 has three breath groups: Mais même avec un petit tarif horaire | si tu avais cinq clients fidèles par semaine | tu gagnerais pas mal. The si + imperfect + conditional is the only hard structure in it.

  • S7: ils veulent juste que tu sois fiable is short; the subjunctive is the commonest one (être).

  • No sentence stacks two mood choices. No word needs searching for.

Step 9. Count and timing

  • Min's words in the box: 118 English (62 in turn 1, 32 in reply 1, 24 in reply 2).

  • Standard conversion (STANDARDS 5): 118 x 0.58 = about 68 seconds of your speech (124 French words at 120 a minute, plus 10% for pauses). That is above the 60-second floor (about 104 English words).

  • Clock from the friend's "Hi! How are you?" (STANDARDS 7): three short examiner turns add about 10 seconds, so the point falls at about 1:20 into Section B, with over eight minutes of buffer.

Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

New kinds of argument, one per push, never "loyal clients" a third time:

  1. Urgency: the ad says "contact us quickly", so they need people now and you could start this week (tu pourrais commencer dès cette semaine).

  2. Social: the clients are often older people who can't kneel to weed any more; you'd really be helping them, and neighbours talk (le bouche-à-oreille).

  3. Stopgap: it doesn't replace the job you want, it's while you look, and it fills the gap in your CV.

  4. Health and mood: moving outdoors instead of sending CVs all day in front of a screen.
  5. Low risk: "if you tried it for a month, you'd see if it's worth it" (ça vaut le coup).
  6. Practical answers: tools (we can ask them; many people have their own mower), transport (choose clients in your neighbourhood), strangers' homes (you work outside, and the site is the go-between), "is it a scam?" (let's call the number together tonight), winter (time to look for something in your field).

After review

  • Required fix 1 ("it would add up" relies on a translator note): applied. Now "you'd make quite a bit" (tu gagnerais pas mal), which keeps the si + imperfect + conditional and the informal pas mal; one word longer by the checker's count ("you'd" is one word).

  • Required fix 2 (argument kinds and C1 claims in Why): applied. Why now says "two kinds plus a callback"; the sorting is named as the one C1 glimpse; the callback is no longer counted as C1 (Working step 7 updated too).

  • Cut 1 (the 28-word sorting sentence): applied, tightened further to 16 words by also dropping "only" (dead under certains... d'autres).

  • Cut 2 ("Hi!"): applied; the greeting is the one word "Great!" (STANDARDS 18).

  • Suggestion 1 (every week is an inference): kept the wording and added a fallback that answers from the chore (grass grows), not from the company. Rebuttal to hedging it: it is general knowledge about mowing, not a claim about the offer, and a hedge would cost a word for no evidence.

  • Suggestion 2 ("used to mow"): applied (+2 words). Without it the English allows tu as tondu, and the imperfect counted in the box would not be produced (STANDARDS 15).

  • Suggestion 3 (loyal clients spent twice): no box change; the reserve already forbids a third use and keeps urgency and the stopgap argument for the next push.

  • STANDARDS 1 (audible subjunctive): checked; que tu sois is audible.

  • STANDARDS 2 (no indirect "what"): checked; none in the box or fallbacks.
  • STANDARDS 3 (hand-over): checked; the named weak spot is the hand-over, no question after it.

  • STANDARDS 4 (grants): checked; "It's true, you don't know much about it" carries y and the concession link; "even with a low hourly rate" carries the logic of the counter and tarif horaire.

  • STANDARDS 5 and 7 (time floor, one basis): applied. 118 x 0.58 = about 68 seconds, above the floor; point at about 1:20 from the friend's first line. Because the box is above the floor, dead words were deleted ("quickly", "That's the good part for you:", "only").

  • STANDARDS 6 (links): applied. The old count relied on même avec (a preposition) and donc before a noun. Now three clause links: consequence ("So", joining S3 and S4), concession ("It's true... But"), addition ("Besides", replacing "And", no word cost).

  • STANDARDS 8 and 15 (English makes its own French): applied. Translator notes list added; each note only chooses between natural renderings.

  • STANDARDS 9 (facts not on the ad): checked; "five loyal clients" is a hypothetical, the lawn is the friend's own history, "every week" is answered above.

  • STANDARDS 12 and 19 (precise words): applied. La tonte and l'arrosage (from the ad's verbs) and s'occuper de (everyday) removed from the counts; eight precise words remain.

  • STANDARDS 13 (lines that depend on the friend): frames added to the reply test.

  • STANDARDS 14 (format): checked; the box opens with the friend's "Hi! How are you?".
  • STANDARDS 16 (one C1 glimpse): applied; no words spent on a second one.
  • STANDARDS 17 (replies open on the friend's words): checked for both replies.
  • STANDARDS 10 and 11: Section A only, not applicable.
↑ top
B-04

VENTECO.COM

Buy, sell & online services · Resale/sharing-economy · est. #11 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement. You talk about it to a friend who wants to save money.

VENTECO.COM

Economical and Ecological

Sell, swap, buy!

www.venteco.com

Clothes, cars, furniture, accessories, household appliances, shoes, jewelry…

Post your ads, discover plenty of good deals and make transactions between private individuals!

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) qui veut faire des économies.

VENTECO.COM

Économique et Écologique

Vendez, troquez, achetez !

www.venteco.com

Vêtements, voitures, meubles, accessoires, électroménager, chaussures, bijoux…

Postez vos annonces, découvrez plein de bonnes affaires et faites des transactions entre particuliers !

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, the second objection reply, after the friend has pushed back twice. That is about 1:20 into Section B, counted from the friend's greeting, with about 68 seconds of your own French on the recording.

The box118 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Great! You told me you wanted to save money, so I found the site you need: Venteco. It's a site where people sell, swap or buy among themselves, from clothes to cars. On one hand, you buy second-hand for much less. On the other, if you sold everything lying around in your closets, you'd make a few hundred dollars. You'll tell me buying from strangers is risky.

[examiner: "Exactly! These sites are full of scams."]

I get that scams scare you. But you meet the person in a public place. You only pay if you're happy with it.

[examiner: "Okay, but if it breaks a week later, there's no warranty."]

It's true that with second-hand, you're never a hundred percent sure. That's why I'd start with things you can check at a glance, like a sofa.

Why this, and why nothing less

By the end of your third turn, every capacity on the Section B grid has been shown, the two that need repeating (presenting and defending) appear in different turns, and both defences open on what the friend just said. After that, only a pattern of trouble pulls you down.

Task (present and debate). Your first turn presents the ad in your own words and makes it a plan for this friend: who uses the site (people, among themselves), what is on it (from clothes to cars), and two ways it saves money, signposted with "on one hand / on the other". That signposting is your one C1 glimpse ("structured presentation"), and one is enough. The selling half is the argument that only works for this ad and this friend: the site turns clutter into cash, and the hypothetical figure illustrates it without claiming anything the ad does not print. Naming the scam worry yourself makes the first objection predictable, so the reply can be prepared and still be a real reply: it opens on the friend's own word (scams) and gives a concrete rule (public place, pay only if happy). The second reply grants part of the push, then adapts the plan to it (start with things you can judge by eye, like a sofa). Two replies in two turns is what "defends clearly and firmly" sounds like. One would only be a glimpse.

Syntax. Five different marked structures (grammar forms that only stronger speakers use reliably), each produced by the English itself: reported speech with the tense shift (tu m'as dit que tu voulais), an if-clause with imperfect and conditional (si tu vendais..., tu gagnerais), an audible subjunctive inside a reply (que les arnaques te fassent peur), a conditional of proposal (je commencerais), and relative clauses (où, qui, que). Natural French adds two more (qu'acheter à des inconnus, c'est risqué; ne... que si). Links of three kinds, one of them a concession: contrast (d'un côté, de l'autre), consequence (alors, c'est pour ça que), concession (c'est vrai que).

Lexis. Seven precise words the English produces on its own, none printed on the ad and none supplied by the friend: d'occasion, traîner, placards, inconnus, lieu public, en un coup d'œil, canapé. Informal register comes with tu, on and tout ce qui traîne.

Delivery. No sentence runs past about 19 English words or carries two hard structures, and no word needs searching for.

Why nothing can go. Without "Great!" the opening sounds like a recording starting. Without the first sentence the ad is neither named nor tied to the friend. Without the presentation sentence the examiner steers you back. Without the two-sided pair there is no structure and no illustrated argument. Without "You'll tell me..." the first objection is the examiner's free choice among eight. Without "I get that scams scare you" the reply no longer picks up the friend's word and loses the only hard structure inside a reply. Without the public place or the payment rule the scam is not answered. Without the concession there is no concession link, and "that's why" has nothing to follow. Without "check at a glance, like a sofa" the second reply only concedes. Working has the clause-by-clause table.

The next cut. At 118 words (about 68 seconds of your speech) the box sits above the 104-word time floor, but no clause left is dead. The cheapest candidate, "from clothes to cars" (4 words), leaves a bare definition of the site, which is the B1 "simple presentation". Cutting "like a sofa" (3 words) leaves a counter with no example, which is the B1 "arguments clear but thin". Cutting the third turn leaves one defence, and replication fails.

What the box assumes. That the friend takes the scam objection you named (the likeliest; fallbacks are in Working), that you can say the subjunctive and the if-clause cleanly at your natural pace, and that the remaining eight and a half minutes sound like a solid NCLC 6: you keep answering each objection with something new (the reserve in Working has eleven), you never give in, and you never repeat one argument three times. You need to keep defending, not to win.

The working for this prompt

1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it counts as vocabulary evidence): VENTECO.COM; Économique et Écologique; Vendez, troquez, achetez; vêtements, voitures, meubles, accessoires, électroménager, chaussures, bijoux; Postez vos annonces; plein de bonnes affaires; transactions entre particuliers. The two obvious arguments ("it's cheap", "it's green") are the ad's own slogan, so saying them is reading.

What it leaves unsaid: whether posting or browsing costs anything (fees, commission); how payment works and whether it is protected; delivery or hand-over in person; any check on sellers (ratings, identity); any warranty or return; whether it is local; how a swap is valued; what happens in a dispute. You must not fill these gaps with facts about the site. You fill them only with hedges ("I'd be surprised if...") or with general know-how about private sales (meet in a public place, check before paying), which is true whatever the site does.

Who is on the other end: a friend who wants to save money (from the instruction, not the ad). That goal cuts both ways. It is the hook (buy for less, and earn by selling what you don't use), and it is also the friend's sharpest objection: the cheapest purchase is the one you don't make.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad.

  • Opens with "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?", lets you present, steers back if you skip the presentation.
  • Section B has no "partial answer" in the Section A sense. The equivalents are the half-objection ("Hmm, I don't know...") and the push after your first reply ("OK, but...").
  • Possible friend questions the ad cannot answer: "Is it free?", "How do you pay?", "Do they deliver?". Hedge, never invent.
  • Objections, from the ad's own content and from stereotypes, ranked by how likely they are on the confidential list:
    1. Scams and trust between strangers (fake ads, paying for something that never comes). The ad itself invites it with "entre particuliers".
    2. Second-hand breaks, no warranty, no returns (invited by "électroménager", "voitures").
    3. Used means worn or dirty (clothes, shoes, a sofa with bedbugs); "I prefer new".
    4. It takes time: photos, messages, haggling, people who don't show up.
    5. Safety of meeting strangers or giving out your address.
    6. The spending trap: "you end up buying things you don't need; the best saving is buying nothing". Specific to this friend.
    7. "My stuff isn't worth anything, nobody will buy it."
    8. "Everybody already uses the big sites."

2. The hard part

Hardest condition: the only-here, illustrated argument without invented facts. The ad is a generic marketplace whose two selling points are printed on it, and it gives no number, no price, no guarantee. A plain "it's cheaper and better for the planet" is the A2 descriptor (reading and paraphrase, repetitive persuasion). An illustration with a price ("a sofa costs 200 dollars there") states a fact the ad does not print, and the friend can challenge it. The way out is a hypothetical figure tied to the friend ("if you sold everything lying around in your closets, you'd make a few hundred dollars"): concrete, only-here, true as a hypothesis, and it carries the if-clause.

Second hardest: a detailed presentation of a generic site without reading the product list. Solution: compress the list into a range ("from clothes to cars"), reword entre particuliers as "among themselves" and troquer as échanger, and organise the rest around the friend's goal (two levers, signposted).

Where the only-here material comes from:

  • the friend's goal meeting the "sell" verb: the site is not only a cheaper shop, it turns clutter into money;
  • "entre particuliers": the scam objection and its specific counter (hand-over in a public place, pay only after checking);
  • the ad's range of items: the adaptation in reply 2 (start with things you can judge by eye, a sofa, rather than appliances or cars).

Precise words this topic offers (none on the ad): d'occasion, revendre, se débarrasser de, traîner, placard, sous-sol, inconnu, arnaque, méfiant, lieu public, remise en main propre, payer d'avance, sur place, vérifier, essayer, garantie, tomber en panne, en bon état, comme neuf, usé, abîmé, négocier, faire baisser le prix, canapé, manteau d'hiver, poussette, garagiste, décharge / déchetterie (Quebec: écocentre, dépotoir), gaspillage, en un coup d'œil, se faire de l'argent.

3. Designs compared

Times below use the standard conversion (seconds of your speech = English words x 0.58) plus about 3 seconds per examiner line, with the clock started at the friend's greeting.

Design A, conventional four turns (about 185 words, point about 2:00). T1 presentation ending on a cleft ("what I like is that..."); the friend asks a neutral question; T2 two arguments (money, ecology) and "what do you think?"; T3 reply to objection 1; T4 reply to objection 2. Rejected: one more turn and one more argument for no new evidence, and the first objection is the examiner's free choice, so reply 1 has to be improvised against eight possibilities.

Design B, two levers plus a named weak spot (118 words after review, point about 1:20). Chosen. T1 presents the site as two ways to save (buy for less, sell what is lying around), with a hypothetical figure, and ends by naming the scam worry. T2 picks up the friend's word, carries an audible subjunctive and gives a concrete rule. T3 grants part of the push and adapts the plan with an example.

Design C, unconventional: the paradox first (about 150 words, point about 1:35). Open on the friend's own goal as the objection: "You'll say the best way to save is to buy nothing. Exactly, that's why you start by selling." It is the most only-here line this prompt offers. Rejected for the box: an objection you raise and answer inside your own monologue is not a reply (it proves nothing about reacting), the examiner's first real objection is then unpredictable, and it spends your best counter to objection 6. Kept in the reserve.

Design D, unconventional: swap as the headline (about 145 words, point about 1:35). "You can even swap: no money changes hands at all." For a friend who wants to save, a cash-free deal is the sharpest argument. Rejected: it invites "how do you decide what's a fair swap?" and "who checks it?", which only invented facts about the site can answer. Kept in the reserve.

Design E, unconventional: let the examiner's turn carry the evidence (about 130 words, point about 1:25). End T1 with "What's the first thing you'd sell?", so the friend's answer gives you an only-here hook. Rejected: the booklet tells examiners to avoid being pulled into personal storytelling, so the likely reply is a deflection ("Nothing, my stuff is worthless"), which is an unscripted objection on turn 2.

Design F, compression to two turns (about 105 words, point about 1:05). Presentation, both arguments and a self-raised objection in T1, then one real reply. Fails replication: one defence can be luck or rehearsal, and "defends firmly" needs a second one. This is why three turns is the floor for Section B here.

Design Min's words Min turns Point First objection predictable? Main weakness
A ~185 4 ~2:00 no extra turn, improvised reply 1
B 118 3 ~1:20 yes (named) relies on the friend taking the named objection
C ~150 3 ~1:35 no self-answered objection proves nothing reactive
D ~145 3 ~1:35 partly invites questions only invented facts can answer
E ~130 3 ~1:25 no likely deflection, unscripted turn 2
F ~105 2 ~1:05 yes no replication

Why B wins. It is the shortest design that keeps three turns with two replies, and it is the only one where both examiner turns are forecastable: the first because you name it, the second because your first reply leaves one obvious gap (you saw the item on the spot, but what if it breaks later?). It also uses the friend's goal twice, once as the hook and once as the illustration, so the only-here condition is met in the presentation itself.

4. Structure notes, sentence by sentence

# English (box) French it is written to carry Structure / job Risk
T1.1 Great! Super ! answers the greeting in one word (standard 18) none
T1.2 You told me you wanted to save money, so I found the site you need: Venteco. Tu m'as dit que tu voulais faire des économies, alors j'ai trouvé le site qu'il te faut : Venteco. reported speech with tense shift (passé composé + imparfait); relative (qu'il te faut or dont tu as besoin); consequence link alors; ties the ad to the friend; tu low
T1.3 It's a site where people sell, swap or buy among themselves, from clothes to cars. C'est un site où les gens vendent, échangent ou achètent entre eux, des vêtements aux voitures. relative où; rewording (troquer becomes échanger, entre particuliers becomes entre eux, the list becomes a range) low
T1.4 On one hand, you buy second-hand for much less. D'un côté, tu achètes d'occasion beaucoup moins cher. contrast link, signposted (the one C1 glimpse: structured presentation); d'occasion low
T1.5 On the other, if you sold everything lying around in your closets, you'd make a few hundred dollars. De l'autre, si tu vendais tout ce qui traîne dans tes placards, tu gagnerais quelques centaines de dollars. si + imparfait + conditionnel (hard); relative tout ce qui; traîner, placards; the only-here illustrated argument med
T1.6 You'll tell me buying from strangers is risky. Tu vas me dire qu'acheter à des inconnus, c'est risqué. infinitive subject with dislocation (spoken French); inconnus; names the weak spot and is the hand-over (standard 3). Not "I know what you'll say" (standard 2) low
T2.1 I get that scams scare you. Je comprends que les arnaques te fassent peur. audible subjunctive of faire (hard) in a reply; opens on the friend's own noun (standard 17); no gender choice med
T2.2 But you meet the person in a public place. Mais tu retrouves la personne dans un lieu public. concrete counter; lieu public low
T2.3 You only pay if you're happy with it. Tu ne paies que si ça te convient. restriction ne... que si (or seulement si, see Translator notes); the anti-scam rule low-med
T3.1 It's true that with second-hand, you're never a hundred percent sure. C'est vrai qu'avec l'occasion, on n'est jamais sûr à cent pour cent. concession link; generic on; rewords the friend's push (standard 17) without conceding an unprinted fact such as "there's no warranty" (standard 9) low
T3.2 That's why I'd start with things you can check at a glance, like a sofa. C'est pour ça que je commencerais par des choses qu'on peut vérifier en un coup d'œil, comme un canapé. consequence link c'est pour ça que; conditional of proposal; relative que; en un coup d'œil, canapé; the well-judged adaptation low-med

Structure types the English forces, one each: reported speech with tense shift, si + imparfait + conditionnel, audible subjunctive, conditional of proposal, relatives (où, qui, que). Natural French adds dislocation and ne... que. Hard structures: two (if-clause, subjunctive), never in the same sentence. Links (standard 6): contrast (d'un côté / de l'autre), consequence (alors, c'est pour ça que), concession (c'est vrai que, feeding c'est pour ça que). Three relations, one a concession. Mais is plain and not counted.

Precise words counted (standard 12 and 19): d'occasion, traîner, placards, inconnus, lieu public, en un coup d'œil, canapé. Not counted: arnaques (the friend's word), vérifier, risqué, payer (everyday), échanger, vendeur (from verbs the ad prints).

5. Substitution pass (same length, denser)

Before After Gain
"For weeks you've been telling me you want to save money" (11 words) "You told me you wanted to save money" (8) still ties to the friend; the English forces the tense shift
"everything you no longer use" "everything lying around in your closets" traîner (informal, precise) and a concrete image, same length
"if you sold..., you'd easily make" "you'd make" "easily" was a claim, not evidence
"I understand that you're wary" (first draft) "I get that scams scare you" (after review) "wary" would come out as tu te méfies, an -er verb whose subjunctive sounds like the indicative; les arnaques te fassent peur is audible, needs no gender choice and echoes the friend's word; 1 word more
"Nothing forces you to pay in advance. You meet... you check... and you only pay if..." "You meet... You only pay if..." the payment rule carries the anti-scam point; 12 words saved over two passes
"That's why I'd start with things that can't break down, like a sofa or a winter coat" "That's why I'd start with things you can check at a glance, like a sofa" fits a warranty push, a "used is dirty" push and a vague push alike; adds en un coup d'œil; 4 words saved
"Great, thanks!" "Great!" standard 18
"Besides, every sofa bought second-hand is one less at the dump" cut after review a second C1 glimpse (argument of a different kind) on top of the structured presentation; standard 16
"Hi!", "everything", "probably", "just the site", "So the risk is tiny" cut dead words

6. Deletion test

Each piece removed on its own, against the five conditions of the point (frame, capacities, replication, control, credibility). Re-run after review on every changed line.

Piece removed Still holds? What breaks
"Great!" no credibility: ignoring "how are you?" sounds like a recording starting
"You told me you wanted to save money" no frame: the ad is no longer tied to this friend (task goal "take ownership of the situation and put it in context"); the reported-speech structure goes
"so I found the site you need: Venteco" no frame: the ad is never named
T1.3 whole no frame: no presentation of the facts; the examiner steers you back
"from clothes to cars" no presentation drops to a bare definition (B1 "simple presentation"); it is the only detail on what is sold
T1.4 "On one hand..." no the signposted structure, the box's one C1 glimpse, collapses into one lever
"if you sold everything lying around..." clause no no illustrated argument, no hypothesis, no only-here argument
"in your closets" no the illustration loses its concreteness and traîner has nothing to attach to
T1.6 "You'll tell me..." no first objection unpredictable; reply 1 can no longer be prepared
T2.1 "I get that scams scare you" no the reply no longer picks up the friend's word, so it could follow any objection (condition 5, standard 17); the only hard structure inside a reply goes
T2.2 "in a public place" no the counter loses its setting; the payment rule alone does not say how you see the item before paying
T2.3 "You only pay if..." no the scam is not actually answered (meeting someone proves nothing without the payment rule)
T3.1 concession no no concession link (standard 6 requires one in B); "that's why" has nothing to follow
T3.2 adaptation no reply 2 only concedes, so it reads as giving ground
"like a sofa" no the counter has no example (B1 "arguments clear but thin")
"with second-hand" no T3.1 stops being a reword of the friend's push and becomes a stock line
"a hundred percent" no "you're never sure" concedes the whole objection
T3 whole no one defence only: replication (three turns, two replies) fails

Cuts that passed and were made: "Hi!", "everything", "probably", "easily", "or a winter coat", "Nothing forces you to pay in advance"; after review, "just", "thanks", "So the risk is tiny" (no condition needs the reaffirm; consequence is carried by alors and c'est pour ça que), "and you check the item" (the payment rule implies it), and the dump sentence (standard 16).

7. Examiner-reply test

Three plausible replies for each examiner turn; does your next line still make sense?

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
Greeting "Hi! How are you?" (scripted in the booklet) "Hi!" "Hi! You look excited, what's up?" "Great!" fits the first and third; after a bare "Hi!" say "Hi!" instead
After T1 "Exactly! These sites are full of scams." "Hmm, I don't know..." "Risky? No, I just prefer new things." T2 fits the first two: you named the risk yourself, so "scams scare you" also answers a hesitant "Hmm". After the third, use fallback F2
After T2 "Okay, but if it breaks a week later, there's no warranty." "Hmm, I'm still not convinced." "Used clothes? Ugh, I prefer new." T3 fits all three: "never a hundred percent sure" grants the gist of any quality or trust push, and "things you can check at a glance" answers warranty, dirt and vagueness

T3 was rewritten for this test. The first version ("That's why I'd start with things that can't break down") only made sense after the warranty push.

Saying T2.1 as a frame (standard 13). The friend picks the noun. Say Je comprends que + their noun + te fasse(nt) peur: "scams" gives les arnaques te fassent peur, "strangers" gives les inconnus te fassent peur; if they give no noun, que ça te fasse peur. Every version keeps the audible subjunctive.

Fallbacks (for replies outside the table; the box line is replaced, the next box line follows when it fits):

  • F1, after T1, "Is it free?" "I don't know, but I'd be surprised if you had to pay just to look. And nothing forces you to buy: you can start by selling." (Ça m'étonnerait qu'il faille payer juste pour regarder. Et rien ne t'oblige à acheter : tu peux commencer par vendre.) Then T2 when the scam objection comes.
  • F2, after T1, "I prefer new." "I get that second-hand isn't your thing. But you see the item before paying, and you only pay if it looks like new. Honestly, on a sofa, nobody can tell." (Je comprends que l'occasion, ce ne soit pas ton truc.)
  • F3, after T2, "It takes so much time." "Even if it takes you an evening, a few hundred dollars for one evening is worth it. And you can do it all from your phone." (concession même si; "from your phone" is general know-how, not a claim about the site)
  • F4, after T2, "The best way to save is to buy nothing." "That's exactly why I'm telling you to start by selling. You don't spend a cent, and your closets get emptier." (Justement, c'est pour ça que...)
  • F5, after T2, "Meeting strangers is dangerous." "It's true that you never know who you'll meet. That's why I'd come with you the first time, to a café."
  • F6, after T2, "My stuff is worth nothing." "I'd be surprised if nobody wanted any of it. And even if you only sold two or three things, it would already be a start." (Ça m'étonnerait que personne n'en veuille.)

8. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6:

  1. "The presentation is the ad's three verbs and its list of goods. That's reading plus paraphrase."
  2. "One kind of argument, money. Not varied."
  3. "'Meet in a public place' is the stock answer every prep book gives for marketplace ads."
  4. "The hard grammar is not spontaneous: the if-clause sits in the prepared presentation and je comprends que + subjunctive is a memorised chunk."
  5. "The candidate scripted my objection for me ('You'll tell me it's risky'), so the first reply was ready-made."
  6. "Barely over a minute and 118 words. Too little to judge control."

How the box answers it:

  1. Every printed element is reworded (troquer as échanger, entre particuliers as entre eux, the list as a range), and the presentation is reorganised around the friend's goal into two signposted levers with a figure. Reading is the A1 descriptor; this is structure.
  2. Variety is a C1 feature, and the box needs one C1 glimpse on task, which the structured presentation supplies (standard 16). The B2 descriptor asks for illustrated arguments and a firm defence, and both are there: the if-clause figure, the payment rule, the sofa. An argument of another kind (the dump) waits in the reserve for the examiner's "anything else?".
  3. Fixed in part: the counter is the payment rule (tu ne paies que si ça te convient), which is what actually defeats a scam, and the public place is only its setting. The push that follows gets an adaptation nobody can prepare word for word, since it depends on the push.
  4. Fixed: the reply opens on the friend's own word, je comprends que les arnaques te fassent peur, so the subjunctive is audible and built on what was just said. The other reply carries a conditional of proposal and a relative with a modal, built on the friend's push.
  5. Anticipating an objection is a persuasive move in its own right (CEFR B2 "putting a case" includes answering counter-points). The friend's noun still decides how the reply opens.
  6. About 68 seconds of your speech is inside the 60 to 90 seconds the point needs; the box presumes control, it does not prove it. The remaining eight and a half minutes are rated anyway.

After the fixes, the deletion test was run again on every changed line (section 6): nothing left is removable.

9. Say-aloud test

  • Longest French sentences: T1.5 (about 19 French words) and T3.2 (about 20). Each has a natural breath break at its comma (De l'autre, | si tu vendais..., | tu gagnerais...; C'est pour ça que je commencerais | par des choses qu'on peut vérifier en un coup d'œil, | comme un canapé).
  • T1 is about 40 seconds of prepared speech. Say it at your normal pace with a pause at each full stop. Speeding up through it is what raters hear as recited.
  • Words to rehearse until automatic: fassent, traîne, en un coup d'œil.
  • No sentence needs two mood choices; the subjunctive sentence is seven French words long.

10. Count and timing (standard 5 conversion)

  • Min's words: 118 (T1 68, T2 23, T3 27).
  • Seconds of your speech = 118 x 0.58 = about 68 seconds: above the 58-second floor and inside condition 4.
  • Examiner lines: greeting about 2 seconds, the two objections about 3 to 4 seconds each.
  • Point: end of T3, about 1:20 into Section B, clock started at the friend's "Hi! How are you?" (standard 7).

11. Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

  1. Environment: "every sofa bought second-hand is one less at the dump" (un canapé de moins à la décharge), the argument of a different kind for "is that all you've got?".
  2. Swap: no money changes hands at all (tu échanges ton vieux vélo contre autre chose), hedged on how the site handles it.
  3. Haggling: tu peux toujours négocier, faire baisser le prix.
  4. Cars and appliances: tu le fais vérifier par un garagiste avant / tu le fais marcher devant toi (causative faire).
  5. Children's things worn for one season: les enfants grandissent tellement vite.
  6. The spending trap rule: sell before you buy (Design C's line, F4).
  7. The fee question: ça m'étonnerait qu'il faille payer pour regarder (F1).
  8. Space at home: en plus, tu gagnes de la place.
  9. A savings goal: put the money from sales aside for a trip (mettre de côté).
  10. A joint plan: Et si on s'y mettait ensemble samedi ? (suggestion with imperfect).
  11. Local deals: no delivery cost if the seller lives nearby (hedged with "if").

12. Translator notes

Each note only chooses between natural renderings of the English (standard 8); none overrules it.

  • "the site you need": le site qu'il te faut or le site dont tu as besoin; both are relatives.
  • "swap": échanger, not the ad's troquer.
  • "you'd make a few hundred dollars": tu gagnerais (or tu te ferais); not counted as lexis.
  • "I get that": je comprends que, which takes the subjunctive here (fassent).
  • "You only pay if...": tu ne paies que si... rather than seulement si; the structure count does not depend on it.
  • "you're never a hundred percent sure", "things you can check": generic on (on n'est jamais sûr, qu'on peut vérifier).

After review

  • Review fix 1 ("you're wary", inaudible te méfies): applied. T2.1 is now "I get that scams scare you" (que les arnaques te fassent peur); F2 is now "I get that second-hand isn't your thing" (ce ne soit pas); méfiant left the lexis lists.
  • Review cut 1 ("So the risk is tiny"): applied. Du coup and minime left the lists.
  • Review cut 2 ("just"): applied.
  • Standard 1 (audible subjunctives): applied; the only counted subjunctive is fassent; fallback subjunctives (faille, soit, veuille) are audible too.
  • Standard 2 (no indirect "what"): checked; none in the box or the fallbacks.
  • Standard 3 (hand-over): checked; the named weak spot is the hand-over, no question added.
  • Standard 4 (grants and reaffirms): applied; the reaffirm went; the one concession (T3.1) carries the concession link and feeds "that's why".
  • Standard 5 (time floor, one conversion): applied; 118 words, about 68 seconds, above the floor, so dead words went ("thanks", "and you check the item").
  • Standard 6 (links): applied; recounted as contrast, consequence, concession (three relations, one a concession); du coup and en plus no longer in the box.
  • Standard 7 (timing basis): applied; clock from "Hi! How are you?", point about 1:20.
  • Standard 8 (translator notes): applied; section 12 added; se faire no longer counted.
  • Standard 9 (facts not on the ad): checked. The figure is a hypothesis, T2 is advice under the friend's control, and T3.1 avoids conceding "there's no warranty", which the ad does not print.
  • Standards 10 and 11 (Section A only): not applicable.
  • Standard 12 (precise means precise): applied; vérifier, risqué, minime and décharge are no longer counted; seven precise words remain.
  • Standard 13 (lines that depend on the examiner): applied; frame for T2.1 added in section 7.
  • Standard 14 (format): checked; the box opens with the greeting stage direction.
  • Standard 15 (English makes its own French): checked line by line (section 4); "make" now targets gagnerais, so the English and the French agree.
  • Standard 16 (one C1 glimpse): applied; the dump sentence (12 words) was cut because the structured presentation already supplies the glimpse; it moved to the reserve.
  • Standard 17 (replies open on the friend's words): checked; T2 opens on "scams", T3 rewords the push.
  • Standard 18 (one-word greeting): applied; "Great, thanks!" became "Great!".
  • Standard 19 (count only chosen words): applied; arnaques (the friend's word) and échanger / vendeur (from the ad's verbs) are not counted.
  • Net change: 141 words to 118; point from about 1:50 to about 1:20 on the standard basis.
