B-07

BECOME A FILM CRITIC

Culture & the city · Civic/cultural-role · est. #20 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement. You talk about it to a friend who loves cinema very much.

BECOME A FILM CRITIC

Is cinema your passion?

The Academy of Cinema is looking for general-public viewers.

Become a film critic for one year.

Get access to film premieres

Share your ideas with various juries

Get free cinema tickets

Information: www.critique-public-academieducinema.com

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette annonce. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) qui aime beaucoup le cinéma.

DEVENEZ CRITIQUE DE CINÉMA

Le cinéma est votre passion ?

L'Académie Du Cinéma recherche des spectateurs grand public.

Devenez critique de cinéma pendant un an.

Accédez aux avant-premières de films

Partagez vos idées avec différents jurys

Obtenez des places de cinéma gratuites

Renseignements : www.critique-public-academieducinema.com

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, the second objection reply that ends on the proposal to apply together. That is about 1:20 after the examiner's "Hi! How are you?" (about 64 seconds of your speech plus the greeting and two objections).

The box111 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Great! I saw an ad made for you: the Academy of Cinema is recruiting people like us to review films for a year. You'd see them before their release and talk about them with juries, for free. Leaving the cinema, you already spend an hour picking apart the ending. You'll tell me you're not a pro.

[examiner: "Exactly! I'm not a professional critic."]

Not a pro? Precisely: they want the opinion of those who love films, not that of the experts. Besides, at premieres you'd probably run into the director: you could give him your verdict on the ending!

[examiner: "Maybe, but a whole year... I work, I don't have time."]

I get that a year scares you, but you already go every week. So what if we applied together?

Why this, and why nothing less

By the end of your third turn, each of the four Section B ratings has its capacity proved, the task twice, and none of the proof sits in a stock line.

Task (present and debate). Your first turn presents the ad in your own words ("people like us", "before their release") and says why you're telling this friend ("made for you"). Then one illustrated argument that only works for a film-critic ad: leaving the cinema, the friend already spends an hour picking apart the ending, so this is a job the friend already does. Then you defend twice, and each reply opens on the friend's words. Reply 1 echoes "Not a pro?", turns it around ("Precisely") and adds a second argument of a different kind: you'd probably run into the director at a premiere and could give your verdict on the ending, a callback to your first turn, so the rater hears an exchange that holds together. That second kind of argument is the one C1 glimpse the task needs. Reply 2 grants the worry about a year, counters with a fact about the friend, and moves the plan forward.

Syntax. Six different marked structures (a marked structure is a grammar form that only stronger speakers use reliably): en (en parler), a gérondif with passer une heure à, ceux qui / celui des, an audible subjunctive (fasse), y, and et si on + imperfect. Four sit in replies, which can't have been memorised. No sentence carries more than one hard structure. Links joining clauses or turns, three relations: addition (en plus), concession (je comprends que..., mais), consequence (alors).

Lexis. Seven precise words, none on the ad, each produced by the English (the translator notes in Working only pick between natural renderings): recruter, sortie, décortiquer, croiser, réalisateur, verdict, postuler. No clichés. Pas un pro ? and t'es pas carry the informal register.

Delivery. No word you'd have to search for. Every sentence is one breath group, or two split at a colon. 111 English words is about 64 seconds of your speech (words x 0.58), enough for the raters to presume control.

Why nothing can go. "Great!" answers the examiner's question. "Made for you" and "people like us" tie the ad to the friend and set up the first objection. The second sentence carries the ad's three benefits; without it the presentation isn't detailed. The ending sentence is your only illustrated argument, and "already" is what makes it an argument. "You'll tell me you're not a pro" makes the box scriptable: you name the objection the ad itself answers, so the examiner very likely takes it, and the objection a one-year commitment invites anyway (time) is what the examiner reaches for next. Both replies are then prepared and still real replies. "Not a pro?" makes reply 1 audibly a reply; "Precisely" turns the objection into your argument. "Besides" is the addition link. The director line carries croiser, réalisateur, verdict, the callback and about 10 seconds; "probably" is there because the ad doesn't say who comes to premieres. In reply 2, the concession carries the subjunctive and the concession link, the counter keeps you above the B1 "but it's nice!", "So" is the consequence link, and the proposal it introduces keeps the plan alive, so the next objection has to be a new one. Without that last sentence the box has two link relations, not three.

The next cut. None is free. Dropping the director line takes you to 93 words, about 54 seconds (under the floor), and to four precise words. Dropping the proposal takes the consequence link with it. Trimming the presentation leaves it less than detailed.

What it assumes about the rest. About eight and a half minutes remain. You keep tu, answer each new objection with something you haven't said yet (the reserve in Working has eight items), and keep plain, correct French. Pro becomes une pro if the examiner plays a woman. If the friend says nothing guarantees meeting the director, Working has the fallback. If the objections come in a different order, the two replies swap, each opening on what the friend actually said.

The working for this prompt

1. Dissection

Printed on the ad (no vocabulary evidence): devenez critique de cinéma, passion, Académie du cinéma, recherche, spectateurs, grand public, pendant un an, accédez, avant-premières, films, partagez vos idées, différents jurys, obtenez, places de cinéma gratuites, renseignements, the website.

Left unsaid: the workload (how many films a week, written or spoken reviews, deadlines); selection (how to apply, how many places, a sample review?); money (paid or not, travel to premieres); where and when (which city, evenings?); what the juries are (festival juries, panels?); whether your name is published; whether you must see every genre; whether the opinions really count or it's marketing; whether you can stop mid-year; whether two friends can both apply; who attends the premieres.

Who is on the other end: a friend who loves cinema. That takes "I don't like films" off the table, so every objection has to be about the scheme, not about cinema. Register: tu.

The examiner's predictable moves. Restates the roles ("I won't agree, you'll have to convince me"), opens with "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?", waits for the presentation, then objects. Section B has no partial answers; its version is the push ("develop that", "is that your only argument?", refusing a repeated argument, pushing the newest thin argument). Ranked guess at the objections (confidential list plus stereotypes):

  1. Time: a year is long, I work (universal, and the ad prints "one year"). Near-certain at some point.
  2. Not a pro: I can't write reviews, I don't know the technical words (the ad's title invites it; its own "grand public" answers it).
  3. It ruins the pleasure: films become homework.
  4. Having to watch films I'd hate (horror, duds).
  5. Shy in front of juries or professionals.
  6. It's a trick: free advertising, unpaid work, our opinions won't count.
  7. Logistics: premieres far away, late evenings. Information questions the friend may slip in first: is it paid? how do you apply? where?

2. The hard part

Argument variety without the ad's words. The three benefits are printed, and "you love cinema" is the ad's own headline ("Is cinema your passion?"). The obvious arguments carry no evidence and sound like reading. The only-here material has to come from the friend's habits and from what you know about cinema: the friend already reviews every film on the way out; the scheme wants non-experts (the ad's logic, your reasoning); premieres probably bring the director; the time is already being spent at the cinema.

Second hardest: predictable objections. Time is near-certain, but its timing isn't. Naming objection 2 yourself fixes the order (see design A).

Precise words this topic offers (none on the ad): réalisateur, sortie (en salle), salle, séance, bande-annonce, scénario, décortiquer, croiser, verdict, navet (informal, a bad film), divulgâcher, VO, postuler, candidature, s'engager, bouche-à-oreille.

3. Designs compared

Design Idea Words to point How it holds up Verdict
A. Named weak spot Present, one illustrated argument, then name the ad-specific objection ("you'll tell me you're not a pro"). Keep the universal one (time) for the examiner's own pick. 111 Both objections predictable; the friend portrait in turn 1 feeds every later argument. Chosen
B. Habit question first Open with "Do you still pick apart every ending?", let the friend confirm, then present, later quote the friend's words back. about 120 plus an extra examiner turn Strong listening evidence, but the Section B examiner waits for a presentation; an open question first can draw "what are you on about?" or a "no", and adds 10 to 15 seconds. Rejected; its callback kept inside your own speech (the ending)
C. Forced-choice hand-over "What puts you off more, a year of commitment or facing juries?" Two probes in one turn. about 110 Sounds staged; the examiner can refuse the frame or say "both"; the jury counter is weak. Rejected
D. Inoculation Answer time and "not a pro" inside the presentation. about 120 before any objection Spends your best counters in the monologue, where they prove least; the examiner then goes to items 3 to 6, which you can't predict. Rejected
E. Joint plan first Present it as "let's both apply" from turn 1, so every objection meets "we". about 105 Spends the proposal (the best second-reply move) up front and invites "go on your own then". Rejected
F. Bare minimum Presentation plus one reply. about 70 Fails replication (two replies) and the time floor (about 40 seconds). Rejected

Why A wins: seven words ("You'll tell me you're not a pro") make both objections predictable, the hard structures stay in the replies, and the portrait set in turn 1 (the hour spent on the ending) makes the later arguments only-here and gives reply 1 its callback. The unconventional part is the order: name the objection the ad already answers, leave the universal one for the examiner, and both replies can be prepared while still being replies.

4. Sentence notes (the French each English line is written to carry)

# English Target French Structures and words
S1 Great! I saw an ad made for you: the Academy of Cinema is recruiting people like us to review films for a year. Super ! J'ai vu une annonce faite pour toi : l'Académie du cinéma recrute des gens comme nous pour critiquer des films pendant un an. One-word greeting, then the ad. Participle agreement (faite), recruter. "People like us" rewords "grand public".
S2 You'd see them before their release and talk about them with juries, for free. Tu les verrais avant leur sortie et tu en parlerais avec des jurys, gratuitement. Conditional of hypothesis without si; en in the everyday form en parler; "release" produces the noun sortie.
S3 Leaving the cinema, you already spend an hour picking apart the ending. En sortant du cinéma, tu passes déjà une heure à décortiquer la fin. Gérondif; passer + duration + à + infinitive; décortiquer. Only-here, illustrated; "already" makes it an argument.
S4 You'll tell me you're not a pro. Tu vas me dire que t'es pas un pro. Near future + que; informal dropped ne. The hand-over: no question after it.
S5 Not a pro? Precisely: they want the opinion of those who love films, not that of the experts. Pas un pro ? Justement : ils veulent l'avis de ceux qui aiment les films, pas celui des experts. Opens on the friend's words; justement turns the objection; "those who" produces ceux qui, "that of" produces celui des (medium).
S6 Besides, at premieres you'd probably run into the director: you could give him your verdict on the ending! En plus, aux avant-premières, tu croiserais sûrement le réalisateur : tu pourrais lui donner ton verdict sur la fin ! Addition link; conditional; lui; croiser, réalisateur, verdict. Hedged ("probably", sûrement). New argument plus callback to S3.
S7 I get that a year scares you, but you already go every week. Je comprends qu'un an, ça te fasse peur, mais tu y vas déjà toutes les semaines. Opens on the friend's words ("a year"); concession; audible subjunctive fasse (the one hard structure here); y (French aller with no place needs it).
S8 So what if we applied together? Alors, et si on postulait ensemble ? Consequence link alors; si + imperfect as a suggestion (syntax, not counted as a link); postuler.

Substitution pass (same length or shorter, denser): "the general public" became "people like us"; "people who pay for their ticket" became "those who love films" (removes a clash with the free tickets, points at the friend, and "those who" produces ceux qui); "after every film" became "leaving the cinema" (gérondif); "it scares you to commit for a year" became "a year scares you" (same subjunctive, fewer words). After review: "before they come out" became "before their release" (produces sortie, one word fewer); "tell him what you think of" became "give him your verdict on" (no indirect "what", one word fewer); "discuss them" became "talk about them" (everyday en parler); "meet" became "run into" (adds croiser); "You already do it:" folded into S3; "just" cut; "the pros" became "the experts" (avoids saying "pro" three times once "Not a pro?" opens the reply). The first deletion test removed "Listen", "Basically", "honestly", "always", "I bet" and "whole" (an earlier draft ran to 117 words).

5. Deletion test (re-run after review; the box is 7 words above the floor of about 104, so dead words go)

Piece What breaks without it Verdict
"Great!" Ignores "tu vas bien ?"; the opening sounds launched (condition 5). Standard 18 wants exactly this one word. Stays
"I saw an ad made for you:" Ad not identified; no reason for telling this friend (condition 1). Stays
"the Academy ... people like us ... for a year" Who, what and how long are missing; "people like us" is the frame the first objection pushes against; "for a year" sets up objection 2. Stays
S2 The ad's three benefits are missing; the presentation is no longer detailed (condition 2, task). en and sortie gone. Stays
"for free" One of the three benefits missing. Stays
S3 Only illustrated argument gone; the only-here argument and the gérondif gone; the callback in S6 loses its anchor. Stays
"already" (S3) The sentence becomes a description; the argument (the friend already does this job) is lost. Stays
"You already do it:" (old S3 start) Nothing: "already" inside S3 carries the same argument. Gone after review
S4 First objection becomes unpredictable; reply 1 can't be prepared; turn 1 ends without handing over. Stays
"Not a pro?" Reply 1 no longer opens on the friend's words (standard 17); sounds recited. Stays
"Precisely:" The objection is no longer turned into the argument; "Not a pro? They want..." reads as a non sequitur. Stays
S5 core No counter to the objection; ceux qui / celui des gone. Stays
"just" (old S5) Nothing: the contrast with "the experts" carries it. Gone after review
"Besides," Addition link gone: two link relations left (condition 2, standard 6). Stays
S6 93 words (about 54 seconds, under the floor), four precise words, reply 1 only restates the ad, the callback gone. Stays
"probably" (S6) An unhedged fact the ad doesn't print, a target for the examiner (standard 9). Stays
"I get that a year scares you," No concession anywhere in the replies (standard 4, link condition); the subjunctive gone; reply 2 no longer opens on the friend's words. Stays
"but you already go every week" Concession with no counter: the B1 "but it's nice!" rung; y gone. Stays
"So" (S8) Consequence link gone: two link relations left. Stays
"what if we applied together?" "So" has nothing to join; si + imperfect and postuler gone; the plan stops moving. Stays

6. Three replies per examiner turn

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Your next line
Opening "Hi! How are you?" "Hey!" "Hi! You look excited." "Great!" fits all three.
Objection 1 "Exactly! I'm not a professional critic." "Hmm, I don't know..." / "And I couldn't write reviews." "No, my problem is the year, I don't have time." Box line after the first. After "I don't know": "You're not sure? Precisely: ..." After the writing variant: "You couldn't write? Precisely: they want the opinion of those who love films..." (the ad asks you to share ideas with juries, not to write). After the third, say reply 2 now and keep S5 for when the competence worry comes. If the friend asks "is it paid?": "No, but the tickets are free, and they want the opinion of those who love films, not that of the experts." Then S6.
Push on S6 "Nothing says I'd meet the director." "Hmm, maybe." "Directors don't listen to the public." "Maybe not him, but the juries will listen to you: that's their job." (Peut-être pas lui, mais les jurys t'écouteront : c'est leur rôle.) Then the next argument.
Objection 2 "A whole year... I work, I don't have time." "I'm still not sure." "I'd have to watch horror films and duds." / "It's a trick, free advertising." / "Writing every week is homework." Box line after the first. Partial: "I get that it scares you a bit, but you already go every week." Horror: "I get that horror scares you, but even if you land on a dud now and then, you'll find films you'd never have picked." Trick: "A trick? I'd be surprised if it were one, it's the Academy." (ça m'étonnerait que ce soit le cas). Homework: "I get that writing scares you, but you already text me a review after every film." Then S8 in every case.

Frames for lines that echo the examiner (standard 13). Reply 1 opens with the friend's own noun or worry as a short question, then "Precisely:" ("Not a pro?", "You couldn't write?"). Reply 2 opens "I get that [the friend's worry] scares you, but..." with the friend's noun in the slot ("a year", "horror", "writing"). S8 works after every version of objection 2: a joint plan is at right angles to any worry.

7. The sceptical rater

Their case for a 6. (1) The first objection was planted, so reply 1 is a prepared line. (2) The presentation is the ad's bullets, nothing beyond. (3) Reply 2 is short and its proposal dodges the time problem. (4) Each argument gets one line; only one is illustrated. (5) 64 seconds is thin evidence of control. (6) "People who pay for their ticket" (earlier draft) contradicts the free tickets: a careless speaker.

How the box answers. (1) Reply 1 opens on the friend's own words, naming the friend's objection is a debate move (anticipation), and reply 1's second half is a new argument with a callback, which a stock answer can't contain; reply 2 answers an objection you didn't name. (2) The presentation is the ad reworded and tied to the friend, which is the B2 descriptor; development comes through the arguments, not through facts the ad doesn't print. (3) The proposal follows from the counter: the time is already spent at the cinema, so doing it together costs nothing new; "So" makes that consequence audible. "That way, it'd be our cinema night, like now" (10 words) was considered; the deletion test removes it, so it sits in the reserve as the answer to "that changes nothing". (4) Three of four arguments carry a concrete detail (the hour after the film, the director at premieres, every week): the grid's "illustrated or detailed". (5) 64 seconds is inside the 60 to 90 window, 7 words above the floor; this is the thinnest margin, which is why the director line stays. (6) Fixed: S5 now says "those who love films".

The case fails on all six. Deletion test re-run after the fixes: section 5.

8. Say-aloud test

Every sentence is 18 English words or fewer except S1 (23), which splits at the colon into 8 and 15. Pause points: the colons in S1 and S6, the comma after the gérondif in S3, the question mark after "Not a pro?", the comma in S7, the comma after alors. Hardest words to say: décortiquer, parlerais, croiserais, réalisateur, avant-premières; all common and regular. No sentence stacks two mood choices.

9. Count and timing (standard conversion: seconds = English words x 0.58)

111 English words, about 64 seconds of your speech. Clock from the examiner's "Hi! How are you?": greeting about 2 seconds; turn 1 (56 words) about 32 seconds, to 0:34; objection 1 about 4, to 0:38; reply 1 (36 words) about 21, to 0:59; objection 2 about 6, to 1:05; reply 2 (19 words) about 11, to 1:16. Point at about 1:20, up to about 1:35 if the examiner's turns run long. Floor: about 104 words (60 seconds); the box is 7 words above it.

10. Reserve (after the point, not part of the box)

  • Films you'd never pick: "même si tu tombes sur un navet de temps en temps, tu découvriras des films que tu n'aurais jamais choisis."
  • No spoilers: at a premiere nobody can spoil the ending for you (personne ne pourra te divulgâcher la fin).
  • Word of mouth: you'd be the one telling everyone which films are worth it (quels films valent le coup; le bouche-à-oreille).
  • Follow-up to the proposal: "That way, it'd be our cinema night, like now."
  • Juries: "I get that you're shy (que tu sois timide), but it's a discussion, not an exam."
  • Trick or unpaid work: "I'd be surprised if it were a scam (ça m'étonnerait que ce soit une arnaque), it's the Academy of Cinema."
  • Pleasure ruined: "On the contrary, you'd watch films differently, and you'd notice things nobody else sees."
  • Next step: "We look at their site tonight and apply together?"

Translator notes

Each note picks between natural renderings of the English; none overrules it.

  • S2: "before their release" as avant leur sortie.
  • S3: "picking apart" as décortiquer (rather than analyser en détail).
  • S4: "you're not a pro" as t'es pas un pro (une pro for a woman).
  • S5: "opinion" as avis; "those who" as ceux qui; "that of" as celui des.
  • S6: "run into" as croiser (rather than tomber sur); "probably" as sûrement; "director" as réalisateur (not directeur).
  • S7: "a year scares you" as qu'un an, ça te fasse peur; "you already go" as tu y vas déjà.
  • S8: "So" as Alors; "applied" as postuler.

After review

Review items (reviews/B2.md):

  • Fix 1 (indirect "what" in S6): applied. "tell him what you think of the ending" became "give him your verdict on the ending"; ce que no longer counted, verdict added.
  • Fix 2 (links and ceux qui): applied. The negated noun phrase is no longer a link; S5 now says "those who", so the English produces ceux qui as well as celui des.
  • Suggestion 1 ("So"): applied. It is now one of the three required link relations.
  • Suggestion 2 (fold "You already do it"): applied in the second pass. On the standard conversion the box stays above the floor (111 words, about 64 seconds), so the dead words go.
  • Suggestion 3 ("talk about them"): applied, for the everyday en parler.
  • Suggestion 4 (director push): applied. "probably" hedges S6, and the reply table has a fallback for the push.
  • Suggestion 5 (writing in the stage direction): applied. The stage direction drops "I couldn't write reviews"; the writing variant is in the reply table.

Standards (build/STANDARDS.md, 19 items):

  • 1 Audible subjunctives: checked; the only counted subjunctive is fasse.
  • 2 No indirect "what": applied in S6 and in the reserve ("which films are worth it").
  • 3 Hand-over: checked; S4 names the weak spot with no question after it.
  • 4 Grants and reaffirms: checked; one concession, in reply 2, carrying the subjunctive and the concession link; reply 1 has no grant line.
  • 5 Time floor, one conversion: applied. 111 words x 0.58 = about 64 seconds, 7 words above the floor; dead words removed ("You already do it", "just").
  • 6 Links: applied. Three relations (addition, concession, consequence); justement and the si clause are not counted.
  • 7 Timing basis: applied; clock from "Hi! How are you?", point about 1:20.
  • 8 Lexis the English doesn't produce: applied; every counted word comes from the English, and the translator notes only choose between natural renderings.
  • 9 Facts not on the ad: applied; "probably" on the director line.
  • 10 and 11: Section A only; not applicable.
  • 12 Precise means precise: applied; pro, avis, experts and "free" removed from the count; seven left.
  • 13 Lines that depend on the examiner: applied; frames for both reply openers in section 6.
  • 14 Format: checked; the box opens with the B greeting.
  • 15 English makes its own French: applied; "release", "those who", "that of", "run into" and "talk about them" chosen so the English produces the target French.
  • 16 One C1 glimpse: applied; the Why names one (the second kind of argument) and no words are spent on another.
  • 17 Replies open on the friend's words: applied; reply 1 now opens "Not a pro?" (3 words added); reply 2 already opened on "a year".
  • 18 One-word greeting: checked; "Great!".
  • 19 Count only chosen precise words: applied; nothing the examiner supplies or the ad prints is counted.

B-26

SPORTY WEEKEND

Sport, adventure & well-being · Adventure/sport-getaway · est. #17 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement in the newspaper. You talk about it with a friend who likes sport.

SPORTY WEEKEND

SPORTY WEEKENDS

Sporty weekends, tailor-made, adapted to your wishes

Alone, as a couple or among friends

Choose your destination: sea or mountain

Hiking, snowboarding, skiing, climbing

kayaking, surfing, diving, boating…

And many other activities to discover!

Contact us at: www.weekends-sportifs.com

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité dans le journal. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) qui aime le sport.

WEEK-END SPORTIF

WEEK-ENDS SPORTIFS

Weekends sportifs, sur mesure, adaptés à vos envies

Seul, en couple ou entre amis

Choisissez votre destination : mer ou montagne

Randonnée, snowboard, ski, escalade

kayak, surf, plongée, bateau…

Et bien d’autres activités à découvrir !

Contactez-nous sur : www.weekends-sportifs.com

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, your reply to the friend's second objection (it ends "so that you can compare"). On the standard basis that is about 58 seconds of your speech and about 1:10 into Section B, counted from the friend's "Hi! How are you?" (1:05 to 1:25, depending on how long the friend's turns run).

The box100 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Great! I found a company that organises sports weekends by the sea or in the mountains, from board sports to diving. What I like is that it's tailor-made. You could do a hard climbing route, whereas I'd take an intro class. The catch is that they don't give prices.

[examiner: "Exactly. Tailor-made, that's going to cost a fortune."]

It's true it's probably not cheap. But if it was too expensive, we'd choose the sea: no ski pass to pay.

[examiner: "Even so, I can organise that myself, for less."]

Organise it yourself? But finding a diving instructor and renting surfboards for one weekend is a hassle. So let's ask them for a quote, so that you can compare.

Why this, and why nothing less

The friend does what the examiner's booklet says, twice, and both times you answer with something only this ad allows.

Task (present and debate). Your first turn presents the ad without reading its list of eight sports: a company, sea or mountain, "from board sports to diving". Then it follows an audible plan: what I like (tailor-made, shown by a hard climbing route for the friend and an intro class for you), then the catch (no prices). A plan like that is the "structured presentation" the grid puts at C1, and it is the box's one C1 glimpse on this criterion. The catch is also your hand-over: it makes price the friend's most likely first objection, so your second turn can be prepared and still answer what was said. That turn takes up the friend's words, concedes, and gives a way out only this ad offers (choose the sea, so no ski pass). Your third turn repeats the friend's words ("organise it yourself?"), counters with a detail built on your own previous answer (at the sea: a diving instructor and surfboards to find, for a single weekend), and proposes a quote so the friend can test their own "for less". Two replies that hold the position under pressure: "defends with determination" (B2).

Syntax. Eight structures in about 105 French words, one of each: a relative clause (une boîte qui organise), a cleft (ce qui me plaît, c'est que: a sentence split to put one part in focus), the conditional (tu pourrais, je prendrais), a contrast clause (alors que), a concession (c'est vrai que..., mais), si + imperfect + conditional, an infinitive as subject (trouver..., c'est la galère), and pour que + a subjunctive you can hear (puisses). The two hard ones sit in replies, where a rater cannot put them down to memorising. Links between clauses: contrast (whereas), concession (it's true that... but), consequence (so).

Lexis. Seven precise words, none printed on the ad: sports de glisse, voie, cours d'initiation, forfait, moniteur de plongée, planches de surf, devis. The informal register comes from boîte, le hic, pas donné and la galère (the translator notes in Working pick these among the natural renderings of the English). No clichés.

Delivery. The box cannot prove fluency; it only has to avoid forcing a pause. Every sentence is one or two breath groups, and no word is rare enough to stall on.

Why nothing can go. At 100 words the box is at the time floor (about 58 seconds of your speech), so a cut no longer brings the point earlier; the box would just end before it. Piece by piece: "Great!" answers the friend's greeting. The company sentence is the presentation; without it there is only an opinion. "What I like is that it's tailor-made" is the plus of the plan and the only-here anchor ("only-here" = it would make no sense for another ad); the climbing sentence illustrates it and carries the contrast link. The catch is the hand-over. "It's true it's probably not cheap" is the box's only concession and the uptake of the friend's words; the sea sentence is the counter. "Organise it yourself?" makes the third turn audibly a reply. The instructor sentence is the second defence. The quote sentence carries the consequence link, the second hard structure and devis.

The next cut. None is available. Any piece you remove either breaks a condition (a link, the concession, the hand-over, the illustration, a reply) or takes the box under 58 seconds. Below about 100 words one of the two replies would have to go, and "defends firmly" would rest on a single reply, which is the lower B2 box (NCLC 6).

What the box assumes. That the friend opens with the usual greeting and objects on price once you name the catch; the fallbacks for other objections are in Working. After the point you have nearly nine minutes left, and you need to sound like a solid NCLC 6 on a quiet day: answer each new objection with a new argument (the reserve has ten), use the sea and ski pass line at most once more, and never give in.

The working for this prompt

1. Dissection

What the ad prints (no lexis credit for any of it): week-ends sportifs, sur mesure, adaptés à vos envies, seul, en couple, entre amis, destination, mer, montagne, randonnée, snowboard, ski, escalade, kayak, surf, plongée, bateau, activités, découvrir, contactez-nous. Saying "tailor-made" or "sea or mountain" is naming the ad, not showing range.

What it leaves unsaid:

  • Price: no figure at all, and "tailor-made" sounds expensive. This is the ad's obvious weak spot.
  • Where exactly: which coast, which mountains, how far from home, how you get there.
  • What is included: accommodation, transport, meals, gear rental, instructors or guides, insurance.
  • Level: whether beginners and experienced people are both catered for.
  • Season: skiing and snowboarding need winter, surfing and diving lean towards summer. The ad mixes them.
  • Group size, dates, booking and cancellation terms, safety. The only contact is a website.

Who is on the other end: a friend who likes sport, tu, informal. That removes the stock objections ("I'm not sporty", "too tiring") and moves the debate to value: "I don't need a company for that", "it's expensive", "I already have my sport and my club", "a weekend is too short to travel", "diving or climbing is dangerous", "the weather", "I'd rather do X".

The examiner's predictable moves (booklet): opens with Salut ! Tu vas bien ?; steers you back if you skip the presentation; has said beforehand that they will disagree; draws objections from a confidential list for this subject plus common stereotypes; pushes thin arguments ("develop that") and asks for new ones; may ask an information question first (c'est où ? c'est combien ?); does not block with objections that cannot be answered.

My estimates of the first objection (reasoning, not data). Unprompted: price about 35%, "I can do it myself" about 25%, time or distance about 15%, danger about 10%, other about 15%. After you name the price catch yourself: price about 65%. Second objection, after the price exchange: "I can do it myself, for less" about 40% (it follows from money talk), "but I want the mountains / diving costs money too" about 15%, "a weekend is too short" about 15%, danger about 15%, other about 15%.

2. The hard part

  • Reading risk. Most of the ad is a list of eight activities. Reading or reciting the list is how the grid describes A1 and A2 in Section B. The presentation has to compress the list into one category and a range: des sports de glisse à la plongée.
  • The hardest condition here is replication with only-here content. Because the friend likes sport, the easy counters ("it's not that tiring") are gone, and the ad gives no facts beyond the list, so every counter has to come from the ad's structure (tailor-made, alone/couple/friends, sea or mountain, a weekend) or from general knowledge, without inventing facts about the company.
  • Where the only-here arguments come from:
    1. tailor-made + "as a couple or among friends": two different levels in one trip;
    2. sea or mountain: a cost lever (no ski pass at the sea) and a season lever;
    3. the activities that need supervision and gear (diving, surfing, climbing): the answer to "I can do it myself" is an instructor and rentals to find;
    4. the weekend format: two days are too short to spend them organising.
  • Precise words this topic offers, none on the ad: sports de glisse, sports nautiques, voie (d'escalade), cours d'initiation, moniteur (de plongée), forfait (de ski), remontées mécaniques, location de matériel, planche (de surf), combinaison, baptême de plongée, dénivelé, hébergement, devis, partager les frais, poser des congés, courbatures, prévisions météo. Informal: une boîte, le hic, pas donné, c'est la galère, du coup, franchement.

3. Designs compared

Design Words to the point Point Strength Weakness
A. Name the catch, then chain on the sea (chosen) 100 end of turn 3, about 1:10 first objection made predictable; the second reply builds on the first (sea, then diving and surf), so the exchange visibly holds together; three argument kinds in three turns the second objection is not called, so turn 3 has fallbacks (section 8)
B. Choice hand-over ("sea or mountain, which would you pick?") about 130 end of turn 4, about 1:45 the friend's answer gives a natural hook the friend's choice turn shows nothing about you; objections arrive one turn later; an extra turn of planning talk carries little evidence
C. Level gambit (self-description: "I'm a beginner, you're the strong one", keep the two-level argument for the reply to "our levels are too different") about 100 if the friend takes the bait, about 125 if not turn 3 or 4 puts the only-here argument in a reply, where it counts more "different levels" is less likely to be on the examiner's list than price; "you're much stronger than me" is a claim about the friend the examiner can deny; if the bait is ignored you still need the price counter
D. Pre-empt "you can do it yourself" in turn 1 about 110 turn 3 shows anticipation spends the best counter in a turn nobody objected to (no reactive credit), and leaves the first objection open
E. Name two catches (no prices, only a weekend) about 110 turn 3 both objections predictable hands the friend two weak spots at once; the "too short" counter is weak because the ad gives no distances

Why A wins. It is the shortest design that keeps both replies reactive and prepared at the same time. Naming the catch is one sentence (8 words) that converts the most likely objection into a near-certain one, and it is the hand-over. The sea answer in turn 2 then sets up turn 3: whatever the friend says next, you are already talking about the sea, so "a diving instructor and surfboards" follows from your own last turn, and the rater hears an exchange that hangs together, not two separate prepared answers.

Versions of A. v1: 145 words. v2: 113 (deletion test). v3: 128, first submission (proposal and echo put back). v4: 100, after the red-team review and STANDARDS.md (section 13).

4. Structure notes, sentence by sentence

# English Natural French What it carries
S1 Great! I found a company that organises sports weekends by the sea or in the mountains, from board sports to diving. Super ! J'ai trouvé une boîte qui organise des week-ends sportifs à la mer ou à la montagne, des sports de glisse à la plongée. one-word greeting; relative clause; the list compressed into a category and a range (no reading); sports de glisse
S2 What I like is that it's tailor-made. Ce qui me plaît, c'est que c'est sur mesure. (or Ce que j'aime, c'est que...) cleft; the plus beat of the plan; the only-here anchor
S3 You could do a hard climbing route, whereas I'd take an intro class. Tu pourrais faire une voie difficile, alors que je prendrais un cours d'initiation. conditional (hedged scenario, so no invented fact); contrast link alors que; voie, cours d'initiation
S4 The catch is that they don't give prices. Le hic, c'est qu'ils ne donnent pas les prix. the minus beat; the hand-over; calls the probe
S5 It's true it's probably not cheap. C'est vrai que c'est sûrement pas donné. uptake (rewords the friend's "cost a fortune"); first half of the concession
S6 But if it was too expensive, we'd choose the sea: no ski pass to pay. Mais si c'était trop cher, on choisirait la mer : pas de forfait de ski à payer. second half of the concession; si + imperfect + conditional (hard, in a reply); only-here counter; forfait
S7 Organise it yourself? L'organiser toi-même ? opens the second reply on the friend's words
S8 But finding a diving instructor and renting surfboards for one weekend is a hassle. Mais trouver un moniteur de plongée et louer des planches de surf pour un seul week-end, c'est la galère. infinitive subject picked up by c'est; counter built on S6's sea; moniteur de plongée, planches de surf
S9 So let's ask them for a quote, so that you can compare. Alors demandons-leur un devis, pour que tu puisses comparer. consequence link; pour que + audible subjunctive (two different subjects, so it cannot collapse into pour pouvoir); devis; answers the friend's "for less"

Load check: no sentence has more than two marked structures ("marked structure" = a grammar form that only stronger speakers use reliably), none has two hard ones, none depends on two mood choices. S1 is the longest at 20 English words, with one marked structure.

5. Translator notes

Each note only chooses between natural renderings of the English; none overrules it.

  • "company": une boîte (informal; une entreprise is also correct).
  • "board sports": les sports de glisse (the usual French term; not sports de planche).
  • "climbing route": une voie (d'escalade), not un parcours.
  • "intro class": un cours d'initiation.
  • "The catch is that": Le hic, c'est que (or Le problème, c'est que).
  • "probably not cheap": sûrement pas donné (or pas bon marché).
  • "a hassle": c'est la galère.
  • "we'd choose the sea": on choisirait la mer (not on prendrait la mer, which means setting sail).
  • "so that you can compare": pour que tu puisses comparer (keep two subjects so the subjunctive is heard).

6. Substitution pass (same length, denser)

  • "the problem" became "the catch" (le hic).
  • "a beginners' class" became "an intro class" (cours d'initiation): same length, a precise word instead of an everyday one.
  • "while" (pendant que, time) became "whereas" (alors que, contrast): same length, and now a link that counts.
  • "Then let's look at their website tonight" became "So let's ask them for a quote": shorter, a consequence link, a precise word (devis), and a direct answer to the friend's "for less".
  • "expensive" in the concession became "not cheap" (pas donné).

7. Coverage audit (section C of the principles)

  1. Frame: tu from the first line; the ad identified and presented in your own words (S1 to S4), not read.
  2. Capacities:
    • Task: two reactive turns at the B2 rung, each opening on the friend's words, the first conceding, both countering with a concrete detail and holding the position; illustrated argument (S3); one C1 glimpse: the audible plan of the presentation (facts, what I like, the catch).
    • Syntax: 8 different structure types (S1 relative, S2 cleft, S3 conditional and alors que, S5-S6 concession, S6 si + imperfect + conditional, S8 infinitive subject, S9 pour que + subjunctive); four inside replies.
    • Links (connectors joining clauses or turns): contrast (S3 alors que), concession (S5-S6 c'est vrai que... mais), consequence (S9 alors). The si clause is counted as syntax only.
    • Lexis: 7 precise words not on the ad (sports de glisse, voie, cours d'initiation, forfait, moniteur de plongée, planches de surf, devis); informal boîte, le hic, pas donné, la galère; no clichés, no English words. Not counted: débutant, louer, site, comparer, trop cher (everyday), fortune and organiser (the friend's words).
    • Delivery: no rare or hard-to-say word.
  3. Replication: turn 1 (cleft and an illustrated only-here argument), turn 2 (si clause, only-here counter), turn 3 (infinitive subject, subjunctive, devis). Three turns, two of them replies.
  4. Control presumed: 100 English words x 0.58 = about 58 seconds of your speech (the floor).
  5. Credibility: both replies open on the friend's words (S5 rewords "cost a fortune", S7 repeats "organise it yourself"); turn 3 follows from your own turn 2.

8. Deletion test (re-run on the v4 box)

The box is at the floor (100 words, 58 seconds), so any deletion also takes it under condition 4. The table records what else breaks.

Piece Removed, what breaks Verdict
"Great!" your first word ignores the friend's greeting (standard 18 keeps one word) keep
S1 whole no presentation, only an opinion (condition 1) keep
S1 "by the sea or in the mountains" S6's sea counter comes from nowhere; the ad's main choice is missing keep
S1 "from board sports to diving" the activities, which are most of the ad, are missing; loses sports de glisse keep
S2 the climbing sentence has no point; the plan loses its plus; no cleft; the argument is no longer tied to this ad keep
S3 the argument is not illustrated (B1: "clear but little developed"); loses the contrast link, so links drop to two keep
S3 "could" / "I'd" the scenario becomes an unhedged claim about what the company offers (standard 9) keep
S4 no hand-over; the first objection becomes a lottery and turn 2 cannot be prepared keep
S5 the reply ignores the friend's words; no concession anywhere, so the link condition fails keep
S6 no counter (B1 rung: "but it's nice!") keep
S6 "no ski pass to pay" the counter becomes a bare claim with no reason keep
S7 the second reply no longer opens on the friend's words (standard 17) keep
S8 the second defence has no content; "defends firmly" fails keep
S8 "for one weekend" loses the weekend format, which is what makes the hassle out of proportion; generic keep
S9 loses the consequence link (links drop to two), the second hard structure in a reply, and devis keep
Deleted in this revision "So, does it tempt you?", "we can each have our own programme", "in the paper", "for the two of us", "Sure, you could do it yourself", "only", "at least", "tonight", "real": nothing broke without them gone

9. Examiner-reply test (three plausible replies per examiner turn)

Frames for lines that echo the friend (standard 13): S5 is "It's true that [reword their worry], but..." and fits any price wording; S7 is "[their verb] yourself?" (organise it / book it / plan it yourself?); for any other objection, open on their noun as a question ("The mountains?", "Dangerous?", "Too short?").

E0, the greeting.

  • Helpful: Salut ! Tu vas bien ? Then S1 as written.
  • Partial: Salut ! Say Salut ! instead of Super !, then the rest of S1.
  • Surprising: Salut... je suis crevé(e), quelle semaine ! Then: "Oh no! Then listen: I found a company that..." The tiredness even helps the weekend idea.

E1, after the catch.

  • Helpful (most likely): "Exactly, tailor-made is going to cost a fortune." Then turn 2 as written.
  • Partial: "Hmm, I don't know" or "How much is it?" Turn 2 still fits, because S5 takes up the catch you named yourself.
  • Surprising 1: "I can organise that myself." Play turn 3 first, with climbing instead of the sea: "Organise it yourself? But finding a climbing instructor and renting the gear for one weekend is a hassle. So let's ask them for a quote, so that you can compare." Keep turn 2 for when the price comes up.
  • Surprising 2: "Diving and climbing are dangerous." Then: "Dangerous? I'd be surprised if they had beginners dive without an instructor." (Dangereux ? Ça m'étonnerait qu'ils fassent plonger des débutants sans moniteur.) Then: "So let's ask them how they supervise beginners, so that you can decide." (comment, not "what").
  • Surprising 3: "A weekend is too short, we'd spend it on the road." Then: "Too short? Precisely: with only two days, I don't want to waste them looking for gear and an instructor." Then S9.
  • Surprising 4: an information question, e.g. "Where is it exactly?" Then: "They don't say; you choose when you contact them." Wait for the objection; the point moves back by one exchange.

E2, after the sea answer.

  • Helpful (most likely): "Even so, I can organise that myself, for less." Then turn 3 as written.
  • Partial: "Hmm, the sea, why not..." Then drop S7: "And honestly, finding a diving instructor and renting surfboards ourselves for one weekend is a hassle." Then S9.
  • Surprising 1: "But I want the mountains, I love skiing!" Then: "The mountains? Fine, but with the ski pass, let's ask them for two quotes, so that you can compare."
  • Surprising 2: "Diving isn't cheap either." Then: "Diving? True, but kayaking along the coast costs almost nothing." Then S9.
  • Surprising 3: "It's dangerous" or "a weekend is too short": the E1 fallbacks above.

10. The sceptical rater (wants to give a 6)

Their best case. "Everything before the first objection could be memorised: a cleft, a contrast clause, the classic prep moves. The candidate then engineered the first objection by naming the price, so turn 2 is a prepared answer with a predictable cue. The replies are short (21 and 29 English words), and the box stops at under a minute of speech. The hard structures are the prep-book set: si + imperfect, pour que tu puisses. I hear a well-prepared lower B2."

How the box answers it.

  • The proof is not in turn 1. Turn 1 frames the ad, gives the one C1 glimpse (the plan) and calls the probe.
  • Turn 2 still has to fit the friend's words (it opens by rewording them), and it uses the ad's own choice (sea or mountain) as a cost lever. No prep list contains "choose the sea, no ski pass".
  • Turn 3 answers an objection you did not call, opens by repeating the friend's words, builds on what you said in turn 2 (sea, so diving and surfing), and its proposal answers the friend's exact "for less" (a quote, to compare). A memorised script does not follow on from its own previous answer and from the friend's last two words like that.
  • The hard structures carry content specific to this ad and this exchange (the ski pass, a quote to compare), not stock phrases.
  • Short replies: 15 to 40 words is the right length for a reply. The time floor is met (58 seconds) and everything after it is the same speaker continuing.

The case fails, and the deletion test (section 8) was re-run on the result.

11. Say-aloud test

  • S1 is the longest sentence (20 English words): one breath to montagne, a pause, then the range. Fine at a natural pace.
  • S2: c'est que c'est sur mesure is a common sequence.
  • S3: alors que je prendrais is one breath group; practise prendrais.
  • S6: practise choisirait until it comes out without a pause.
  • S8: the long subject is easy because c'est la galère picks it up; pause after week-end.
  • S9: pour que tu puisses comparer is one breath group.
  • Nothing in the box agrees with the friend's gender, so you never have to choose partant / partante or crevé / crevée on the fly.

12. Count and timing (standard conversion)

  • Your words in English: 100 (turn 1: 50, turn 2: 21, turn 3: 29).
  • French words = 100 x 1.05 = about 105. At 120 a minute plus 10% for pauses: 100 x 0.58 = about 58 seconds of your speech.
  • Examiner turns: the greeting and two objections, about 10 seconds, plus about a second at each change of speaker.
  • The clock starts at the friend's "Hi! How are you?". The point: about 1:10 into Section B (1:05 to 1:25). That leaves nearly nine minutes in which only a disaster counts.

13. Reserve (after the point; not part of the box)

  1. Two levels, one weekend together: "we can each have our own programme, and in the evening we meet up" (on peut avoir chacun notre programme, et le soir, on se retrouve), the answer to "then why go together?".
  2. Season logic: sea in summer, mountains in winter, "we could do two weekends" (on pourrait en faire deux), the answer to "I prefer the mountains".
  3. Novelty for a sport lover with a routine: a first dive (un baptême de plongée), something you cannot do in town.
  4. Cost again, a different kind of argument: "with friends, we'd share the accommodation, so it works out cheaper" (entre amis, on partage l'hébergement, ça revient moins cher).
  5. Safety hedge: ça m'étonnerait qu'ils fassent plonger des débutants sans moniteur.
  6. Time: "a weekend is enough to change scenery without taking days off" (sans poser de congés).
  7. Weather: "if the forecast is bad, we'll ask them for another activity" (si la météo est mauvaise, on leur demandera une autre activité).
  8. A late compromise: "How about we try one weekend at the sea, just to see?" (Et si on essayait un seul week-end à la mer, juste pour voir ?).
  9. Plain questions to keep the exchange going: "Would you choose the sea or the mountains?", "Which sport would you like to try?", "When are you free?"
  10. The general closer that fits any objection: "Let's look at their website tonight, so that you can decide" (pour que tu puisses te décider).

After review

Review B2, B-26 section:

  • Cut 1, "So, does it tempt you?": applied. The catch is the hand-over (standard 3); the consequence link now comes from S9 "So".
  • Cut 2, "we can each have our own programme": applied. The climbing sentence carries the idea; the phrase moved to the reserve.
  • Cut 3, "in the paper": applied, and S1 merged with the company sentence ("Great! I found a company that..."), which also removed "for the two of us" (standard 18: one-word greeting, straight into the ad).
  • Cut 4, "Sure, you could do it yourself": applied. Standard 17 still needs the reply to open on the friend's words, so it is replaced by the 3-word echo "Organise it yourself?" (6 words became 3).
  • Suggestion 1, translator notes: applied (section 5). Lexis lists now count only precise words the English produces; truc is gone, and boîte is counted as register, not precision.

Across the chunk:

  • 1 (audible subjunctives): S9 puisses is the only one counted; the fallback fassent is also audible. No change needed.
  • 2 (indirect "what"): none in the box; reserve item 7 reworded from "ask them what they offer instead" to "ask them for another activity"; the danger fallback uses "how".
  • 3 (hand-over): applied (cut 1).
  • 4 (grants): the turn-3 grant is cut; the turn-2 grant stays because it carries the only concession link and the uptake.
  • 5 (time floor): the box is now at the floor, 100 words, about 58 seconds.
  • 6 (links): recounted as contrast, concession, consequence. "while" became "whereas" (temporal pendant que does not count); "Then" became "So"; the si clause and "no ski pass" are no longer counted as links.
  • 7 (timing basis): the clock starts at "Hi! How are you?"; the point is about 1:10.
  • 8 (translator-dependent lexis): applied (section 5).
  • 9 (facts not on the ad): the climbing scenario is now hedged ("could", "I'd"); the price line keeps "probably"; the proposal asks for a quote and claims nothing.

B-09

A CHEF IN YOUR KITCHEN

Food · Chef/caterer-service · est. #26 of 81
The prompt, English | French
Prompt

You have read this advertisement. You talk about it to a friend who wishes to organize an evening party.

A CHEF IN YOUR KITCHEN

A Chef in your kitchen!

Book a professional cook in your home.

Experience a unique culinary adventure.

Discover our varied menus and

our know-how...

Our Chefs take care of everything!

New clients: 10% discount

Information: www.unchefdansvotrecuisine.com

Consigne

Vous avez lu cette publicité. Vous en parlez à un(e) ami(e) qui souhaite organiser une soirée.

UN CHEF DANS VOTRE CUISINE

Un Chef dans votre cuisine !

Réservez un chef cuisinier chez vous.

Vivez une expérience culinaire unique.

Découvrez nos menus variés et

notre savoir-faire...

Nos Chefs s’occupent de tout !

Nouveaux clients : 10 % de réduction

Informations : www.unchefdansvotrecuisine.com

The point

The point falls at the end of your third turn, the reply to the friend's second objection. That is about 1:20 after the examiner's "Hi! How are you?": about 66 seconds of your French plus three short examiner turns.

The box114 words

[examiner: "Hi! How are you?"]

Great! For your party, I've found something. You get a real chef to come and cook at your place. You pick a menu, and they do everything, normally even the shopping and the dishes. What I like is that you'd be with your guests instead of stuck at the stove. You'll tell me it costs a fortune.

[examiner: "Exactly! A chef at home must cost a fortune."]

It's true that it's not cheap. But compared with a restaurant bill, I'd be surprised if it was much more expensive per head. Besides, at home you serve your own wine.

[examiner: "Maybe, but cooking myself is still cheaper."]

Since you'd rather cook, how about we ask them to do just the main course? The quote would be lower, and you'd still make the dessert.

Why this, and why nothing less

A 6 is "B2"; a 7 is "B2 beyond doubt, with a glimpse of C1". This box gives each of the four criteria that in three turns, two of them replies to the friend.

Task. Your first turn presents the ad in your own words, in a clear order: what it is (a real chef comes to cook at your place), how it works (you pick a menu), what it covers (everything, probably even the shopping and the dishes), and why it suits a host (you'd be with your guests, not at the stove). Then you name the objection yourself; that is the hand-over. Price is the obvious one, so the first objection is close to certain. Each reply opens on the friend's own point: "it's not cheap" rewords "a fortune", and "since you'd rather cook" rewords "cooking myself". Reply 1 concedes, then counters with a comparison and a concrete detail (a restaurant bill per head against your own wine at home): an illustrated argument. Reply 2 follows a push you did not choose and keeps the plan alive with a proposal that answers both likely pushes at once: it costs less, and you still cook. That is "defends firmly". The C1 glimpse is the variety: three arguments of three kinds (your evening as host, money, a split of the meal), at no extra words.

Syntax. Six different marked structures (grammar forms only stronger speakers use reliably): faire venir (have someone come), a cleft (a sentence split to put the focus first: ce qui me plaît, c'est que), a conditional, an audible subjunctive (ça m'étonnerait que ce soit), a suggestion with the imperfect (et si on leur demandait), and pronoun plus verb plus infinitive (leur demander de faire). The two hardest sit inside the replies, where they can't have been recited against the friend's words. Three kinds of link joining clauses: concession (c'est vrai que... mais), addition (en plus), cause (puisque, opening reply 2).

Lexis. Six precise words, none on the ad, none supplied by the examiner, none everyday: coincé, les fourneaux, l'addition, par personne, le devis, le plat principal. Household words (invités, courses, vaisselle) are not counted. Coincé is the informal expression.

Delivery. Short sentences of everyday words, one or two breath groups each.

Why nothing can go. "Great!" answers the greeting, and "for your party" ties the ad to the friend. "A real chef" is the ad's one claim about who comes. Without "you pick a menu" the presentation loses its "how"; without "even the shopping and the dishes" it loses its detail. The cleft sentence is the only argument before the objection. The hand-over makes the first objection predictable; without it reply 1 is a bet. In reply 1, the concession is the box's only concession and rewords the friend, the restaurant line carries the counter, the subjunctive and two precise words (bill, per head), and the wine line turns a bare claim into an illustrated one and carries the addition link. In reply 2, "since you'd rather cook" is the uptake and the cause link; the proposal is the second defence; "the quote would be lower" (with devis) and "you'd still make the dessert" each answer one half of the push.

The next cut. At 114 words (about 66 seconds) the box is 10 words above the floor of condition 4. The deletion test found no dead word, so nothing goes; the next cut would take out a precise word, a link or a reply.

What the box assumes. That the first objection is price (you invite it) and the second is "still too expensive" or "I'd rather cook myself"; for anything else, the matching reply in Working swaps in. That after the point you keep answering each new objection with a reason, in plain correct French, tu throughout, drawing on the reserve (the 10 percent, a stranger in the kitchen, a small kitchen, guests' diets, the chef as entertainment), without giving in or repeating one argument three times. And that you can say ça m'étonnerait que ce soit and et si on leur demandait at your normal pace.

The working for this prompt

1. Dissection

What the ad prints (none of it counts as lexical evidence): un chef dans votre cuisine, réservez un chef cuisinier chez vous, vivez une expérience culinaire unique, nos menus variés, notre savoir-faire, nos chefs s'occupent de tout, nouveaux clients 10 % de réduction, the website.

What it leaves unsaid: the price (per person? a minimum number of guests? groceries on top?), what "tout" covers (shopping, cooking, serving at table, washing up), how many guests a chef can handle, diets and allergies, drinks, the size and equipment of the kitchen, how far ahead to book, cancellation, whether the chef stays through dessert. The missing price matters most: you can't quote a figure, so any price argument has to be a comparison or a hedge.

Who is on the other end: a friend (tu) who wants to organise a party and has been told to disagree. The friend's natural alternatives to a chef are cooking themselves, a restaurant, or a caterer or pizza.

The examiner's predictable moves for this ad:

  • Opens with "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?".
  • May ask an information question in character before objecting ("C'est combien ?", "Ils font quoi exactement ?").
  • Objections drawn from the ad and from stereotypes, my ranking of likelihood as the first one:
    1. price ("ça doit coûter une fortune", "c'est pour les riches");
    2. do it myself ("je sais cuisiner", "je préfère cuisiner moi-même, c'est plus personnel", "c'est moins cher");
    3. a stranger at home ("je n'aime pas qu'un inconnu touche à ma cuisine"), or "ma cuisine est trop petite";
    4. the guests ("j'ai des amis végétariens / allergiques", "et si ce n'est pas bon ?", "c'est trop chic pour mes potes").
  • Pushes on the same point again ("même avec ça, c'est plus cher que de cuisiner moi-même") and asks for new arguments ("c'est tout ?").

2. The hard part

Hardest condition: an only-here argument with precise words. The ad's selling points ("unique experience", "they take care of everything") are generic; every candidate says c'est pratique, c'est original. The food domain is everyday vocabulary (menu, chef, repas), so precision has to come from the host's evening and from money talk, not from the ad.

Where the only-here material comes from:

  • The host's evening. At a party you throw at home, the host is usually stuck in the kitchen; a chef in your kitchen is the only option that keeps the party at home and frees the host (a restaurant moves the party; a caterer still leaves serving and washing up).

  • Reading "tout" precisely. "They do everything" becomes les courses et la vaisselle, hedged with normalement because the ad doesn't spell it out.

  • The split service. "The chef does the main course, you make your dessert" only makes sense for a chef cooking in your own kitchen.

Precise words this topic offers (not on the ad): les invités, les courses, la vaisselle, aux fourneaux, coincé, le plat principal, l'entrée, le dessert, une dizaine, le resto, son propre vin, l'apéro, un traiteur, un devis, par personne, les avis (reviews), végétarien, allergique, prévenir à l'avance, mettre de l'ambiance.

Second hard part: replies that stay credible. Two objection replies are needed, and the friend's second move is not yours to choose. The fix is a second reply that answers the two likeliest pushes at once (cost and "I'd rather cook").

3. Designs compared

Design Shape Min's words to the point Against the playbook Verdict
D1 Classic full presentation (menus, offer, site) + two arguments + "what do you think?", then two replies to whatever comes about 150 to 165 first objection unknown, so reply 1 is improvised; two arguments before the hand-over spend reserve rejected: more words, weaker first reply
D2 Called probe (chosen) presentation with one judging clause, then "You'll tell me it costs a fortune" as the hand-over; reply 1 on price; reply 2 opens on the friend's point and makes a proposal that fits both likely pushes 114 first objection close to certain; second reply survives the two likeliest pushes chosen
D3 The friend's numbers ask "how many are you inviting?" first, then build the price reply on their number about 140 adds an examiner turn; the answer may be "I don't know", a huge number, or an objection; the reply needs a slot rejected: a slot and a coin flip for 15 extra words
D4 Two-headed hand-over "you'll say it's expensive, or that you don't want a stranger in your kitchen" about 135 both objections predictable, but you plant the one with the weakest counter, and naming two straw men sounds like a technique rejected; the stranger reply goes to the fallbacks
D5 The friend's answer as evidence open with "are you cooking everything yourself again?" so the presentation becomes a reply ("precisely...") about 135 makes the presentation contingent, but the presentation's content is the ad either way, so the gain is small; one more examiner turn rejected: cost above value
D6 Pre-buttal answer price inside the presentation so the friend must object to something else about 140 the strongest counter is spent in a turn that can't count as a reply, and the objections become unpredictable rejected

Why D2 wins: it has the fewest words and the most predictable examiner turns. The hand-over costs 7 words and buys a near-certain first objection. Reply 2 is written at right angles to the second push: "the chef does the main course, you make the dessert" answers "still too expensive" (it costs less), "cheaper if I cook" (it costs less, and you cook), and "I like cooking for my friends" (you still cook), so the proposal holds whichever of the three the friend picks; only the opening reword changes (section 7).

4. Structure note for each sentence (the French the English is written to carry)

# English Target French What it carries
T1.1 Great! For your party, I've found something. Super ! Pour ta soirée, j'ai trouvé un truc. one-word greeting; tu; ties the ad to the friend's party
T1.2 You get a real chef to come and cook at your place. Tu fais venir un vrai chef pour cuisiner chez toi. causative faire venir (marked); the ad reworded (un vrai chef for "chef cuisinier")
T1.3 You pick a menu, and they do everything, normally even the shopping and the dishes. Tu choisis un menu, et ils font tout : normalement, même les courses et la vaisselle. the how and the coverage; les courses, la vaisselle; hedge normalement (the ad says only "tout")
T1.4 What I like is that you'd be with your guests instead of stuck at the stove. Ce qui me plaît, c'est que tu serais avec tes invités au lieu d'être coincé aux fourneaux. cleft (marked, low risk); conditional serais; invités, coincé, fourneaux; the only-here argument. coincé / coincée sound the same, so the friend's gender costs nothing
T1.5 You'll tell me it costs a fortune. Tu vas me dire que ça coûte une fortune. the hand-over: names the weak spot, no question after it
T2.1 It's true that it's not cheap. C'est vrai que c'est pas donné. concession link; rewords the friend's "fortune"
T2.2 But compared with a restaurant bill, I'd be surprised if it was much more expensive per head. Mais par rapport à l'addition d'un resto, ça m'étonnerait que ce soit beaucoup plus cher par personne. audible subjunctive soit (hard, inside a reply; the mapping in FIRST_PRINCIPLES section E); l'addition, par personne (transaction words); a hedge, so no invented price
T2.3 Besides, at home you serve your own wine. En plus, chez toi, tu sers ton propre vin. addition link; the concrete detail that makes the counter illustrated
T3.1 Since you'd rather cook, how about we ask them to do just the main course? Puisque tu préfères cuisiner, et si on leur demandait de faire seulement le plat principal ? opens on the friend's words; cause link puisque; et si on + imperfect (suggestion, medium); leur demander de + infinitive; le plat principal; a question to ask the company, not an invented fact
T3.2 The quote would be lower, and you'd still make the dessert. Le devis serait moins élevé, et toi, tu ferais quand même le dessert. two conditionals that follow from the suggestion, so the cost claim sits inside the hypothesis; le devis; each clause answers one half of the push

Load per sentence: no sentence has more than two marked structures or more than one hard one; the longest English sentences are 17 words (T2.2) and 16 words (T1.4). No two dependent mood choices in one sentence.

5. Coverage audit against the five conditions

  1. Frame. tu from the first sentence; the ad identified and presented in your own words (T1.2, T1.3, nothing read out); tied to the friend's party (T1.1, T1.4).

  2. Capacities.

    • Task: detailed presentation (what, how, coverage, why it suits a host); illustrated argument (T2: restaurant against your own wine at home); two replies at the B2 rung, each opening on the friend's words (T2 concedes and counters; T3 keeps going when pushed, with a counter-proposal); one C1 glimpse: three arguments of different kinds (the host's evening, money, a split of the meal); only-here argument (T1.4, and the split service in T3).

    • Syntax: six marked types (causative, cleft, conditional, subjunctive, et si + imperfect, pronoun + demander de + infinitive), two of them inside replies (subjunctive in T2, et si in T3). Links joining clauses, three relations: concession (c'est vrai que... mais), addition (en plus), cause (puisque). Not counted: au lieu de (a preposition), plain et / mais.

    • Lexis: six precise words, none on the ad, none supplied by the examiner: coincé, fourneaux, l'addition, par personne, devis, plat principal. Not counted: menu, chef, cuisine (on the ad); invités, courses, vaisselle, restaurant, wine, dessert (everyday household words); fortune (the examiner repeats it). Informal: coincé; c'est pas donné if the translator note is followed.

    • Delivery: one word to practise (fourneaux; coincé devant la cuisinière or coincé en cuisine also works). 3. Replication. Evidence in T1 (presentation, causative, cleft), T2 (reply, subjunctive, concession) and T3 (reply, et si + imperfect, conditional, cause link); two of the three are replies.

  3. Control presumed. 114 English words x 0.58 = about 66 seconds of your speech (standard conversion: French words = English x 1.05, 120 French words a minute, plus 10 percent for pauses).

  4. Credibility. Both replies open on the friend's point; T3 answers a push you did not choose.

6. Deletion test (re-run on the revised box)

Pieces that stay, and what breaks without each:

Piece Without it Stays?
"Great!" the first words ignore the examiner's greeting (standard 18 asks for one word) yes (1 word)
"For your party, I've found something." the presentation is no longer tied to the friend's situation (frame) yes
T1.2 "You get a real chef to come and cook at your place" no statement of what the ad is in your own words (frame); loses the causative yes
"real" the ad's one claim about who comes (a professional) drops out of the presentation yes (1 word, closest call)
T1.3 "You pick a menu" the presentation loses its "how" and its only mention of the menus yes
T1.3 "and they do everything" "even the shopping and the dishes" has nothing to hang on yes
T1.3 "normally even the shopping and the dishes" presentation less detailed; the only line that reads the ad's "tout" precisely yes
"normally" the shopping and dishes become an unhedged invented fact (standard 9) yes
T1.4 cleft judging clause no argument before the objection; the only-here argument, the cleft and two precise words (coincé, fourneaux) go yes
T1.5 hand-over the first objection becomes a bet, so reply 1 may not fit yes
T2.1 concession no concession link (Section B needs one) and reply 1 no longer opens on the friend's words yes
T2.2 comparison + subjunctive no counter at all; the hard structure inside a reply goes yes

"per head" (T2.2) par personne goes; lexis drops to five yes (2 words, added in the audit)
