T3 "The quote would be lower" the cost half of the push is unanswered, and devis goes (lexis drops to five) yes (2 words more than "It'd cost less", for the precise word)
T3 "and you'd still make the dessert" the "I'd rather cook" half is unanswered yes

Pieces cut, across the first draft and the revision (all five conditions held without them):

  • "the party you told me about" (dont): a structure in a rehearsable opener earns little; six types remain.
  • "It's a service where...", then "Basically," (review cut 1): register is carried elsewhere.
  • "online" (review cut 2): the ad prints a website for information, not online ordering.
  • "and you?", "for you", "Listen", "Fine, thanks" (now "Great!"): no evidence; the greeting is one word.
  • "I know what you're going to say:" (review fix 2): the indirect "what" trap; now "You'll tell me".
  • "New clients get ten percent off": a printed fact, moved to the reserve for the next price push.
  • "for once": aimed the argument at the friend but carried no structure, link or precise word.
  • "for about ten people": the wine detail already illustrates the counter; lexis holds without une dizaine.

  • "OK, let's meet halfway" (review cut 3) and then "OK, so": replaced by "Since you'd rather cook", which is both the uptake and the third link.

  • "your famous chocolate cake": now "the dessert", which pairs with "the main course" and makes the split explicit.

  • "But we keep the chef!": firmness already shows, because the plan survives.

7. Examiner-reply test (three plausible replies per examiner turn)

Examiner turn Helpful Partial Surprising Min's next line
Greeting "Salut ! Tu vas bien ?" "Salut, ça va ?" "Salut ! Quoi de neuf ?" "Super ! Pour ta soirée, j'ai trouvé un truc." fits all three
After the hand-over "Exactement, ça doit coûter une fortune !" "Ben oui... c'est combien ?" "Non, l'argent ça va, mais un inconnu dans ma cuisine..." T2 fits the first two: "c'est vrai que c'est pas donné" rewords the friend's "fortune", and after "c'est combien ?" it answers without a figure (the ad gives none). For the third, swap in fallback F1; T2 waits for the next price push
After reply 1 "Peut-être, mais c'est moins cher si je cuisine moi-même." "Même avec ça, c'est trop cher pour moi." "Moi, j'aime cuisiner pour mes amis, c'est plus personnel." T3's proposal fits all three (the split costs less and you still cook). Only the opening reword follows the friend (standard 13): after the first and third, "Puisque tu préfères cuisiner"; after the second, "Puisque c'est trop cher pour toi". A new objection of another family gets F1 to F4

Fallbacks (each opens on the friend's words and is a reply, so the point still falls on the third turn):

  • F1, stranger in the kitchen: "Un inconnu chez toi, je comprends que ça te fasse bizarre, mais c'est son métier : il a l'habitude de cuisiner chez des gens qu'il ne connaît pas. Et toi, tu seras là toute la soirée." (audible fasse)

  • F2, kitchen too small: "Ta cuisine est petite, d'accord, mais c'est un pro, il sait se débrouiller. Et on peut leur poser la question en réservant." (gérondif en réservant)

  • F3, guests' diets: "Tes amis végétariens ? Justement, ils ont plusieurs menus : on en choisit un sans viande, et on les prévient des allergies à l'avance." (en, prévenir à l'avance)

  • F4, "what if it's bad?": "Si ce n'est pas bon ? On peut lire les avis sur leur site avant de réserver. Et franchement, un pro qui rate un repas, ça m'étonnerait." (avant de + infinitive; rate would be an inaudible subjunctive, so the hedge comes after the clause)

  • "Are you sure they do the dishes?": "Pas sûr, non : ils disent qu'ils s'occupent de tout. On leur demandera." (future)

8. The sceptical rater

Their best case for a 6. "The candidate announced the objection, so both replies may be prepared. Strip the prepared material and you have two short turns. The grammar is set phrases (ce qui me plaît, c'est que, ça m'étonnerait que), the vocabulary is everyday, the presentation leaves out the only figure on the ad (10 percent), and the second reply gives ground: half a chef. That is lower B2: clear, detailed enough, defends once, then compromises."

How the box answers it.

  1. Prepared replies. Calling the probe is the published playbook used in your favour, and both replies open on the friend's own point ("not cheap" for their "fortune", "since you'd rather cook" for their "cooking myself"). The second push is the friend's choice, and T3's proposal fits all three likely pushes, with only the opening reword following what they said.

  2. Set phrases. Two of the six marked types are common frames, each completed with content built for this ad; the other four (causative, et si + imperfect, leur demander de + infinitive, conditionals that follow from the suggestion) are assembled on the spot. Six types in about a minute is the C1 "variety of structures" glimpse.

  3. Everyday words. The six counted words (coincé, fourneaux, l'addition, par personne, devis, plat principal) are precise words for a host and a bill, chosen by you; none is on the ad and none comes from the examiner. The household words (invités, courses, vaisselle) are not counted.

  4. Omitted offer. The grid rates a presentation as clear and detailed, not complete; the offer is kept for the next price push, where it is an argument. The shopping and dishes are hedged and come from the ad's own "s'occupent de tout".

  5. Giving ground. Giving in means dropping the plan ("tu as raison, laisse tomber"). T3 keeps the chef and negotiates the scope: the CEFR puts negotiation at B2, and it answers both halves of the push.

After the revision the deletion test was run again (section 6); every remaining piece holds a condition or a standard.

9. Say-aloud test

Turn French breath groups Note
T1.1 Super ! / Pour ta soirée, / j'ai trouvé un truc. easy
T1.2 Tu fais venir un vrai chef / pour cuisiner chez toi. easy
T1.3 Tu choisis un menu, / et ils font tout : / normalement, / même les courses et la vaisselle. a list; pause before normalement
T1.4 Ce qui me plaît, / c'est que tu serais avec tes invités / au lieu d'être coincé aux fourneaux. longest; practise fourneaux
T1.5 Tu vas me dire / que ça coûte une fortune. easy
T2.1 C'est vrai que c'est pas donné. easy
T2.2 Mais par rapport à l'addition d'un resto, / ça m'étonnerait que ce soit / beaucoup plus cher par personne. the hard structure; que ce soit must not come out as que c'est
T2.3 En plus, chez toi, / tu sers ton propre vin. easy
T3.1 Puisque tu préfères cuisiner, / et si on leur demandait / de faire seulement le plat principal ? rising intonation on the question; demandait, not demande
T3.2 Le devis serait moins élevé, / et toi, tu ferais quand même le dessert. easy

No sentence needs a stop in the middle; every pause falls at a clause boundary.

10. Count and timing (standard conversion)

  • Min's English words: 114 (T1 57, T2 31, T3 26). French: 114 x 1.05 = about 120 words.

  • Your speech: 114 x 0.58 = about 66 seconds (120 French words a minute plus 10 percent for pauses). The floor of condition 4 is about 104 English words, so the box sits 10 words above it.

  • Examiner turns: greeting about 2 seconds, two objections about 4 seconds each, plus turn-taking.

  • The point: end of T3, about 1:20 after "Hi! How are you?". If the friend slips in an information question ("c'est combien ?") before objecting, add 10 to 15 seconds.

11. Reserve (after the point; not part of the box)

  • The offer, for the next price push: "Et en plus, comme t'es un nouveau client, t'as dix pour cent de réduction."

  • Time and effort: "Si tu faisais tout toi-même, tu passerais la journée à faire les courses et la soirée à faire la vaisselle." (si + imperfect + conditional, kept out of the box on purpose)

  • The chef as entertainment (only-here): "Tes invités pourront le regarder travailler et lui poser des questions : ça met de l'ambiance."

  • A party people talk about, said plainly, not as a cliché: "Pour une fois, ce ne serait pas pizza ou barbecue."

  • Fallbacks F1 to F4 (section 7) for the stranger, the small kitchen, diets, and "what if it's bad".

  • Too formal for my friends: "Les menus sont variés, on n'est pas obligés de prendre un truc chic ; on peut choisir quelque chose de simple."

  • Closing move that keeps the plan alive: "On regarde leurs menus ensemble ce soir ? Si rien ne te plaît, on n'en parle plus."

  • Rules for the rest: tu throughout; a reason with every answer; no argument more than twice; never "tu as raison, laisse tomber".

Translator notes

None of these overrules what the English says; each picks between natural renderings, and no count in this file depends on them except where marked.

  • "it's not cheap": c'est pas donné (informal) rather than ce n'est pas bon marché. Not counted as a precise word.

  • "I'd be surprised if it was much more expensive": ça m'étonnerait que ce soit beaucoup plus cher (FIRST_PRINCIPLES section E). If it comes out as je serais surpris si c'était..., that is still an audible marked structure; never que c'est. The subjunctive count depends on this line.

  • "stuck at the stove": coincé aux fourneaux, not devant la cuisinière (which echoes the ad's chef cuisinier). The fourneaux count depends on this line.

  • "a restaurant bill": l'addition (or la note); "per head": par personne (or par tête); "the quote": le devis.

  • "a restaurant": un resto is fine in this register; un restaurant too. Not counted.
  • "I've found something": j'ai trouvé un truc or quelque chose. Not counted.

  • "how about we ask them": et si on leur demandait (imperfect), not pourquoi pas leur demander. The et si count depends on this line.

  • "Since you'd rather cook": Puisque tu préfères cuisiner (or préférerais).

After review

Review items (reviews/B2.md, B-09):

  • Fix 1, inaudible subjunctive in "if it cost much more": applied. Now "if it was much more expensive" (que ce soit).
  • Fix 2, indirect "what" in the hand-over: applied. Now "You'll tell me it costs a fortune" (7 words, not 11).
  • Cut 1, "Basically,": applied.
  • Cut 2, "online": applied (also standard 9).
  • Cut 3, "let's meet halfway": applied. "OK," was kept at first, then replaced by "Since you'd rather cook" (standard 17).
  • Suggestion, translator note for pas donné: taken, under Translator notes.
  • Across the chunk 6, par rapport à is not a link: agreed; no longer counted.
  • Across the chunk 8, lexis that depends on the translator: resto, pas donné, en principe no longer counted.

Standards (STANDARDS.md, 19 items):

  • 1 audible subjunctives: the only subjunctive counted is soit; F4's inaudible rate is not counted.
  • 2 no indirect "what": fixed in the hand-over; no other indirect question in the box.
  • 3 hand-over: the named weak spot is the hand-over; no question after it.

  • 4 grants and reaffirms: one concession (T2.1), which also rewords the friend; the T3 grant ("let's meet halfway") and the reaffirm ("we keep the chef") are gone.

  • 5 time floor: on the standard conversion 109 words = about 63 seconds after the review (114 words = about 66 seconds after the audit), above the 104-word floor. Above the floor, dead words went: "Listen", "for once", "for about ten people", "OK", "famous chocolate cake" became "the dessert" (124 to 109 overall).

  • 6 links: counted only clause connectors; three relations including a concession: concession, addition, cause. au lieu de and par rapport à are no longer counted. Adding puisque replaced the consequence "so".

  • 7 timing basis: the clock starts at "Hi! How are you?"; the point is at about 1:20 (1:15 before the audit).

  • 8 translator notes: added.

  • 9 facts not on the ad: "online" cut; "normally" hedges the shopping and dishes; the price claim is a hedge; the cost of a main course only sits inside the "how about" hypothesis; nothing else goes beyond the ad.

  • 10, 11 (Section A only): not applicable.

  • 12 and 19 precise words: after the final audit the list counts coincé, fourneaux, l'addition, par personne, devis, plat principal; everyday household words, ad words and the examiner's "fortune" are out.

  • 13 lines that depend on the examiner: T3's opening is a frame ("Puisque" + the friend's point in your words), given for all three likely pushes in section 7.

  • 14 format: the box opens with [examiner: "Hi! How are you?"].

  • 15 the English makes its own French: checked line by line (section 4); no note overrules the English.
  • 16 one C1 glimpse: the Why now names one (varied arguments); no words are spent on a second.
  • 17 replies open on the friend's words: T2 rewords "a fortune"; T3 now opens "Since you'd rather cook".
  • 18 one-word greeting: "Fine, thanks!" became "Great!".

Final audit (reviews/audit-B.md, B-09):

  • Item 1, only two or three real precise words: applied by swapping, not adding sentences. "a restaurant" became "a restaurant bill" (l'addition), "much more expensive" gained "per head" (par personne), "It'd cost less" became "The quote would be lower" (devis); invités, courses, vaisselle are no longer counted. Six precise words; 109 to 114.

  • Item 2, the "next cut" wording: applied. Why now says the box is 10 words above the floor and the deletion test found no dead word.
